# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 2

## Page 383

Part Two
Book of Allah’s Oneness in Lordship

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

All praise belongs to Allah alone, and blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet.

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyya—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds. I bear witness that there is no god but Allah alone, without partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant them peace.

A Foundational Principle:

For those who believe, the foundation, starting point, and primary evidence for knowledge of divine matters is faith in Allah and His Messenger. For the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, it is Allah’s revelation to him, as stated by

---

## Page 384

the Seal of the Prophets:

“I have been commanded to fight the people until they testify that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah. When they do so, they safeguard their lives and property from me, except by due right.”

Allah Most High said to him:

“Say, ‘If I go astray, it is only to my own detriment; but if I am guided, it is through what my Lord reveals to me.’”

And He said:

“And He found you lost and guided you.”

And He said:

“We relate to you the best of stories through Our revelation to you of this Quran, though before it you were among those unaware.”

---

## Page 385

Thus He stated that, before it, he had been among those unaware. And He said:

“Thus We have revealed to you a spirit by Our command. You did not know what the Book was, nor what faith was; but We made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants.”

Sahīh al-Bukhārī records, in Umar’s address when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, died, a statement to this effect: “Allah guided your Prophet through this Quran, so hold fast to it, for you . . .” (1) (*).

The Quran frequently establishes the proof through the messengers, as in His saying:

“So that people would have no argument against Allah after the messengers.”

And His saying:

“We would never punish until We had sent a messenger.”

And His saying:

“Had We destroyed them with a punishment before it, they would have said, ‘Our Lord, why did You not send us a messenger, so that we might have followed Your signs?’”—to the end of the verse (2).

And His saying:

“Your Lord would not destroy the towns until He had sent a messenger to their principal town”—to the end of the verse.

And His saying:

“Whenever a group is cast into it, its keepers ask them, ‘Did no warner come to you?’”

And His saying:

“Those who disbelieved will be driven to Hell in groups until, when they reach it

## Page 386

its gates will be opened, and its keepers will say to them, ‘Did messengers from among yourselves not come to you?’”—to the end of the verse.

And His saying:

“O company of jinn and mankind”—to the end of the verse.

For this reason, some of the leading compilers of Sunan collections arranged in topical chapters, when bringing together the various branches of knowledge, began with the foundation of knowledge and faith. Thus al-Bukhārī began his Sahīh with the beginning of revelation and its descent, first describing how knowledge and faith were revealed to the Messenger. He then followed this with the Book of Faith, which is the acknowledgment of what the Messenger brought, and then with the Book of Knowledge, which is the understanding of what he brought. He thereby arranged it in its proper order. Likewise, Imam Abū Muhammad al-Dārimī, the author of (al-Musnad), began his book with the proofs of prophethood and included a substantial selection of material on that subject.

These two men are far superior to Muslim, al-Tirmidhī, and others like them. For this reason, Ahmad ibn Hanbal held these two and their peers in great esteem, because they possessed a profound understanding of hadith, both in its foundations and in its branches.

Since the foundation of knowledge and guidance is faith in the message, which comprises the Book and wisdom, His references to the path of guidance through the message—the Quran and what the messengers brought—are exceedingly numerous. For example, He says:

“This is the Book about which there is no doubt, a guidance for those mindful of Allah.”

And His saying:

“This is a clear statement to mankind, and a guidance and admonition for those mindful of Allah.”

And His saying:

“Indeed, this Quran guides to that which is most upright.”

And His saying:

“And He sent down the Torah and the Gospel before, as guidance for mankind.”

And His saying:

“A Book which We have revealed to you so that you may bring mankind out of darkness into light, by their Lord’s permission.”

And His saying:

“When guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray nor suffer misery. But whoever turns away from My remembrance will have a life of hardship, and We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection.”

And His saying: “And indeed, you

---

## Page 387

guide to a straight path—the path of Allah.”

And He, the Exalted, says:

“How can you disbelieve while Allah’s verses are recited to you and His Messenger is among you?”

Thus one understands that Allah’s verses and the Messenger prevent disbelief. Such passages are numerous.

Likewise, the Quran is replete with statements that guidance and success belong to believers, not to others, as in His saying:

“A guidance for those mindful of Allah, who believe in the unseen”—to the end of the verse.

He then condemns those who disbelieve and those who act hypocritically. And He says:

“By time, indeed, mankind is in loss, except those who believe and do righteous deeds.”

And His saying:

“Then We reduced him to the lowest of the low, except those who believe and do righteous deeds.”

Thus He has pronounced loss and utter abasement upon the entire human species—all of humanity—except righteous believers.

Likewise, in such verses as Allah has willed, He identifies the people of Paradise as the people of faith, and the people of the Fire as the people of disbelief. This has become a universally known teaching of the Messenger’s religion, established through widespread transmission and known necessarily to everyone whom his message has reached.

He makes happiness depend upon faith together with righteous conduct, as in His saying:

“Whoever does righteous deeds, whether male or female, while being a believer, We will surely grant them a good life.”

And His saying:

“Whoever desires the Hereafter and strives for it as they should, while being a believer—it is they whose striving will be appreciated.”

He also renders righteous deeds void when faith is lost, as in His saying:

“As for those who disbelieve, their deeds are like a mirage on a level plain.”

And His saying:

“The example of those who disbelieve in their Lord is that their deeds are like ashes.”

And His saying: “The example of what they spend

---

## Page 388

in this worldly life is like a wind containing bitter cold that strikes the crops of a people”—to the end of the verse. And His saying:

“And We will turn to whatever deeds they did and make them scattered dust.”

There are many similar passages.

He also states that all rightly guided communities share this condition, saying:

“Indeed, those who believe, the Jews, the Christians, and the Sabians—whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does righteous deeds”—to the end of the verse.

For this reason, He commands those endowed with reason to ponder the Quran, and those endowed with hearing to listen to it. In it, He calls for contemplation, reflection, remembrance, reasoning and understanding; for listening, seeing and attentive hearing; and for being moved to trembling awe, weeping and other responses. This is a vast subject.

Acknowledgment of the Creator is innate, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every newborn is born upon the innate disposition”—to the end of the hadith.

This innate disposition includes acknowledging Allah and turning to Him in devotion. This is the meaning of “There is no god but Allah,” for a god is one who is known and worshipped. I have explained this meaning at length elsewhere.

The purpose of the call is to bring Allah’s servants to fulfil the purpose for which they were created: worshipping their Lord alone, without any partner. Worship is rooted in the worship of the heart, which brings the limbs into its service, for the heart is the king and the bodily members are its soldiers. It is the piece of flesh which, when sound, makes the rest of the body sound, and which, when corrupt, makes the rest of the body corrupt. This depends upon its knowledge and its state.

Thus this foundation—worshipping Allah through knowing Him and loving Him—is the foundation of the call in the Quran. He, the Exalted, says:

“I did not create jinn and mankind except to worship Me.”

---

## Page 389

And at the beginning of Surah al-Baqarah, after dividing people into three categories—believers, disbelievers and hypocrites—He says:

“O mankind, worship your Lord, who created you and those before you, that you may become righteous.”

He mentions His blessings, which manifest His bounty and power, then follows this by establishing prophethood through His saying:

“And if you are in doubt about what We have sent down upon Our servant.”

A speculative theologian admires such an arrangement and considers it magnificent, since lordship is established first, followed by messengership. He supposes that this accords with his own theological method of first examining rational propositions: establishing lordship, then establishing prophethood, and then receiving through prophethood those matters known through revelation. This is the well-known method of speculative theology followed by the Muʿtazilah, Karrāmiyyah, Kullābiyyah and Ashʿariyyah.

Those who follow this path first establish the existence of the Creator on the basis that the world began to exist, then affirm and negate His attributes through rational inference—with their various agreements and disagreements concerning either the questions themselves or the proofs. Only afterward do they discuss, in general terms, matters known through revelation, such as resurrection, reward and punishment, the caliphate, relative merit and faith.

The mainstay and bulk of their theological discourse consist of those propositions they call rational, which constitute the foundations of their religion. They have based these on inferences that entail rejecting much of what the Sunnah has brought. They have therefore incurred censure both for the weakness of the inferences on which they have built and for their rejection of what the Sunnah has brought.

They fall into two groups: one group, such as the Ashʿariyyah, bases doctrinal principles, but not practical principles, on these rational propositions derived by inference,

---

## Page 390

and another group, such as the Muʿtazilah, bases both doctrinal and practical principles on them. Indeed, this latter group takes what is common to the actions of Allah and His servants as its standard: whatever is good when done by Allah is good when done by a servant, and whatever is bad when done by a servant is bad when done by Allah. For this reason, people have called them those who liken divine actions to human actions.

There is no doubt that these are the speculative theologians censured by the early generations, because they so extensively base religion on the unsound reasoning of speculative theology and reject what the Quran and Sunnah have brought. Since the others share some of these tendencies, they incur censure and reproach in proportion to their agreement with them. They agree with them in many of the arguments by which they claim to establish the foundations of religion and faith, and in some of their positions that contradict the Sunnah, transmitted reports, and what is upheld by people of reason and religion.

The purpose here is not to give a detailed account of their positions, for we have written about them elsewhere. Rather, the point here is that the Quran presents the most perfect approach to the foundations of religion and its subsidiary matters, both in its proofs and in the questions it addresses.

The speculative theologian supposes that his own distinctive method accords with the Quran’s method—whether in establishing lordship, divine oneness, prophethood, or resurrection. Yet he is mistaken in much, or most, of this, as in the present instance. There are several respects in which the speculative theologian has erred in assuming that the Quran’s method accords with his own.

---

## Page 391

One of these is that the Quran establishes the existence of the Maker through His signs themselves: knowledge of them necessarily entails knowledge of Him, just as knowledge of sunlight entails knowledge of the sun. There is no need for a universal syllogism stating, “Whatever comes into being must have something that brings it into being,” or “Every contingent being must have a determining cause,” or “Every motion must have a final or an efficient cause.”

Nor is there any need to ask whether the reason for dependence on the Maker is temporal origination alone—as the Muʿtazilah maintain—or contingency—as the majority maintain. They then infer that the latter is an enduring state dependent on the Maker, according to the second, correct view, but not the first.

I have discussed this matter at length elsewhere and explained the truth: created entities themselves depend on the Maker, and their dependence upon Him and need for Him are intrinsic attributes of these created beings, just as self-sufficiency is an intrinsic attribute of the Lord, the Creator. This dependence has no cause other than their very essence and actual being, just as His self-sufficiency has no cause other than His very essence.

You may therefore say that there is no cause for their dependence or His self-sufficiency, since not everything has a cause. Just as there is no cause for His existence and self-sufficiency, there is no cause for their nonexistence when He does not will them to exist, nor for their dependence upon Him when He does will them to exist. Or, if you prefer, you may say that the cause of this dependence and this self-sufficiency is the essence itself and the very reality.

This is demonstrated by the fact that a person knows his own dependence and need for his Creator without it occurring to him that he is contingent—a contingent being being one that admits of both existence and nonexistence—or that he has come into being—a being that has come into being being one whose existence was preceded by nonexistence. Indeed, he may be uncertain whether he has existed from eternity, or may believe that he has, while knowing his dependence and need for his Creator. Thus, if the only cause of dependence on the Maker were contingency or

---

## Page 392

temporal origination, it would not be possible to know their dependence upon Him until this cause was known, since, according to them, this is the only evidence of their need for an efficient cause.

Thus, knowledge of the dependent entities themselves and of actual beings whose need is inescapable necessarily entails knowledge of their need for their Creator and their dependence upon Him. This is why Allah calls them signs.

There are therefore two stages in this argument:

The first is that they depend upon an efficient cause that necessitates their existence or brings them into being, on account of these two causes.

The second is that anything dependent upon an efficient cause—whether one that necessitates its existence or brings it into being—cannot do without it. This statement is correct in itself, but the path to the conclusion does not require it; it introduces length and obstacles that make the intended conclusion more remote.

As for the first stage, knowledge of their dependence does not require evidence drawn from contingency or temporal origination.

As for the second, their dependence upon Him does not need to be established through a universal syllogism stating that every contingent being must have a cause that necessitates its existence, and that everything brought into being must have something that brings it into being. For they are signs of Him: it is impossible for them to exist without Him or not to be signs of Him. The heart knows this by its innate disposition, even if the attributes of contingency and temporal origination never occur to it.

The essential point is that the heart need not consider contingency and temporal origination, either to know the dependence of these entities themselves or to know that they are signs of their Creator, even though both attributes are established. Both also constitute sound evidence. Yet the individual contingent beings are signs pointing specifically to their Creator Himself, whose like there is nothing, in such a way that no other can share in what they indicate.

---

## Page 393

As for our statement, “Every contingent being has a cause that necessitates its existence, and everything brought into being has something that brings it into being,” it indicates only an originator and a cause that necessitates existence—a universal description that can apply to more than one. Thus, a rational syllogism does not identify a particular entity; it indicates only an unrestricted universal, so particular identification is still required. A syllogism therefore establishes an unrestricted, universal description.

Moreover, if one argues for the Maker from their contingency, their temporal origination, or both together, this does not require a universal syllogism stating, “Everything brought into being must have something that brings it into being,” or, “Every contingent being must have a cause that necessitates its existence,” much less a demonstration of these two premises. Rather, the heart’s knowledge of the dependence of this contingent being and this temporally originated being is like its knowledge of the dependence of this contingent being and this temporally originated being. Knowledge of what holds true of particular entities is not derived from universal knowledge encompassing them. Indeed, the mind may know what holds true of a particular entity before knowing the general, universal rule.

Similarly, knowing that ten is twice five does not depend upon knowing that every number that has two halves is twice its two halves.

In keeping with this is His statement:

“Or were they created by nothing, or were they the creators?”

Jubayr ibn Mut‘im said, “When I heard it, I felt my heart split apart.” This is a rhetorical question denying the possibility: “Were they brought into existence without an originator?” They know that they did not come into being without someone bringing them into being, and they know that they did not bring themselves into being. Their knowledge of what holds true of themselves is self-evident through their innate disposition. It does not need to be established by arguing that every existent is temporally originated, or that no contingent being exists by itself or without someone bringing it into existence. Although this general proposition concerning the class is true, knowledge of that specific, particular case, if it does not precede knowledge of the general proposition, is neither subsequent to it nor less clear.

---

## Page 394

I have explained this meaning at length elsewhere and shown that the call of the prophets, peace be upon them, followed the path of innate human disposition, as in their words:

“Is there any doubt about Allah, the Creator of the heavens and the earth?”

And the words of Moses:

“Lord of the heavens and the earth.”

And His words in the Quran:

“Worship your Lord, who created you and those before you, that you may become righteous.”

“Who made the earth a resting place for you.”

He made clear that these very entities are signs pointing to Allah, as we indicated earlier, without any need for those two stages of argument. When He rebuked them, He made their need for the Creator clear through their own selves, without requiring a universal premise under which they and all its other instances fall equally. Indeed, their own case is clearer. I have established this meaning at length elsewhere.

The second point of difference between the Quranic method and the method of speculative theology is that Allah commanded people to worship Him, which is the perfection, soundness, ultimate purpose, and final end of their souls. He did not stop at merely acknowledging Him, as is the ultimate aim of the method of speculative theology. Thus, its proponents accord with the Quranic method neither in their means nor in their ends. As we have indicated, the Quranic means accord with innate human disposition, are direct, and lead to the very One sought. Their means, by contrast, are syllogistic and remote, leading only to the general category of what is sought, not to the particular One Himself.

As for the ends, the Quran speaks of knowing Him and acting for His sake. It thus brings together the two human faculties of knowledge and action: the sensory and voluntary motor faculties, the cognitive and intentional faculties, and the verbal and practical faculties. Thus He says:

“Worship your Lord.”

Worship necessarily involves knowing Him, turning to Him, humbling oneself before Him, and recognizing one’s need for Him. This is the intended goal. The method of speculative theology, however, yields only acknowledgment and admission of His existence.

---

## Page 395

and if this acknowledgment is attained without worship and turning to Him, it brings ruin and misery upon the one who possesses it, as stated in the hadith:

“The person most severely punished on the Day of Resurrection will be a scholar whom Allah did not benefit through his knowledge.”

Such is the case of the accursed Iblīs: he acknowledges his Lord and admits His existence, yet because he did not worship Him, he became the foremost of the wretched. Everyone who becomes wretched does so by following him. As Allah says:

“I will surely fill Hell with you and all those among them who follow you.”

He will therefore surely fill Hell with him and his followers, even though he acknowledges the Lord and admits His existence. His failing was that he refused obedience and worship out of arrogance. The faculties of knowledge and action stand in relation to one another as an agent and its end. Hence the saying: “Knowledge without action is like a tree without fruit.” What is meant by action here is the action of the heart: turning to Allah and fearing Him, so that one becomes His worshipper.

The messengers and the revealed scriptures commanded this and made it obligatory. Indeed, it is the foremost concern, purpose, and foundation of their call. The deviant practical method based on listening to sounds agrees with this practical aim, but pursues it without knowledge—through mere sound, stirring poetry, or vague descriptions of love. Just as the method of speculative theology offers deficient knowledge without action, this method offers deficient action without knowledge. The prophetic, Quranic way of the Sunnah and the community, however, combines knowledge and action in their fullness.

Thus, the messengers begin their call with the command to worship. Allah Most High says:

“O mankind, worship your Lord, who created you and those before you.”

And the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I have been commanded to fight

---

## Page 396

the people until they testify that there is no deity but Allah and that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger.”

This entails acknowledging Him and worshipping Him alone, for a deity is one who is worshipped. He did not say, “until they testify that there is no Lord but Allah,” for the name Allah more clearly conveys the purpose of worshipping Him—the purpose for which creation was brought into being and which they were commanded to fulfil.

Likewise, he said to Mu‘ādh:

“You are going to a people from among the People of the Book. Let the first thing you call them to be the testimony that there is no deity but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.”

Noah, peace be upon him, said:

“Worship Allah, fear Him, and obey me.”

The messengers made the same call in Surah al-A‘rāf and elsewhere. Allah says:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every nation, saying: ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods.’”

He said to all the messengers:

“O messengers, eat of the good things and do righteous deeds. Indeed, I know all that you do. And indeed, this religion of yours is one religion, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.”

Allah Most High says:

“For the accustomed security of Quraysh—their accustomed security in the journeys of winter and summer—let them worship the Lord of this House, who has fed them against hunger and made them safe from fear.”

He says:

“I have only been commanded to worship the Lord of this city, who has made it sacred, and to whom all things belong.”

He says:

“Say: ‘O disbelievers, I do not worship what you worship, nor do you worship what I worship.’”

He says in al-Fātihah:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

He says:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

He says:

“So worship Him and persevere in His worship. Do you know of any equal to Him?”

He says:

“They were commanded only to worship Allah, sincerely devoting their religion to Him, inclining to true faith.”

---

## Page 397

And Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section: Establishing First Principles and Demonstrating the Proofs

This is done by explaining and precisely defining the foundation of knowledge and faith, as I previously wrote in explaining the foundation of knowledge of Allah. What I write here explains the difference between the prophetic approach, grounded in faith and knowledge and fostering righteousness, and the Sabian philosophical approach, together with its offshoots in speculative theology and devotional practice that run counter to the path and practice of the prophets.

The prophets, peace be upon them, first called people to worship Allah with their hearts and tongues. Worshipping Him entails knowing Him and remembering Him. Thus, the foundation of their knowledge and deeds is knowledge of Allah and acting for His sake.

This is innate, as I have established elsewhere in two or three places. I have explained that the foundation of knowledge of Allah is innate and self-evident, and that it is more firmly rooted in souls than the first principles of mathematics—as when we say that one is half of two—or the first principles of natural science—as when we say that a body

---

## Page 398

cannot be in two places, for these forms of knowledge are names that most innate dispositions may disregard. As for knowledge of the Divine, no innate disposition can conceivably disregard it. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is simply that Allah—glory be to Him—is the First, who created all beings, and the Last, to whom all that comes into being returns. He is therefore the all-encompassing foundation. Knowledge of Him is the foundation of all knowledge and unites it; remembrance of Him is the foundation of all speech and unites it; and acting for His sake is the foundation of all action and unites it. Created beings can find well-being only in knowing and worshipping their Lord. Once they attain this, anything else is either a beneficial addition, a useless excess, or something harmful.

From knowledge of Him branch the various kinds of knowledge, and from worshipping Him and seeking Him branch the various forms of righteous purpose. Through worshipping Him and seeking His help, the heart is protected and holds firm: it has taken refuge in a secure support and held fast to guiding evidence and sure proof. It thus continues either to grow in knowledge and faith or to remain safe from ignorance and disbelief.

This is what the divine texts teach when they state that, through faith, people emerge from darkness into light. Allah likens the believer—who acknowledges his Lord in knowledge and action—to the living, the sighted, and those who hear, and to light and shade. He likens the disbeliever to the dead, the blind, and the deaf, and to darkness and scorching heat.

They said of the whisperer who withdraws:

“He is the one who withdraws when Allah is remembered and whispers when the remembrance of Allah is neglected.”

---

## Page 399

It is thus clear that remembering Allah is the foundation for warding off whispering, which is the source of all disbelief, ignorance, disobedience, and injustice. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, you have no authority over My servants.”

He also says:

“Indeed, he has no authority over those who believe and put their trust in their Lord.”

And He says:

“Whoever holds fast to Allah has been guided to a straight path.”

There are other scriptural texts to the same effect.

Among the supplications that Imam Ahmad taught some of his companions was:

“O Guide of the bewildered, guide me to the path of the truthful, and make me one of Your righteous servants.”

For this reason, most of Ahl al-Sunnah, both the scholars of our school and others, held that Allah may be called dalīl (Guide). Ibn ‘Aqīl and many of al-Ash‘arī’s followers, however, did not permit calling Him dalīl, because they understood dalīl to mean that by which something is inferred, whereas Allah is the dāll (the One who guides). Their position reflects the distinction between dāll and dalīl that predominated in their customary usage.

This can be answered in two ways.

First: dalīl is an intensive form of dāll, emphasizing the quality of guiding. Thus, every dalīl is a dāll, but not every dāll is a dalīl. It is not a noun denoting an instrument by which an action is performed, for fa‘īl is not one of the patterns used for instrument nouns, such as mif‘al and mif‘āl.

Statements, actions, and physical bodies from which conclusions are drawn are called adillah (evidence) because they guide those who draw conclusions from them. Likewise, they are described as guiding, directing, recognizing, knowing, speaking, answering, judging, giving legal opinions, recounting, and bearing witness, even though they have neither intention nor will in any of this, nor sensation nor perception. Such usage is well known in Arabic and other languages.

The distinction and restriction they propose therefore have no basis in Arabic usage.

---

## Page 400

Second: even if dalīl were an instrument noun denoting something by means of which an action is performed, Allah Most High has said concerning His beloved servant, as His Prophet reported from Him:

“Through Me he hears, through Me he sees, through Me he understands, through Me he speaks, through Me he grasps, and through Me he moves.”

A Muslim also says, “I sought Allah’s help and held fast to Him.”

If created entities and attributes—everything that exists apart from Allah—can serve as evidence, whether living or not, and even what does not exist can serve as evidence, then it is all the more fitting and appropriate to draw inferences through the Ever-Living, the Self-Sustaining. Moreover, the wording of the transmitted supplication:

“O Guide of the bewildered, guide me to the path of the truthful, and make me one of Your righteous servants,”

implies that He is called dalīl because He guides His servants, not merely because inferences may be drawn through Him, as they may be drawn from entities, statements, and actions that have no intention of directing or guiding. Among His names is al-Hādī (the Guide), and al-Burhān (the Proof) has also been reported.

For this reason, one of them is reported to have said, “I came to know things through my Lord; I did not come to know my Lord through things.” Another said, “He is my guide to everything, even though everything—so that He may not punish me—is evidence of Him.”

Ibn ‘Abbās was asked, “Through what did you come to know your Lord?” He replied:

“Whoever seeks his religion through analogy will spend his life in confusion, departing from the proper path and travelling a crooked course. I came to know Him through what He made known about Himself, and I described Him as He described Himself.”

He thus stated that the heart’s knowledge was attained through Allah’s making Himself known, and this is the light of faith; and that the tongue’s description was attained through Allah’s speech, and this is the light of the Quran.

---

## Page 401

And another said to the shaykh:

“They said, ‘Bring us proofs,’ and I replied to them …
‘How can any proof establish the Proof?’”

The shaykh versed in spiritual knowledge said to the speculative theologian:

“For us, certainty consists of inward impressions that come upon souls and that souls are powerless to repel.”

The theologian replied that this was immediate, non-inferential knowledge.

Shaykh Ismāʿīl al-Kūrānī said to the shaykh who was a speculative theologian:

“You say that Allah is known through evidence. We say that He made Himself known to us, and so we came to know Him.”

That is, He made Himself known through Himself and through His grace. Nevertheless, the words of these two shaykhs allude to the devotional method, which I have discussed elsewhere.

Thus, if the Truth, the Ever-Living, the Self-Sustaining—who is the Lord and Sovereign of everything, who establishes every foundation and brings about every cause and causal factor—is the Guide and the Proof, the First and the foundation through whom the servant draws inferences, to whom he turns for refuge, and to whom he refers everything that follows in matters of knowledge, then this is the way and path of guidance. Likewise, since Allah is the source of deeds and movements, and to Him they return, whoever relies upon Him in his deeds and declares that there is no might or power except through Allah is strengthened and granted help.

The essence of the matter is that Allah is the Guide and the Helper:

“And sufficient is your Lord as a guide and a helper.”

All knowledge requires guidance, and all action requires strength. Therefore, what is required is

---

## Page 402

that He be the source of all guidance and knowledge, and the source of all help and strength, and that the servant seek guidance from none but Him and help from none but Him.

Since the servant is created, governed by his Lord, brought into being, and fashioned, he turns in his knowledge and deeds to his Creator, Originator, Lord, and Maker. This establishes an order that accords with the truth and an arrangement consistent with reality. For building the branch upon the root and giving precedence to the root over the branch is what is right. This, then, is the sound method, in harmony with the natural disposition Allah has instilled, His creation, His Book, and His sunnah.

It is established in Sahīh Muslim, on the authority of ‘Ā’isha (1), that when the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, rose for the night prayer, he would say:

“O Allah, Lord of Gabriel, Michael, and Isrāfīl, Originator of the heavens and the earth, Knower of the unseen and the seen! You judge between Your servants concerning their disagreements. Guide me, by Your permission, to the truth concerning which they differ. Indeed, You guide whom You will to a straight path.”

As for the method of philosophy and speculative theology, its adherents began with themselves, making themselves the root from which they derive the branches and the foundation upon which they build. They discussed how they acquire knowledge: sometimes through the senses, sometimes through reason, and sometimes through both.

They made sensory knowledge, self-evident knowledge, and the like the foundation without which no knowledge can be acquired. Then they claimed that through this they perceive only matters close to them—natural, mathematical, and ethical matters—and made these three the foundations

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The printed edition reads “ʿĀmir”; the correct reading is “ʿĀʾisha,” as found in the hadith collections.

---

## Page 403

upon which they build all other branches of knowledge. Thus, when discussing the foundations of knowledge and speculative theology, they give such examples as: one is half of two; a body cannot occupy two places; and two opposites—such as blackness and whiteness—cannot coexist. There is agreement, then, on these two disciplines.

As for ethical matters, such as regarding knowledge, justice, chastity, and courage as good, most philosophers and speculative theologians count these among the foundations, though among the general foundations. Some, however, do not regard them as foundations, but as derivative matters requiring proof. This is the position of most speculative theologians who champion the sunnah in their interpretation of divine decree. Thus, the knowledge they established as foundational and agreed upon was of little benefit and scant value: it concerned matters of the lower realm.

When they ascend from these premises and proofs to matters of the higher realm, they follow two paths.

As for speculative theologians who follow prophetic teachings, their aim is generally only to establish the existence of the Maker of the world and the attributes through which prophethood is established according to their method. Once they have established prophethood, they receive through it the teachings known through revelation: the Book, the sunnah, consensus, and what is derived from these.

As for the philosophers, they generally explore natural matters and their implications at length, then ascend to the celestial spheres and their conditions. Those among them concerned with divine matters then ascend to the Necessary

---

## Page 404

Existent, and to the intellects and souls. Some of them establish the Necessary Existent at the outset, on the grounds that existence must include a necessary being.

These methods are deeply flawed, both in their means and in their ends.

As for their ends, after great toil—and assuming one comes through unscathed—they yield little good. They are like the meat of an emaciated camel atop a rugged mountain: neither is the mountain easy enough to climb, nor is the meat fat enough to be worth carrying away. Moreover, these methods cause one to miss obligatory and praiseworthy ends too numerous to set out here.

As for their means, these methods involve numerous premises, and those who follow them often falter before reaching their destination. Their premises are generally either ambiguous and disputed, or obscure and accessible only to the highly intelligent. Hence, scarcely ever do two leading figures among them agree on all the premises of a proof.

Each leading philosopher or speculative theologian has a method of reasoning that differs from that of another, so that the followers of each attack the other’s method. Each believes that Allah can be known only through his own method, even though the majority of Muslims—indeed, the early generations generally—disagree with him about it.

For example, most speculative theologians believe that Allah can be known only by establishing that the world began to exist, and then reasoning from this to the One who brought it into existence.

They have various methods for establishing that it began to exist. Most argue from the origination of accidents, which are the properties of bodies.

Then the Qadariyyah among the Mu'tazilah and others believe that the existence of the Maker and the truth of prophethood can be established only after believing

---

## Page 405

that the servant is the originator of his own acts—otherwise, the proof would fail—and other such principles on which the majority of Muslims disagree with them.

Most of these speculative theologians, who infer that bodies began to exist from the origination of motion, use this as their proof for denying what the apparent meaning of the revealed texts indicates: that Allah comes, descends, and so forth. The Mu'tazilah and others use this as their proof that Allah has no attributes: neither knowledge nor power, neither might nor mercy, nor any other attribute. For these, they claim, are accidents that indicate that the one possessing them began to exist.

Most writers on philosophy—such as Ibn Sīnā—begin with logic, then turn to natural philosophy and mathematics—or omit it—before proceeding to their account of metaphysics.

You will find that writers on speculative theology begin with its preliminaries: rational inquiry, knowledge, and proof, which belong to the same kind of study as logic. They then proceed to establish that the world began to exist and that there is One who brought it into existence. Some proceed instead to divide the objects of knowledge into the existent and the nonexistent, examining existence and its divisions, as a philosopher may do at the beginning of metaphysics.

As for the prophets, the first thing they call to is the testimony that there is no god worthy of worship except Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.

---

## Page 406

Al-Ghazālī acknowledged that the Sufi path is the ultimate goal, for they purify their hearts of everything other than Allah and fill them with the remembrance of Allah. This is the starting point of the Messenger’s call. Yet the Sufi who lacks detailed knowledge of the prophetic teachings gains through this a general faith, unlike one who possesses those teachings, whose knowledge is detailed.

Reflect, then, on the paths of knowledge and practice, so that you may distinguish the path of the people of the Sunnah and faith from that of the people of innovation and hypocrisy, and the path of knowledge and understanding from that of ignorance and denial.

---

## Page 407

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

Some theologians, philosophers, and Sufis have discussed how contingent and originated beings depend for their existence on the Necessary, Eternal Being. This meaning is true, for Allah is the Lord and Sovereign of everything. However, they cite as evidence His words:

“Everything will perish except His Face.”

They say that the verse means that every contingent being, considered in itself, is perishing, or is sheer nonexistence and pure negation, and that it has existence only from its Lord. Thus, considered in itself, it is perishing, but it exists through the Face of its Lord—that is, its existence is from Him.

Some of them may then proceed from this interpretation to the doctrine of the Jahmiyyah who espouse union and indwelling. Such a person says that this Face is the existence of created beings, and that Allah’s Face is His existence. His existence would therefore be the existence of created beings, with no distinction drawn between necessary existence and contingent existence—as maintained by Ibn ‘Arabī, Ibn Sab‘īn, and others like them.

This is a necessary consequence for anyone who regards His existence as absolute existence, without a reality peculiar to Him that distinguishes Him from anything else, whether he regards it as absolute existence conditioned by absoluteness—as Ibn Sīnā and philosophers like him claim—or as absolute existence without any condition—as the proponents of union maintain.

---

## Page 408

And they acknowledge rational principles, self-evident to reason, which entail that a being conditioned by absoluteness exists only in the mind, not in external reality—as with an animal taken absolutely and conditioned by absoluteness, a human being taken absolutely and conditioned by absoluteness, and similar examples. They also acknowledge that what is absolute without any condition has no reality apart from external and mental existence. There is no absolute existence within existing individual entities apart from those entities themselves, just as there is no absolute human being within this person and that person beyond the individual person. Thus, on the first view, the Lord’s existence would be merely mental; on the second, it would be the very existence of created beings.

The position of the Jahmiyyah, both earlier and later, falls within these two views. This is the very essence of denying the divine reality; yet they also affirm Him. They thus combine negation with affirmation and remain bewildered. For this reason, they make bewilderment the ultimate attainment of knowledge and attribute to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, a fabricated hadith:

“The one among you who knows Allah best is the most bewildered.”

They also claim that he said:

“O Allah, increase my bewilderment concerning You.”

They combine two contradictories, expressly accepting this consequence.

This is the position of the Qarmatian esotericists and the proponents of union. It is also a necessary consequence of the position of the philosophers and the Muʿtazilah, even though the latter do not explicitly acknowledge their acceptance of it. By contrast, the esotericists and proponents of union among the Sufis expressly accept it and report it from al-Hallāj.

The point here is this: the claim that the Creator’s existence is the existence of the created being is unequivocal disbelief, by the agreement of the people of faith. It is among the most utterly false notions according to every human being’s self-evident reason, even though its adherents claim that it represents the pinnacle of insight and spiritual knowledge. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 409

As for the fact that the created being has no existence except from the Creator—glorified be He—this is true. He is the Creator, Lord, and Sovereign of all beings. Nothing comes into existence except through His power, will, and creative act. He is the Creator of everything, glorified and exalted be He.

The discussion here, however, concerns interpreting the verse in this way. Meanings are either true or false. A false meaning may not be used to interpret Allah’s words. If a true meaning is what the Quran indicates, then it may be used to interpret it. Otherwise, not every sound meaning may be used to interpret the wording merely because of some correspondence, like the correspondence between a dream and its interpretation—even if that correspondence lies outside the ways in which the wording conveys meaning, as is the practice of the Qarmatians and esotericists.

For the meaning conveyed by a word is known through attested usage. The word must therefore have been used in that sense so as to convey that meaning; it is not enough that the word could suitably be assigned that meaning. Only Allah can count the words that could suitably be assigned meanings but have not been assigned them.

This is so according to those who take into account a correspondence between wording and meaning, as do some scholars of speculative theology and rhetoric. As for those who do not take such a correspondence into account, any word could be assigned any meaning. In particular, when it is known that a word has been assigned a meaning and is being used in that sense, construing it otherwise merely because of some correspondence is to lie about Allah.

If, moreover, this contradicts what is known from the revealed law, it is the habitual practice of the Qarmatians. If it does not contradict it, it is the practice of many ignorant preachers and Sufis who offer allusive interpretations that the wording does not indicate

---

## Page 410

either explicitly or by analogy. As for those who offer allusive interpretations while affirming what the wording indicates, and understand the alluded meaning through analogy and reflection, their approach is like that of jurists versed in analogy and reflection. This is sound when the analogy is valid rather than invalid, and the reflection sound rather than distorted.

Since our purpose here is to discuss the interpretation of the verse, we say:

The interpretation transmitted from those among the early generations and Quran commentators who gave it—namely, that everything will perish except that by which His Face is sought—is better than that newly introduced interpretation. Indeed, interpreting the verse according to that newly introduced interpretation is impermissible. This can be demonstrated in several ways: some establish the superiority of the former interpretation, while others establish the invalidity of the latter.

First:

He did not say, “Everything will perish except insofar as it is from Him,” or “except insofar as it is from His Face.” Rather, He said, “except His Face.” This entails that there are things that perish, with His Face excepted.

If “His Face” is taken to mean His existence, this entails that everything other than His existence perishes, and thus that created beings perish. Yet this is not the case. The same objection also applies to the view of the proponents of the unity of existence: according to them, there is only one existence. It therefore cannot correctly be said, “Everything other than His existence perishes,” since there is nothing that can be described as other than His existence. The foundation of their doctrine is the denial that, in reality, anything else or anything other exists.

This is completed by the second point:

If it is said that “perishing” means a contingent being that has no existence from itself, then the meaning would be: “Nothing has its existence from itself except Him.”

The response is that using the word “perishing” for an existing created thing merely because its existence comes from its Lord rather than from itself is unknown in the language, whether literally or figuratively.

---

## Page 411

The Quran distinguishes between one thing and another in its use of the term “perishing.” The Most High says:

“If a man dies leaving no child.”

And the Most High says:

“Do not throw yourselves into destruction with your own hands.”

And the Most High says:

“They prevent others from it and keep away from it themselves. Yet they destroy none but themselves, though they do not realize it.”

And the Most High says:

“They say, ‘There is nothing but our life in this world. We die and live, and nothing destroys us but time.’”

And the Most High says:

“How many towns We have destroyed! Our punishment came upon them by night or while they rested at midday.”

And the Most High says:

“How many generations We destroyed before them!”

And He says:

“There is no town that We will not destroy before the Day of Resurrection.”

And He says:

“In the city there were nine men who spread corruption in the land and did not set things right.”

“They said, ‘Swear to one another by Allah that we will attack him and his family by night. Then we will say to his next of kin, “We did not witness the destruction of his family.”’”

And He says:

“How many generations We have destroyed after Noah!”

And the angels said:

“We will destroy the people of this town.”

And He says:

“Did We not destroy the earlier peoples?”

“Then We will follow them with the later ones.”

These verses indicate that perishing involves transformation and corruption in an existing thing, as we shall explain. It does not mean that the thing does not derive its existence from itself, for all created things share that characteristic.

Third:

It may be said that, on this interpretation, the meaning would be that everything other than Him is contingent, capable of nonexistence, and does not derive its existence from itself. But this is not the meaning they intend. Rather, they intend that everything other than Him derives its existence from Him. There is a clear difference between these two meanings: stating that something is contingent, capable of nonexistence, and does not derive its existence from itself is different from stating that it exists and that its existence comes from Allah.

---

## Page 412

Fourth:

It may be said: if what is meant is that everything other than Him is contingent, and the pronoun refers to the Necessary Existent—to Allah, who created all beings—then this merely explains the obvious. For it is well known that everything other than the Necessary Existent is contingent, and that everything He has created is contingent.

Fifth:

It may be said: the term “Face” in the Quran and Sunnah is mentioned only in the context of worshipping Him, acting for His sake, and turning to Him. It therefore serves to affirm His divinity and the worship and obedience due to Him, not His uniqueness as Creator and Lord. The former concerns the final cause, whereas the latter concerns the efficient cause. The final cause is the intended end, which is higher and nobler; indeed, it is an efficient cause of the efficient cause itself. Hence it is given precedence in such statements as:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

And:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

The Most High says:

“He owes no one any favour to be repaid,”

“but seeks only the Face of his Lord, the Most High,”

“and he will surely be pleased.”

The Most High says:

“They give food, despite their love for it, to the needy, the orphan, and the captive.”

“‘We feed you only for the Face of Allah. We desire neither reward nor thanks from you.’”

The Most High says:

“Do not drive away those who call upon their Lord morning and evening, seeking His Face.”

Since this is so, interpreting the term “Face” in this verse according to what it denotes in the other verses is more appropriate than interpreting it according to what the word “Face” denotes in any passage of the Quran and Sunnah. Indeed, the former interpretation, rather than the latter, is obligatory; for this uses the term in a sense in which the Quran has not employed it, whereas the Quran employs it in a different sense wherever it is mentioned.

Sixth:

The term “perishing” means corruption and a thing’s departure from the purpose for which it is intended

---

## Page 413

and desired. This meaning is appropriate to whatever is not for Allah, for it is corrupt and of no real benefit; rather, it falls outside what ought to be sought and desired.

The Most High says:

“They keep others away from him and keep away from him themselves. They destroy none but themselves, though they do not realize it.”

He states that they destroy themselves by keeping others away from the Messenger and keeping away from him themselves. It is well known that whoever refrains from following the Messenger and prevents others from following him—and such a person is a disbeliever—is destroyed through his disbelief by suffering the punishment he dreads rather than attaining the bliss he seeks.

The Most High also says:

“If a man dies.”

And He says:

(1)

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 414

He also said—may Allah sanctify his soul:

(*)

Section

It may then be said: This also entails that neither of them is necessarily existent in itself, self-sufficient, and self-subsisting. Rather, each depends on another in its essence and attributes, just as it depends on that other in what it brings about. For if each depends on the other in what it brings about and is incapable of doing so alone—as joint agency necessarily entails, as explained above—(*) then either it can possess the power to act independently, such that this is possible for it, or this is not possible.

The latter is impossible. For if it were impossible for a thing to be within the power of one agent and possible for that agent to bring about, it would also be impossible for it to be within the power of two agents and possible for them to bring about. Whether a thing is possible and within an agent’s power does not vary according to whether the agents capable of bringing it about are one or many. Thus, if it is impossible for a thing to be brought about through the power of one agent, it is impossible for it to be brought about through the power of two. Conversely, if a thing is possible and can be brought about through the power of two agents, it can also be brought about through the power of one.

This is clear if possibility and impossibility arise from a characteristic of the possible thing—the thing brought about and subject to power—for its intrinsic attributes do not differ between the two cases. The same applies if they arise from a characteristic of the agent possessing the power. For the power that subsists in two is not precluded from subsisting

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 20):

The references in “this also,” “as previously explained,” and similar expressions refer to the sections preceding this one there. Comparing the two versions reveals some minor differences—I have noted those concerning that section in my discussion of volume twenty. Here, the principal differences are:
> 1 – (2/33): “Thus, combination and joint participation in creation”; at 20/179: “in created things,” which is the more plausible reading.
> 2 – (2/33): “Even if one of them remains”; at 20/179: “Even if their states remain.”
> 3 – (2/35): “None of them is, on its own, a subsisting cause”; at 20/181: “is, on its own, a complete cause,” which is the more plausible reading.
> 4 – (2/35–36): “Rather, it has been said: among created things there can be no cause possessing a single attribute, or no created thing can be a cause on its own”; at 20/182: “since no created thing can be a cause on its own,” which is the more plausible reading.
> 5 – (2/36): “Their dependence and their being min bad'ihi (from his origination)”; at 20/183: “their dependence and their being subject to lordship,” which is the correct reading.

---

## Page 415

in one. Indeed, the possibility of this is self-evident to reason. Moreover, it is self-evident to reason that all attributes—power and otherwise—are more perfect when the subject in which they subsist is unified and integrated than when it is multiple and dispersed. Thus, coming together and acting jointly give created beings strength and power that they do not possess when separated and acting individually, even if one of them remains. Indeed, individuals, limbs, and other separate bodies each possess a power. If they are supposed to be united and brought together, that power would be stronger and more perfect, because their union and combination would give it, within the bounds of possibility, something it lacked when they were separate and multiple. This shows that the power subsisting in two—if those two are supposed to be one thing—would be more perfect. How, then, could it fail to equal the power subsisting in two subjects?

It is known that, if two distinct subjects in which two powers subsist are supposed to be a single subject in which both powers subsist, the power would not thereby diminish but increase. Accordingly, what can possibly be brought about through the power of two separate agents would not diminish but increase if those very agents were supposed to be a single agent possessing what both possessed. It follows that each of them could possess the power to act independently, and that this is possible for it.

It has thus become clear that, where two agents jointly bring about a single effect, it is possible for each to have the power to bring it about. Indeed, it is possible for them to be a single thing with the power to bring it about. Thus, each could be more perfect than it presently is and could possess a different attribute.

---

## Page 416

if the essence and attributes of each can change. It is known that neither can perfect and change itself on its own, since it is assumed to be incapable of independently producing an effect separate from itself. Is it not, then, all the more incapable of perfecting and changing itself? If it can change and become more perfect, yet cannot do so by itself, it is not necessarily existent in itself; rather, it possesses contingency and depends on something other than itself. Yet it is supposed to be necessarily existent in itself while not being necessarily existent in itself, making it both necessary and contingent. This is a contradiction, for whatever is necessarily existent in itself is self-sufficient in the reality of its essence and attributes, depending on nothing else in any aspect of them. All of this falls within what is meant by its essence. Indeed, it must also depend on nothing else in any of its acts or effects.

For its acts that subsist in it fall within what is meant by its own self. Its dependence on something else for some of its effects entails dependence in its act and the attribute subsisting in it, since its effect proceeds from that act and attribute. If its essence were perfect and self-sufficient, it would not depend on anything else in its action. Thus, its dependence on anything else in any respect demonstrates its lack of self-sufficiency and its need for another. This is precisely the contingency that contradicts its being necessarily existent in itself.

For this reason, since necessary existence is exclusive to the Lord of the worlds, and independence from all others is exclusive to the Lord of the worlds, acting independently is likewise one of the exclusive attributes of

---

## Page 417

the Lord of the worlds. Being free of any partner in His acts and their effects is likewise exclusive to the Lord of the worlds. Nothing among created things independently produces any effect; none by itself constitutes an independently operative cause; and none can dispense with a partner in producing any effect. Rather, nothing in the world comes into existence through any cause without another cause acting together with it.

Thus, even if it is called a cause, it is a cause conducive to an effect—a contributing cause—not a complete cause. Each of the two is a condition for the other. Likewise, every cause in the world has an impediment that prevents it from acting. Everything within creation that is called a cause, a causal factor, an entity possessing power, an agent, or a governor therefore has a partner that serves as a condition for it, and an opposing factor that acts as an impediment and an opposite. He, glorified be He, has said:

“And of all things We created two mates.”

A member of a pair may be a similar counterpart or a contrasting opposite—a peer. Thus, every created thing has a partner and a peer. The Lord alone, glorified be He, has neither partner nor peer. Whatever He wills comes to be, and whatever He does not will does not come to be.

For this reason, no one else deserves to be called Creator or Lord without qualification, or anything similar, for such a designation entails independently and exclusively bringing about the effect produced. This belongs to none but Allah alone.

Accordingly, although some people have disputed whether a cause can possess multiple attributes, claiming that a cause can possess only one attribute, most people have disagreed with them and said that it may possess multiple attributes. Indeed, it has even been said that among created things there is no

---

## Page 418

cause possessing only one attribute, or that no created thing can constitute a cause on its own, and that any cause within creation must consist of two or more factors.

Thus, nothing proceeds from any single created thing, let alone that it should be said:

“From the One, only one proceeds.”

Rather, nothing within creation proceeds except from two or more things. As for the One who acts alone, that is none but Allah.

Just as oneness necessarily and inseparably belongs to Him, acting in partnership necessarily and inseparably belongs to created things. Oneness entails perfection, and perfection entails oneness; partnership entails imperfection, and imperfection entails partnership.

Likewise, oneness entails independence of all others, self-subsistence, and necessary existence in and of itself. These qualities—self-sufficiency, necessary existence in and of itself, and self-subsistence—entail oneness. Partnership, by contrast, entails dependence on others, intrinsic contingency, and the absence of self-subsistence. Likewise, dependence, contingency, and the absence of self-subsistence entail partnership.

These considerations, and others like them, are proofs and signs of Allah’s exclusive lordship. They also demonstrate the contingency and dependence of observable created things, and that they originate from Him. They are therefore proofs establishing the existence of the Maker, for the separation, multiplicity, and partnership found in these things necessarily entail their dependence and contingency. Whatever is contingent and dependent must depend upon One whose existence is necessary and who is self-sufficient; otherwise, it would not exist. Even if an infinite regress of contingent, dependent beings were assumed, taken collectively they would still be contingent. And a contingent being is known

---

## Page 419

with self-evident certainty to depend on another for its existence. Thus, whatever is known to be contingent and dependent is also known to depend on another for its existence. There must therefore be One who is self-sufficient and whose existence is necessary in and of itself; otherwise, nothing dependent and contingent could exist at all.

These considerations demonstrate both Allah’s exclusive lordship and His exclusive right to worship. This is the complete and obligatory affirmation of His oneness brought by the Quran. They demonstrate this in several ways, some of which we have discussed elsewhere. For example, things in motion necessarily require motion arising from will, and will necessarily requires an object sought for its own sake. That object is God. A created being cannot be sought for its own sake, just as it cannot act independently. Thus, just as there cannot be two independently acting agents, there cannot be two objects each sought for its own sake.

Furthermore, God, who is sought for His own sake—if He were not Lord—could not be worshipped for His own sake. One who is neither lord nor creator cannot be invoked, petitioned, or sought for the sake of something else; still less, then, can he be worshipped and sought for his own sake. Affirming the right to worship therefore entails affirming lordship, and denying lordship entails denying the right to worship. For being the object of worship is the ultimate end, which necessarily entails the starting point, just as a final cause entails an efficient cause.

Each of these—Allah’s exclusive lordship and His exclusive right to worship—although known through the innate disposition with its immediate, self-evident knowledge, and through divinely revealed prophetic teaching, is also known through self-evident comparisons that constitute rational arguments. Yet the speculative theologians devoted themselves only to establishing rational arguments for Allah’s exclusive lordship,

---

## Page 420

and this is something no descendant of Adam has disputed in principle. They have disputed only some of its details, as have the Magians, dualists, naturalists, the Qadariyyah, and others like them among misguided adherents of philosophy, the Mu‘tazilah, and those counted among them.

As for Allah’s exclusive right to worship, it is in this sphere that widespread and predominant polytheism has taken hold among the various kinds of polytheists who acknowledge that there is no creator but Allah and no lord besides Him. As the Most High says:

“And most of them believe not in Allah except while they associate others with Him.”

We have discussed this at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 421

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

A Principle

I have written about matters relating to this principle in the preceding notebook.

The foundation of affirmation and negation, love and hatred, is the soul’s awareness of existence and nonexistence, and of what is congenial or uncongenial to it.

When it perceives the existence of a thing itself or its attributes, it believes in that existence and acknowledges it. Then, if these are attributes of perfection, it believes that the thing deserves reverence and honor, affirms it, and praises and extols it.

When it perceives the thing’s nonexistence, or the absence of attributes of perfection from it, it believes in that absence.

If it perceives neither existence nor absence, it believes in neither, and neither affirms nor denies. Yet it may believe something to be absent when it does not perceive its existence, even though it does not perceive its nonexistence either. There is a clear difference between awareness of nonexistence and lack of awareness of existence. This is the state of ignorance through which most people fall into error: they deny what they have not fully comprehended, and whoever is ignorant of something becomes hostile toward it.

---

## Page 422

Then, when the soul believes something to be absent, it denies its existence, condemns it, and disparages it. This applies when what it perceives as existing or absent is praiseworthy; if it is blameworthy, the reverse applies.

Likewise, when it perceives something congenial to it, it loves and desires it; when it perceives something uncongenial, it hates and dislikes it. If it perceives neither, or perceives something that is neither congenial nor uncongenial, there is neither love nor hatred. Yet you may hate something that is not uncongenial merely because it is not congenial. There is a clear difference between perceiving what is uncongenial and not perceiving what is congenial.

This, however, is praiseworthy, for whatever is not congenial to a person offers him neither benefit nor advantage. Inclining toward it is therefore a matter of futility and harm. One should turn away from it because it offers no benefit, and inclining toward what offers no benefit is harmful.

Love for a person or a deed is then followed by prayers of blessing and praise, just as hatred is followed by curses and disparagement. Whatever is neither loved nor hated is followed by neither praise nor supplication, neither disparagement nor curses.

Since there truly exists One who is loved and worshipped, the foundation of happiness is faith in this reality. The foundation of faith is the heart’s affirmation, which is assent, and the heart’s action, which is love accompanied by submission. For nothing is more perfectly congenial to the souls of Allah’s servants than their God, Allah, besides whom none is worthy of worship.

And since faith encompasses both these meanings, and the definition given by those who describe it as mere

---

## Page 423

assent is incomplete and inadequate, the community has divided into three groups:

Those who combine both have fully realized its two meanings: affirmation expressing assent and volitional action.

Two other groups lack one of these meanings:

The dialectical theologians chiefly direct their reflection and discourse toward affirmation and negation, existence and nonexistence, and propositions. Their ultimate aim is merely assent, knowledge, and the communication of information.

The Sufis chiefly direct their pursuit and activity toward love and hatred, desire and aversion, and practical activity. Their ultimate aim is love, submission, action, and desire.

As for the people of knowledge and faith, they combine both aspects: assent grounded in knowledge and action motivated by love. Moreover, their assent rests on knowledge, and so do their action and love. They are thus free of the two failings of the theologians and Sufis who have deviated, and have attained what each of these two groups lacked.

For each of those who have deviated has two failings:

The first is speaking without knowledge—if he is a theologian—and acting without knowledge—if he is a Sufi. Such are the theological and practical innovations that have arisen in opposition to the Quran and Sunnah.

The second is that the theologian has neglected action, while the Sufi has neglected affirmation and discourse.

As for those who adhere to the Sunnah inwardly and outwardly, their speech and action, both inward and outward, are grounded in knowledge, and each is accompanied by the other. These are the true Muslims

---

## Page 424

who remain on the straight path—the path of those whom Allah has favoured, not of those who have incurred His wrath, nor of those who have gone astray. For the dialectical theologians who have deviated bear a resemblance to the Jews, while the Sufis who have deviated bear a resemblance to the Christians. Thus, the former chiefly emphasize letters and the knowledge and belief they signify, while the latter chiefly emphasize sounds and the ecstasy and movement they arouse. Complementing this, Allah commanded His Prophet to invite people to the way of his Lord with wisdom and good admonition, and to debate with them in the best manner.

These three methods are beneficial in knowledge and action, and resemble what logicians call demonstration, rhetoric, and dialectic. There remain poetry and sophistry—which is disguised falsehood—and Allah repudiated these in His words:

“Shall I inform you upon whom the devils descend?”

“They descend upon every sinful liar.”

“They lend an ear, and most of them are liars.”

“And the poets are followed by those who go astray.”

And so on to the end of the surah. He thus mentioned the liars, who are the sophists, and mentioned the poets.

Likewise, when ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb said to Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, “O successor to the Messenger of Allah, win the people over,” Abū Bakr took hold of his beard and said: “O Ibn al-Khattāb! A tyrant in the pre-Islamic days of ignorance, but a weakling in Islam? On what basis am I to win them over? With a fabricated account or contrived poetry?”

He thus mentioned fabricated accounts and contrived poetry, just as Allah mentioned liars and poets. Falsehood pertains to the cognitive faculty, while poetry pertains to the practical, volitional faculty: the former is misguidance, and the latter is waywardness.

---

## Page 425

For this reason, the two often occur together among religious adherents such as monks, corrupt Sufi ascetics, and others. Poetry derives its name from perception: it makes the soul perceive what moves it, even if it is untrue, arousing love or aversion, desire or fear through the imaginative representations it contains. This is poetry’s distinctive feature, and it is therefore that Allah described poets as being followed by those who go astray. Waywardness consists in following desires, for poetry stirs people to desire and aversion, joy and sorrow, without knowledge. This is waywardness, whereas falsehood misleads people in matters of knowledge, causing them to believe something contrary to its actual nature.

If the soul is moved sometimes by conviction and faith, and sometimes by poetry, the latter is blameworthy except for what has been exempted. Allah Most High says:

“We have not taught him poetry, nor is it fitting for him. This is only a reminder and a clear Quran.”

The reminder is unlike poetry: it is truth and knowledge that the heart recalls, whereas poetry merely moves the soul. Thus, among Sufis who have deviated, listening to odes and poems has largely replaced listening to the Quran and the reminder. It merely stirs love or some other emotion in them, without that emotion arising from knowledge and conviction.

For this reason, those who prefer it to listening to the Quran offer the excuse that the Quran is truth sent down from the True One, whereas souls love falsehood. This is because truthful and sound speech imparts knowledge and belief to the heart as a whole, while souls devoted to falsehood do not love the truth. Its effect is therefore baseless, spreading from the soul, for it is a branch without a root. Yet it affects the soul by stirring, agitating, and influencing it, not by imparting conviction and knowledge

---

## Page 426

and understanding. For this reason, they call such speech a “driver,” because it urges souls onward—that is, it rouses and drives them, just as a camel-driver urges white camels onward. Wisdom, good exhortation, and argument conducted in the best manner, however, impart conviction and prompt action, and thus confer immense benefit.

I said that these three resemble, in some respects, the three types of syllogism—demonstrative, rhetorical, and dialectical—but are not identical to them; rather, they are more complete in many respects. There are several reasons for this:

- First: the methods found in the Quran combine knowledge and action, statements and directives, in the most complete manner, unlike logical syllogisms.

A rational, logical syllogism serves only to produce assent to declarative propositions, whether or not action follows from that assent. If its premises are certain, it constitutes a demonstration, whether or not those premises are widely accepted or conceded, and it yields certainty. If its premises are widely accepted or accepted, it is called rhetoric, whether or not they are certain. It produces belief and assent on the spectrum between certainty and conjecture; this does not mean that it produces conjecture rather than certainty, since nothing about premises being widely accepted prevents them from being certain and yielding certainty. There is a difference between what does not necessarily yield certainty and what precludes certainty.

Widely accepted premises, simply by virtue of being widely accepted, produce assent, persuasion, and belief. Then, if it is known that they are

---

## Page 427

certain, they also yield certainty. If they are known not to be certain, they yield only conjecture; and if the soul is aware of neither, what remains is mere belief, whose certainty is neither affirmed nor denied.

Wisdom in the Quran means knowing the truth, speaking it, and acting upon it, as I have explained elsewhere. Good exhortation combines assent to what is stated with obedience to what is commanded. Hence, exhortation in the Quran is used to mean commands and prohibitions accompanied by encouragement and warning, as in His words:

“If only they had done what they were instructed to do.”

And His words:

“Allah warns you against ever repeating anything like this.”

And His words:

“So We made it a deterrent to those present and those who came after them, and an admonition.”

That is, they take heed of it, becoming alert and deterred. Likewise, argument conducted in the best manner combines argument aimed at securing assent with argument aimed at securing obedience.

Second—this may also be subdivided to yield another point—it may be said that people fall into three categories. Those who acknowledge the truth and follow it possess wisdom. Those who acknowledge it but do not act upon it are exhorted until they act. Those who do not acknowledge it are engaged in argument in the best manner, because argument is liable to arouse anger. When conducted in the best manner, its benefit is attained to the fullest extent possible, as when repelling an assailant.

Third: Allah’s speech contains nothing but certain truth. It does not contain what distinguishes rhetoric and dialectic from demonstration: a premise that is widely accepted or conceded but not

---

## Page 428

certain. Rather, whenever Allah presents an example containing a widely accepted or conceded premise, that premise must also be certain.
