Merely relying on an opponent’s concession when the premise is not true, or on its being widely accepted even though it is not true—such premises have no place in Allah’s speech, which is entirely true and truthful, the most truthful speech and the finest discourse.

Thus, a person possessing wisdom is addressed through true premises, whether widely accepted, conceded, or neither, because he can grasp subtle matters and follows the truth.

A person in need of exhortation is addressed through those true premises that are widely accepted, because he may not understand subtle truths, whereas he does not dispute widely accepted premises.

A person engaged in argument is addressed through the true premises he concedes, whether widely accepted or not. He may not submit to what he does not concede, whether obvious or subtle, yet submits to what he does concede, whether obvious or subtle. Such is the distinction.

The matter is not as the ignorant and misguided among the unbelieving philosophers and some speculative theologians imagine: that the Quran employs the rhetorical method but lacks the demonstrative method, or contains only a little of it. Rather, everything the Quran contains follows the demonstrative method. At times it is also rhetorical and at times dialectical, while remaining demonstrative. The rational inferences contained in the Quran are the highest means of calling people to Allah, as He says:

“We have certainly presented for people in this Quran every kind of example.”

This occurs near the beginning and end of Surah Subhān, and in Surah al-Kahf. An example is a logical inference; for this reason, the Quran contains

---

## Page 429

a distillation of the sound methods found in the discourse of all rational thinkers—speculative theologians, philosophers, and others. Allah has kept it free of the unsound methods found in their discourse, and it contains sound methods entirely absent from human discourse.

The fourth point:

There is a subtle point here that deserves attention, for it is useful. A premise stated in a logical inference, which constitutes an example, has both an intrinsic attribute and a relational attribute. Its intrinsic attribute is that it either corresponds to reality and is therefore true, or does not correspond to reality and is therefore false. All the premises stated in the Quran’s examples are true. Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

As for its relational attribute:

Whether a premise is known to Zayd, regarded by him as probable, conceded by him, or not conceded is not something fixed. A premise may be certain to someone who knows it, yet unknown—let alone regarded as probable—to someone who does not know it. Thus, whether a premise is certain or uncertain, widely accepted or not widely accepted, conceded or not conceded depends on relative and relational considerations that arise according to a person’s awareness of it. Accordingly, what he regards as probable—or even what is unknown to him—may become certain and known; what he rejects may become conceded, and even what he concedes may become rejected. The Quran is Allah’s speech, through which He has warned all creation. He did not address it to one particular individual such that he would be addressed through premises that he personally regards as certain, widely accepted, or conceded.

Thus, in the premises of its examples, what is taken into account is their intrinsic attribute: their being true and sound

---

## Page 430

and requiring acceptance. As for the grounds for accepting a premise as true, they are many and varied. One person may have ways of accepting that premise as true which are not available to ʿAmr. For example, one may know it through sensory perception and sight, while another knows it through hearing reports and mass transmission—as with the signs of the Messenger, the account of the People of the Elephant, and other matters.

Where the grounds for accepting a premise as true are common to people generally, those grounds can be stated—as with the signs of divine lordship that are continually known through sensory perception. Where those grounds vary, each group is referred to the means by which it accepts the premise as true. In such a case, one may invoke the words:

“Invite to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good instruction, and argue with them in a way that is best.”

For by addressing a particular individual, one may learn what he regards as certain or widely accepted among certainties, or what he concedes.

This makes it clear to you that the logicians’ classification of syllogistic premises into the certain, the widely accepted, and the conceded does not describe an inherent attribute of the proposition. Rather, it depends on the circumstances of the person who accepts it as true, and his position may even be reversed. From this, it also becomes clear to you that their judgment that a premise is not certain, not widely accepted, or not conceded is unsound. Denying these attributes is valid only with respect to particular groups, not all humanity. Likewise, judging a premise to be certain, widely accepted, or conceded applies only to those for whom that description has been established. Furthermore, syllogistic inference is true and stable and does not change, whereas what these people say changes and shifts

---

## Page 431

and does not remain constant—except, of course, in matters which Allah’s established way has made common to people, such as arithmetic and natural science. These two disciplines are not the aim of the prophetic call, nor is knowledge of them a condition for attaining felicity or a means of securing it. Rather, the intended aim is the study of divine matters.

The premises of syllogisms in this field belong to the first category, in which judgments about premises vary according to their relation to particular people. Reflect on this, for it is a sound and beneficial insight of great importance. This discussion makes clear that, although the Quran is Allah’s speech, Allah attributes it to the messenger who conveys it, whether angelic or human. He attributes it to the angel in His words:

“So I swear by the retreating stars, those that run their courses and disappear,”

through to His words:

“Indeed, it is the word of a noble messenger, endowed with strength and firmly established with the Lord of the Throne, obeyed there and trustworthy.”

This is Gabriel, for these are his attributes, not those of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Then He says:

“And your companion is not mad.”

He calls him our companion, reminding us of His favour upon us in making him our companion, just as He says:

“By the star when it descends, your companion has neither strayed nor erred.”

“And he certainly saw him on the clear horizon. And he does not withhold knowledge of the unseen.”

This refers to Muhammad—that is, he is not suspected of falsehood; according to the other reading, he is not withholding. Some would-be philosophers have claimed that this, too, refers to Gabriel, whom they identify as the active intellect, the source of emanation. This is a distortion of words from their proper places: Gabriel’s attributes have already been mentioned, whereas this describes Muhammad.

Then He says:

“And it is not the word of an accursed devil.”

Having established that it is the word of

---

## Page 432

the angel, He denied that it was the word of the devil. Likewise, He says in Surah al-Shuʿarāʾ:

“The Trustworthy Spirit has brought it down upon your heart,”

through to His words:

“It was not the devils who brought it down. It is not fitting for them, nor are they able to do so,”

and then to His words:

“Shall I inform you upon whom the devils descend? They descend upon every sinful liar. They give ear, and most of them are liars.”

He attributes it to the human messenger in His words:

“So I swear by what you see and what you do not see: indeed, it is the word of a noble messenger. It is not the word of a poet—little do you believe! Nor is it the word of a soothsayer—little do you take heed! It is a revelation from the Lord of all worlds.”

He thus denied that it was the word of a poet or a soothsayer, both of whom are human. Likewise, at the end of Surah al-Shuʿarāʾ, He mentions that the devils descend upon every sinful liar, such as the soothsayers who lend them their ears, and that poets are followed by those who have gone astray.

Since these two classes of people might be confused with a human messenger, His exclusion of both makes it clear that the noble messenger is the one chosen from among humankind. For Allah chooses messengers from among the angels and from among people. Likewise, in Surah al-Takwīr, since a devil might be confused with an angel, His denial that it is the word of an accursed devil makes it clear that the messenger mentioned is the one chosen from among the angels.

Its attribution to one messenger on one occasion and to the other on another is evidence that this is an attribution of conveyance and delivery, not of originating or composing any part of it. Yet some Ashʿarī innovators maintain that its letters originated with Gabriel or Muhammad. In half of their doctrine, they thus echo those Arab polytheists who said, “It is the word of a human being,” claiming that he composed it

---

## Page 433

through his own excellence and strength of soul, and those who profess philosophy, who claim that he composed both its meanings and its letters, but that they flowed into him just as knowledge flows into other scholars.

The soothsayer draws upon the devils:

“And poets are followed by those who have gone astray.”

Both employ rhythmic speech: the former uses rhymed prose, the latter verse. Both convey meanings inspired by the devils. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I seek refuge in Allah, the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing, from the accursed devil—from his prodding, his spitting, and his blowing.”

He also said:

“His prodding is madness, his spitting is poetry, and his blowing is arrogance.”

The Almighty’s words, “Nor is it the word of an accursed devil,” exclude both, just as He says in the other surah, “It is not the word of a poet,” and, “Nor the word of a soothsayer.” Likewise, in Surah al-Shuʿarā, He says without qualification:

“It was not the devils who brought it down.”

He then identifies the distinguishing mark of those upon whom the devils descend: such a person is a sinful liar. He also states that poets are followed by those who have gone astray. Thus, the apparent meaning of the Quran does not indicate that the devils descend upon poets unless a poet is a sinful liar. His lying concerns what he says and reports; his sinfulness concerns what he does and commands.

This, and Allah knows best, is because poetry sometimes comes from the devil and sometimes from the soul. When it expresses truth, it may come from the Holy Spirit, as when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed for Hassān ibn Thābit:

“O Allah, support him with the Holy Spirit.”

He also said:

“Satirize them—or exchange satire with them—and Gabriel is with you.”

Having excluded what comes from the devil, He also excluded what comes from the soul. Hence His words, “They are followed by those who have gone astray,” and His exclusion of the pursuit of desires, which are the inclinations of souls.

---

## Page 434

For this reason, Abū Hayyān said:

“Whatever arises within you and your soul loves for your own sake comes from your soul; restrain it from that. Whatever arises within you and your soul dislikes for your own sake comes from Satan; seek refuge in Allah from it.”

This, and Allah knows best, is the reason for that. As for the distinction between the soothsayer and the poet in terms of meaning, it is—and Allah knows best—because speech falls into two kinds: declarative and non-declarative utterances.

The soothsayer reports matters of the unseen, mixing truth with falsehood. They do not convey unadulterated truth. When Satan casts something into the inward thoughts of one of them, into his heart, it is not removed. Rather, most of them are liars, as the Almighty says, and as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained in the hadith about soothsayers when he said:

“They add a hundred lies to the one word.”

This is unlike the messenger, the prophet, and the person who receives inward inspiration, as in the reading of Ibn ʿAbbās and others:

“Then Allah abolishes what Satan casts.”

The commonly accepted reading does not include the person who receives inward inspiration, since he may be left uncorrected in some error, and Satan may introduce something into his inward thoughts without its being removed. This is unlike the messenger and the prophet: what Satan casts must be abolished, and Allah must make His verses firm, because it is the truth. The person who receives inward inspiration is commanded to test what he is inwardly told against what the Messenger brought.

Thus Satan cast suggestions into the heart of ʿUmar, who received inward inspiration, during the incident at al-Hudaybiyya, the incident of the Prophet’s death, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his disagreement with Hishām ibn Hakīm over Surah al-Furqān. The light of prophethood dispelled those suggestions from him.

---

## Page 435

As for the poet, his role is to stir people’s souls; thus his speech belongs to the particular kind of exhortation that arouses desire. For this reason, it is said of them:

“Those who go astray follow them.”

Their harm therefore concerns actions, not beliefs, whereas the harm of the soothsayers concerns beliefs, with actions following from them. Hence Allah says:

“Every sinful liar.”

The substance of soothsaying and poetry is found among many who engage in philosophy, Sufism, speculative theology, and jurisprudence, as well as among ordinary people and those who profess spiritual poverty, who depart from the Sharia. They speak of unseen matters through soothsaying and stir people’s souls through poetry and the like. They are followers of the lying claimants to prophethood and receive support from devils. We have often witnessed this among various groups belonging to these categories and others; it is discernible to those whose breasts Allah has illuminated and into whose hearts He has cast some of His light.

---

## Page 436

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

Those who have deviated and resemble the Sabians are either mujarrida or inclined toward Judaism or Christianity. They include practitioners of logic and syllogistic reasoning who seek knowledge and speculative discourse, and practitioners of deeds and ecstasy who seek spiritual knowledge and spiritual states—the people of letters and the people of sounds. In pursuing the foundations of knowledge concerning Allah, they have followed two paths, each taking one of them. Some may follow one path at one time and the other at another, while others may combine both.

Most of them recognize no path to Allah other than these two, as various writers maintain, including Ibn al-Khatīb and those who follow his approach. Even Abū Hāmid confined the paths to speculative theology and philosophy, consisting in rational inquiry and syllogistic reasoning, or to Sufism and worship, consisting in deeds and ecstasy. He mentioned no categories beyond these three.

Indeed, in Deliverance from Error and Disclosure of Spiritual States, Abū Hāmid described his experiences with the paths to knowledge and the circumstances of the scholar. He stated that the first difficulty he encountered was one that obstructs their path: sophistical skepticism, with its familiar doubts. He related that this malady afflicted him severely for nearly two months, during which he adhered to sophistical skepticism in his actual state, rather than in his reasoning or expressed views, until he was healed

---

## Page 437

by Allah of that malady, and his soul regained health and balance. The self-evident truths of reason were once again accepted and trusted with assurance and clarity. This did not come about through constructing a proof or arranging an argument, but through a light that Allah cast into his breast. That light is the key to the greatest forms of knowledge. He said:

“Whoever supposes that spiritual unveiling depends on abstract proofs has restricted Allah’s vast mercy.”

Then he said:

“I came to regard the paths of seekers as confined to four groups:

The speculative theologians, who claim to be people of reasoned judgment and rational inquiry.

The Bātiniyyah, who claim to be devoted to receiving instruction and specially designated to draw knowledge from the infallible Imam.

The philosophers, who claim to be people of logic and demonstration.

The Sufis, who claim to be the elect of the divine presence and people of unveiling and witnessing.

“I said to myself: ‘Truth cannot lie outside these four groups, for these are the people following the paths to truth. If truth is barred to them, no hope remains of attaining it.’”

He then explained that the purpose and benefit of speculative theology is to defend the Sunnah through disputation, not to ascertain the realities of things; that the teachings of the Bātiniyyah are false; and that philosophy is partly true and partly disbelief, while its true elements do not fulfill the intended aim. He then related that he devoted his efforts to the Sufi path and learned that it could only be attained through knowledge

---

## Page 438

and practice. He therefore began acquiring their knowledge by studying their books, such as Qūt al-Qulūb (Nourishment of Hearts) by Abū Tālib al-Makkī, the books of al-Hārith al-Muhāsibī, and the scattered teachings transmitted from al-Junayd, al-Shiblī, and Abū Yazīd, until he grasped the essence of their intellectual aims. He then knew with certainty that they were people of spiritual states, not of words, and that he had acquired all that could be acquired through study. Nothing remained except what could not be attained through instruction and listening, but only through direct experience and following the path.

He said:

“Through the disciplines I had practised and the paths I had followed in investigating both religious and rational knowledge, I had acquired certain faith in Allah, prophethood, and the Last Day. These three foundations of faith had become firmly rooted in my soul through Allah—not through any single, isolated proof, but through circumstances, indications, and experiences whose details were beyond enumeration. It had also become clear to me that there was no hope of happiness in the Hereafter except through piety.”

He mentioned that he had spent ten years in seclusion, and went on to say:

“During these retreats, matters were unveiled to me that could neither be counted nor fully described. What I shall mention, so that others may benefit from it, is that I came to know with certainty that the Sufis, in particular, are those who follow the path to Allah; that their way of life is the finest, their path the most correct, and their character the purest. Indeed, if the understanding of all rational people, the wisdom of all sages, and the knowledge of all scholars versed in the mysteries of the Sacred Law were brought together to change anything in their conduct and character and replace it with something better, they would find no way to do so.

---

## Page 439

For all their movements and moments of stillness, outward and inward, draw from the niche of the light of prophethood. Beyond the light of prophethood, there is no light on earth by which to find illumination. In short, what can anyone say of a path whose purification—its first requirement—is the complete cleansing of the heart of everything other than Allah, and whose key is the heart’s absorption in the remembrance of Allah?”

I say:

His words indicate that the foundation of the path is the testimony that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, as I have explained more than once. This is the beginning of Islam, yet he made it the final destination. I have also explained the difference between the path of the prophets and that of the philosophers and speculative theologians. He did not, however, know the way of those who know Allah among the people of the Sunnah and hadith, and therefore did not mention it. It is the unadulterated path of Muhammad, by which all other paths are judged.

Al-Suhrawardī al-Halabī, who was put to death, pursued both rational inquiry and devotional practice. Yet he was a thoroughgoing Sabian philosopher who accepted from prophethood only what agreed with his philosophy, unlike those two and others like them.

Some of them initially know only the method of rational inquiry and syllogistic reasoning, as is the case with most speculative theologians among the Jahmiyyah, Muʿtazilah, Ashʿariyyah, and some Hanbalīs.

Others initially know only the method of spiritual discipline, detachment, and Sufism, as is the case with many Sufis and dervishes who fell into the doctrine of union and unrestricted devotional practice, such as ʿAbd Allāh al-Fārisī, al-ʿAfīf al-Tilimsānī, and others like them. Still others may combine both methods, such as al-Sadr al-Qūnawī and others like him.

---

## Page 440

The realm of imagination predominates among them. Sometimes they imagine things that have a reality, and sometimes things that have none: the imagined divinity of human beings, the imaginings of Christians, the notion of the Awaited One, and the notion of the ghawth (Helper) residing in Mecca, through whom they imagine the affairs of heaven and earth are governed. Thus al-Tilimsānī says, “Through spiritual unveiling, we have established what contradicts clear reason.”

For this reason, the practitioner of spiritual seclusion is afflicted with three delusions:

First, he believes that he himself has the greatest aptitude of all people.

Second, he imagines that his shaykh is the most perfect person on earth.

Third, he imagines that he can attain his goal without any means, relying chiefly on the power of imagining. Imagined notions may indeed produce effects, but they are false, as with the shaykhs who have not followed the paths prescribed by religious law and prophethood, whether in rational inquiry or practice, but have instead followed the Sabian path.

Resembling these people in certain respects are most of the Ahmadiyyah, Yūnusiyyah, and Harīriyyah; many of the ʿAdawiyyah and the followers of al-Awhad al-Kirmānī; and a great many Sufis and dervishes in the lands of the East. Thus permissiveness prevails among them, and they do not believe in the obligations and prohibitions of religious law.

When they engage in unrestricted devotional practice, they do not know in their hearts who their deity is. If their heretical adepts define him, they identify him with absolute existence. Some of them worship righteous human beings, their graves, and the like. At times they resemble the polytheists, and at times they resemble

---

## Page 441

the Christians; at other times they resemble the Sabians, and at others the Pharaonic deniers and similar eternalists. They belong to the Sabians, but are unbelievers from the outset.

The untainted among them worship Allah alone, but most of their worship does not follow the Quranic law brought by Muhammad. Thus they deviate either from the testimony that there is no god but Allah or from the testimony that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah. I have written about this elsewhere.

Each of the two paths—rational inquiry and detachment—offers great benefit and considerable value. Indeed, each is obligatory and indispensable, and happiness cannot be complete without it. Throughout, the Quran calls for inquiry, reflection, and contemplation, as well as purification, asceticism, and worship.

In several places, the Quran speaks of perfecting both the intellectual faculty concerned with knowledge and the volitional faculty concerned with action, as in His words:

“He is the One Who sent His Messenger with guidance and the religion of truth to make it prevail over all religion.”

Guidance is the perfection of knowledge, and the religion of truth is the perfection of action. Likewise, He says:

“Possessors of strength and insight.”

And:

“He has inscribed faith in their hearts and strengthened them with a spirit from Him.”

And:

“They believed and did righteous deeds.”

And:

“To Him good words ascend, and righteous deeds.”

In the Prophet’s sermon, Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him, he says:

“The best speech is the speech of Allah, and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad.”

Beneficial inquiry, however, must examine evidence. Examining something that is not evidence yields no knowledge of what is to be established. Evidence is what leads to the desired conclusion and points toward the intended object. Complete evidence consists in the prophetic message and works. Likewise, complete worship consists in doing what the servant has been commanded to do and what the messengers brought. There has, however, been

---

## Page 442

error in both paths because each of them, or both together, was adopted from the outset apart from faith in Allah and in a messenger … (1).

Rather, both were confined to what the heart’s inquiry and spiritual experience alone could yield—sometimes agreeing with what the messengers brought and sometimes contradicting it. This took the form of purely rational inquiry and purely rational acts of worship, or an ascent beyond these to religious inquiry and religious acts of worship. What is required, however, is that inquiry and action must each incorporate the rational, the religious, and the revealed-law dimensions. When they fell short, each of the two groups fell into misguidance, waywardness, or both.

Their resulting condition was either simple ignorance, simple disbelief, compound ignorance, or compound disbelief accompanied by ignorance and wrongdoing.

This is because the method of those who rely on inquiry and syllogistic reasoning revolves around a premise indispensable to every syllogism employed by human beings: a universal, inclusive premise encompassing both the object sought and something other than it. That is, it does not prevent something else from falling within its scope, even if no such other thing actually exists. It therefore encompasses the object sought not by virtue of its distinctive character, but by virtue of what it shares with others. The object sought through this premise is Allah Most High. Yet they reached Him only through the common ground between Him and others in affirmative and negative propositions. What is common to Him and others does not make Him known in His distinctiveness at all, and so they did not know Allah

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space of approximately one line in the original.

---

## Page 443

; rather, when they conceived of Him in terms of what He shared with others, they came to associate partners with Him. They made negative or affirmative judgments about that shared element, and these are valid in general: whatever is denied of the general, shared concept is also denied of the distinct particular, whereas denying something of the distinct particular does not entail denying it of the general.

Thus, whatever you deny of animals or prophets is also denied of human beings or messengers. But denying something of human beings or messengers does not entail denying it of animals or prophets. This is why his saying,

“There is no prophet after me,”

also rules out any messenger after him. Likewise, whatever is universally affirmed of the shared concept is also affirmed of the particular, whereas whatever is affirmed of it without qualification need not be affirmed of the particular. If a judgment applies to every prophet, it includes messengers. If, however, it applies to a prophet without qualification, it need not apply to a messenger.

A combination of negative and affirmative propositions may yield descriptions that apply only to Him and cannot rightly be applied to anyone else—just as a prophet may be described by a combination of attributes found in no one else. Yet this establishes only that nothing else is identical to Him; He Himself is not made known through the combination of those universal propositions. Thus, provided the syllogism is sound, all that the intellect gains through syllogistic reasoning concerning the Lord is knowledge of negation and absence.

This is why the examples set forth in the Quran—which are rational forms of inference—establish negation, as in His saying:

“He presents to you an example from yourselves. Do you have, among those whom your right hands possess, any partners in what We have provided for you?”

—and the rest of the verse; and His saying:

“And Allah presents an example of two men,”

—and the subsequent verses; and His saying:

“O mankind, an example is presented, so listen to it. Indeed, those you invoke besides Allah,”

—and the rest of the verse; and His saying:

“Say: If there were other gods with Him, as they claim,”

—and the rest of the verse; and His saying:

“Allah has not taken

---

## Page 444

any son, nor has there ever been any deity with Him. Otherwise, each deity would have taken away what it created, and some would have overcome others.”

There are similar examples—which are syllogisms—whose import is to negate an antecedent because its necessary consequent is absent, or something of that kind.

For this reason, the knowledge concerning divine lordship attained by philosophers and speculative theologians who employ syllogistic reasoning is predominantly knowledge of negations. Yet they did not confine themselves to what reason can know through syllogisms; they went beyond it. They thus made denials resembling faulty syllogistic reasoning, such as denying the attributes conveyed in prophetic reports. Indeed, the philosophers and the Muʿtazilah even deny the attributes affirmed by theologians who uphold the divine attributes. These theologians call them “rational attributes” because they establish them through rational syllogisms.

It is well known that, through syllogistic reasoning, the intellect can negate only the common element signified by the universal proposition indispensable to a syllogism. For example, it may negate will, mercy, or knowledge insofar as it is shared by the things bearing that name. In created beings, this common element is accompanied by qualities that do not apply to Allah Most High. They therefore deny the unqualified common meaning applicable to both the attributes of the True One and those of His creatures—because they deny what is peculiar to creatures—and thus negate the divine attributes. Likewise, those who liken Him to His creatures affirm what is peculiar to creatures on account of the common element. Both are faulty syllogisms.

Thus, in these attributes—and indeed in the essences themselves—there are three aspects to consider:

First: what is peculiar to the Lord’s essence and attributes.

Second: what is peculiar to the creature and its attributes.

---

## Page 445

Third: the unqualified meaning common to both.

Using a syllogism based on a common meaning to negate the first is mistaken, as is using it to affirm the second. Using it to affirm the third, however, requires the intellect to recognize that the common, universal meaning is established. This is the foundation of syllogistic reasoning and proof. Unless the intellect knows that this is established—either independently or through another syllogism—the syllogism cannot be sound.

Likewise, in their affirmative knowledge, they offer only unqualified, broadly stated meanings: existence, necessary existence, or His being a Lord, a Maker, the First, a Principle, or eternally existent, and similar universal meanings. These do not disclose what is distinctive about the Lord Most High, since syllogistic reasoning does not establish particulars. When someone argues that every contingent existent must have a cause that necessitates its existence, and that everything brought into being must have one who brings it into being, the conclusion of this syllogism is something general. I have discussed this at length elsewhere.

The same applies to those who practise spiritual discipline and detachment. Their elite occupy themselves with a simple form of remembrance, such as “There is no deity but Allah”—unless they go to excess and restrict themselves to merely saying “Allah, Allah,” believing this to be better and more perfect, as many of them have done. Some may even restrict themselves to “He, He,” or to saying, “There is no ‘He’ but He.”

For this innovated remembrance, “He,” conveys by itself only an unspecified reference; it does not in itself mention Allah except through the speaker’s intention. This may also be accompanied by the practitioner’s belief that nothing exists except Him, as some explicitly state, saying, “There is no ‘He’ but He,” or, “There is no existent but He.” And this, according to the advocates of union,

---

## Page 446

is better than saying “There is no deity but Allah,” because it explicitly expresses the reality of their Pharaonic, Qarmatian doctrine. Some of them even say: “‘There is no deity but Allah’ is the remembrance of worshippers; ‘Allah, Allah’ is the remembrance of those who possess spiritual knowledge; and ‘He’ is the remembrance of those who have attained realization.” Such a person makes his remembrance: “O You besides whom there is no ‘He’!”

When someone says “Allah, Allah,” this conveys only the affirmation of His existence. It may be accompanied by the denial of anything other than Him—not merely the denial of the divinity of anything other than Him—and so the practitioner falls into the doctrine of the unity of existence. Sometimes the heart may cease to perceive anything other than Him when the practitioner is in a state of effacement; this comes close. But believing that the existence of created beings is He Himself—that is misguidance.

They combine this with a form of purification, such as renouncing bodily appetites for food and drink, relinquishing leadership, practising seclusion, and other forms of asceticism and worship pursued without qualification. They thus also arrive at an unqualified devotion and a general knowledge affirming the Lord’s reality and existence, and similar conclusions resembling those reached by practitioners of syllogistic reasoning.

This knowledge and understanding may then fade through engagement in ordinary activities, such as eating and mixing with people, because its sole cause is that detachment: when the detachment disappears, the knowledge disappears. Hence the saying: “Every spiritual state that hunger gives you disappears when you are full.” Likewise, the former group’s general knowledge may fade when the heart becomes inattentive to those theoretical syllogisms.

There is no doubt that syllogistic reasoning leads to knowledge corresponding to what it entails, and that spiritual discipline and devotion lead to knowledge corresponding to what they entail. But this is general knowledge arising from a cause that may persist or disappear. It very often leads to union, indwelling, and permissiveness. This is because they detach devotion from what is indispensable to human well-being; when they need those things, they turn away from devotion. Thus, they are either gods in their own eyes, or heretics or sinners. For this reason, I was told by the shaykh

---

## Page 447

Yūsuf, a righteous man and one of our companions, that he had seen me in a dream addressing them (1).

The knowledge attained through this is the knowledge that sets the servant’s condition aright and is obligatory upon him. Yet sincere seeking—through syllogistic reasoning or spiritual experience—may lead him to the prophetic message. He then receives from that message what sets his condition aright and imparts to him the complete understanding and beneficial knowledge that are obligatory upon him. This is the divinely prescribed prophetic path that we mentioned earlier.

This may not happen, however, and many of them come to regard prophethood as dispensable, whether in belief or in practice, by turning away from what it brought. They thus miss out on the faith, knowledge, and understanding brought by the Messenger, the loss of which causes them to stray from guidance in this world and suffer the greatest misery. Such is the condition of those Jews, Christians, and Sabians who disbelieve in the Messenger, even if they believe in the Lord’s existence. Indeed, among Muslims there are those who conceal their disbelief in the Messenger, just as those others openly disbelieve in him. This hypocrisy has been exceedingly widespread, both in earlier times and today.

Corrupt syllogisms and corrupt spiritual experiences may also take root in a person’s heart, leading him to make corrupt judgments concerning divine lordship. These resemble the judgments of those philosophers, speculative theologians, and Sufis who have strayed into Sabian, Jewish, or Christian doctrines. They have deviated either into negating the divine attributes and denying their truth; into equating and likening them to the attributes of created beings; or into believing that the Lord is absolute, undifferentiated existence, and that the very reality of

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Approximately two lines are missing from the original.

---

## Page 448

existence is identical with the Creator, and that there is nothing else beyond the heavens and the earth. Rather, all these things are levels of the attributes, and lordship and divinity are conjectural gradations in the mind. In reality, however, there is nothing but His very essence. Those who are veiled perceive the gradations, whereas the one granted spiritual unveiling sees nothing but the very being of the Real.

They suppose—and through their influence many others suppose—that this monotheism is the monotheism of the eminently truthful who knew Allah and said:

“Indeed, everything apart from Allah is vain.”

Likewise, the deviant speculative theologian supposes that his monotheism—which consists of negating the divine attributes—is the monotheism of the prophets and the eminently truthful who knew Allah. Thus, associating partners with Allah is common among these people, to the point that some prostrate themselves before others. Similarly, the other group declares lawful contracts and permissible acts of worship forbidden. The two groups have thus divided between themselves the practices for which Allah condemned the polytheists: associating partners with Him and forbidding what is lawful. . . . (1)

Such things are also frequently found among these people who resemble the Christians. Among the others there have appeared burdens and shackles, denial of the truth, and hardness of heart—things frequently found among these people who resemble the Jews.

This concerns those among them who are not extremists. As for the extremists of both groups, they hold that their knowledge and spiritual state surpass those of the prophets. As al-Tilimsānī says:

“The Quran leads to Paradise, whereas our words lead to Allah.”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) One line is missing from the original.

---

## Page 449

And likewise, al-Fārābī claims that the philosopher is more perfect than the prophet, and that the prophet’s only distinctive quality is his skill in representing truths through imaginative images. They proceed to forms of heresy and unbelief in which they align themselves with the Ismā‘īlīs, Nusayrīs, Qarmatians, and Bātinīs, and follow Pharaoh, Nimrod, and others like them who reject the prophetic missions, or both prophethood and divine lordship.

This is exceedingly common among both groups. Its cause is the absence from their hearts of a foundation: faith in Allah and the Messenger. If this foundation does not accompany the rational inquirer, the spiritual aspirant, and the seeker at every stage, he will suffer manifest loss. His need for it is like the body’s need for nourishment, or life’s need for the soul. Without life and nourishment, a person cannot subsist at all, nor can he be taught or teach.

Likewise, without faith in Allah and His Messenger, a person cannot attain knowledge of Allah or guidance to Him. Without being guided to his Lord, he can only be wretched and tormented. Such is the condition of those who disbelieve in Allah and His Messenger. With faith in Allah and His Messenger, however, when he reflects and reasons, his reflection rests on evidence and proof—namely, the established reality of divine lordship and prophethood. When he detaches and purifies himself, he possesses faith that he thereby tastes and experiences. This reflection and this spiritual tasting then bring him various further signs of the Lord and spiritual experiences relating to Him. Knowledge and spiritual experience are inseparable.

This is because the prophets and messengers knew Allah through revelation with true knowledge, and worshipped Him with the worship that is His due, according to what Allah Most High granted them. They differ in rank in this regard; yet they knew particular aspects of divine lordship that cannot be established by

---

## Page 450

theoretical reasoning alone, nor attained through spiritual tasting through the will alone. They then conveyed what they knew. In describing Him and conveying knowledge of Him, they necessarily referred to the One named and described using names and descriptions with shared meanings, while distinguishing Him from created beings in ways that exclude any sharing in His distinctive reality. For the purpose of conveying and describing is to impart knowledge to those addressed, who do not know the realities peculiar to Allah’s essence and attributes. Had the prophets conveyed only those distinctive realities, without anything else, their hearers would have understood nothing; indeed, they might even have denied them.

When people are addressed using shared meanings, and the harmful implications of this sharing are removed by statements excluding any likeness, such as His words:

“There is nothing like unto Him,”

and:

“And there is none comparable to Him,”

and similar statements, they fall into one of two categories.

The first is a believer who believes in the meanings of those attributes in general, considered without qualification, and affirms them for Allah in a manner befitting Him and peculiar to Him, in which no created being shares. This is the utmost attainable by people in this condition.

The second is someone into whose heart Allah has cast such light and special guidance that He enables him to witness something of the distinctive realities actually denoted by those names and attributes. He knows this neither through reasoning alone nor through spiritual experience alone, but through direct witnessing corresponding to what the messengers conveyed. The agreement of his witnessing with what the messengers announced shows him that his witnessing is sound. He thereby receives a share of prophethood: prophethood in its entirety has ceased, but the existence of some of its constituent parts has not ceased.

In some matters, he must nevertheless remain veiled from witnessing what the prophet witnessed. He therefore believes the prophet concerning them, because he has witnessed some of what the prophet conveyed. What he has witnessed remains firmly established for him as a basis for affirming what he has not witnessed. This is the condition of those foremost in truthfulness in relation to the prophets.

---

## Page 451

This is comparable to someone who has been given a description of a city’s government and its various features, then travels there and witnesses some of its affairs, which indicate to him that the informant is truthful concerning what he has not witnessed. I do not regard this witnessing alone as sufficient confirmation, for an informant may be believed on some points yet be mistaken on others. Rather, this confirmation comes through the informant’s report—that is, the Messenger of Allah—and through witnessing in him what establishes that he cannot lie, as explained elsewhere.

You may ask:

“What initially establishes for him the soundness of faith in Allah and His Messenger, so that this becomes a foundation upon which he builds and proceeds to what follows?

“Those who follow syllogistic reasoning and spiritual experience have laboured so long precisely to establish this foundation within themselves. Hence, speculative theologians call everything that establishes divine lordship and prophethood matters of reason and theoretical inquiry, whereas the others call them matters of spiritual tasting and experience. They hold that whatever is indispensable to knowing Allah and His Messenger must itself be known beforehand; otherwise, circular reasoning would result. They therefore classify these as rational matters, and rational matters can be attained only through rational, logical syllogism.”

I reply: This can be answered in several ways.

First, the same objection can be raised in return. How does someone following the path of syllogistic inquiry, or pursuing spiritual tasting through the will, initially know that following this path will yield knowledge and understanding? At the outset, he has nothing beyond someone’s report that he followed this path and attained his goal, or a thought arising in his heart to follow it—whether merely considering attainment possible, actively seeking it, or otherwise—or simply beginning to follow it without reaching its end. This is not peculiar to knowledge of the divine. Rather, in every field of knowledge, anyone embarking upon its study must initially accept certain premises as given until they are demonstrated later.

---

## Page 452

For if every seeker of knowledge had already attained that knowledge when seeking it, he would not be seeking it. He may know that the path he follows will lead him to knowledge. But the question concerns the first of all starting points, the proof underlying all proofs, and the foundation of all foundations.

If, when examining a proof, he already knows that it leads to its conclusion, this is only possible once he knows its connection to that conclusion. For unless a proof entails its conclusion, it is not a proof. Knowledge of entailment depends upon knowledge of both what entails and what is entailed. Thus, he cannot know that something is proof of a particular conclusion until he knows that this particular conclusion holds and that the proof entails it. Once he knows this, he no longer needs to use that proof to establish the conclusion; it serves only to remind him of it, not to give him knowledge of it for the first time.

Confusion arises here because a person often knows that something exists, then seeks a way to know its attributes and directly witness the thing itself, either through the senses or through the heart. He therefore follows a path that he knows will lead to his goal, because he already knows that this path necessarily leads to that goal, whose existence he had known beforehand.

This is like someone who wishes to make pilgrimage to the Kaaba, which he already knows exists. He follows a route that he knows leads to the Kaaba because people have told him so, or he takes as his guide someone whom he knows to be familiar with that route. Whether he follows the route himself, having learned from those who have reached the destination that it leads to his goal, or travels with an expert guide—who directs him at every stage—he does so only after knowing that his goal exists and that the route and guide are indeed such.

Such is also the case with those seeking knowledge of Allah, those who desire Him, and those journeying towards Him: they have first come to know His existence

---

## Page 453

and they seek to know His attributes or to witness Him with their hearts in this world. They therefore follow the path leading to this through faith and the Quran.

Faith is like a man’s following a route described to him by those who have travelled it, whose accounts all agree.

As for the Quran, it means accepting as true what the messengers report. This is like following a guide stage by stage. Both are indispensable on the path to Allah.

As for something whose existence reason did not know beforehand, anyone following a path leading to knowledge of it can only begin by relying on others and accepting unproven assumptions—as with the other first principles of the sciences. Thus, if syllogistic and practical methods necessarily involve reliance on others at the outset—when following what one does not yet know to be a path leading to the goal, or when one does not yet know that the goal exists—then the faith-based method, even supposing it were likewise, would not be discredited on that account. Rather, it would have the stronger claim to acceptance for many reasons. We shall mention some of them, Allah willing.

Indeed, there is no path other than this, or one that leads to it or accompanies it. It is therefore unquestionably necessary for attaining the goal, whereas other methods are not: the goal can be attained without them. They may even obstruct its attainment, so that it is not reached, or its opposite is reached instead—either outcome being the greatest misery. This path therefore certainly leads to the goal and contains no unsoundness, whereas other methods are frequently beset by unsoundness and cannot lead to it on their own. The faith-based method is indispensable.

---

## Page 454

The second aspect of the reply:

If a man follows the methods of syllogistic reasoning and discipline, and they lead him to knowledge—if indeed they do—he then knows that he has followed a sound path and attained his goal. Before that, however, he does not know. At the very least, the faith-based method—though there is nothing inferior about it—would be in the same position.

For if he accepts faith in Allah and His messengers as a premise, reflects on what it entails, and acts accordingly, he attains, with the slightest effort, the knowledge of Allah that he seeks, together with the knowledge that the path he has followed is sound. Indeed, the very act of believing the Messenger’s reports concerning his Lord and obeying him establishes in him certain knowledge of their truth, far stronger than what was mentioned earlier.

The third aspect:

Acknowledgment of Allah is of two kinds: innate and faith-based.

Innate acknowledgment—recognition of the Creator’s existence—is firmly rooted in human nature, as Allah has affirmed in several places in His Book. I have discussed this at length elsewhere. It requires no proof; rather, it is the most deeply rooted knowledge, the most firmly established understanding, and the foundation of all foundations.

As for acknowledging the Messenger, the slightest reflection on what he brought, his character and conduct, his signs, or other aspects of his life yields knowledge of his prophethood far stronger than that obtained through syllogistic and experiential inquiries into divine matters.

Then, if one examines his circumstances more closely, one gains such compelling certainty, impossible to resist, that it becomes a firmly established foundation. This has been explained at length elsewhere. The purpose here is to show the error of following the path of syllogistic reasoning or discipline without beginning with faith. As for establishing the faith-based method, that is a momentous undertaking, far greater than what I have written here.

The fourth aspect:

We are addressing Muslims who profess faith, whose individual aim is

---

## Page 455

the special knowledge of Allah that distinguishes scholars and those who know Him from ordinary people. Some therefore follow the method of those who employ innovated syllogistic reasoning, philosophers, and speculative theologians; others follow the innovated method of discipline and spiritual striving practiced by philosophical and Sufi practitioners, turning away from what the Messenger brought concerning the details of these matters. Yet if they know the truthfulness of the Messenger—who conveys his Lord’s message, guides people to Him, and calls them to Him, and for whom Allah perfected the religion and to whom He revealed the Book as an explanation of all things—how can they abandon reasoning from what he brought and following his example in favor of the two methods mentioned?

The fifth aspect:

Most of those who followed these two deviant paths did not believe that there was a third path—as certain eminent men, mistaken about fundamental principles, maintain. Thus, they move from Sabaean philosophical doctrines to Christian teachings centered on spiritual striving, and then to Jewish speculative theology. Their philosophers side one day with their practitioners of spiritual striving and another day with their speculative theologians, bewildered as they go along with these groups.

As for the faith-based, prophetic path of Muhammad—the religious path of the Sunnah and transmitted reports—they do not find their way to it, do not know it, and do not think that it offers a way to what they seek or leads to their goal. This is because they believe that no one follows it, or because they have unjustly restrained themselves from following it. Thus, because they have lost their way to it, or because of their waywardness and ignorance of it, or because they wrong themselves, they have turned away from it.

If you say:

“But the Quran commands reflection upon the signs.”

---

## Page 456

I reply:

There is no doubt that reflection is valid in principle: reflecting upon evidence leads to knowledge of what it establishes, and reflecting upon Allah’s signs leads to faith in Him, which is the foundation of worship. Likewise, there is no doubt that worship and volition are valid in principle and that, when they follow the way of the prophets, they lead to Allah’s good pleasure. But you must distinguish between signs and syllogistic reasoning, as we have explained elsewhere.

A sign is an indication: it intrinsically entails what it indicates, without an intervening middle term through which a syllogism containing a universal premise is constructed. Thus, sunlight is a sign of the sun; likewise, vegetation in barren land is a sign of rain, and smoke is a sign of fire—even if no syllogism is formulated in the mind. Rather, the intellect itself knows the necessary connection between the two, so the presence of the sign establishes the presence of what it necessarily entails. Knowledge of this necessary connection may or may not be innate.

The sixth aspect:

Those two paths are not wholly false. Rather, each leads to some truth, but not the truth that one is obliged to attain; moreover, falsehood often accompanies it. Neither path, taken alone, therefore ensures the fulfillment of obligations or the avoidance of what is forbidden. Nor, after the Messenger has been sent, do they attain the goal in which the servant’s happiness lies: his salvation and bliss.

As for the method of speculative, syllogistic reasoning:

It necessarily involves inferring the Necessary Existent from contingent existence, the originator from what has come into being, or the mover from motion. This establishes a mighty agent in general terms. Likewise, the method of spiritual discipline and experiential perception brings the heart into submission and humility before the Maker

---

## Page 457

in an absolute sense. Each of these two methods necessarily involves knowledge that the heart is compelled to accept, for the heart acquires knowledge only of this compelling kind, beginning through the mediation of necessary knowledge. Reasoning is built upon premises that ultimately rest on necessary knowledge, whether mediated by sensory perception or independent of it.

The syllogistic method yields knowledge through necessary premises, as when one says:

“That which is known to exist is either contingent or necessary, and the contingent can exist only through a Necessary Existent. Thus, the existence of a Necessary Existent is established on either supposition.”

Or when one says:

“The world has come into being, or much of it has come into being.” The latter is necessarily known, while the former requires proof.

Then one says:

“Everything that has come into being has an originator.”

Or one says:

“There is no doubt that something exists. It is either beginningless or has come into being, and whatever has come into being necessarily requires something beginningless. Thus, the existence of something beginningless is established on either supposition.”

Likewise, one says:

“There is no doubt that something exists. It is either necessary or contingent, and the contingent necessarily requires a Necessary Existent. Thus, the existence of a Necessary Existent is established on either supposition.”

One may also say:

“There is no doubt that something exists. It is either made or unmade, created or uncreated, or brought into being or not brought into being. Whatever is made, created, or brought into being necessarily requires a maker, a creator, and one who brings it into being. Thus, the existence of something that is neither made, nor brought into being, nor created is established on either supposition.”

---

## Page 458

These arguments and others like them establish the existence of a beginningless Necessary Existent that is not made. The question, however, is how to identify it, for most of the Dahriyya say: “It is the world, or something subsisting in it.”

Moreover, the dependence of the contingent upon the necessary, of what has come into being upon the beginningless, and of what is made upon a maker is a necessary premise. Some speculative thinkers nevertheless seek to prove this premise, as well as the proposition that neither of the two alternatives for a contingent being can prevail over the other without a determining factor. The majority consider their necessary character sufficient in both cases.

The devotional method yields knowledge through spiritual discipline and purity of soul, for necessary knowledge then arises in the heart. Thus, when ‘Izz al-Dīn ibn ‘Abd al-Salām came to Shaykh Ismā‘īl al-Kūrānī seeking knowledge of Allah—having pursued the method of speculative theology—the shaykh said to him:

“You say that Allah is known through proof, whereas we say that He made Himself known to us, and so we knew Him.”

Likewise, when Ibn al-Khatīb and his Mu‘tazilī companion asked Najm al-Dīn al-Kubrā about knowledge of certainty, he said:

“It consists of impressions that come upon souls, which the souls are unable to repel.”

He thus answered them that, for us, knowledge of certainty is attained necessarily, not through reasoning. This is a good answer.

Necessary knowledge is that which binds a person’s soul so inescapably that he cannot free himself from it. Unless someone employing syllogistic reasoning acquires necessary knowledge at the outset, he must base his reasoning and syllogism on necessary premises. Knowledge then results for him.

For this reason, a number of scholars, including Abū al-Ma‘ālī al-Juwaynī, said that all knowledge is necessary

---

## Page 459

in its various forms: once the evidence has been properly examined, knowledge necessarily results. Some knowledge, however, is necessary as soon as the two terms of a proposition are conceived; some becomes necessary after reflection and examination; and some becomes necessary after examining a proof consisting of two or more premises.

The shaykh endowed with spiritual knowledge said:

“We experience knowledge as something necessary through the path we follow: purifying the soul and reforming the heart, which bears knowledge and calls it forth.”

Thus, Allah pours knowledge into the heart of each of them and sends it down upon his innermost heart. One, however, attains this by acquiring the knowledge accompanying the knowledge sought—that is, the premises—whereas the other does so by reforming that which seeks knowledge and wishes to possess it: the heart.

This is like a man seeking to marry a woman. In one case, he makes himself attractive to her and places himself before her until she sees him, desires him, and seeks him in marriage. In another, he sends her someone with whom she feels at ease and whom she obeys; this person proposes to her on his behalf, and she accepts. The first man's effort and work consist in improving himself and presenting himself to her until she desires him. The second man's effort consists in securing an intermediary whom she will obey, so that she accepts. It is also like someone hunting game.

Yet reasoning and devotional practice alone, whether combined or pursued separately, yield only knowledge of a general nature. This is what actually occurs, and it is valid, for the general truth thus established is indeed true. If they supplement this with the detailed knowledge gained through the light of the prophetic message, beneficial faith is attained, and the feared adverse consequences of those two paths are removed.

This is the condition of those practitioners of theological reasoning and devotional discipline who have aligned themselves with following the Messenger and believing in him, accepting what he brought and learning from him.

---

## Page 460

And if either of them does not supplement this with what the Messenger brought, he either adds its opposite or adds nothing. If he adds the opposite of what the Messenger brought, he falls into denial, which is compound disbelief. If he adds nothing, he remains in simple disbelief, whether he is in doubt or in a state of aversion and heedlessness.

A disbeliever either conceives of the message or does not. If he does not conceive of it, he is heedless of it and has no faith in it. As Allah says:

“Do not obey one whose heart We have made heedless of Our remembrance, who follows his own desires, and whose conduct exceeds all bounds.”

And He says:

“So We exacted retribution from them and drowned them in the sea because they denied Our signs and were heedless of them.”

However, complete heedlessness occurs only in someone whom the message has not reached, and disbelief for which a person is punished occurs only after the message has reached him. This is why He coupled denial with heedlessness. If someone conceives of what the Messenger brought but turns away, he is averse to it, as Allah Most High says:

“When guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray nor suffer.”

“But whoever turns away from My remembrance will have a life of hardship.”

And as He says:

“You see the hypocrites turning away from you in aversion.”

And as He says:

“When they are told, ‘Follow what Allah has revealed,’ they say, ‘Rather, we follow what we found our forefathers following.’”

If, despite this, he has no share in it—neither affirming nor denying it, neither loving nor hating it—then he is in doubt about it. Allah has described this as the condition of many disbelievers, whether hypocrites or others, as He says:

“Only those who do not believe in Allah and the Last Day ask your permission. Their hearts are filled with doubt, and in their doubt they waver.”

And as Moses said: “Has there not come to you the news of those before you—the people of Noah

---

## Page 461

and Ād and Thamūd, and those after them, whom none but Allah knows? Their messengers came to them with clear proofs, but they put their hands to their mouths and said, ‘We reject what you have been sent with, and we are indeed in troubling doubt about what you call us to.’”

“Their messengers said, ‘Can there be doubt about Allah, the Originator of the heavens and the earth? He calls you so that He may forgive you some of your sins and grant you respite until an appointed term.’ They said, ‘You are only human beings like us. You wish to turn us away from what our forefathers worshipped. So bring us clear proof.’”

“Their messengers said to them, ‘We are only human beings like you, but Allah bestows His favour upon whomever He wills of His servants. We cannot bring you any proof except by Allah’s permission. And in Allah let the believers put their trust.’”

Thus, Allah—glorified be He—relates how the disbelievers disputed with the messengers, first concerning lordship, for they doubted Allah, to whom the messengers were calling them; and second concerning prophethood, saying:

“You are only human beings like us.”

This is precisely the inquiry pursued by the disbelieving philosophers. If a person denies it, however, that is denial; and denial is narrower in scope than disbelief. Everyone who denies what the messengers brought is a disbeliever, but not every disbeliever denies it. Rather, he may be doubtful if he is examining it, or turn away from it after having given it no consideration. He may also be heedless of it, having formed no conception of it whatsoever. Yet his punishment depends upon the message being conveyed to him.

Both possibilities—adding denial to a general knowledge, or adding disbelief without denial—occur frequently among those who follow either of the two paths: reasoning through abstract inference, and practising worship alone. For example, many speculative thinkers have affirmed the Necessary Existent or the Maker of the world, yet have adopted various positions concerning His identity and attributes, positions whose details cannot be set out here—which are well known

---

## Page 462

in works on doctrines by members of our faith and those outside it—the doctrines of Muslims who pray and the doctrines of others. Many later worshippers likewise affirmed this in general terms, yet entertained various unbelieving notions concerning Him, as described by those familiar with them.

Some imagined Him to be the absolute existence common to existing beings, like universal humanity in relation to its concrete instances and individuals. If existence becomes determinate, it is not Him, since the absolute is not the determinate, as al-Sadr al-Qūnawī maintains. Others imagined that the existence of contingent beings is His very existence emanating upon them, as the author of al-Fusūs (The Bezels) states.

Others imagined Him to be the totality of existence, with every particular being constituting a part of Him, like the sea in relation to its waves, or a human being in relation to his limbs. Thus, He is not what is peculiar to each particular being, but rather the aggregate of all beings. This is the view of al-Afīf al-Tilmisānī and Abd Allāh al-Fārisī al-Balyānī. They say that every existing being is a level of existence or one of its manifestations, like the waves of the sea in relation to the sea, a human being’s limbs in relation to him, or parts of hawā in relation to air; or like this particular human being and this particular animal in relation to universal animality and universal humanity.

Their poet Ibn Isrā’īl says:

“You are not other than the cosmos; rather, you are its very self …
Only one who has tasted it understands this secret.”

He also said:

“My hand delights, if it passes over my body …
For in truth I am none other than you.”

---

## Page 463

For this reason, they hold that a person has no ultimate goal beyond himself. His only goal is for the veil over his own self to be lifted, so that he sees that his self is the Real. Previously, he had been veiled from it; when he beholds the reality, he sees that he himself is it. As Ibn Isrā’īl said:

“What ails your pale camels, that they never come to rest? … How long will you wander astray, ever moving on?
When you reach your destination, you will surely learn … That your journey was only to yourself.”

And as some of them say:

“In everything there is a sign of Him … Indicating that He is its very self.”
[Things]
(*)

Yet Allah says:

“Indeed, to your Lord is the return.”

And He says:

“O mankind, indeed you are laboring toward your Lord with great exertion.”

And He says:

“Then they are returned to Allah, their true Master.”

And He says:

“Indeed, we belong to Allah, and indeed to Him we will return.”

And there are other verses to the same effect.

Al-Tilmisānī, who was deeply entrenched in this heresy—which they called monotheism and reality—said:

“I once imagined that Laylā had veiled her face … And that a veil before her prevented a kiss.
Then she appeared: no, by Allah, her concealment was nothing … But my gaze being blind to her love.”

He has much poetry of this kind:

“She is the pure, beginningless substance; and if there appears … Any impurity in her that she has brought forth, it is of later origin.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) The word al-ashyā’ (“things”) is an interpolation, not part of the verse (see Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā, p. 254).

---

## Page 464

I swore to them that nothing that came from her was other than her own essence … They said, ‘Be cautious concerning her, for you have sworn falsely.’”

He also wrote:

“Tell your beloved: die of yearning and melt in ecstasy … For her; and tell the loss of reason: do not depart.
Remain silent until you see her speaking within you … And if you find a tongue that speaks, then speak.”

For this reason, they reach a station at which they no longer believe that obligatory duties are binding or that forbidden acts are prohibited. They regard obligations and prohibitions as applying only to those whom they consider veiled—those who have not witnessed that He is the very reality of the universe. Who, then, is the worshipper, and who is the one worshipped? Who gives the command, and who receives it?

As the author of al-Futūhāt says at its beginning:

“The Lord is the Real, and the servant is the Real … If only I knew: who bears the obligation?
If you say, ‘The servant,’ he is dead … Or if you say, ‘The Lord,’ how can obligations be imposed on Him?”

In their view, religious obligation belongs to one of the ranks of the names and attributes: the rank of the one who tests.

One of them said:

“All is but a single order … In which there is neither praise nor blame.
It is only custom, nature, and the Lawgiver … That have assigned particular judgments.”

---

## Page 465

These two originate with the Sabians—as becomes clear upon reflection. For the Sabians who have departed from affirming the oneness of Allah alone, without partner—like the polytheists and Magians, such as Moses’ Pharaoh, Abraham’s Nimrod, and other human beings—acknowledge absolute existence.

For this reason, the highest of the philosophers’ sciences was metaphysics. By philosophers, I mean the Peripatetics who follow Aristotle, whom they regard as the First Teacher. He wrote on various disciplines, including the branches of logic and natural science, such as animals, place, the heavens and the world, and meteorological phenomena. He also wrote on metaphysics—which they regard as the culmination of their wisdom and the pinnacle of their philosophy. This is the science that later philosophers, such as Ibn Sīnā, call “divine science.”

According to its practitioners, the subject of this science is absolute existence and its concomitants. It thus discusses the existent and the nonexistent, then divides the existent into necessary and contingent, eternal and originated, cause and effect, substance and accident, and the like. It then discusses the various kinds within these divisions and their properties.

Examples include dividing causes into four kinds: the efficient and final causes, which account for a thing’s existence, and matter and form, which account for the essential reality of a composite; and dividing accidents into the nine categorical genera: quality, quantity, position, place, time, relation, possession, acting, and being acted upon—or classifying them into five, according to their differing views.

---

## Page 466

And at the end of metaphysics comes the section designated by the letter lām, in which the First Teacher briefly discusses the Necessary Existent in Himself—as though He were the final cause toward which motion is directed, just as the First Teacher established His existence through an argument from motion—mentioning only a few of His attributes. This was the subject of a statement Ibn Sīnā used to make (1). Such, then, is the First Teacher’s knowledge of Allah.

As for prophethood and messengers, these philosophers have no known discussion of them, either denying or affirming them. As for the later philosophers: the upright faith—the Abrahamic faith of monotheism—rose to prominence first through the prophethood of Jesus, when the Christians prevailed over the kingdom of the Sabians in Syria, Egypt, the Byzantine lands, and elsewhere, and then through the prophethood of the Seal of the Messengers. Allah brought forth from the light of prophethood a sun that blotted out the light of the stars. The early generations lived in its light for a long time. Then some of the light of prophethood became obscured, and some books by foreign philosophers from the Byzantine lands, Persia, and India were translated into Arabic during the Abbasid dynasty.

Then, during the reign of al-Ma’mūn, their books were sought from the Byzantine lands, translated into Arabic, and studied by people. As a result, various religious innovations arose. Most of their learning that became widespread belonged to the mathematical sciences, such as arithmetic and astronomy; the natural sciences, such as medicine; or logic. As for divine science, their discussion of it is scant, and, scant though it is, they themselves do not regard most of it as certain. Yet the Muslims possess knowledge of divine matters inherited from the Seal of the Messengers that has filled the world with light and guidance,

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Ibn Sīnā’s statement is missing from the original text.

---

## Page 467

indeed, even their speculative theologians who are accused of religious innovations possess, through the methods of reasoning they have devised, many, many times more knowledge of divine matters than the most accomplished practitioners of philosophy.

Later, when some among them, such as al-Fārābī, Ibn Sīnā, and others, became proficient in metaphysics according to the philosophers’ method, Ibn Sīnā composed books in which he added matters that his predecessors had not mentioned, drawing on principles they shared. This was called divine science. He discussed prophethood, miraculous gifts, and the spiritual stations of those who know Allah in terms that were noble and elevated compared with those of his predecessors.

Yet, measured against prophetic knowledge of divine matters, his discussion contains deficiencies, failings, hypocrisy, ignorance, misguidance, and unbelief that are plain to anyone with even the slightest insight into knowledge and faith. It found acceptance only among those who followed the philosophers’ path, because he made knowledge of Allah, prophethood, prophetic miracles, and friendship with Allah more accessible to them according to the principles of the Sabian philosophers—not according to the truth itself—through the light of the message and the proof of prophethood that had shone upon their ignorance.

This was akin to what was done by Nestorius, the Christian who lived in the time of al-Ma’mūn and to whom the Nestorian doctrine concerning the Trinity and the union is attributed. Through the light of the Muslims that illuminated him, however, he removed much of the corruption in the Christian creed, though substantial remnants of it remained with him. Likewise, when the Christian Yahyā ibn ʿAdī took up philosophy, he brought the Christian doctrine of the Trinity closer to the philosophers’ principles concerning the intellect, the subject that understands, and the object understood.

---

## Page 468

For this reason, the pure philosophers—those who adhere strictly to the teachings of the Peripatetics—believe that Ibn Sīnā was pandering to adherents of religion, because of the rapprochement he achieved. But they spoke in ignorance and lied. He was not pandering; rather, whatever truth he acknowledged, he stated because truth required it and because it accorded with their rational principles.

Likewise, the metaphysical philosophers, both Peripatetic and otherwise, agree in affirming the Necessary Existent, the survival of the soul after death, and the benefit of righteous deeds after death. Many natural philosophers and others disagree with them on these matters. Indeed, even the metaphysical philosophers disagree among themselves on some of them. It is even said that al-Fārābī—whom they regard as the Second Teacher—made conflicting statements on this subject. At one time he affirmed the survival of all souls; at another, the survival of knowledgeable souls but not ignorant ones, as he stated in Ārā’ al-Madīna al-Fādila (The Opinions of the Virtuous City); and at yet another, he denied both views. This misguided unbeliever claimed that the distinctive quality of prophethood was skill in imaginatively representing spiritual realities. Their contradictory statements on this subject are numerous, but recounting them is not the purpose here.

The point is simply that, in their view, the highest science and first philosophy are metaphysics: the study of absolute existence and its concomitants. Indeed, even Muslim speculative theologians with a philosophical background—such as Ibn al-Khatīb and others—discuss the principles of Islamic jurisprudence, a purely Islamic discipline, yet base it on those philosophical principles.

For example, Ibn al-Khatīb and others, following Ibn Sīnā and his predecessors, state at the outset of their treatment of the principles of Islamic jurisprudence: the first principles of the particular sciences are not established within those sciences, lest this entail circular reasoning, for the starting point of a science is its foundational principles

---

## Page 469

and it is known only after those principles are known. If its principles were known through its constituent questions, which themselves depend on those principles, circular reasoning would follow. Rather, its principles are accepted as given and posited within a higher science, until one reaches the highest science, which studies existence and its concomitants.

They say this about such disciplines as medicine and arithmetic. A physician, insofar as he is a physician, examines an animal’s body, humours, and organs to preserve its health if it is healthy and restore its health if it has been lost. The animal’s body is one of the things generated on earth, as are its humours. Broader than this is the study of things generated from the four elements: water, air, fire, and earth. Broader still is the study of bodies subject to transformation, then of body in the absolute sense. Thus, whenever a science concerns a subject comprising certain entities existing externally or in thought, a broader study concerns what that subject shares with others.

But to include knowledge of Allah—the highest and noblest of all sciences—in this scheme, making it a branch of what they regard as the highest science, namely the study of existence and its concomitants, and likewise including the associated knowledge of His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day: this is the source of their error in logical reasoning.

This becomes clear in several ways.

First: Allah, glorified is He, is the Most High and is greater than all else. Hence the distinguishing proclamation of the most perfect religion is “Allah is greater,” uttered by its adherents in their prayers, calls to prayer, and festivals.
