The same applies to these proponents of the identity of Creator and creation: their leading figures are leaders of unbelief who must be put to death. The repentance of any of them is not accepted if he is apprehended before repenting, for he is among the worst heretics who outwardly profess Islam while concealing the gravest unbelief. These are the ones who understand their own teachings and their opposition to the religion of the Muslims.

Everyone who affiliates himself with them, defends them, praises them, venerates their books, is known to assist and support them, dislikes criticism of them, or begins making excuses for them must be punished. Such excuses include claiming not to know what this discourse means or who said that he authored this book, and similar excuses offered only by an ignorant person or a hypocrite. Indeed, everyone who knows their true condition but does not help take action against them must be punished, for taking action against them is among the greatest obligations. They have corrupted the minds and religion of many shaykhs, scholars, kings, and rulers. They spread corruption throughout the land and turn people away from the path of Allah.

The harm they cause to religion is greater than that caused by those who ruin the Muslims’ worldly affairs while leaving their religion intact, such as highway robbers and the Tatars who take their wealth but leave them their religion. Those unfamiliar with them do not underestimate them. Their misguidance and their misleading of others are beyond description, and they resemble the esotericist Qarmatians more closely than anyone else.

For this reason, they desire Tatar rule and prefer their victory over the Muslims—except for ordinary people among their adherents and followers, who do not know the true nature of their cause. For this reason, they endorse the Jews and Christians in their existing beliefs and regard them as following the truth, just as they regard idol worshippers as following the truth. Each of these positions is among the gravest forms of unbelief. And whoever

---

## Page 515

has thought well of them—and claimed that he did not know their true condition—and then learns their true condition is to be classed with them and counted as one of them if he does not dissociate himself from them and openly express his disapproval to them.

As for anyone who says that their words admit an interpretation consistent with the Sharia, he is one of their leading figures and leaders. If he is intelligent, he knows that what he has said is a lie; if he believes this both inwardly and outwardly, his unbelief is greater than that of the Christians. Thus, anyone who does not declare these people unbelievers and offers an interpretation of their words would be even further from declaring Christians unbelievers for their doctrines of the Trinity and the union of the divine and human natures.

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 516

And Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyya—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

“All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

“The Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.”

“Master of the Day of Judgment.”

I bear witness that none is worthy of worship except Allah, the One, the True, the Manifest. And I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger, the Seal of the Prophets. May Allah grant him blessings and abundant peace, and likewise all his fellow messengers.

To proceed:

Your letter has reached me, requesting an explanation of the doctrine of these proponents of the unity of existence and a demonstration of its falsity. You mentioned that you had already heard from me part of an explanation of the unsoundness of their views, but that you had insufficient time to hear the remainder, as your journey required you to depart in haste. Subsequently, you encountered among your people some who defend their views and profess adherence to the spiritual path and spiritual reality. Your letter struck a chord with me, and you found a receptive audience. I have therefore written what I hope Allah will make beneficial to the believers and use to ward off the harm of these

---

## Page 517

heretical hypocrites who deviate concerning Allah’s names and His signs—both those in His creation and those revealed in His clear Book—and make clear the distinction between the rightly guided people of knowledge and spiritual insight, whose understanding is sound and whose faith is certain, and these heretics who imitate those possessed of spiritual insight, just as pretenders to prophethood have imitated the prophets, and just as they have likened invented poetry and the tales of fabricators to Allah’s speech. This is so that it may become clear that these people belong to the same category as the disbelievers, hypocrites, and apostates: the followers of Pharaoh, the Qarmatian esotericists, and the followers of Musaylima, al-Ansī, and other such fabricators. It should likewise become clear that the people of knowledge and faith—the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous, whether they are the foremost, brought near to Allah, or those who follow a middle course, the people of the right—are followers of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend; Moses, to whom Allah spoke; and Muhammad, who was sent to all humanity.

In His clear Book, which He made the judge between people concerning the truth over which they differed, Allah has distinguished truth from falsehood, guidance from misguidance, and believers from disbelievers. He, Most High, said:

“Do those who commit evil deeds think that We will make them like those who believe and do righteous deeds, equal in their life and death? How evil is their judgment!”

And He said:

“Or shall We treat those who believe and do righteous deeds like those who spread corruption on earth? Or shall We treat the God-fearing like the wicked?”

And He said:

“Shall We then treat those who submit to Allah like the criminals?”

“What is the matter with you? How do you judge?”

He has also explained the condition of those liars and wicked people who imitate the prophets and the people of knowledge and faith—those who are themselves deceived and deceive others. He has informed us that they receive visitations and inspiration, but from devils. He said:

“Indeed, the devils inspire their allies to argue with you. And if you obey them,

---

## Page 518

you will surely be associating partners with Allah.” And He, Most High, said:

“Shall I inform you upon whom the devils descend? They descend upon every sinful liar.”

He has also declared that whenever anyone turns away from Allah’s religion, Allah will assuredly replace him with someone who upholds His clear religion. He said:

“O you who believe, whoever among you turns away from his religion—Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him, humble toward the believers and firm toward the disbelievers, striving in Allah’s cause and fearing no blame from anyone. That is Allah’s bounty, which He grants to whom He wills. And Allah is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing.”

For the doctrine of these heretics, expressed in their discourse and the poetry they compose, consists of fabricated speech and contrived verse.

Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, may Allah be pleased with him, referred to both when, during one of their exchanges, Umar ibn al-Khattāb said to him, “O successor to Allah’s Messenger, win the people over.” He then took hold of his beard and said, “O son of al-Khattāb! A tyrant in the days of ignorance, yet a weakling in Islam? On what basis should I win them over? With fabricated speech? Or contrived verse?”

He meant: “I am not calling them to fabricated speech like the Quran of Musaylima, nor to contrived verse like the poetry of Tulayha al-Asadī.”

These are the two forms with which people of wickedness and blatant falsehood challenge the Quran. He, Most High, said:

“So I swear by what you see and what you do not see: it is indeed the word of a noble messenger. It is not the word of a poet—how little you believe! Nor is it the word of a soothsayer—how little you reflect!”

---

## Page 519

a revelation from the Lord of all worlds.”

He, Most High, also said:

“Indeed, it is a revelation from the Lord of all worlds, brought down by the trustworthy Spirit,”

up to His words:

“The devils did not bring it down,”

and onward to the end of the surah. In this surah, He described the distinguishing mark of lying soothsayers and misguided poets, and ruled out any association with either group, as in Sūrat al-Hāqqah.

He, Most High, also said:

“It is indeed the word of a noble messenger, endowed with power and held in high honour by the Lord of the Throne,”

and onward to the end of the surah. The messenger here is Gabriel, whereas in the first passage it is Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Thus, in the former passage He declared Muhammad to be neither a poet nor a soothsayer, while here He declared that the messenger sent to him was not one of the devils.

---

## Page 520

Section:

Know—may Allah guide you and direct you aright—that a proper understanding of these people’s doctrine reveals its falsity without requiring any further proof. Confusion arises only because most people do not understand the true nature of what they assert and intend, owing to their use of vague expressions and terms with multiple meanings. Indeed, they themselves do not understand the true nature of what they intend and say. This is why they frequently contradict themselves: they merely adopt a notion and profess it or follow it.

For this reason, they have split into factions, yet cannot distinguish between their factions despite being aware of their divisions. Thus, when I explained to groups of their followers and leaders the true nature of their assertions and the underlying secret of their doctrine, they came to hold that explanation in great esteem. Had I not accompanied it with condemnation and refutation, they would have made me one of their leading authorities and offered me obedience in person and wealth beyond description—just as Christians offer it to their leaders, the Ismāʿīlīs to their notables, and Pharaoh’s people offered it to Pharaoh.

Anyone who accepts these people’s assertions is one of two kinds: either someone ignorant of their true nature, or a wrongdoer seeking supremacy and corruption on earth; or someone who combines both qualities. Such is the condition of

---

## Page 521

Pharaoh’s followers, of whom Allah said:

“So he made fools of his people, and they obeyed him.”

Such too is the condition of the Qarmatians in relation to their leaders, and of the disbelievers and hypocrites in relation to their leaders, who call to the Fire and will not be helped on the Day of Resurrection.

“Indeed, Allah has cursed the disbelievers and prepared for them a blazing Fire,” up to His words: “And curse them with a great curse.”

And He, Exalted is He, said:

“And among people are those who take rivals besides Allah,” up to His words: “And they will never leave the Fire.”

---

## Page 522

Section

The substance of these people’s doctrine

The existence of created things is identical to the existence of Allah, Exalted is He. Their existence is not distinct from His, and there is absolutely nothing besides Him. Thus, they regard anyone who calls them proponents of indwelling, or says that they profess indwelling, as veiled from understanding their doctrine and as having failed to penetrate the inner reality of their position. For anyone who says that Allah indwells created beings thereby maintains that what is indwelt is distinct from what indwells it. In their view, this constitutes duality and affirms two existences: first, the existence of the Real who indwells; and second, the existence of the created being in which He indwells. They do not acknowledge two existences at all.

There is no doubt that this doctrine involves less unbelief than theirs. It is the doctrine of many of the Jahmiyyah whose teachings the early generations refuted: those who claim that Allah, in His essence, is in every place. Numerous imams and members of the early generations reported this doctrine from the Jahmiyyah and declared them disbelievers on its account. Indeed, many imams—including Ibn al-Mubārak, Yūsuf ibn Asbāt, and a group of scholars and hadith scholars among the followers of Ahmad and others—regarded them, on this account, as falling outside the seventy-two sects. This is the doctrine of some Jahmī theologians and many of those among them devoted to worship.

There is no doubt that the deviation, Jahmism, and heresy of these later figures are an elaboration and completion of the deviation, Jahmism, and heresy of those earlier Jahmiyyah.

---

## Page 523

As for why they are called proponents of union, there are two explanations. They reject the first, because ittihād (union) follows the same morphological pattern as iqtirān (conjunction), and conjunction presupposes two things, one united with the other, whereas they never acknowledge two existences. The second explanation justifies this designation on the grounds that multiplicity has become unity, as I shall explain when discussing their inconsistencies.

This explanation can follow Ibn ʿArabī’s doctrine: he distinguishes existence from fixed reality and says that the existence of the Real governs the fixed reality of contingent things. Thus, union between existence and fixed reality can properly be spoken of. Alternatively, according to those who make no such distinction, imagined multiplicity has become unity after spiritual unveiling, or concrete multiplicity has become unqualified unity.

---

## Page 524

Section

Their foundational premise is that the existence of created beings and things—even the existence of jinn, devils, unbelievers, the wicked, dogs, pigs, impurities, unbelief, wickedness, and disobedience—is identical to the existence of the Lord, rather than distinct from Him and separate from His Essence, even if created by Him, subject to His lordship, fashioned by Him, and subsisting through Him.

Yet they perceive differentiation and multiplicity among existing things, evident to both the senses and reason. They therefore needed a unification that would eliminate multiplicity and a unity that would remove differentiation, despite the reality of that multiplicity. In their confusion, they arrived at three doctrines. I shall explain these to you, even though some of them do not clearly explain either their own doctrine or that of others, because their apprehension and conception of the truth are incomplete.

---

## Page 525

The First Doctrine:
The doctrine of Ibn ʿArabī, author of Fusūs al-Hukm.

Although this doctrine constitutes unbelief, he is the closest of them to Islam, because his writings contain much that is sound, and because he does not adhere as firmly as the others to the doctrine of identity between Creator and creation. Rather, he wavers considerably in it. He merely follows his expansive imagination, which sometimes pictures truth and sometimes falsehood. Allah knows best what belief he held when he died.

His doctrine rests on two principles:

The first: that a nonexistent thing has an established reality in nonexistence, in agreement with those among the Muʿtazilah and the Rāfidah who maintained this view.

The first to introduce this doctrine into Islam was Abū ʿUthmān al-Shahhām, the teacher of Abū ʿAlī al-Jubbā’ī. Groups of innovating Qadariyyah among the Muʿtazilah and the Rāfidah followed him in this. They maintain that every nonexistent thing whose existence is possible has its reality, essence, and individual identity established in nonexistence. For, were these not established, there would be no distinction between what is known and spoken of and what is not known and spoken of. Nor would it be possible to intend to bring something into existence, since intention requires distinction, and distinction is possible only with respect to something established.

Yet these people, although they introduced this doctrine—which is false in itself—and have been declared unbelievers

---

## Page 526

for this doctrine by groups of Sunni theologians—they nevertheless acknowledge that Allah created these things’ existence, and do not say that their very existence is the very existence of the Real.

The author of al-Fusūs and his followers, however, say:

“Their very existence is the very existence of the Real. Thus, they are distinct through their essences, which are established in nonexistence, but united through the existence of the Real that subsists in them.”

Most of his discourse rests on this, as anyone who carefully considers and understands it will recognize. If Ibn ʿArabī holds individual identities to be established, it follows that every possible thing must exist. This is not the position of the Muʿtazilah, so this is a third distinction.

Those who maintain that a nonexistent thing has an established reality in nonexistence—whether they say that these things’ existence is created by Allah or that it is Allah—say that essences and individual identities are neither made nor created, and that the existence of each thing is something additional to its essence. They may also say that existence is an attribute of the existent thing.

Although this position resembles that of those who maintain the eternity of the world, or the eternity of the world’s matter and its material substrate as distinct from its form, it is not identical to it, even though the two share something in common. For these newly originated forms—animals, plants, and minerals—are not eternal, as all rational people agree; rather, they have come into existence after not existing. Likewise, the attributes and accidents that subsist in the heavenly bodies, and the transformations that occur in the elements, including the movements of the planets, the sun, the moon, and the clouds,

---

## Page 527

and the rain, thunder, lightning, and other such things—all of these are newly originated, not eternal, as anyone whose senses are sound recognizes, for he sees this with his own eyes. Those who say that the individual identity of a nonexistent thing is eternally established, or that its matter is eternal, say that the individual identities of all these things are eternally established, and that the matter of the entire world is eternal, though its forms are not.

Know that when a doctrine is intrinsically false, its critic cannot present it in a way that allows it to be truly conceived; this is possible only for the truth. As for a false position, once it is explained, the explanation itself reveals its unsoundness, until one asks, “How could anyone have been confused about this?” and marvels that they could have believed it. Yet one should not be astonished: there is no conceivable form of falsehood that some group of people has not embraced. This is why Allah describes the adherents of falsehood as dead; as “deaf, dumb, and blind”; as those who “do not understand” and “do not reason”; as being “indeed of conflicting opinions”—“Whoever is turned away is turned away from it”; as those who “waver in their doubt”; and as those who “wander blindly.”

Their confusion arose—and Allah knows best—from their recognition that Allah—glorified is He—knows what does not yet exist before it comes into being—or that:

“His command, when He wills a thing, is only to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

They saw that the nonexistent thing He creates is distinguished in His knowledge, will, and power, and supposed that this was because it possessed a distinct, established essence. But that is not the case. Rather, it is distinguished in Allah’s knowledge and His Book. Any one of us has knowledge of what exists and of what does not exist

---

## Page 528

that is possible, and of what does not exist and is impossible. He knows what existed in the past, such as Adam and the prophets, and what will occur, such as the Resurrection and the Reckoning. He also knows how something that did not occur would have been had it occurred, just as he knows what Allah has said concerning the people of the Fire:

“If they were sent back, they would return to what they were forbidden to do.”

And that:

“Had Allah known any good in them, He would have made them hear.”

And that:

“Had there been gods in the heavens and the earth besides Allah, both would have been ruined.”

And that:

“Had there been other gods with Him, as they say, they would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne.”

And that:

“Had they gone forth among you, they would only have increased you in confusion.”

And that:

“Were it not for Allah’s favour and mercy upon you, not one of you would ever have become pure.”

There are other such conditional statements in which the condition is known either not to hold or to hold.

These things that we know and conceive—whether we deny their existence in external reality, affirm it, or remain uncertain—do not acquire an established individual reality outside our knowledge and minds merely because we conceive them. Thus, we can conceive a mountain of ruby, a sea of mercury, a man of gold, or a horse of stone. A thing’s being established in knowledge and determination is not the same as its individual reality being established externally. Rather, someone may know a thing, speak of it, and write it down, although its essence has no established reality or existence whatsoever outside the mind.

Such is the nature of Allah’s decree preceding His creation, as reported in Sahīh Muslim from ‘Abd Allah ibn ‘Amr, from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who said:

“Allah wrote the decrees of all creatures fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth.”

And in Sunan Abī Dāwūd, it is reported from ‘Ubādah ibn al-Sāmit, from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who said:

“Allah first created the Pen and said, ‘Write.’ It said, ‘My Lord, what shall I write?’ He said, ‘Write

---

## Page 529

what will occur until the Day of Resurrection.’”

Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“Allah created creation and knew what they would do. Then He said to His knowledge, ‘Become a book,’ and it became a book. He then revealed confirmation of this in His Book, saying: ‘Do you not know that Allah knows what is in heaven and earth? Indeed, that is in a Book.’”

This is the meaning of the hadith narrated by Ahmad in his Musnad from Maysarah al-Fajr, who said:

“I asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, when were you a prophet?’”

In another narration: “When were you recorded as a prophet?” He replied: “When Adam was between spirit and body.” This is the wording of the authentic hadith.

As for what these ignorant people narrate—such as Ibn ‘Arabī in al-Fusūs and other ignorant laypeople—“I was a prophet when Adam was between water and clay,” and “I was a prophet when Adam was neither water nor clay,” this has no basis. None of the truthful scholars narrated it, nor does it appear with this wording in any authoritative scholarly book. Rather, it is false, for Adam was never between water and clay. Allah created him from dust, mixed the dust with water until it became clay, and dried the clay until it became sounding clay like pottery. Thus, he was never in a state between water and clay, composed of water and clay. Had it been said, “between water and dust,” that would have been less impossible, although there would still have been nothing distinctive about that state.

Rather, he said, “between spirit and body,” and, “while Adam was still lying in his clay form,” because Adam’s body remained for forty years before the spirit was breathed into it. As Allah Most High says:

“Has there come upon man a period of time”—the verse.

And Allah Most High says:

“And when your Lord said to the angels, ‘I am creating a human being from sounding clay’”—the two verses.

And Allah Most High says:

“He who perfected everything He created and began the creation of man from clay”—the two verses.

And Allah Most High says:

“When your Lord said

---

## Page 530

to the angels, ‘Indeed, I am creating a human being from clay’”—the verse. The hadiths concerning Adam’s creation and the breathing of the spirit into him are well known in the books of hadith, Quranic commentary, and other works. Thus, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stated “that he was a prophet—that is, he was recorded as a prophet—while Adam was between spirit and body.”

This—and Allah knows best—is because it is at this stage that the decree entrusted to the angels responsible for creation is determined. It is determined for them and made known to them, and what will happen to the created being is recorded before the spirit is breathed into it. This is illustrated by the hadith of the truthful one whose truthfulness was confirmed, recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim in their two Sahīh collections and in the other principal hadith collections. It is among the widely transmitted hadiths that the scholars have accepted and unanimously affirmed as true. It is the hadith narrated by al-Aʿmash, from Zayd ibn Wahb, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the truthful one whose truthfulness was confirmed—told us: ‘Each of you is brought together in his mother’s womb as a drop of fluid for forty days, then becomes a clot for a similar period, then a lump of flesh for a similar period. Allah then sends the angel, who is commanded to record four matters. He is told: “Write down his provision, his lifespan, his deeds, and whether he will be wretched or blessed.” Then the spirit is breathed into him.’”

He also said:

“By Him in whose hand is my soul, one of you may perform the deeds of the people of Paradise until only a cubit separates him from it. Then what has been written overtakes him, and he performs the deeds of the people of the Fire and enters the Fire. And one of you may perform the deeds of the people of the Fire until only a cubit separates him from it. Then what has been written overtakes him, and he performs the deeds of the people of Paradise and enters Paradise.”

Since the truthful one whose truthfulness was confirmed stated that the angel records a person’s provision, deeds, lifespan, and whether he will be wretched or blessed after the body has been created but before the spirit is breathed into it, and since Adam is the father of mankind, it was likewise fitting that, after his body had been created and before the spirit was breathed into him, there should be recorded what would come to be

---

## Page 531

from him. Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the master of Adam’s descendants: he is the greatest among them in rank and the most exalted in renown. He therefore stated that he was recorded as a prophet at that time. The recording of his prophethood is what is meant by its existence: it existed in the written decree, not in actual reality. His prophethood did not come into existence until Allah Most High made him a prophet at the age of forty, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as the Most High said to him:

“And thus We have revealed to you a spirit by Our command”—the verse.

He also said:

“Did He not find you an orphan and give you refuge?”—the verse.

And He said:

“We relate to you the best of stories”—the verse.

This meaning is therefore explicitly explained in the hadith of al-ʿIrbād ibn Sāriya, who reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I am the servant of Allah, recorded as the Seal of the Prophets while Adam still lay in his clay form. I shall tell you how my mission began: Abraham’s supplication, Jesus’ glad tidings, and the vision my mother saw when she gave birth to me, when a light shone forth for her, illuminating for her the palaces of the Levant.”

This is the wording of the hadith in Ibn Wahb’s narration: Muʿāwiya ibn Sālih narrated to us, from Saʿīd ibn Suwayd, from ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Hilāl al-Sulamī, from al-ʿIrbād. Al-Baghawī related it in this form in Sharh al-Sunnah (Commentary on the Sunnah). Al-Layth ibn Saʿd also narrated a similar version from him. Imam Ahmad related it in his Musnad from Ibn Mahdī: Muʿāwiya ibn Sālih narrated to us, through this chain of transmission, from al-ʿIrbād, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I am the servant of Allah, the Seal of the Prophets, while Adam still lay in his clay form. I shall tell you how this began: the supplication of my father Abraham, the glad tidings of Jesus, and the vision my mother saw; the mothers of the prophets likewise see such visions.”

And his words

---

## Page 532

“still lay in his clay form” mean that he was curled up and lying upon the ground as a form made of clay, into which the soul had not yet entered. It has also been narrated that “Allah inscribed his name upon the Throne and upon the gates, domes, and leaves in Paradise.” Several reports have been narrated concerning this, in agreement with these established hadiths, which explain how his name was honoured and his renown exalted at that time.

The wording of the hadith in the Musnad, reported from Maysara al-Fajr, has already been cited: “He was asked, ‘When were you a prophet?’ He replied, ‘While Adam was between soul and body.’” Abū al-Husayn ibn Bishrān also narrated it through Shaykh Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī in al-Wafā bi-Fadā’il al-Mustafā (The Comprehensive Account of the Virtues of the Chosen One), may Allah bless him and grant him peace: Abū Jaʿfar Muhammad ibn ʿAmr narrated to us; Ahmad ibn Ishāq ibn Sālih narrated to us; Muhammad ibn Sālih narrated to us; Muhammad ibn Sinān al-ʿAwfī narrated to us; Ibrāhīm ibn Tahmān narrated to us, from Yazīd ibn Maysara, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sufyān, from Maysara, who said:

“I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, when were you a prophet?’ He replied, ‘When Allah created the earth, turned towards heaven and fashioned them into seven heavens, and created the Throne, He inscribed upon the leg of the Throne: Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, the Seal of the Prophets. Allah created the Garden in which He settled Adam and Eve, and inscribed my name upon its gates, leaves, domes, and tents, while Adam was between soul and body. When Allah Most High brought him to life, he looked at the Throne and saw my name. Allah informed him: “He is the master of your descendants.” When Satan deceived them both, they repented and sought intercession with Him through my name.’”

Abū Nuʿaym, the hadith master, narrated in his book Dalā’il al-Nubuwwa (Proofs of Prophethood), and through Shaykh Abū al-Faraj: Sulaymān ibn Ahmad narrated to us; Ahmad ibn Rushdīn narrated to us; Ahmad ibn Saʿīd al-Fihrī narrated to us:

---

## Page 533

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ismāʿīl al-Madanī narrated to us, from ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam, from his father, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When Adam committed the sin, he raised his head and said, ‘O my Lord, by the right of Muhammad, forgive me!’ Allah revealed to him, ‘What is Muhammad, and who is Muhammad?’ He replied, ‘O my Lord, when You completed my creation, I raised my head towards Your Throne and saw written upon it: There is no god but Allah; Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah. I therefore knew that he was the most honoured of Your creatures in Your sight, since You joined his name with Yours.’ He said, ‘Yes, I have forgiven you. He is the last of the prophets among your descendants, and were it not for him, I would not have created you.’”

This hadith supports the preceding one, and the two serve as an explanation of the authentic hadiths.

In the two Sahīh collections, ʿĀ’isha said:

“The revelation first came to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through true dreams. Every dream he saw came true as clearly as the break of dawn. Then solitude was made dear to him. He would go to the cave of Hirā’ and devote himself to worship there for several nights before turning back to his family, taking provisions for those stays. He would then return to Khadīja and replenish his provisions for another such stay, until the truth came upon him suddenly while he was in Hirā’. The angel came to him and said, ‘Read.’ He replied, ‘I cannot read.’

“He said, ‘He took hold of me and squeezed me until I could bear no more, then released me and said, “Read.” I replied, “I cannot read.” He took hold of me and squeezed me until I could bear no more, then released me and said, “Read.” I replied, “I cannot read.” Then he took hold of me and squeezed me until I could bear no more, then released me and said, “Read in the name of your Lord who created; created man from a clinging clot.”’

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, returned with these words, the flesh between his neck and shoulders trembling.” The hadith continues at length.

In this authentic hadith, he thus stated that he could not read. This surah was the first revelation Allah sent down to him, and through it he became a prophet. Then He revealed to him Sūrat al-Muddaththir (The Cloaked One), and through it he became

---

## Page 534

a messenger, as He says:

“Arise and warn.”

For this reason, He, glorified be He, mentioned in this surah both actual existence and existence in knowledge. This is a plain matter that a person grasps in his heart without needing to be told, for a thing does not exist before it comes into existence.

As for things being known to Allah before they come into existence, this is undoubtedly true. Likewise, they are recorded with Him or with His angels, as the Quran and Sunnah indicate and the transmitted reports attest.

This knowledge and recording constitute the divine decree denied by the extremists among the Qadariyya. They claim that Allah does not know His servants’ actions until those actions occur. They are disbelievers; the imams, including al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others, declared them to be disbelievers.

The Quran and Sunnah have explained this divine decree, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, answered the question raised concerning it: whether one should abandon action on account of it. His answer is related in the two Sahīh collections, where ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib said:

“We were attending a funeral in Baqī‘ al-Gharqad when the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, came to us. He sat down, and we sat around him. He had a stick with him. He lowered his head and began tapping the ground with his stick. Then he said, ‘There is none among you—or he said, no soul born—whose place in Paradise or Hell Allah has not already recorded, and who has not already been recorded as wretched or blessed.’

“A man then said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, should we not rely on what has been recorded for us and abandon action? For whoever is among the blessed will come to perform the deeds of the blessed, and whoever is among the wretched will come to perform the deeds of the wretched.’

“He replied, ‘Act, for everyone will be enabled. As for the blessed

---

## Page 535

, they will be enabled to perform the deeds of the blessed, while the wretched will be enabled to perform the deeds of the wretched.’ Then he recited, ‘As for he who gives and fears Allah,’ through to the end of the verses.”

In another narration:

“One day, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sitting with a stick in his hand, tapping the ground with it. He raised his head and said, ‘There is no soul among you whose place in Paradise or Hell is not already known.’

“They said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, what, then, is the purpose of action? Should we not simply rely on this?’

“He replied, ‘No. Act, for everyone will be enabled to do that for which he was created.’ Then he recited, ‘As for he who gives,’”—the verse.

Also in the two Sahīh collections, ‘Imrān ibn Husayn said:

“It was asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, are the people of Paradise already known and distinguished from the people of Hell?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’ Then it was asked, ‘Why, then, should people act?’ He replied, ‘Everyone will be enabled to do that for which he was created.’”

In another narration:

“Two men from Muzayna came to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, tell us about what people do today and strive to accomplish: is it something already decreed for them and settled by a prior divine decree, or something they face afresh in response to what their Prophet has brought them, with the proof established against them?’

“He replied, ‘No. Rather, it is something already decreed for them and settled. This is confirmed in the Book of Allah: “And by the soul and He who proportioned it, and inspired it with its wickedness and its righteousness.”’”

In Sahīh Muslim, Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh said:

“Surāqa ibn Mālik ibn Ju‘shum came and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, explain our religion to us as though we had only just been created. How does our action today stand: does it concern what the pens have already dried upon and the decrees have already determined, or what remains to be determined?’

“He replied, ‘No. Rather, it concerns what the pens have already dried upon and the decrees have already determined.’

“He asked, ‘What, then, is the purpose of action?’

“He replied, ‘Act, for everyone will be enabled.’”

---

## Page 536

Also in Sahīh Muslim, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr said:

“I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say, ‘Allah wrote the decrees of creation fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth.’ He added, ‘And His Throne was over the water.’”

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, ‘Ubāda ibn al-Sāmit said to his son:

“My son, you will not taste the reality of faith until you know that what befell you could not have missed you, and what missed you could not have befallen you. I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say:

‘When Allah first created the Pen, He said to it, “Write.” It asked, “My Lord, what shall I write?” He replied, “Write the decrees of everything until the Hour comes.”’

“My son, I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say, ‘Whoever dies believing otherwise is not of me.’”

Al-Tirmidhī related it through another chain from al-Walīd ibn ‘Ubāda, who said:

“He—meaning his father—summoned me as he was dying and said, ‘My son, fear Allah, and know that if you fear Allah, you will believe in Allah and in the divine decree in its entirety, both its good and its evil. If you die believing otherwise, you will enter the Fire. I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say:

“When Allah first created the Pen, He said, ‘Write.’ It asked, ‘What shall I write?’ He replied, ‘Write the decree: all that has been and all that will be, forever.’”’”

Al-Tirmidhī also related from Abū Khuzāma (1), from his father:

“A man came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and asked, ‘What do you say about healing invocations we ask to have recited, medicines with which we treat ourselves, and protective measures we take? Do they avert anything of the decree of Allah Most High?’ He replied, ‘They are part of Allah’s decree.’”

Yet what is established in predetermination is only that which does not yet exist, is possible, and will come into being. As for that which does not exist

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The printed edition reads “Abī Harātha”; the correct reading is the one we have adopted from al-Tirmidhī and Ibn Mājah.

---

## Page 537

but is possible and will not come into being, examples include sending believers into the Fire, bringing about the Resurrection before its appointed time, turning mountains into rubies, and similar things. Such a nonexistent thing is possible and, according to those who say that the nonexistent is a thing, it subsists in nonexistence. Nevertheless, its coming into being has not been decreed. Allah knows it as it is: He knows that it is possible and that it will not come into being.

The same applies to impossibilities, such as the Creator having a partner or a child. Allah knows that:

“He neither begets nor is born,”

“And there is none comparable to Him.”

He knows that He has no partner in His dominion and no protector on account of weakness. He knows that He is the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all:

“Neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes Him.”

And He knows that:

“Not an atom’s weight in the heavens or on earth escapes Him.”

These impossible, nonexistent things are not things at all, as all rational people agree, although they are established in knowledge. It is thus clear that knowledge encompasses what does not exist and what cannot possibly exist, for knowledge is vast. If someone uses language more broadly and says that the nonexistent is a thing in knowledge, exists in knowledge, or is established in knowledge, this is correct. But to say that it is a thing in itself is false. This resolves the confusion surrounding this question.

The position of the people of the Sunnah and the Community, and of rational people generally among all categories of humanity, is that the nonexistent is not a thing in itself, and that its subsistence, existence, and actuality are one and the same. This is established by the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations. Allah Most High said to Zechariah:

“I created you before, when you were nothing.”

He thus stated that Zechariah had been nothing. Allah Most High also said:

“Does man not remember that We created him before, when he was nothing?”

And Allah Most High said:

“Were they created by nothing, or were they themselves the creators?”

---

## Page 538

Thus He rejected their belief that they had been created without anything that created them, or that they had created themselves. This is why Jubayr ibn Mut‘im said:

“When I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recite this surah, I felt my heart split open.”

If the nonexistent were a thing, this rejection would not be conclusive, since one could say that they were indeed created by something, but that this thing was nonexistent. Their creator would thus be a nonexistent thing.

Allah Most High said:

“Such people will enter Paradise and will not be wronged in the least.”

If the nonexistent were a thing, the meaning would be: they will not be wronged in respect of anything existent or nonexistent. Yet they cannot conceivably be deprived unjustly of something nonexistent, since it is not theirs.

As for His statement:

“Indeed, the shaking of the Hour is a tremendous thing,”

this describes the shaking when it occurs as a tremendous thing; it does not describe the shaking in its present state. This is why He said:

“On the Day you see it, every nursing mother will forget the child she is nursing.”

If the Hour were intended by it, the meaning would be that it is a tremendous thing in knowledge and decree.

His statement, Most High:

“Our word to a thing, when We intend it, is only to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is,”

has been cited as evidence by those who say that the nonexistent is a thing, whereas it is evidence against them. For He states that He wills the thing and brings it into being. According to them, however, it subsists in nonexistence, and only its existence is willed, not the thing itself. Yet the Quran states that the thing itself is willed and comes into being. This is one of the subsidiary questions arising from this issue.

The position of the people of the Sunnah and the Community, and of rational people generally, is that essences are brought into being; that the essence of every thing is identical with its existence; and that a thing’s existence is not something additional to its essence. Rather, in external reality there is only the thing itself: its very self, its essence, and its reality. Its existence and subsistence in external reality are not additional to that.

---

## Page 539

Those people, however, say that existence is something additional to essence, that essences are not brought into being, and that the existence of every thing is additional to its essence. Some adherents of philosophy draw distinctions concerning existence, the necessary, and the contingent, saying:

“Necessary existence is identical with essence, whereas contingent existence is additional to essence.”

Their specious argument is the one mentioned earlier: a person may know a thing’s essence without knowing its existence, and existence is common to existing things, whereas each thing’s essence is peculiar to it. Whoever reflects will discern the truth of the matter, for we have explained the distinction between existence in knowledge and actual existence.

This distinction applies to existence, the thing itself, subsistence, essence, and other such matters. Their subsistence in knowledge, writing, and speech is not their subsistence in external reality, which is the subsistence of their very reality and essence. When a person conceives an essence, he knows its mental existence, but this does not entail its real, external existence.

Thus, when someone says, “I have conceived the thing’s reality, its very being, its self, and its essence, but I do not know its existence,” or, “Its existence in knowledge has been attained, but not its actual, real existence,” its real essence, its real being, and its real self in external reality remain unknown. There is therefore no difference between the expression “its existence” and the expression “its essence,” except that one expression may refer to what is in the mind and the other to what is in external reality. The distinction thus arises from where they subsist, not from essence and existence themselves.

As for their assertion that existence is shared, whereas reality is not, the same answer applies. The particular existence found in external reality is not shared, just as the particular reality found in external reality is not shared. Rather, knowledge apprehends the common existent

---

## Page 540

just as it apprehends the common essence. What is common subsists in the mind, not in external reality; nothing in external reality is shared at all. If the mind apprehends a particular essence existing in external reality, that essence is not shared. Commonality belongs only to the unrestricted, general concepts that the mind apprehends. Nothing in external reality is unrestricted and general as such. Rather, what exists there is what is unrestricted without unrestrictedness being stipulated as a condition, and it exists there only as something particular.

A rational person should therefore distinguish between a thing’s subsistence and existence in itself and its subsistence and existence in knowledge. The former is actual, external, real existence; the latter is called mental or cognitive existence. Everything has these two modes of subsistence. Knowledge is expressed in words, and words are recorded in writing. Thus, every thing has four levels of existence:

Existence in external reality, existence in minds, existence on the tongue, and existence through the fingers: actual, cognitive, verbal, and written existence.

For this reason, the first revelation Allah sent down to His Prophet was the surah beginning:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created.”

In it, He mentioned both kinds of existence, saying:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created.”

“Created man from a clinging clot.”

He thus mentioned all created things in their actual existence, first generally and then specifically, singling out man as created after speaking generally of the rest. Then He said:

“Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous.”

“Who taught by the pen.”

“Taught man what he did not know.”

He thus singled out man as being taught after speaking generally of teaching by the pen. He mentioned the pen because teaching by the pen is teaching writing, which necessarily entails teaching verbal expression, since writing corresponds to it. Teaching verbal expression is imparting the ability to communicate, which necessarily entails imparting knowledge, since the wording corresponds to the meaning.

---

## Page 541

Thus, His teaching by the pen necessarily entails the three levels: verbal, cognitive, and written. By contrast, had He spoken of teaching without qualification, or mentioned only the imparting of knowledge, this would not have encompassed all the levels. In this surah, He therefore mentioned actual and cognitive existence, and that Allah, glorified be He, is the giver of both. He is the Creator of creation and the Creator of man, and He is the One who teaches by the pen and who teaches man.

As for affirming a thing’s existence in external reality before it exists, this is known to be false on the grounds of both reason and revelation, and it contradicts the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus.

---

## Page 542

Section

This, then, is one of Ibn Arabī’s two fundamental principles. The other is their claim that the existence of entities is identical to the very existence of the Real. In this they stand apart from all those who affirm the Maker among Muslims, Jews, Christians, Magians, and polytheists. This is, in fact, the substance of the position of Pharaoh and the Qarmatians who deny the existence of the Maker, as we shall explain, Allah willing.

Whoever understands this understands all Ibn Arabī’s statements, in verse and prose, including his claim that the Real is nourished by creation, because the existence of entities is nourished by the entities fixed in nonexistence. Hence he affirms unity with respect to existence, but distinction with respect to quiddities and entities. He alleges that this is the secret of divine decree, because quiddities accept only what is intrinsically established for them in nonexistence. Thus, it is they themselves that have done good and evil, praised and blamed; the Real has given them nothing beyond what they already possessed in their state of nonexistence.

Consider, then, how his statements combine two things: denial of the existence of the Real, and denial that He created His creatures. He thus denies the Lord who created, acknowledging neither a Lord nor creation. He denies the Lord of the worlds: there is neither a Lord nor worlds subject to His lordship, since there are only fixed entities and an existence subsisting in them. The entities are not subject to a Lord, nor is existence; the entities are not created, nor is existence.

He distinguishes between the loci of manifestation and that which is manifest, and between that which makes manifest and that which manifests itself. For, according to him, the loci of manifestation are the entities fixed in nonexistence, whereas that which is manifest is the existence of creation.

---

## Page 543

Section

As for his associate, al-Sadr al-Fakhr al-Rūmī, he does not maintain that existence is something additional to quiddity. He was more deeply versed in speculative reasoning and theology than his master, but greater in unbelief, poorer in knowledge and faith, and less familiar with Islam and the sayings of the shaykhs. Since their doctrine was unbelief, the more adept anyone became in it, the greater his unbelief.

When he saw that distinguishing the existence of things from their entities was untenable—while holding that Allah is existence and that some distinction between the two was necessary—he distinguished between the absolute and the determinate. In his view, Allah is absolute existence, which is neither determinate nor differentiated. When it becomes determinate and differentiated, it is creation, whether it becomes determinate at the level of divinity or elsewhere.

This position expresses unbelief more explicitly than the first and is the very substance of the doctrine of Pharaoh and the Qarmatians, although the first is more flawed in its distinction between the existence of things and their fixed reality. For, on the first position, it is possible to posit that the Real has an existence apart from the entities of contingent beings, and that this existence emanated upon them. This would entail an acknowledgment of the self-subsisting Lord, who has no need of His creation, even though the position contains unbelief insofar as it identifies the creature with the Creator and that which is subject to lordship with the Lord. Indeed, it does not affirm any creation at all. Even so, I have not seen him explicitly affirm the existence of the Lord as distinct from the existence subsisting in the entities of contingent beings.

---

## Page 544

As for this man, however, he explicitly states that there is nothing but absolute existence permeating determinate existents. Yet what is absolute does not exist in an absolute form: in external reality there is no absolute body under the condition of absoluteness, no absolute human being, and no absolute animal under the condition of absoluteness. Rather, it exists only in something determinate.

Realities may be considered in three respects: generality, particularity, and absoluteness. When we speak of an animal, human being, body, or existence in a general sense, this occurs only in knowledge and language. Outside these, nothing exists in external reality that encompasses two things. Thus, generality is a property that can attach to the attributes of a living being. We therefore speak of knowledge, will, or anger that is general in scope, and of a general statement or command.

The possessor of an attribute may also be described as acting generally, as in the hadith in Sunan Abī Dāwūd:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, passed by Ali while he was supplicating and said: ‘Ali, include others, for the superiority of a general supplication over a particular one is like the superiority of heaven over earth.’”

Another hadith relates:

“When His words, ‘And warn your closest kindred,’ were revealed, he addressed people both generally and specifically.”

Muslim narrated this through Mūsā ibn Talha from Abū Hurayra.

An attribute may likewise be described as general in scope, as in the hadith concerning the tashahhud (testimony of faith in prayer):

“Peace be upon us and upon Allah’s righteous servants. When you say this, it reaches every righteous servant of Allah in heaven and on earth.”

As for the unqualified assertion that generality is a property of words alone, this is incorrect. The meanings of words that subsist in the heart are more properly described as general than the words themselves; and the other attributes

---

## Page 545

such as will, love, hatred, anger, and pleasure—can be general or particular just as speech can. It is only in the case of realities outside the mind, existing in external reality—as when people speak of “widespread rain” and “widespread abundance”—that people have disputed whether describing them as general is literal or figurative. There are two views:

- (One view is that it is figurative, because each portion of rain or abundance occurs only where the other occurs, so there is no generality here. The other view is that it is literal, because rain, considered absolutely, has extended over the whole area.

As for particularity, it applies to realities when they exist externally, since everything has an essence and an individual identity peculiar to it, distinguishing it from anything else. I mean its concrete, individual reality, in which nothing else shares—for example, this man, this grain, and this dirham. Whatever applies to these realities externally also applies to them in the mind. Mental conception has a broader scope than external realities, encompassing what exists, what does not exist, what is impossible, and what is hypothetical.

As for absoluteness, it undoubtedly applies to realities when they are in the mind, for the intellect conceives an absolute human being and absolute existence. But can anything absolute be conceived as existing externally? There are two views on this. One holds that the absolute exists externally because it forms part of the determinate. The other holds that it has no external existence, since only determinate, qualified things exist externally, and the absolute that several things share cannot form part of a determinate thing that is not itself shared.

The precise account is that the absolute, without any condition whatsoever, includes the qualified, determinate thing. As for the absolute

---

## Page 546

subject to the condition of absoluteness, it does not include the determinate, qualified thing. This is analogous to the jurists’ expression “unqualified water”: it is subject to the condition of being unqualified, and therefore does not include qualified water. By contrast, the absolute without any condition does include qualified water.

Thus, when we say that water falls into three categories—pure and purifying, pure, and impure—all three are categories of water. Pure and purifying water is unqualified water, which excludes anything that is not purifying, such as juices and impure water. Water as the category being divided is therefore the absolute without any condition, whereas water constituting one category alongside the other two is the absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness.

However, the absoluteness and qualification that the jurists discuss concerning the designation “water” concern unqualified and qualified wording: what falls within an unqualified expression such as “water,” or within a qualified expression such as “impure water” or “rose water.” Our initial discussion, by contrast, concerned absoluteness and qualification in the meanings of expressions. These are two distinct matters, and people make very many errors through failing to distinguish between them.

Every designation has a referent that is either determinate and cannot be shared by several things—as with “I,” “this,” and “Zayd,” which is called “the determinate” and “the part”—or can be shared by several things. The meaning that can be shared is the absolute universal meaning, which can be considered in three ways, as explained earlier.

As for unqualified and qualified expressions, examples include “freeing a slave” and “you find no water.” A meaning may fall within an expression considered without qualification, yet not within the unqualified expression: that is, it falls within the expression without the condition of being unqualified, but not within the expression subject to that condition, as we have said

---

## Page 547

with regard to the word “water”; for “water” is used of semen and other things, as He says:

“From a gushing fluid.”

One also says “rose water,” but this does not fall within the designation “water” when it is unqualified, only when it is qualified. If we consider what is common to the unqualified expression “water” and the qualified expression “water,” this is the absolute without the condition of being unqualified. Thus, one says: “Water is divided into unqualified water and water with an added qualifier.” The category being divided has no unqualified designation of its own; rather, the context indicates comprehensiveness and generality, as when we say: “Water falls into three categories.”

Here, too, there are three things: the category being divided, namely water in its general sense, which is the absolute without any condition, but which has no single-word expression of its own, only a compound expression; the unqualified category, namely the expression subject to the condition of being unqualified; and the second category, the qualified expression, namely the expression subject to the condition of being qualified. This is because a speaker either leaves an expression unqualified or qualifies it: there is no third possibility. When he leaves it unqualified, it has one meaning; when he qualifies it, it has another. Furthermore, when he qualifies it, he does so either with a qualification that makes it general or with one that makes it specific. An example of a qualification that makes it general is his saying, “Water falls into three categories”; an example of one that makes it specific is his saying, “Rose water.”

Once the distinction between qualifying and leaving an expression unqualified, on the one hand, and qualifying and leaving a meaning absolute, on the other, is understood, it becomes clear that a meaning likewise has three states: it may be absolute, qualified by generality, or qualified by specificity.

Absolute meanings are of two types: the absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness, and the absolute without any condition. The same applies to expressions: an unqualified expression may be unqualified subject to the condition of being unqualified, as when we say “unqualified water”

---

## Page 548

and “unqualified slave”; it may also be unqualified without the condition of being unqualified, as when we say “human being.” An absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness does not include anything qualified in a way incompatible with that absoluteness. Thus, rose water does not fall within “unqualified water.” An absolute without any condition, however, includes what is qualified, just as a deficient human being falls within the designation “human being.” It is therefore clear that an absolute meaning subject to the condition of absoluteness has no existence in external reality. No absolute human being exists there; rather, a human being must be individuated as this person or that. Nor does an absolute animal exist there, or absolute rain subject to the condition of absoluteness.

As for expressions that are unqualified subject to the condition of being unqualified, such as “unqualified water,” their referents do exist in external reality. Here, the condition of being unqualified applies to the expression, so it does not prevent its meaning from being determinate. In the former case, however, the condition of absoluteness applies to the meaning. A referent that is absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness cannot be conceived, for every existent has a reality by which it is distinguished, and whatever has no reality by which it is distinguished is nothing. If it has a reality by which it is distinguished, that distinction prevents it from being absolute in every respect, since what is absolute in every respect has no distinguishing feature. There is therefore no existent that is absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness.

Pure nonexistence, however, may be said to be absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness, since there is neither a distinct reality nor an actual entity of which one could say that its reality, by its defining limits, prevents anything else from being identical with it.

---

## Page 549

As for absolute meanings without any condition, if they are said to exist in external reality, they exist only as determinate, distinct, particular entities. A determinate, particular entity falls within the absolute without any condition, but not within the absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness, since the former is more general. The existence in external reality of the absolute without any condition does not entail the existence there of the absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness, because the latter is more restricted.

Thus, when we speak of an animal, a human being, a body, or existence in an absolute sense, if we mean the absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness, it has no existence in external reality. If we mean the absolute without any condition, it exists only as something determinate and particular. Nothing exists in external reality except as something determinate and distinct, separate from everything else by its defining limits and its reality.

Whoever says that the existence of the Real is absolute existence rather than determinate existence is, in effect, saying that the Real has no existence at all, nor any reality other than the individual, distinct things themselves. Yet those individual things are not Him; thus, He is nothing at all.

The essential point may be summarized as follows: if he means the absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness, it has no existence in external reality, and so the Real would have no existence at all. If he means the absolute without any condition, then, if it is said not to exist in external reality, there is nothing further to discuss. If it is said to exist, however, it exists only as something determinate, and so the Real would have no existence other than the existence of individual entities. Two unacceptable consequences therefore follow. (The first is that the Real has no existence other than the existence of created things. The second is the contradiction in his statement that He is absolute existence rather than determinate existence.

---

## Page 550

So reflect on what this man says. He places the Real within created beings as a universal is present in its particulars, and as genus, species, property, and differentia are present in all its existent individual entities fixed in nonexistence.

The proponent of this view locates manifestations and ranks in determinate entities, just as the first proponent located them in individual entities fixed in nonexistence.

---

## Page 551

Section

As for al-Tilimsānī and those like him, he makes no distinction between essence and existence, nor between the absolute and the determinate. Rather, in his view, nothing exists other than Him, nor is anything distinct from Him in any respect. Created beings are merely parts and portions of Him, like the sea’s waves within the sea, or the parts of a house within the house. Among their verses are:

“I have no doubt that the sea is one … though waves and foam make it many.
Do not be deceived by the forms you behold … for the One, the Lord, pervades the many in His very being.”

And also:

“The sea is nothing but the waves, nothing else … though the multitude of its forms divides it.”

There is no doubt that this view is more sophisticated in its disbelief and heresy. For distinguishing between existence and essence and treating the nonexistent as something, or distinguishing in external reality between the absolute and the determinate and treating the absolute as something beyond the determinate entities in the mind, are two weak and false positions.

Those who have examined the matter closely know that whoever posits two things in these entities existing in external reality:

(one) their existence.

---

## Page 552

and, second, their essences—or posits for them an existent, absolute reality additional to their actual existent identity—has made a grave error. He has confused the abstract concepts free of particular determination and the essences abstracted from external existence that he derives from the mind with what actually exists in external reality. He has failed to realize that what the mind conceives and postulates extends beyond what actually exists in itself. The mind conceives of nonexistent things, impossible things, and things subject to conditions; it postulates things that have no existence whatsoever, whether possible or impossible. From particular things it abstracts attributes that are absolute within it, and from existing things, essences conceived within it.

But this view involves even greater ignorance and disbelief in Allah Most High. Its proponent does not distinguish between manifestations and that which manifests, and regards multiplicity and distinction as existing only in the human mind while a person is veiled from witnessing reality. Once his veil is lifted, he sees directly that there never was anything other, that the seer is the very thing seen, and that the witness is the very thing witnessed.

---

## Page 553

Section

Know that I am not aware of anyone in any community before these people who held these doctrines in this form. However, in some philosophical works transmitted from Aristotle, I have seen him report that a philosopher said, “Existence is one,” and then refute it. It is condemnation enough of a doctrine that even the Sabian dialectical theologians will not accept it.

These doctrines arose only with the emergence of the Tatar state. Previously, disbelief had taken the form of universal indwelling, union, or particular indwelling. The classification is fourfold: whoever maintains that the Lord is truly the servant must assert either that He indwells the servant or that He unites with him. In either case, he must hold that this is restricted to certain created beings, such as the Messiah, or that it extends to all created beings. Thus, there are four categories.

The first is particular indwelling. This is the doctrine of the Nestorian Christians and others like them, who say that the divine nature indwelt the human nature and clothed itself in it, like water within a vessel. Through their interaction with Muslims, these people gave definite form to Christian disbelief. The first of them appeared during the reign of al-Ma’mūn.

This is also the doctrine of those extremists within this community who agree with these Christians, such as the extremists among the Rāfidah, who say that He indwelt ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib and the imams of his household, and the extremist ascetics

---

## Page 554

who maintain that He indwells the saints and those whom they believe to possess sainthood, or some of them, such as al-Hallāj, Yūnus, al-Hākim, and others like them.

The second is particular union. This is the doctrine of the Jacobite Christians, whose doctrine is even more vile. They are the black peoples and the Copts, who say that the divine and human natures intermixed and blended like milk mixed with water. This is also the doctrine of those extremists who profess Islam and agree with them.

The third is universal indwelling. This is the doctrine that the leading scholars of the Sunnah and hadith reported from a group of the early Jahmiyyah. It is held by most of the devout worshippers among the Jahmiyyah, who say that Allah is in every place in His essence. They appeal to ambiguous passages of the Quran, such as His words:

“And He is Allah in the heavens and on the earth,”

and His words:

“And He is with you.”

Refutations of these people are numerous and well known in the writings of the leading scholars of the Sunnah, those endowed with knowledge, and the scholars of hadith.

The fourth is universal union. This is the doctrine of these heretics who claim that He is the very existence of created beings. Their disbelief is greater than that of the Jews and Christians in two respects. First, the latter said that the Lord unites with His servant whom He has drawn near and chosen, after the two had not been united. These people, however, say that the Lord has always been the servant and the other created beings, and is not distinct from them. Second, the latter restricted this to those whom they revered, such as the Messiah, whereas these people

---

## Page 555

extend this to dogs, pigs, filth, and dirt. If Allah Most High has said:

“They have certainly disbelieved who say, ‘Allah is the Messiah, the son of Mary,’”

and the rest of the verse, then what of someone who says that Allah is the unbelievers, hypocrites, children, madmen, impure and foul-smelling things, and everything else?

If Allah rejected the statement of the Jews and Christians when they said:

“We are the children of Allah and His beloved,”

and said to them:

“Say, ‘Then why does He punish you for your sins? Rather, you are human beings from among those He has created,’”

and the rest of the verse, then what of someone who claims that the Jews and Christians are the very existence of the Lord, the Creator—not other than Him or distinct from Him—and that it is inconceivable for Allah to punish anyone but Himself? Or that everyone who speaks in the universe is identical with the one who listens, as in the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Allah has pardoned my community for what they say within themselves.”

And that the one who has intercourse is identical with the one with whom he has intercourse? Their poet even said:

“My hand delights when it passes over my body,
For in truth, I am none other than you.”

Know that these people’s disbelief—in saying that Allah is all His created beings—is greater than the disbelief of the Christians in saying:

“Allah is the Messiah, the son of Mary.”

The Christians are astray, and most of them do not understand their doctrine of divine unity, for it is something imagined that can neither be known nor rationally understood. They make the Lord one substance, then make Him three substances, explaining this in terms of a multiplicity of properties and persons, which are the hypostases. Yet according to them, properties are not substances; thus, besides their disbelief, they contradict themselves. Likewise, these heretics who profess universal union are astray, and most of them do not understand the teaching of

---

## Page 556

their leaders, nor do they grasp it. In this they resemble the Christians: the more foolish and ignorant the shaykh is, the more knowledgeable about Allah he is in their eyes, and the greater their esteem for him.

Like the Christians, they have some share in worshipping the Lord in whom they have disbelieved. This lasts as long as one of them remains behind the veil. But if the veil is lifted from his heart and he recognizes that he himself is He, then he has a choice: either to exempt himself from commands and prohibitions and remain unrestrained, doing whatever he pleases; or to maintain the station of command and prohibition in order to preserve the ranks, and so that those who remain veiled—the majority of people—may follow his example. They claim that the prophets were likewise, since they counted them among those who had attained perfection.

---

## Page 557

Section

The doctrine of these proponents of the identity of Creator and creation—such as Ibn ʿArabī, Ibn Sabʿīn, al-Qūnawī, and al-Tilmisānī—is composed of three elements:

The negation and denial espoused by the Jahmiyya;

Ambiguous Sufi statements: the indeterminate, equivocal expressions found in the words of some of them. The Christians likewise went astray through similar expressions in what they relate from the Messiah, following what is ambiguous and abandoning what is clear. This also includes the utterances of those whose reason was overwhelmed and who spoke in a state of intoxication;

And philosophical heresy, which is the root of Jahmī doctrine, together with their discussions of absolute existence, intellects, souls, revelation, prophethood, necessity, and contingency, and the truth and falsehood contained therein.

This last element predominates in Ibn Sabʿīn and al-Qūnawī, while the second predominates in Ibn ʿArabī. For this reason, he is the closest of them to Islam.

They all share in Jahmī doctrine. Al-Tilmisānī is the most thoroughgoing exponent of this heresy and the doctrine of identity peculiar to them, and the most extreme of them in his disbelief in Allah, His Books, His Messengers, His revealed laws, and the Last Day.

---

## Page 558

This is illustrated by his statement:

“He was manifest in me in the oneness of His essence, knowing Himself and what proceeds from Him. All objects of knowledge were unveiled within the reality of knowledge, and He witnessed them.”

He may be answered as follows:

His knowledge of what proceeds from Him, and of objects of knowledge other than Himself which He witnesses, has thus been affirmed. You then stated that He presented Himself to these cosmic realities, which were witnessed yet nonexistent. At that point, He expressed Himself as “I,” and the reality of prophethood appeared, in which the Real appeared clearly and absolute existence was reflected. You stated that this bears the name “the Most Merciful,” just as the former bears the name “Allah.”

You continued until you said: “He is now as He was.” Now, is that which He knew would proceed from Him, and which He witnessed while it was nonexistent in itself, the Real or something other than Him? If it is the Real, it follows that the Lord was nonexistent and proceeded from Himself. This is also self-contradictory. If it is something other than Him, you have made that other thing a mirror reflecting absolute existence, and identified it as the Most Merciful. Creation would therefore be the Most Merciful.

You are thus caught between holding that He knew something nonexistent which proceeded from Him—in which case there would be something other than Him, and that other would not be the Most Merciful—and holding that this being who manifests and describes Himself is identical with Him and is the Most Merciful, in which case it would neither be nonexistent nor proceed from Him. Alternatively, you ascribe to the same thing, at one time, the distinctive attributes of the Real, the Creator, and, at another, those of the servant, the creature. Besides being contradictory, this is among the gravest forms of disbelief. It resembles the Christians’ statement: “The divine nature is the human nature.” Yet this is more disbelieving in several respects.

---

## Page 559

Section

The first point:

These cosmic realities—which you said were nonexistent in themselves, their individual realities being witnessed in His knowledge during His absolute self-manifestation, in which He was united with Himself in the oneness of His essence—did He create and bring them into existence after their nonexistence, or have they remained nonexistent?

If they have remained nonexistent, then nothing in the universe can exist. This obstinately contradicts sensory perception, reason, and revelation. No rational person would say this, nor has any rational person ever said it.

If they came into existence after their nonexistence, they cannot be identical with Him, for Allah was not nonexistent and then brought into existence. This disproves the identity of Creator and creation. It must then follow that there are existent beings that are not Allah, but are His creation, His possessions, and His servants. This disproves your statement: “He is now as He was, with nothing alongside Him.”

The second point:

As for your statement, “The divine constitution was composed”—from “He was” to “the secret of His affair”—or your statement, “The Real appeared in it,” or similar expressions used in this connection by these proponents of the identity of Creator and creation, such as:

“The Real appeared and manifested Himself”; “These are the loci of the Real’s appearance and manifestation”; “This is a divine locus of appearance and manifestation”—

do you mean by this that His very essence came to be there?

---

## Page 560

Or do you mean that He became manifest and revealed Himself to them, so that they know Him? Or do you mean that He appeared to His creation through them and revealed Himself through them? There is no fourth possibility.

If you mean the first—which is the position of those who maintain the identity of Creator and creation—then you have explicitly declared that created things themselves—even dogs, pigs, impurities, devils, and unbelievers—are Allah’s essence, or that they and Allah’s essence are united, or that Allah’s essence indwells them. This unbelief is greater than the unbelief of those who said:

“Allah is the Messiah, the son of Mary,”

and that Allah is the third of three, that Allah begets and is begotten, and that He has sons and daughters.
