If you state this explicitly, the Muslims will recognize your position and place you among your own kind. There is thus no need for ambiguous expressions that the thirsty man takes for water. If only, upon reaching them, he found nothing! Instead, he finds deadly poison.

If you mean that He became manifest and revealed Himself to them, this really means that He became known to them. There is no doubt that Allah becomes known to His servant, but what you say on this matter is false on two grounds.

You have made Him known to nonexistent things that have no existence, simply because He has known them. You have assumed that, because they are known, they can themselves come to possess knowledge. This is sheer falsehood: if He knows that something will come into being, that thing cannot possess knowledge, power, or the ability to act before it exists. Moreover, this does not hold true of all the entities known to Him; only some of them are capable of knowledge.

---

## Page 561

But if you say that Allah is known through them—because they are signs pointing to Him—then this is true. It is the religion of the Muslims and what those who know Him witness. Yet this is not what you have said, for two reasons.

First: They become signs only after He creates them and brings them into existence, not while they are nonexistent, though known to Him. You have not affirmed that He created them, brought them into existence, or endowed anything with its created nature. Rather, you have made Him Himself the one who reveals Himself to them.

Second: You have explicitly stated that He revealed Himself to them and became manifest to them, not that He used them to guide His creation to Himself and made them signs providing insight and a reminder to every servant who turns to Him. Allah has stated in His Book that He places signs in these created things. A sign is like a mark or an indication, as He says:

“Your God is one God. There is no god but Him, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful,”

through to His words:

“are signs for people who use reason.”

At other times, He calls the created thing itself a sign, as He, Most High, says:

“And a sign for them is the dead earth: We have brought it to life.”

What Allah has stated in His Book is the truth. Accordingly, if one says, “He revealed Himself through them and became manifest through them,” just as one says, “He is known and recognized through them,” the meaning would be correct. However, the terms “self-revelation” and “manifestation” in such a context are not established transmitted expressions, and they are potentially misleading and ambiguous. Manifestation and self-revelation are understood to mean manifestation and self-revelation to the eye, especially the term “self-revelation,” which is predominantly used for revelation to the eye. This is the doctrine of those who maintain the identity of Creator and creation. Ibn ‘Arabī stated it explicitly, saying:

“The eye therefore falls upon none but Him.”

If they hold that what is seen by the eye is Allah, then this is explicit unbelief by the consensus of the Muslims. Indeed, it is established in Sahīh Muslim that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“And know that no one

---

## Page 562

of you will see his Lord until he dies.”

This is particularly the case when it is said, “He became manifest in them and revealed Himself,” for the wording then admits two meanings: either His essence is within them, or they have become like a mirror in which the image of the object being viewed appears. Both are false. Allah’s essence is not within created things, nor are created things seen within His very essence as an object is seen in a mirror. Rather, their manifestation consists in their pointing to Him, bearing witness to Him, and being signs from Him indicating Himself and His attributes—glory and praise be to Him—as Allah’s Book declares.

(The third point)

As for the conjunction of the letters alif and nūn, expressed by “anā” (“I”), and the expression “the reality of prophethood” and “the relational spirit”: are these things included in what Allah’s names designate, such that they fall within what His explicit names and the pronouns referring to Him designate, or are they not included in what His names designate?

If the former is the case, then all created things would be included in what Allah’s names designate, and created things would be part of Allah and an attribute of Him. If the latter is the case, these things are nonexistent and have no existence in themselves. How, then, can they conceivably be existent yet not existent, established yet not established, absent yet not absent? This is a clear division of the alternatives, and one of the ways of exposing the true nature of this deception.

For these things were known to Him but nonexistent; when the khaliyya descended, the things he mentioned appeared. Thus, these manifest, known things, following this descent, became “I,” a reality of prophethood, a relational spirit, an act of an essence, an object of an essence’s action, and the meaning of intermediaries. If all of this is within Allah, then it entails two grave forms of unbelief: all created things being part of Allah

---

## Page 563

and His undergoing these changes—from deficiency to perfection and from perfection to deficiency. If, however, these things are outside His essence, then they were nonexistent, and, according to them, He did not create them outside Himself. How, then, can this be explained?

(The fourth point)

The “knot of the reality of prophethood” and what accompanies it must be either a self-subsisting entity or an attribute of Him or of another. If it is self-subsisting, it must be either Allah or something other than Him. If it is Allah, then Allah would be the manifest point, the reality of prophethood, and the relational spirit.

He subsequently said that He placed the relational spirit in the form of an act of His essence and gave Muhammad the knot of his prophethood. Thus, He would have made Himself the form of His own act and given Muhammad His own essence. Besides being among the clearest and most repugnant forms of unbelief, this is contradictory: who gives, and who is given, if He has given His own essence to another?

If these things are self-subsisting entities other than Allah, then, whether they are angels or anything else, every entity other than Allah is one of His creatures—made by Him and subject to His lordship. Allah is the Creator of everything. Yet he has represented the Real as appearing as one who describes Himself, and as the one designated by the name “the Most Merciful.” Thus, the one designated by the name “the Most Merciful,” who describes Himself, would be a created being. This is explicit unbelief, worse than the deviation of those who:

“They were told, ‘Prostrate to the Most Merciful,’ and they said, ‘What is the Most Merciful?’”

And worse than the deviation of those of whom it was said:

“They disbelieve in the Most Merciful.”

For those people disbelieved in His name and His attribute while acknowledging the Lord of the worlds. These people, however, acknowledge the name but make the one it designates one of His creatures.

If, on the other hand, what is meant by this reality and what accompanies it is an attribute, then it must be either an attribute of Allah

---

## Page 564

or of another. If it is an attribute of Allah, it cannot be the one designated by the name “the Most Merciful,” for that is a name of Allah Himself, not of His attributes. Prostration is to Allah, not to His attributes, and supplication is addressed to Allah, not to His attributes. If it is an attribute of another, the implication is all the more grave.

There is no escaping this division of possibilities. For this man, who deviates concerning Allah’s names, made this knot—which he called “the knot of the reality of prophethood” and regarded as the form of the Real’s knowledge of Himself and as a mirror reflecting absolute existence—a locus in which His eternal attributes become distinct. He also held that the Real appeared in it in His form and with His attribute, as one who describes Himself and encompasses Him, and who is designated by the name “the Most Merciful.” He then stated that He gave Muhammad this knot.

It is well known that the one designated by the name “the Most Merciful” is the same as the one designated by the name “Allah,” as the Most High says:

“Say, ‘Call upon Allah or call upon the Most Merciful. Whichever you call upon, to Him belong the most beautiful names.’”

Thus, He—glory be to Him—would Himself be this knot that He gave to Muhammad. If, however, it is an attribute of Him or of another, then that attribute would be the Most Merciful. This deviant’s position therefore entails either that the Most Merciful is one of Allah’s creatures or one of His attributes, or that Allah has bestowed the Most Merciful upon Muhammad. Both alternatives are among the most repugnant and abhorrent forms of unbelief.

(The fifth point)

His statement is: “This reality has two ends: one directed toward the Real facing it, in whom the highest existence appeared as one who describes; and another directed toward the world’s emergence from it, which is designated the relational spirit.”

In this statement, he mentioned the manifestation of existence and the manifestation of the world. It has already been stated that the Real existed when nothing existed with Him, manifesting Himself through His essential unity, and that when the khaliyya descended

---

## Page 565

—which was divine—the knot of the reality of prophethood appeared and became a mirror reflecting existence. The Real then appeared in it, in a form and with an attribute, as one who describes.

In this statement, he mentioned the Real facing it, the highest existence that appeared in this Real, and the end of this reality directed toward the Real. He thus mentioned three things here: the Real, existence, and the end. Yet earlier he identified the Real as the absolute existence that was reflected, and as the Real who appeared in it as one who describes. At one moment he identifies the Real with absolute existence; at another, he makes absolute existence appear in this Real. This is a contradiction.

It is then said to him:

These two expressions, in your view, refer to the Lord, Exalted is He. You have therefore made Him both manifest and a locus of manifestation. If by manifestation you mean existence, then the Lord would have come into existence time and again. This is abhorrent unbelief. How could His existence recur? How could He have come to exist in Himself after not having existed in Himself? If, however, you mean becoming evident and manifesting Himself, there was no created being to whom He could appear and manifest Himself, since the world had not yet been created. You said that the Real appeared in it as one who describes, and you called Him the Most Merciful; you did not present His manifestation as something known or witnessed. How, then, could He have become manifest to Himself after not having been manifest? This describes Him as not having known Himself until He came to know Himself.

Furthermore, you have already said that He was manifest to Himself through His unity. Thus, this is unbelief and contradiction.

(The sixth point)

This bewilderment and contradiction resemble the Christians’ bewilderment and contradiction concerning the hypostases.

---

## Page 566

For they say:

“The Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit are three gods, yet they are one god.”

The one clothed in Christ’s human nature is the Son. They also say:

“They are existence, knowledge, life, and power.”

They are answered:

“If these are attributes, they are not gods. Nor can the one clothed in Christ conceivably be a god unless he is the Father. If, however, they are substances, they necessarily cannot be one god, for three substances cannot be a single substance.”

They sometimes illustrate this by our saying, “Zayd, who is knowledgeable, capable, and alive”: his being knowledgeable is not the same as his being capable.

If, then, they are told:

“None of this precludes his being a single entity possessing multiple attributes, yet this is not what you maintain. Moreover, if the one united with Christ is a god, he cannot be an attribute; rather, he must be the one possessing the attribute. Yet you do not maintain that. Thus, you do not affirm what is true, and what you affirm is not true.”

Allah Most High has said:

“O People of the Scripture, do not go to extremes in your religion, and say nothing about Allah except the truth.”

The Christians are therefore bewildered and contradictory. If they make the hypostasis an attribute, Christ cannot be a god; if they make it a substance, God cannot be one. They wish to make Christ Allah, to make him the son of Allah, and to make the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit

---

## Page 567

one god. For this reason, Allah sometimes describes them in the Quran as associating partners with Him, and sometimes places them in a category distinct from the polytheists: they affirm both positions, despite their contradiction.

Such is also the condition of these people. They wish to affirm union and maintain that nothing other than Him exists, while also affirming the existence of the world. They therefore locate the world's subsistence in His knowledge, with Him witnessing it, and hold that He manifests Himself to what He witnesses. When He manifests Himself in it, He alone is the one manifesting Himself, and those witnessed entities are the world.

This man and Ibn Arabī agree on this, but differ in another respect. Ibn Arabī says:

“The existence of the Real appeared in the entities that are fixed in themselves. If you wish, you may say that it is the Real; if you wish, you may say that it is creation; if you wish, you may say that it is the Real and creation; if you wish, you may say that it is neither the Real in every respect nor creation in every respect; and if you wish, you may profess bewilderment concerning this.”

This man, however, says:

“The entities witnessed by Him became manifest.”

Thus, concerning all creation, both have said something resembling what the Melkite Christians say concerning Christ: that the divine nature and the human nature became one substance with two hypostases. Al-Tilimsānī, however, affirms no multiplicity whatsoever. He thus resembles the Jacobite Christians, who are the most extreme of them in unbelief. The Christians maintained this concerning a single person, saying:

“The divine nature clothes itself in the human nature after previously not having been clothed in it.”

---

## Page 568

These people, however, said that He is present throughout the entire world and has always been so. They thus held this to be universal and permanent, whereas the Christians held it to be particular and to have begun at a certain time. One of them even said: “The Christians disbelieved only because they restricted it.”

Ibn Arabī expressed this idea in several places in al-Fusūs. He stated that the prophets rebuked the idol worshippers only because they restricted their worship; otherwise, the knower who brings others to perfection is one who worships Him in every manifestation, and He is both the worshipper and the worshipped.

He further stated that, had the idol worshippers abandoned their worship, they would have abandoned the Real in proportion to the worship they abandoned; and that Moses rebuked Aaron only because Aaron had forbidden them to worship the calf, owing to Aaron’s narrow outlook and Moses’ knowledge that they had worshipped none but Allah. He also stated that Aaron was not given power over the calf so that they might worship Allah in every form; and that the greatest manifestation in which He was worshipped was desire, for nothing greater than desire has ever been worshipped.

Ibn Arabī, however, affirms entities fixed in nonexistence, whereas this man, Ibn Hamawayh, affirms them only as witnessed in His knowledge. This latter view is correct, but the union he sought cannot be established on that basis. Hence, of all of them, he was the furthest from establishing union and the closest to Islam, although he was the most contradictory and the most given to incoherent rambling. Much rambling, however, is better than much unbelief.

The implication of these words of his is that he made His existence conditional upon the existence of the world, even if He has some existence distinct from the world, just as the light of the eye is conditional upon the existence of the eyelids, even though it resides in the pupil. On this basis, Allah would depend upon the world and need it, just as the light of the eye needs

---

## Page 569

the two eyelids.

Allah Most High has said:

“Allah has certainly heard the statement of those who said, ‘Allah is poor, while we are rich.’”

And so on to the end of the verse. If this is what He says of those who described Him as needing their wealth to give it to the poor, what would He say of those who made His very essence dependent upon His creatures, such that, were it not for His creatures, His essence would disperse, scatter, and cease to exist, just as the light of the eye disperses, scatters, and ceases to exist when the eyelid is absent?

He has said in His Book:

“Indeed, Allah holds the heavens and the earth lest they cease to be. And if they should cease…”

The verse continues. Who, then, holds the heavens and the earth?

He has also said in His Book:

“And among His signs is that the heaven and the earth stand by His command.”

The verse continues. He has also said:

“He raised the heavens without pillars that you can see.”

And He has said:

“His Kursī extends over the heavens and the earth, and their preservation does not weary Him. And He is the Most High, the Most Great.”

“Does not weary Him” means that it neither burdens Him nor causes Him hardship. It is also stated in the hadith—the hadith of Abū Dāwūd:

“The heavens, the earth, and all that lies between them, in relation to the Kursī, are nothing but a ring cast upon a desert plain; and the Kursī, in relation to the Throne, is like that ring in the desert.”

He has also said in His Book:

“They have not appraised Allah with true appraisal, while the earth entirely will be in His grasp on the Day of Resurrection.”

The verse continues. It is established in the authentic hadith collections, in reports from Abū Hurayrah, Ibn Umar, and Ibn Masūd:

“Allah holds the heavens and the earth in His Hand.”

Thus, He in whose grasp are the heavens and the earth, whose Kursī encompasses the heavens and the earth, and whom preserving them does not weary

---

## Page 570

and by whose command the heaven and the earth stand, and who holds them lest they cease to be—could He need them and depend upon them, so that if they ceased to be, He would disperse and scatter?

If Muslims declare a person who says that the heavens carry Him or shelter Him to be a disbeliever, because this implies His dependence upon His creatures, then is not someone who says that, in His being established over the Throne, He needs the Throne just as something carried needs its bearer, a disbeliever? For Allah has no need of the worlds. He is the Ever-Living, the Self-Sustaining Sustainer of all, absolutely free of need, while everything besides Him depends upon Him. Yet His being established over the Throne is itself affirmed by the Quran, the Sunnah, and the agreement of the early generations of this community and the leading scholars of the Sunnah. Indeed, it is affirmed in every Book revealed to every prophet sent. What, then, of someone who says that He depends upon the heavens and the earth, and that if the heavens and the earth were removed, He would disperse, scatter, and cease to exist? How could His supposed need for the Throne to carry Him compare with the supposed dependence of His very essence upon what is below the Throne?

Then these people are told: If you maintain that the heavens and the earth have existed without beginning and will endure forever, this is disbelief. It amounts to affirming that the world has no beginning and denying that the heavens and the earth will split and break apart. But if you maintain that they came into existence, then how was He before their creation? Was He scattered, dispersed, and nonexistent, only to become existent and gathered together when He created them? Would any rational person say this?

You are thus caught between two forms of disbelief, coupled with the utmost ignorance and misguidance; choose whichever you wish. The forms of the world continually perish, and replacements come into existence within it, such as animals, plants, and minerals, and such things as Allah brings into being in the atmosphere: clouds, thunder, lightning, rain, and other things. Thus, whenever any of these ceases to exist, the light of the Real diminishes and disperses

---

## Page 571

and ceases to exist in proportion to what ceases to exist of these things. Whenever any of these things is added, His light increases, gathers together, and comes into existence. But if he means that Allah’s light remains after the heavens and the earth pass away, yet nothing appears within it, then what is it that appears after these things cease to exist? And what role do the heavens and the earth have in preserving Allah’s light?

It is established in the Sahīh, on the authority of Abū Mūsā al-Asharī, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah does not sleep, nor is it fitting for Him to sleep. He lowers and raises the balance. The deeds of the night are raised to Him before the deeds of the day, and the deeds of the day before the deeds of the night. His veil is light—or fire. Were He to remove it, the radiance of His Face would burn everything in His creation encompassed by His sight.”

Abd Allāh ibn Masūd said:

“With your Lord there is neither night nor day. The light of the heavens comes from the light of His Face.”

Thus, the truthful and trustworthy one has informed us that, were Allah to remove His veil, the radiance of His Face would burn everything encompassed by His sight—the heavens, the earth, and everything else. If the radiance of His Face would burn the heavens and the earth, and only His veil prevents this burning, could His light depend upon the heavens and the earth for its preservation?

(Seventh point: his statement, “The higher realms are its upper eyelid, and the lower realms its lower eyelid. Human multiplicity in the lower realms constitutes the lashes of the upper eyelid; the universal soul is its dark part, and the supreme spirit its white part.”

He is told: If, then, the world is this

---

## Page 572

eye, then what is the other eye? And where are the remaining bodily parts? This necessarily follows from your statement if by ‘ayn (eye) you mean an individuated entity. If, however, you mean the essence and self—that in which individuation occurs—then you have made the heavens, the earth, animals, and angels themselves parts and portions of Allah. This is the doctrine of those Pharaonic heretics who profess the unity of Creator and creation, whom Allah has caused a curse to follow in this world and who, on the Day of Resurrection, will be among the despised.

He is therefore told: On this understanding, Allah has created nothing, nor is He the Lord of the worlds. For He must create either Himself or something other than Himself. His creating Himself is impossible: it is self-evident that a thing cannot create itself. Hence the Most High says:

“Or were they created by nothing, or were they the creators?”

That is: Were they created without a creator, or did they create themselves? Hence Jubayr ibn Mut‘im said:

“When I heard the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recite this verse, I felt my heart split open.”

Thus, they know self-evidently that the Creator cannot be the created. Yet His creating anything other than Himself is impossible according to their premise, because these things are parts of Him, not something other than Him.

(Point) Eight: He has made human beings the eyelashes of the eyelid of Allah’s reality. Yet they continually increase and decrease in number, die and live, and include unbelievers and believers, the wicked and the righteous. Thus, the eyelashes of the eyelid of Allah’s reality would be perpetually scattered, snarling, and corrupt. The polytheists, Jews, and Christians would be the eyelids of His reality. He has cursed those who made them His chosen sons; what, then, of those who make them part of His very self?

---

## Page 573

(Point Nine)

He contradicts himself by making the spirit the white of the eye, the universal soul its dark part, the heavens its upper eyelid, and the earths its lower eyelid. It is well known that the two eyelids of the human eye enclose its dark and white parts. Yet, according to him, the spirit and the soul are above the heavens and the earth, not between heaven and earth as the dark and white parts of the eye are between the eyelids. Thus, besides being among the most abominable forms of unbelief, this analogy contains the ignorance and contradiction you can plainly see.

(Point Ten)

The universal soul is a term that the philosophers received from the Sabians. As for the spirit, he means by it what they call the intellect, which is the first of the emanations; he himself calls it a spirit. He based this on the doctrine of the Sabians, not on the religion of the pure monotheists. We have explained its falsity elsewhere.

Nevertheless, the Sabian philosophers are better than these people, for they acknowledge the Necessary Being from whom the intellects, souls, celestial spheres, and earth emanated. They do not identify these things with Him, whereas these people do. Their doctrine therefore accords only with that of deniers such as Pharaoh—and his followers—who said:

“And what is the Lord of the worlds?”

He also said:

“I know of no god for you other than myself.”

And he said:

“O Hāmān, build me a lofty tower so that I may reach the ways—the ways to the heavens,” to the end of the verse.

Pharaoh acknowledges the existence of this world but says that there is no Lord above it and that it has no creator other than itself.

---

## Page 574

Thus, when these people say that He is identical with the heavens and the earth, they deny what Pharaoh denied and acknowledge what Pharaoh acknowledged. The difference is that Pharaoh did not call it a god or say that it was Allah, whereas these people say, “This is Allah.” They acknowledge the Creator, but identify Him with His creation. In reality, therefore, they deny the Creator, although they believe that they acknowledge Him.

With Pharaoh, the reverse was true: outwardly he denied the Creator, while inwardly he acknowledged Him. His unbelief is therefore greater than theirs, but they are more misguided and ignorant than he was. This is why they hold him in such high esteem.

(Point Eleven)

The speaker’s statement: “Rather, this is the plain truth to be followed—not what is held by one who has strayed from the paths and religion of Islam, wandering bewildered in the wilderness of his error and ignorance.”

The reply is: Who among the earlier or later generations ever proclaimed this truth? Here is the Book of Allah, from beginning to end—His speech, His revelation, and what He has sent down—and it contains nothing of this. Nor is any of this found in a single hadith from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or reported from any of the imams and masters of Islam. It comes only from these fabricators of lies against Allah, who stand among religious masters as Genghis Khan does in warfare. Their religion resembles his regime, and perhaps his acknowledgment of the Creator is better than theirs. Some of them, however, may hold that embracing Islam is obligatory, in which respect they would be better than the Tatars.

As for their accomplished exponents and the majority of them, they regard adopting Judaism, Christianity, or Islam as permissible

---

## Page 575

and associating partners with Allah; they prohibit none of these. Indeed, in their view, nothing is forbidden to an accomplished exponent, nor is anything obligatory upon him.

It is well known that the unbelieving Tatars are better than these people, for these people are apostates from Islam—among the most loathsome apostates—and an apostate is worse in many respects than someone who has never been a Muslim. If Abū Bakr al-Siddīq fought the apostates for withholding zakat, then there is even greater reason to fight these people.

As for what he relates in al-Sham'a (The Candle) from the man whom he calls “the accomplished shaykh, the godly scholar, the seventh ghawth,” namely his statement, “Know that the world in its entirety is the dark center of Allah’s eye, which never sleeps,” and so forth, this must be addressed from several angles.

(First) To call someone who makes such a statement accomplished, learned, and godly is the very essence of misguidance and error. Indeed, neither Jews, Christians, nor idol worshippers say such a thing. If the person who said it had lost his reason, he would be treated like others in that condition: Allah has exempted him from accountability. But if he was of sound mind, this is an act of insolence toward Allah, who says:

“They say, ‘The Most Merciful has taken a son.’”

“You have certainly made a monstrous assertion.”

“The heavens almost rupture because of it”—to the end of the verses.

He also says:

“They say, ‘The Most Merciful has taken a son.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, they are honoured servants.”

“They do not precede Him in speech”—up to His words: “the wrongdoers.”

And He says:

“They have certainly disbelieved who say, ‘Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary.’ Say, ‘Who could prevent Allah in any way if He intended to destroy the Messiah, son of Mary?’”—up to His words: “And to Him is the final return.”

---

## Page 576

If this is what He says of those who claim to be His sons and His beloved, then what would He say of those who claim to be the lashes of His eyelid? Exalted is Allah, far above what the wrongdoers say.

The second point: This misguided shaykh—who uttered these words of disbelief and misguidance—contradicted the end of his statement with its beginning. The word ‘ayn can mean the thing itself, the organ of sight, or various other things. When he uses the expression “the thing itself,” ‘ayn means the thing itself—that is, it is distinguished in itself from other things. But when he says, “The world in its entirety is the dark center of Allah’s eye, which never sleeps,” ‘ayn here means the organ of sight.

Then, at the end of his statement, he says, “By Allah’s ‘ayn we mean that in which Allah becomes distinct.” Here, ‘ayn means the thing itself, and this ‘ayn has neither a dark center nor eyelids. This is like someone saying, “The ‘ayn (spring) gushed forth and overflowed; we drank from it and bathed. I weighed it on the scales and found it to weigh ten mithqāls, and its gold was pure.” The reason for this is that he frequently manipulated words without regard to their meanings.

The third point: His statement is contradictory in another respect. If the world is the dark center of the eye, then Allah’s other organs, besides the eye, would have to remain. Yet when he says at the end of his statement, “Allah is the light of the eye,” Allah would be part of the eye, or an attribute of it. Thus, at the beginning of his statement, he made the world a part of Allah, while at its end he made Allah a part of the world. Both statements constitute disbelief. Indeed, this is greater than the disbelief of those whom Allah mentions in His words:

“Yet they have made some of His servants a part of Him. Indeed, man is clearly ungrateful.”

“Or has He taken daughters from what He creates

---

## Page 577

and favored you with sons?”

If Allah has declared those who make some of His servants a part of Him to be unbelievers, then what of those who sometimes make His servants a part of Him and sometimes make Him a part of them? May Allah curse the proponents of these doctrines and vindicate Himself, His Book, His Messenger, and His believing servants against them.

The fourth point:

His statement is contradictory in yet another respect. When he says, “‘Ayn is that in which Allah becomes distinct, and the world in its entirety is the dark center of His eye, which never sleeps,” he makes Him distinct throughout the entire world. Yet when he then says, “He is the light of the eye,” the remaining parts of the eye—the eyelids, lashes, dark center, and white—are left as parts in which He does not become distinct. Thus, he makes Him both distinct and not distinct in them.

(The fifth point)

The light of the eye depends on the eye and needs it, because it subsists in it. If Allah were in the world as light is in the eye, He would necessarily need the world. Know that this statement resembles the doctrine of those who hold that Allah dwells within creation. They say, “He is in the world as water is in a tuft of wool, or as life is in the body,” and other such things. They also say, “He is present in His essence in every place.”

This is the doctrine of the early Jahmiyyah, whom the imams of Islam declared to be unbelievers. It is reported that al-Jahm used to say, “He is like this air,” or that he said, “He is this air.”

His initial statement, “It is the dark center of Allah’s eye,” resembles the doctrine of those who hold that Allah and creation are one. They say, “He is like a piece of wax that assumes different forms while remaining one and the same.” According to them, He is existence itself, and His differing states are like the differing states of that piece of wax.

---

## Page 578

For this reason, the proponent of these doctrines was confused and inconsistent. He has no firm standing among sincere Muslims who affirm Allah’s oneness, nor is he regarded by these heretics who hold that Allah and creation are one as one of their accomplished, knowledgeable adepts. All of these people are of the same kind as the Nusayriyyah and the Ismā‘īliyyah: their doctrines concerning the Lord resemble those of these sects. Among those sects are people who adhere to the Sharia and others who abandon it; the same is true of these people. Those sects, however, are more adept in heretical unbelief and know themselves to be deniers, like Pharaoh, whereas these people are ignorant and think that they are doing good.

The sixth point:

His statement: “If the higher and lower realms were removed, the light of Allah Most High would spread out so that nothing whatsoever would appear within it.”

This statement is ambiguous. Undoubtedly, its proponent is one of those who waver between the unbelievers and the believers: he is neither one of the believers nor a thoroughgoing proponent of the doctrine that Allah and creation are one. Rather, he has mixed truth with falsehood. For the proponents of that doctrine say that if the very heavens and earth ceased to exist, Allah would cease to exist. Some state this explicitly, but most merely allude to it. Their ordinary followers do not understand that this is part of the doctrine held by the others. These people are of the same kind as the Qarmatians and the Bātiniyyah; the latter attain this only at al-balāgh al-akbar (the supreme attainment), the final rank of their elite.

For this reason, one of the leading proponents of the doctrine that Allah and creation are one told me that the author of this statement used to say, “There is only a subtle difference between monotheism and heresy.”

I said to him, “This is utterly false. Indeed, no two doctrines differ more greatly than monotheism and heresy.” He said this on the basis of the confusion and intermingling he had introduced, as in

---

## Page 579

his statement: “If the higher and lower realms were removed, the light of Allah would spread out so that nothing would appear within it.”

He should therefore be asked:

“If the higher and lower realms were removed, what would you mean by its spreading out? Would you mean its dispersal and cessation of existence, as the eye’s light disperses when the eyelids are absent? Or would you mean that something existent spreads out? What, then, would spread out? Would it be Allah Himself, or one of His attributes? Over what would it spread? And what would appear—or not appear—within it?”

If you mean the former, this is what the opening of your statement entails, for you said:

“We said that the higher and lower realms are the eyelids of Allah’s eye only because they preserve the manifestation of the light. If a person’s eyelids were cut off, the light of his eye would disperse and spread out so that he could see nothing at all. Likewise, if the higher and lower realms were removed, the light of Allah would spread out so that nothing whatsoever would appear within it.”

You also said:

“Allah is the light of the eye, the Supreme Spirit is its white part, and the Universal Soul is its black part.”

It is clear that, according to your account, the existence of the eye’s light depends on the eyelids. When the condition ceases to exist, what depends upon it also ceases to exist. Thus, in your view, the world is a condition for Allah’s existence: if the world ceased to exist, the reality of Allah would cease to exist because its condition was no longer present—even if you affirm that He has an essence distinct from the world. This is one of the two positions held by the proponents of the doctrine that Allah and creation are one.

For they sometimes hold that the existence of the Real is the very existence of created beings, not something distinct from them.

---

## Page 580

and on this view, His existence cannot be conceived of in the absence of created beings. This amounts to outright denial of the Creator. It is the position of al-Qūnawī and al-Tilimsānī, and the position expressed by the author of al-Fusūs in much of his writing. At other times, they attribute to Him a self-subsisting existence, then maintain that this very existence is also the existence of created beings, in the sense that it flowed forth to them. This is less unbelieving than the first position, although both are among the gravest and most repugnant forms of unbelief. Some passages in the writings of the author of al-Fusūs and others accord with this position. The statements of this man likewise sometimes suggest this meaning.

Even so, do they make His existence conditional upon the existence of the world, thereby making Him dependent upon the world, or do they not? Sometimes they say one thing, and sometimes the other.

Seventh:

They praise misguidance, bewilderment, injustice, error, and the punishment with which Allah punished the nations. They invert the words of Allah and His Messenger in ways whose invalidity is evident from the most basic truths of reason. An example is the statement of the author of al-Fusūs:

“Had Noah not combined the two calls for his people, they would have answered him. He called them publicly, then called them privately.”

He goes on to say:

“He related that his people turned a deaf ear to his call because they knew what answering his call would require of them. Those who know Allah thus understood what Noah was indicating concerning his people: praise expressed in the language of blame. And he knew that they did not answer his call only because of the furqān (distinction) it contained, whereas the matter is Quran, not furqān. Whoever is established in the Quran does not heed furqān, even though it is contained within it.”

Thus they praise and commend what Allah has condemned, cursed, and forbidden, and perpetrate falsehood

---

## Page 581

and fabrications against Allah, and distort His names and signs so grievously that:

“The heavens almost rupture therefrom, and the earth splits open, and the mountains collapse in devastation.”

An example is the statement of the author of al-Fusūs in the chapter on Noah:

“‘Because of their sins they were drowned.’ Their sins were what carried them forward, so they drowned in the seas of knowledge of Allah, which is bewilderment. ‘Then they were admitted into a Fire’—within the water itself, in al-mahmadatayn (the two objects of praise). ‘And when the seas are set ablaze.’ One says, ‘I fired the oven,’ when one kindles it. ‘And they found no helpers besides Allah.’ Thus their helpers were none other than Allah, and they perished in Him forever. Had you brought them out onto the shore—the shore of nature—they would have descended from this lofty rank, even though everything belongs to Allah and exists through Allah; indeed, it is Allah.

“‘And Noah said: My Lord, do not leave upon the earth, of the disbelievers’—those who covered themselves with their garments and put their fingers in their ears, seeking concealment, because he called them so that He might forgive them, and forgiveness means concealment—‘a single inhabitant,’ meaning anyone at all, so that the benefit might encompass everyone, just as the call encompassed everyone.

“‘Indeed, if You leave them’—that is, let them remain and leave them alone—‘they will lead Your servants astray,’ meaning that they will bewilder them and bring them out of servitude into the mysteries of lordship within them. They will then see themselves as lords, after having regarded themselves as servants. Thus they are servants and lords.

“‘And they will beget’—that is, produce and bring forth—‘none but a wicked one,’ meaning one who reveals what he has concealed, ‘an obstinate disbeliever,’ meaning one who conceals what has appeared after its appearance. They reveal what was concealed, then conceal it after it has appeared. The observer is thus bewildered: neither the wicked person's intention in his wickedness nor the disbeliever's intention in his disbelief is known, though the person is one and the same.

“‘My Lord, forgive me,’ meaning: conceal me and conceal the stages of my journey, so that my station and worth remain unknown, just as Your worth was unknown, as You said:

---

## Page 582

‘They have not appraised Allah with true appraisal.’

‘And my parents’—meaning those from whom I originated, namely intellect and nature. ‘And whoever enters my house’—meaning my heart—‘as a believer,’ accepting the divine tidings it contains, which are what the souls have told themselves. ‘And the believing men’—from among the intellects—‘and the believing women’—from among the souls. ‘And do not increase the wrongdoers’—the word being taken from ‘darkness’: the people of the unseen, sheltered within dark veils—‘except in destruction,’ meaning annihilation, so that they no longer know their own souls, because they behold the face of the True One to the exclusion of themselves.”

All of this is among the most abominable alterations and distortions of Allah’s words. Allah condemned the People of the Book in the Quran for things less grave than this. He condemned them for distorting words from their proper places, and because they:

“Write the Scripture with their own hands, then say, ‘This is from Allah,’ in order to exchange it for a small price.”

“They say, ‘It is from Allah,’ but it is not from Allah. And they speak untruth about Allah while they know.”

These people have distorted Allah’s words from their proper places in the most abominable manner. They have written books of hypocrisy and heresy with their own hands and claimed that they are from Allah. Sometimes they claim to receive from the same source as the angel who conveys revelation to the Prophet, thus placing themselves one degree above the Prophet. At other times, they claim to receive from the same source as Allah, so that one of them, in his knowledge of himself, stands on a par with Allah’s knowledge of him, because both draw from one and the same source. At still other times, one of them claims that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave him, in a dream, this hypocrisy

---

## Page 583

in all its enormity, together with this profound heresy, and commanded him to present it to his community. He further claims that he made it public exactly as the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had prescribed for him, without addition or omission.

A number of distinguished men—even some who spoke to me about him and defended him—believed that he regarded lying as permissible. They preferred to say that he lied deliberately, considering that less grave than unbelief. They then explicitly declared that his teaching constituted unbelief. More than one sensible and distinguished person, including shaykhs and scholars, testified that he deliberately lied.

It is well known that this is among the most outrageous lies against Allah and His Messenger, and that he is among those to whom Allah’s words most aptly apply:

“Who is more unjust than one who invents a lie about Allah or says, ‘It has been revealed to me,’ when nothing has been revealed to him?”

Many lying claimants to prophethood—such as al-Mukhtār ibn Abī ʿUbayd and others like him—did not reach this degree of lying and fabrication. Indeed, even Musaylima the Liar did not reach this degree of lying and fabrication. Each of these men revered the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and acknowledged his messengership, but claimed to be another messenger himself. He did not deny the existence of the Lord, nor did he outwardly deny the Quran.

These people, by contrast, have denied the Lord, associated everything with Him, and fabricated these books, which they may claim are greater than the Quran. They consider themselves superior to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in certain respects, as the author of al-Fusūs explicitly stated concerning the Seal of the Saints.

A trustworthy person told me that the wicked al-Tilimsānī used to say:

“The Quran is entirely polytheism; it contains no monotheism. Monotheism is found only in our words.”

---

## Page 584

As for error and bewilderment:

Allah has never praised either of these, nor did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ever say:

“Increase my bewilderment concerning You.”

No scholar of hadith has narrated this report. It appears in no hadith collection, nor in any book written by someone knowledgeable about hadith—nor even by anyone who knows Allah and His Messenger. The same applies to his citing as evidence Allah’s words:

“Whenever it gives them light, they walk in it; when darkness falls upon them, they stand still.”

This describes only the condition of the apostate hypocrites. Error and bewilderment are among the things Allah has condemned in the Quran. Allah, Exalted is He, says in the Quran:

“Say, ‘Shall we invoke besides Allah that which neither benefits nor harms us, and turn back on our heels after Allah has guided us, like one whom the devils have led astray on earth, bewildered?’”

—and the rest of the verse.

This is precisely what these misguided, bewildered people wish to do to the believers. They want them to invoke besides Allah that which neither harms nor benefits them: created beings, idols, images, and everything worshipped besides Allah. They want to turn the believers back on their heels, turning them away from belief in Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and resurrection after death, so that they become bewildered and astray:

“Like one whom the devils have led astray on earth, bewildered, while he has companions calling him to guidance, ‘Come to us.’”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“And We turn their hearts and eyes away,”

up to His words:

“Wandering blindly”—that is, bewildered.

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“And their hearts are filled with doubt, so in their doubt they waver.”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“Guide us to the straight path—the path of those You have blessed, not those who have incurred Your anger, nor those who have gone astray.”

He thus commanded that

---

## Page 585

we ask Him to guide us to the straight path, the path of those He has blessed, who are distinct from those who have incurred His anger and those who have gone astray. Yet these people condemn the straight path and praise the way of those lost in error and bewilderment, in opposition to Allah’s Books and messengers and to the innate reason and understanding with which Allah has endowed His servants.

---

## Page 586

Section:
Some statements of Ibn Arabī that clarify what we have described of his doctrine, which most people may not understand.

In the chapter on Joseph—after likening the world’s relation to Allah to a person’s shadow, and contradicting himself in the analogy—he said:

“Everything you perceive is the existence of the Real in the entities of possible beings. In respect of the Real’s identity, it is His existence; in respect of the different forms within it, it is the entities of possible beings. Just as the diversity of forms does not deprive it of the designation ‘shadow,’ so the diversity of forms does not deprive it of the designation ‘world’ or ‘other than the Real.’ In respect of the oneness of its being a shadow, it is the Real, for He is the One, the Unique; in respect of the multiplicity of forms, it is the world. Pay careful attention and fully grasp what we have explained to you.

If matters are as I have described to you, then the world is imagined and has no real existence. This is the meaning of imagination: you are made to imagine that it is something additional, existing independently and outside true existence, whereas in reality it is not so. Do you not perceive it through the senses as joined to the person from whom it extends, unable to sever that connection? For a thing cannot be separated from its own essence. Know, then, your own entity, who you are, and what your identity is. What is your relation to the Real? In what respect are you the Real, and through it a knower, something other and different?”

And other expressions of this kind.

---

## Page 587

He also said near the beginning of al-Fusūs (The Seals), after “A Seal of Divine Wisdom in the Word of Adam” and “A Seal of Wisdom of the Soul in the Word of Seth”—having classified gifts bestowed by Allah’s command as either granted in response to a request or granted without one, and discussed the kind that is not requested, because something is Allah’s gift—until he said:

“Among these are those who know that Allah’s knowledge of them in all their states is what they were in the state of fixity of their entities before those entities came into existence. Such a person knows that the Real gives him only what his entity has imparted to Him of knowledge about him. This is what he was in his state of fixity; thus he knows whence Allah’s knowledge of him was obtained. No class among the people of Allah is higher or possesses greater unveiling than this class. They are those acquainted with the secret of divine decree, and they fall into two groups: those who know this in general and those who know it in detail.

The one who knows it in detail is higher and more complete than the one who knows it in general. He knows what has been determined concerning him in Allah’s knowledge, either through Allah informing him of the knowledge about him that his entity imparted to Him, or through His unveiling to him his fixed entity and the succession of its states without end. The latter is higher, for his knowledge of himself is on the same level as Allah’s knowledge of him, since both draw from a single source. On the servant’s part, however, this is a favour from Allah already granted to him, itself among the states of his entity. The possessor of this unveiling knows it when Allah discloses this to him—that is, the states of his entity. For when Allah discloses to a created being the states of his fixed entity—upon which the form of existence falls—it is not within that being’s capacity, in this state, to perceive the Real’s knowledge of these fixed entities in their state of nonexistence, because they are essential relations that have no form.

---

## Page 588

Thus, to this extent we say: divine solicitude had already favoured this servant with this equality in the imparting of knowledge. Hence Allah says:

‘Until We know.’

This is an expression whose meaning is fully realized; it is not as someone unfamiliar with this spiritual approach imagines. The furthest that one who affirms divine transcendence can go is to attribute this arising in knowledge to its relation. This would be the highest understanding of this issue that a speculative theologian could attain, were it not that he posits knowledge as something additional to the Essence, thus attributing the relation to knowledge rather than to the Essence. In this respect he differs from the realized knower among the people of Allah who possesses unveiling and witnessing.

We now return to gifts and say: gifts are either from the Essence or from the divine names. As for favours, bestowals, and gifts from the Essence, they can only come through a divine self-disclosure. Self-disclosure from the Essence can only accord with the form of the preparedness of the servant to whom it is disclosed; nothing else is possible. Thus, the recipient of the self-disclosure has seen nothing but his own form in the mirror of the Real. He has not seen the Real, nor can he see Him, although he knows that he has seen his own form only in Him. It is like a mirror in the visible world: when you see images in it, you do not see the mirror, although you know that you have seen those images—or your own image—only in it.

Allah has therefore presented this as an example of His self-disclosure from the Essence, so that its recipient may know that he has not seen Him. There is no example closer or more similar to vision and self-disclosure than this. Strive as you may, when you see the image in the mirror, to see the physical substance of the mirror: you will never see it at all. Indeed, some who have perceived something like this concerning the image seen have held that the visible image lies between the beholder’s sight and the mirror. This is the utmost knowledge such a person could attain; the matter is as we have stated and maintained.

We have explained this in al-Futūhāt al-Makkiyya (The Meccan Openings). When you taste this, you have tasted the ultimate limit, which has no

---

## Page 589

higher limit for a created being. Do not aspire, then, or weary yourself trying to rise above this degree, for no higher degree exists at all; beyond it is only pure nonexistence. He is your mirror in which you see yourself, and you are His mirror in which He sees His names and the manifestation of their effects; they are none other than His very Essence. Thus the matter becomes entangled and obscure. Some of us are ignorant even as they know, and say:

“And inability to attain comprehension is itself comprehension.”

Others among us know and do not utter such a statement, though it is the loftiest statement. Rather, knowledge has brought them silence, not incapacity. Such a person has the highest knowledge of Allah.

This knowledge belongs only to the Seal of the Messengers—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the Seal of the Saints. None of the prophets or messengers perceives it except through the lamp-niche of the Seal of the Messengers—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and none of the saints perceives it except through the lamp-niche of the Seal of the Saints. Indeed, even the messengers, whenever they perceive it, do so only through the lamp-niche of the Seal of the Saints. For messengership and prophethood—by which I mean lawgiving prophethood and messengership—come to an end, whereas sainthood never ends.

Thus, insofar as the messengers are saints, they perceive what we have mentioned only through the lamp-niche of the Seal of the Saints. What, then, of the saints below them? Although the Seal of the Saints follows, in matters of law, the legislation brought by the Seal of the Messengers, this neither detracts from his station nor contradicts our position. For in one respect he is lower, just as in another respect he is higher.

Support for our position also appears in the outward aspect of our revealed law: in Umar’s merit concerning the judgment on the captives of Badr, and in the pollination of date palms. Thus, the perfect man need not take precedence in every

---

## Page 590

matter or at every rank. Such men seek precedence only in the degree of knowledge of Allah; that is their goal. As for events in the created world, their thoughts are not concerned with them. Thus, what we have mentioned is established.

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—likened prophethood to a brick wall, complete except for the space of a single brick, and the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was that brick. Yet he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—sees it only, as he said, as a single brick.

As for the Seal of the Saints, he must have this vision. He sees what the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—depicted, but sees spaces for two bricks in the wall, whose bricks are of gold and silver. He sees the two bricks that the wall lacks and through which it becomes complete: a gold brick and a silver brick. He must see himself impressed into the spaces of those two bricks. Thus, the Seal of the Saints is those two bricks, and the wall becomes complete.

The reason he necessarily sees two bricks is that, outwardly, he follows the revealed law of the Seal of the Messengers—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This corresponds to the place of the silver brick: his outward aspect and the rulings in which he follows the Messenger. At the same time, inwardly, he receives from Allah Most High the very thing in which he outwardly follows the Messenger. Since he has seen the matter as it truly is, he must see it in this way. This corresponds inwardly to the place of the gold brick, for he was drawn from the source from which the angel through whom revelation is conveyed to the Messenger draws.

If you understand what I have indicated, you have attained beneficial knowledge. Every prophet, from Adam to the last prophet, receives only through the lamp-niche of the Seal of the Prophets, even though his clay came into existence later

---

## Page 591

for he exists in his true reality. This is expressed in his saying—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I was a prophet while Adam was between water and clay.”

Other prophets were not prophets until they were sent. Likewise, the Seal of the Saints was a saint while Adam was between water and clay, whereas other saints became saints only after fulfilling the conditions of sainthood: acquiring divine qualities and embodying them, since Allah is called the Protecting Friend, the Praiseworthy. Thus, in respect of his sainthood, the Seal of the Messengers stands in the same relation to the Seal of Sainthood as the prophets and messengers stand to him, for he is the saint, the messenger, and the prophet.

The Seal of the Saints is the saint who inherits, receives from the source, and witnesses the ranks. He is one of the good works of the Seal of the Messengers, Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the foremost of the company and the master of Adam’s descendants in opening the door to intercession. Through his intercession, he singled out a particular situation, without extending it generally. In this particular situation, he took precedence over the divine names, for the All-Merciful interceded with the Avenger on behalf of those undergoing affliction only after the intercessors had interceded. Muhammad thus attained preeminence in this particular station. Whoever understands the ranks and stations will have no difficulty accepting such words. End of quotation.

In this section, he has set out the true nature of his doctrine, upon which he bases everything else he says. Reflect, then, on the disbelief it contains, such that:

“The heavens almost rupture from it, the earth splits open, and the mountains collapse in devastation.”

And reflect on its denial of Allah’s creation and command, its denial of His lordship and divinity, its insults and abuse directed at Him, and its disparagement of His messengers and the utterly truthful, together with its claims to precedence over them

---

## Page 592

through false claims for which there is no proof, and whose falsity is evident with even the slightest degree of reason and faith, and the simplest passages heard from Scripture and the Quran. He also makes the disbelievers, hypocrites, and Pharaohs Allah’s people and His elect—the people of spiritual unveilings. This is false in several respects:

First:

He posited a fixed entity for him before his existence, and likewise for all other existing things, maintaining that this holds for him and all his states. Every existing entity or attribute, substance or accident, has its entity fixed before it exists. This is an error in which others preceded him, as explained earlier.

Second:

He maintained that Allah’s knowledge of the servant comes solely from His knowledge of that fixed entity in nonexistence, which constitutes the servant’s true reality, rather than from His own holy Essence. He further maintained that His knowledge of the fixed entities in nonexistence and their states prevents Him from doing anything otherwise, and that this is the secret of divine decree.

This entails describing Allah Most High as dependent upon these entities while they are independent of Him. It denies the perfection of knowledge and power that belongs to Him by virtue of His own Essence, and necessarily imputes ignorance and incapacity to Him. Part of this discourse resembles what Allah relates concerning those of whom He said:

“Allah has certainly heard the statement of those who said, ‘Indeed, Allah is poor, while we are rich.’”

The verse continues. For he makes the realities of the fixed entities in nonexistence independent of Allah with respect to their realities and entities, while making the Lord dependent upon them for His knowledge of them. He thus acquires His knowledge of them only from them. A servant acquires knowledge of perceptible things by perceiving them, while those perceived things are independent of the perceiver.

---

## Page 593

Muslims know that Allah knows all things before they come into being, through His eternal, beginningless knowledge, which is inseparable from His holy Essence. He does not derive His knowledge of them from them:

“Does He who created not know, while He is the Subtle, the All-Aware?”

This verse establishes the necessity of His knowledge of all things in several ways, encompassing the rational proofs advanced by practitioners of speculative theology, philosophers, and others who engage in reflection and reasoned inference.

First:

He is their Creator, and creation means bringing things into being according to a determined measure. This entails determining them in His knowledge [before] they come into external existence.

Second:

This necessarily entails will. Will and volition necessarily entail conceiving what is willed and being aware of it. This is the well-known line of argument used by most practitioners of speculative theology.

Third:

All things proceed from Him, and He is their sufficient cause. Knowledge of a thing’s source and cause necessarily entails knowledge of its resulting effect. His knowledge of Himself therefore necessarily entails knowledge of everything that proceeds from Him.

Fourth:

He is, in Himself, Subtle, perceiving the finest details, and All-Aware, perceiving what is hidden. This is what knowledge of things entails. The necessary consequence must exist because its sufficient cause exists. Thus, in His knowledge of things, He is self-sufficient and independent of them, just as He is self-sufficient in all His attributes.

Then, when He sees things after they come into existence, hears His servants’ speech, and so forth, He perceives only what He has brought into being and created—what depends upon Him and needs Him in every respect. In His knowledge and perception, He has absolutely no need of anything other than Himself. It is therefore impermissible to say that He derives His knowledge of things from the fixed entities themselves, supposedly independent of Him in their fixed existence.

---

## Page 594

As for his denial of Allah’s power:

He portrays the Lord as capable only of manifesting Himself in those entities that are fixed in nonexistence and independent of Him. His power is thus bounded by them and confined to them, even though they are independent of Him and their realities are fixed without Him. This, in his view, is the secret that has rendered Allah powerless to create anything other than what He has created. According to him, Allah cannot add a single particle to the world or remove one from it, add a drop to the rain or remove one from it, increase or decrease a person’s height, or change any of his attributes, movements, or states of rest. Nor can He move a stone from its place, divert water from its course, guide someone who is astray, lead someone who is guided astray, set something motionless in motion, or bring something moving to rest. In short, He can bring about only what has actually come into existence, because the entity of whatever has come into existence is fixed in nonexistence, and He can do no more than manifest Himself in those entities.

This attribution of ignorance and incapacity, which he has set out and claimed to be the secret of divine decree—even though it incorporates some of what other misguided people have said—contains such disbelief as even other misguided people would not accept.

For those who hold that the nonexistent is a thing say this of every possible thing, whether it exists or not. They do not regard His knowledge of things as derived from those things before they come into existence, nor do they hold that His creation and power are confined to what He knows of them. For He knows kinds of possible things that He has not created; thus, the possible things encompassed by His knowledge are more extensive than what He has created. Nor do they hold that what prevents Him from creating anything other than what He has created is that the entities fixed in nonexistence admit of no existence other than this. Rather, in their view, these entities could exist with different attributes, which are likewise among the possibilities fixed in nonexistence. Their position therefore does not entail attributing either ignorance or incapacity to Him in this respect. They may, however, say:

---

## Page 595

what prevents Him from doing so is that this is the most perfect and beneficial of all possible arrangements. His knowledge that nothing is more perfect than this prevents Him, in His wisdom, from willing anything less perfect. Thus, they make the impediment something pertaining to His own holy Being, rather than something imposed upon Him by another. How different is the position of those who hold that nothing outside Him can hinder Him or repel His decree from that of those who portray Him as restrained and obstructed! And how different is the position of those who hold that His knowledge is intrinsic to Him from that of those who portray Him as deriving knowledge from another—and from one of whom He has no need?

Yet most people have rejected the claim:

“Nothing more wondrous than this world is possible.”

Third:

He claimed that, among the class he ranked as the highest of Allah’s people, some possess knowledge on a par with Allah’s knowledge, because both draw from the same source. When the states of the entities fixed in nonexistence are unveiled to such a person, he knows them from the same standpoint from which Allah knows them. The only difference, from the servant’s side, is a prior divine favour bestowed upon him, which is itself among the states of his entity. The possessor of this unveiling recognizes it when Allah makes it known to him. Thus, he made his knowledge and Allah’s knowledge derive from the same source.

Fourth:

He portrayed Allah as coming to know these things after not knowing them. He followed the ambiguous wording of His statement:

“Until We know,”

and claimed that these words are true in their literal meaning, basing this on his corrupt premise that the servant’s existence is identical to the Lord’s existence. Thus, whenever a creature learns something it did not previously know, it is Allah who has learned something He did not previously know.

No disbeliever before him ever professed such disbelief. For the furthest that someone who denies Allah’s decree goes is to say:

“Allah came to know something He did not previously know.”

As for his claim that every new item of knowledge acquired by a creature is in fact newly acquired

---

## Page 596

by Allah, and that Allah did not know what each creature knew until that creature came to know it—this is a fabrication no one but he has made.

(Fifth)

He claimed that the self-disclosure of the divine Essence takes a form corresponding to the preparedness of the one manifesting, and that the recipient of this disclosure sees nothing but his own form in the mirror of the Real. He further claimed that the recipient cannot see the Real, although he knows that he has seen his own form only in Him. He used the analogy of a mirror, making the Real the mirror and the image within it the recipient’s own form.

This confirms what I have explained about his doctrine: in his view, the existence of the entities is the existence of the Real. The entities were fixed in nonexistence, and the existence of the Real then appeared within them. Thus, the recipient of the disclosure—the servant—does not see existence apart from individual essences; he sees only the essences in which existence has appeared. He therefore has no means of ever seeing existence itself. In his view, this is the ultimate limit for a creature, beyond which there is no further limit, and beyond it lies only pure nonexistence. Thus, He is your mirror in which you see yourself, and you are His mirror in which He sees His names and the manifestation of their effects.

This is because the servant sees himself—his own entity—only within the existence of the Real, which is his own existence. The servant is His mirror in which He sees His names and the manifestation of their effects, because, in his view, the names of the Real are the relations and connections between the entities and the existence of the Real. The effects of the names are the entities fixed in nonexistence, and these effects become manifest through the Real’s self-disclosure within the entities.

The entities, which constitute the reality of what is directly seen, are the mirror of the Real in which He sees His names;

---

## Page 597

and the manifestation of their effects. For when He appears within the entities, the relation between existence and the entities—which constitutes the names—comes into being, and their effects—which are the entities—become manifest. The existence of these entities is the Real. Hence he said: “They are nothing other than His very essence, so the matter became confused and obscure.”

Reflect on these words of his and others like them, so that you may understand what he believes concerning the Essence of the Real and His names: in his view, the Essence of the Real is the very existence of created beings; His names are the relations between existence and the entities; and their effects are the entities themselves. You will then understand how his words encompass denial of Allah, His names, His attributes, His creation and His command, as well as deviation concerning Allah’s names and signs. Indeed, what he has stated represents the utmost deviation concerning Allah’s names and signs—both the signs in creation and the recited signs. He affirms neither a name nor a sign for Him, since there is only a single existence, and that is neither a name nor a sign. Nor are the fixed entities His names or His signs. When he posited two things and distinguished between them in terms of existence and fixedness—although there is no difference between them—the matter became confused and obscure to him.

This is the true substance of his statements and the secret of his doctrine, by virtue of which he claims to be the most knowledgeable of all people about Allah, and thereby to have surpassed al-Siddīq (the eminently truthful), who, according to him, was ignorant when he said:

“Inability to attain comprehension is itself comprehension.”

He also claims thereby to have surpassed the messengers, who learned this only from his light. His doctrine contains so many forms of disbelief and misguidance that listing them would take too long.

Among them is disbelief in the Essence of Allah, since, in his view, there is nothing but the existence of created beings.

---

## Page 598

And among them is disbelief in Allah’s names, for, in his view, they are nothing but things without existence. Thus, when we say:

“All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds,”

“The Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful,”

in his view, the Lord is nothing but a relation to fixedness.

Sixth: He said, “So the matter became confused and obscure.” Or, on his corrupt premise, it is confused and obscure, whereas on the foundation of guidance and faith it is distinct and clear. Through His Book, Allah has distinguished truth from falsehood and guidance from misguidance.

He said: “Among us are those who, in their knowledge, are ignorant and say, ‘Inability to attain comprehension is itself comprehension.’” These words are widely attributed among them to Abū Bakr al-Siddīq. He thus portrayed him as ignorant, although this wording has not been preserved from Abū Bakr, nor is it transmitted from him in any reliable accounts. Ibn Abī al-Dunyā merely mentioned something similar in his Book of Gratitude from an unnamed member of the generation following the Companions. It is attributed to Abū Bakr only through disconnected reports by people whose disconnected reports frequently contain errors. Similarly, they relate that ‘Umar said:

“When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and Abū Bakr spoke to one another, I was like a Zanj man between them.”

This too is a lie, by the agreement of those versed in the subject. What is actually recorded in the authentic collection is the report of Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed us from the pulpit and said, ‘Allah gave a servant the choice between this world and the Hereafter, and that servant chose what is with Allah.’ Abū Bakr wept and said, ‘Rather, we would ransom you with our lives and our wealth.’”

Or words to that effect. The people began saying: “How astonishing that this old man should weep because the Messenger of Allah mentioned

---

## Page 599

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, a servant whom Allah gave the choice between this world and the Hereafter.” It was the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who had been given that choice, and Abū Bakr understood him best among us. Thus, Abū Bakr had the greatest understanding of what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, meant and intended by his words, although they all shared an understanding of them.

This is comparable to the report in the authentic collection that ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, was asked:

“Did the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, leave anything with you?”

In another wording:

“Did the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entrust you with anything concerning what he entrusts to people?”

He replied:

“No, by Him who split the seed and created the living soul, except an understanding of His Book that Allah grants a servant, and what is in this document. It contains provisions concerning blood money, the release of captives, and that a Muslim must not be killed in retaliation for an unbeliever.”

On the basis of this hadith and other authentic hadiths like it, scholars have established that all claims that ‘Alī and the Prophet’s household possessed knowledge imparted exclusively to them by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, are lies attributed to them. Examples include the claims concerning al-Jafr, al-Bitāqa, al-Jadwal, and other such things, as well as the accounts that the esoteric Qarmatians transmit from them. Indeed, more lies have been attributed to Ja‘far al-Sādiq, may Allah be pleased with him, than to anyone else. Lies have likewise been attributed to ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, and other imams of the Prophet’s household, may Allah be pleased with them, as has been explained and discussed at length elsewhere.

Similarly, some ascetics and claimants to knowledge of spiritual realities fabricate accounts about Abū Bakr and others, claiming that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would address him with truths that ‘Umar understood while present. They may then claim to have come to know those truths themselves, whereas their actual substance is heresy and unbelief.

---

## Page 600

And many of these heretics and ignorant people may support such claims by citing the report of Abū Hurayra:

“I retained two bags of knowledge from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. One I have disseminated among you; as for the other, if I were to disseminate it, you would cut this throat.”

This hadith is authentic. However, the other bag contained nothing of religious knowledge, knowledge of Allah, or affirmation of His oneness that was reserved for His close servants. Nor was Abū Hurayra among the foremost Companions who would have been singled out for such knowledge—if it were something imparted exclusively to anyone. Rather, that bag contained reports about the strife that would occur among Muslims. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had told them of the strife that would arise among Muslims and the battles that would take place between them and the unbelievers.

Thus, when ‘Uthmān was killed, the strife involving Ibn al-Zubayr occurred, and other such events took place, Ibn ‘Umar said:

“If Abū Hurayra had told you that you would kill your caliph, demolish the House, and do other such things, you would have said, ‘Abū Hurayra has lied.’”

Abū Hurayra therefore refrained from relating reports about these tribulations before they occurred, because neither the leaders nor the common people could bear to hear such things.

Likewise, they may cite the report of Hudhayfa ibn al-Yamān and his being the keeper of a secret known to no one else. Hudhayfa’s report is well known, but the secret known to him alone was the identity of the hypocrites who had been present during the expedition to Tabūk.

It is said that they had plotted to assassinate the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, so Allah revealed their affair to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who then informed Hudhayfa of their identities. For this reason, ‘Umar would offer the funeral prayer only for those for whom Hudhayfa offered it, because offering the funeral prayer for hypocrites is prohibited. It is also established in the authentic collection, on the authority of Hudhayfa, that when he spoke of the tribulations and of his being the most knowledgeable about them,

---

## Page 601
