“O you who blame me, you forbid me and command me;
Yet inward feeling is truer in its prohibitions and commands.

---

## Page 642

If I obey you and defy inward feeling, I turn away, blind,
From direct vision to the illusions of reports.
The very thing you call me to, when you examine it closely,
You will see to be what is forbidden, O my neighbor.”

Abraham also said to his father:

“O my father, do not worship Satan. Indeed, Satan has ever been disobedient to the Most Merciful.”

Yet according to them, Satan is a divine manifestation who ought to be revered; whoever worships him worships none other than Allah. Satan is not distinct from the Most Merciful, such that we should disobey him. He, glorified be He, has said:

“Did I not enjoin upon you, O children of Adam, that you should not worship Satan? Indeed, he is a clear enemy to you.”

“And that you should worship Me? This is a straight path.”

Up to His words:

“Use reason.”

Thus, He forbade them to worship Satan and commanded them to worship Allah alone, glorified be He. Yet according to them, worshipping Satan is also worshipping Him. It would therefore follow that Satan and all existing things should be worshipped, since they are His very essence.

Allah Most High also relates concerning the leader of all creation, the close friend of the Most Merciful, that when he

“saw a star, he said, ‘This is my Lord.’ But when it set, he said, ‘I do not love those that set.’”

“Then when he saw the moon rising, he said, ‘This is my Lord.’ But when it set, he said, ‘Unless my Lord guides me, I will surely be among those who go astray.’”

“Then when he saw the sun rising, he said, ‘This is my Lord; this is greater.’ But when it set, he said, ‘O my people, I am free of whatever you associate with Allah.’”

“Indeed, I have turned my face”—up to His words—“and they are rightly guided.”

He also said:

“You have indeed had an excellent example in Abraham and those with him, when they said to their people, ‘We are free of you’”—up to His words—“‘until you believe in Allah alone.’”

Allah Most High said:

“And when Abraham said to his father and his people, ‘Indeed, I am free of what you worship.’”

“‘Except the One who created me.’”

The verse. Allah Most High also said:

“Have you considered what you have been worshipping,”

“you and your forefathers?”

—up to His words—

“When we equated you with the Lord of all worlds.”

---

## Page 643

And Allah Most High said:

“When he said to his father and his people, ‘What are these statues to which you are devoted?’”

—up to His words—

“They said, ‘Burn him and support your gods—if you are going to act.’”

This, then, is Allah’s close friend, whom Allah made the leader of those leaders who are guided by His command—the prophets and messengers after him, and all other believers. He said:

“Indeed, I am free of whatever you associate with Allah.”

“Indeed, I have turned my face toward the One who created the heavens and the earth, inclining to the truth.”

Yet according to these heretics, whatever they associated with Allah is the Real Himself, not something other than Him. How, then, could Abraham disavow Allah, toward whom he turned his face? Their premise necessarily entails one of two alternatives: either he worships Him in every manifestation, without restriction or limitation—which, according to them, is the state of the perfected person—and thus disavows nothing; or he worships Him in only some manifestations, as those whom they consider deficient do.

As for disavowing some existing things, he has said that if Noah’s people had abandoned their idols, they would have abandoned as much of the Real as they abandoned of those idols. The messengers, however, did disavow the idols. It therefore follows that the messengers abandoned a great deal of the Real and disavowed Allah, to whom they called creation. According to their claim, the polytheists are thus better off than the messengers: the polytheists worshipped Him in some manifestations without disavowing the rest, whereas the messengers disavowed Him in most manifestations.

Furthermore, Abraham’s words:

“I have turned my face toward the One who created the heavens and the earth”

are false on their premise, since He did not create them: they are not other than Him. How aptly, then, His words apply to them:

“Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scripture, who believe in superstition and false objects of worship?”

---

## Page 644

—and the rest of the verse.

Then there are the words of Allah’s close friend:

“How can I fear what you have associated with Allah, while you do not fear that you have associated others with Allah?”

—and the rest of the verse.

This is the argument Allah gave Abraham against his people when he said: “How can I fear what you have worshipped besides Allah?” These are created beings worshipped besides Him. According to them, however, they are not worshipped besides Him, and whoever does not fear them does not fear Allah. Thus, the messengers did not fear Allah.

The words of Allah’s close friend:

“You have associated with Allah that for which He has sent down to you no authority”

cannot be true according to them, for they have associated nothing with Allah: there is nothing other than Him that they could associate with Him. Rather, the object they worshipped is Allah. At most, they worshipped Him in some manifestations; this does not mean that they made something other than Him a partner with Him in worship.

And His words:

“Those who believe and do not mix their faith with wrongdoing—it is they who will have security, and they are rightly guided.”

It is reported in the two Sahīh collections that ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“When this verse was revealed, it weighed heavily upon the Prophet’s Companions, and they said, ‘Which of us has not wronged himself?’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Have you not heard the words of the righteous servant: “Do not associate anything with Allah. Indeed, associating others with Him is a tremendous wrong”?’”

Allah and His Messenger have thus declared that associating others with Allah is a tremendous wrong, and that security belongs to those who believe in Allah and do not mix their faith with such association. Yet according to these heretics, the faith of those who mix their faith with associating others with Allah is complete and perfect faith: it is the faith of the consummate knower, as they see it. For someone who believes in Allah in all His manifestations and worships Him in every existing thing is more perfect than someone who does not believe in Him where He has not manifested Himself, and worships Him only where He is neither witnessed nor known. According to them, He cannot conceivably exist except in what is created. Thus, whoever does not worship Him in anything

---

## Page 645

among created beings at all has not, in reality, worshipped Him at all. When they say that he has worshipped Him, it is an expression without meaning. That is, if they explain it in terms of singling out, this means that he singled out certain manifestations for worship. In their view, this is a deficiency—not because of what he associated with Allah and worshipped, but because of what he left out. Thus, according to them, associating others with Allah involves no wrongdoing or deficiency except insofar as it is limited; when it is all-embracing, it is more complete and better.

Likewise, consider the words of Allah’s close friend to his people:

“We disown you and whatever you worship besides Allah.”

According to them, he disowned the Real who manifested Himself in them and in their gods. Likewise, they regard his rejection of it and his hostility towards them as rejection of the Real and hostility towards Him.

Then his words:

“Until you believe in Allah alone”

have no meaning according to them, for his people already believed in Allah alone, since they consider the existence of anything other than Him inconceivable. At most, they worshipped Him in some manifestations and left others out, without rejecting Him in those other manifestations.

Likewise, everything else Allah relates about Abraham’s hostility towards what those people worshipped amounts, according to them, to hostility towards Allah. For, as the heretics claim, the worship was directed to none other than Allah. They cite His words as proof:

“Your Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him.”

They say, “Whatever Allah decrees necessarily comes to pass.” This is a perversion of Allah’s verses, a distortion of words from their proper places, and a lie against Allah. For, by the consensus of Muslims—indeed, of all rational people—“decreed” here does not mean predestination and bringing things into being, such that one could say, “Whatever Allah predestines necessarily comes to pass.” Rather, it means “commanded,” and what Allah commands may or may not be done. Reflect, then, on this distortion.

---

## Page 646

Likewise, his statement, “Whatever Allah decrees necessarily comes to pass,” is ambiguous. [For a decree can mean a religious command—that is, the rulings of Islamic law—as in His words:

“O you who believe, fulfil your contracts. Grazing livestock have been made lawful for you,”

and the rest of the verse.] (*)

And His words:

“And who is better than Allah in judgment?”

And His words:

“That is Allah’s judgment; He judges between you.”

A judgment can also be a judgment in truth, involving bringing things into being and action, as in His words:

“I will never leave this land until my father permits me or Allah judges in my favour.”

And His words:

“He said, ‘My Lord, judge in truth.’”

For this reason, some of the early generations used to recite: “And your Lord has enjoined that you worship none but Him.” Thaʿlab related this from Ibn ʿAbbās, and they mentioned that this wording appeared in some copies of the Quran. This is also why He says, in the course of the passage:

“And show kindness to parents,”

and the rest of the verse. He continues setting out His commands and injunctions until He says:

“That is part of the wisdom your Lord has revealed to you. And do not set up another god alongside Allah, lest you be cast into Hell, blamed and banished.”

Thus, He closes the passage as He began it: by commanding that He alone be worshipped and forbidding the association of others with Him. This is not a statement that no one has worshipped anything but Allah, or that Allah predestined this and brought it into being. How could it be, when He says:

“And do not set up another god alongside Allah”?

Yet, according to them, nothing exists that could be made another god: whatever is worshipped is the very same deity, not another besides Him.

Similarly, Abraham and the believers were hostile to Allah—according to their claim—since Abraham was hostile to the worshippers and those worshipped. None but Allah was worshipped, and Allah worshipped none but Allah, for He is the very being of every worshipper and every object of worship. The same applies to the words of the Most High:

“Do not take My enemy and your enemy as allies

offering them affection.” According to their claim, Allah has no enemy at all, and there is nothing other than Him or besides Him whereby He could conceivably be an enemy to Himself or to the entities through which alone He manifests.

They hold that calling people to Allah is a deception practised upon them, as he expressly stated: “Calling someone to Allah is a deception practised upon that person, for there was no absence at the beginning to warrant a call to the ultimate goal.”

The author of al-Fusūs also said:

“‘And give good tidings to the humble’—those whose natural fire has died down, so that they said ‘a god’ rather than ‘nature.’

They are the first of the three groups, and He placed them before those who take a middle course and those who are foremost. ‘Except in error’—that is, except in bewilderment. “‘Whenever it lights up for them, they walk in it; and when darkness falls upon them, they stand still’—for Him. Thus, for the one who causes bewilderment, there is rotation and circular movement around the pole, from which he never departs. The one who follows an extended path deviates and moves away from the intended goal, seeking what he is already within. He pursues an imagined object towards which his journey is directed. He therefore has a ‘from,’ a ‘to,’ and what lies between them. But the one whose movement is circular has no beginning that would bind him to ‘from,’ nor an endpoint that would subject him to ‘to.’ His existence is therefore the most complete, and he is the one endowed with concise, comprehensive speech.” End quote.

## Page 648

And one of their poets said:

“Why do your pale camels never come to rest,
And how long will your shadow keep moving ceaselessly?

According to them, a person is his own ultimate goal and the object of his own worship. Beyond himself, there is nothing for him to worship, seek, call upon, or answer. Thus, their doctrine is, in essence, the doctrine of Pharaoh.

I used to tell those whom I addressed that their doctrine was, in essence, Pharaoh’s doctrine, until one of the trustworthy, knowledgeable people with whom I had discussed this matter told me that one of their leaders had invited him to embrace their doctrine and disclosed to him the true nature of their secret. I then said to him: “Praise be to Allah that they have admitted this, for once an opponent acknowledges the matter, no further evidence is needed.”

He has portrayed the one who follows an extended path as pursuing an imagined object, and has praised bewildered circular movement. Yet the Quran commands people to follow the straight path, praises it, and commends those who follow it—not the circular path. Thus, in the Mother of the Book:

“Guide us to the straight path.”

And He said:

“And this is My path, which is straight, so follow it; and do not follow other paths.”

This and the following verse.

## Page 650

That was because he imagined that he himself was Allah, and that there was no return or going back to Him beyond the state he was already in. Likewise, one of our companions related to me, through someone I know who has connections with these people, that the wicked al-Tilimsānī underwent a change and became agitated as death approached. The informant said:

“I said, ‘Glory be to Allah! Someone like you fears loss, when you take a spiritual seeker into seclusion and bring him to Allah in three days?’

Seventh:

These hypocritical heretics regard claims of divinity as valid, whether made by a human being such as Pharaoh or the awaited Dajjāl, or made on behalf of someone else. Such a person may be one of Allah’s friends—a prophet, such as the Messiah, or someone who is not a prophet, such as ʿAlī—or may not be one of Allah’s friends, such as al-Hākim in Egypt and others.

The author of al-Fusūs explicitly affirmed the validity of such claims, including Pharaoh’s claim. They frequently exalt Pharaoh, for no earlier leader of theirs in unbelief has equaled him, nor will any later one equal the one-eyed, lying Dajjāl. When they hypocritically profess faith before the believers, they say that Pharaoh died a believer and will not enter the Fire. They also say, “There is nothing in the Quran indicating that he will enter the Fire.”

---

## Page 651

As for what they truly believe, in their view he had always been one who knows Allah; indeed, they hold that he is Allah. Nor, in their view, is there any Fire involving pain whatsoever, as we shall explain concerning them, Allah willing. This should, however, alert us to the fact that religious innovations are places where hypocrisy is to be expected, just as observances of the Sunnah are emblems of faith.

The author of al-Fusūs, in the chapter on the wisdom contained in “The Word of Moses,” said, when discussing the words “What is the Lord of the worlds?”:

“Herein lies a great secret: he answered in terms of action someone who had asked for an essential definition. He thus made the essential definition identical with His relation to the forms of the world that appear through Him, or the forms of the world that appear within Him. It is as though, in answer to the question ‘What is the Lord of the worlds?’, he said: the One in whom the forms of the worlds appear—above, namely heaven, and below, namely earth—‘if you have certainty’; or the One who manifests through them.

When Pharaoh said to his companions, ‘He is indeed mad’—which, as we have explained concerning his being mad, means that knowledge of what I asked him about is veiled from him, since it is inconceivable that he should know it at all—Moses explained further, to show Pharaoh his own rank in divine knowledge, for he knew that Pharaoh understood this. He said:

‘Lord of the east and the west.’

Thus he invoked what appears and what is concealed: the Manifest and the Hidden.

‘And whatever is between them.’

This is expressed in His words:

‘He has knowledge of all things.’

‘If you use reason’—that is, if you are those who set bounds, for the intellect serves to set bounds.

The first answer is the answer addressed to those who possess certainty, who are the people of unveiling and wujūd. He therefore said to him, ‘If you have certainty’—that is, if you are people of unveiling and wujūd, then I have informed you of what you have ascertained through your unveiling and wujūd.

---

## Page 652

If you are not of this class, then I have given you the second answer, if you are people of reason and delimitation and confine the Real to what the proofs of your reason establish. Moses thus presented both aspects to make Pharaoh aware of his excellence and truthfulness. Moses knew that Pharaoh had understood this, or would understand it, because he had asked about the essence. He therefore knew that Pharaoh’s question did not follow the ancients’ convention of inquiry, and for that reason he answered. Had he understood him otherwise, he would have declared his question mistaken.

When Moses identified the object of the question with the world itself, Pharaoh addressed him in these terms, without the people realizing it. He said to him:

‘If you take a god other than me, I will certainly make you one of the imprisoned.’

The sīn in sijn (imprisonment) is an added letter; the meaning is: ‘I shall conceal you, for your answer has supported me in making such a statement. If you say to me in the language of allusion, “Pharaoh, you have shown ignorance by threatening me, for the reality is one—how, then, have you distinguished between us?”’ Pharaoh replies: ‘It is the ranks alone that have differentiated the reality; the reality itself has neither become separate nor been divided in its essence. My rank now is to exercise actual power over you, Moses. I am you in reality, but I am other than you in rank.’”

He continued until he said:

“Since Pharaoh occupied the seat of rule, held authority in his time, was the ruler by the sword, and acted within the established normative order, he said:

‘I am your lord, the most high.’

That is: although all are lords in some respect, I am the highest among them by virtue of the outward power over you that I have been given.

When the sorcerers recognized the truth of what he had said to them, they did not deny it, but acknowledged it and said to him:

‘Decree whatever you will decree; you can decree only concerning this worldly life.’

Thus, dominion is yours.

## Page 653

and so his statement was true:

Although he was identical with the Real, the form belonged to Pharaoh. Thus he cut off hands and feet and crucified, with the very reality of truth in the form of falsehood, to attain ranks attainable only through that act. For causes cannot be set aside, since the fixed entities required them. ---

## Page 654

Section

Among the chief principles relied upon by these heretical proponents of the identity of Allah and creation, who claim spiritual realization and knowledge, is a saying they attribute to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. According to these heretical proponents of divine identity, he said:

“Allah existed, and nothing was with Him, and He is now as He was then.”

This addition—“and He is now as He was then”—is a lie fabricated against the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Hadith scholars agree that it is fabricated and invented. It appears in none of the hadith collections, large or small, nor has any scholar transmitted it through any chain of transmission, whether sound, weak, or unknown. Rather, this phrase was uttered by some later Jahmī speculative theologians. From them it was taken up by those who carried Jahmī doctrine to its ultimate conclusion—negation and heresy.

Those earlier theologians, however, might say: “Allah existed when there was neither place nor time, and He is now as He was then.” These people instead said: “Allah existed, and nothing was with Him, and He is now as He was then.”

Even Ibn Arabī, the most knowledgeable about Islam among them, acknowledged that this was not a statement of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. In his book *Mā lā budda lil-murīd minhu* (*What the Spiritual Aspirant Cannot Do Without*), he stated that it is likewise reported in the Sunnah:

“Allah existed, and nothing was with Him.”

He said: “The scholars added: ‘And He is now as He was then.’ Thus there did not accrue to Him

---

## Page 655

through His creation of the world any attribute that He did not possess when no world existed. Thus, while the world exists, hold the same belief in His transcendence that you hold when there is no world and nothing besides Him.”

This statement expresses the position of many speculative theologians within the Muslim community. Had he remained consistent in this position, his statement would have been of the same kind as those of others. But he contradicts himself. Hence the wicked al-Tilimsānī, leader of the proponents of divine identity, refutes him wherever he approaches the position of the Muslims, just as the Muslims refute him wherever he departs into the doctrine of identity between Allah and creation.

The actual hadith transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the one recorded by al-Bukhārī from Imrān ibn Husayn, who reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah existed, and nothing existed before Him. His Throne was upon the water, and He wrote everything in the Record, then created the heavens and the earth.”

By this heretical addition—“and He is now as He was then”—the speculative theologians who adopted Jahmī doctrine intended to deny the attributes with which Allah described Himself, such as His rising over the Throne, His descent to the lowest heaven, and others. They said: “In eternity past, He was not over the Throne, and He is now as He was then. Therefore, He cannot be over the Throne, since that would entail transition and change.”

The people of the Sunnah who affirm the divine attributes answer them with two well-known replies. The first is that what newly arises is a relation and relative attribution between Him and the Throne, comparable to His being with

---

## Page 656

others; Ibn Aqīl calls these “states.” The arising of new relations and relative attributions is accepted by everyone, Muslims and non-Muslims alike, since it entails neither change nor transformation.

(The second reply) is that, even if this entails a transition from one state to another and from one condition to another, it is comparable to His coming, His arrival, His descent, His speaking to Moses, His coming on the Day of Resurrection in a form, and similar matters established by the scriptural texts. These are affirmed by most adherents of the Sunnah and hadith, as well as many speculative theologians, and are a necessary implication of the positions of all the sects. We have discussed people’s disagreement over this in our treatise on the distinction between attributes and created things, and on attributes of action.

As for these Jahmīs who hold that Allah and creation are identical, they say:

“He is now as He was then: nothing other than Him exists with Him, just as He existed in eternity past with nothing alongside Him.”

They say: “Since created beings are neither other than Him nor distinct from Him, there is only Him. Thus, nothing else exists with Him, either in eternity past or ever thereafter. Rather, He is identical with all existing things and is Himself all created beings.” They make the things created and fashioned identical with the Creator, the Originator, the Fashioner.

They incessantly repeat this phrase in their ravings:

“And He is now as He was then.”

They esteem it more highly than “Say, ‘He is Allah, the One,’” and the Verse of the Throne, because it indicates the identity of Allah and creation that constitutes their heresy. They believe that it is authentically transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that it is among his words, and that it expresses one of the secrets of his knowledge. Yet we have shown that it is a lie fabricated against the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: he never said it, no scholar transmitted it, and it is not found in any of the hadith collections;

---

## Page 657

rather, those versed in hadith agree that it is fabricated. This addition is not transmitted from any imam recognized by the Muslim community as a leading authority. It originates only with those known for some degree of Jahmī thought and denial of certain divine attributes. The wording known to hadith scholars and recorded by the compilers of the authentic collections is:

“Allah existed, and nothing existed with Him. His Throne was upon the water, and He recorded everything in the Record.”

This negates only the existence of created things—the heavens and the earth, and the angels, humans, and jinn within them—not the existence of the Throne. For this reason, many earlier and later scholars held that the Throne preceded the Pen and the Tablet, citing this hadith as evidence. They understood his statement:

“The first thing Allah created was the Pen. He said to it, ‘Write.’ It asked, ‘What shall I write?’ He said, ‘Write whatever will occur until the Day of Resurrection.’”

as referring to the creation mentioned in His words:

“And it is He who created the heavens and the earth in six days, and His Throne was upon the water.”

This is comparable to the well-known hadith of Abū Razīn al-Uqaylī, recorded in the Musnad and Sunan collections. He asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“O Messenger of Allah, where was our Lord before He created His creation?”

He replied:

“He was in a cloud, with no air above it and no air below it. Then He created His Throne upon the water.”

Thus, the creation mentioned in this hadith did not include the cloud. Some have stated that this is the cloud mentioned in His words:

“Are they waiting for anything but that Allah should come to them in canopies of clouds?”

There are well-known reports concerning this.

---

## Page 658

The evidence that this statement—their claim, “He is now as He was before”—is false and contrary to the Quran, the Sunnah, consensus, and sound reasoning takes several forms:

(The first)

Allah has stated in several places in the Quran that He is with His servants, both generally and specifically. For example, He says:

“He is the One who created the heavens and the earth in six days, then rose over the Throne,”

continuing to His words:

“And He is with you wherever you are.”

He also says:

“There is no private conversation among three but He is their fourth,”

continuing to His words:

“Wherever they may be.”

And He says:

“Indeed, Allah is with those who fear Him and those who do good.”

In two places, He says:

“And Allah is with the patient.”

He also says:

“Indeed, I am with you both, hearing and seeing.”

“Do not grieve; indeed, Allah is with us.”

“And Allah said, ‘Indeed, I am with you.’”

“Indeed, my Lord is with me; He will guide me.”

When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, travelled, he would say:

“O Allah, You are the Companion on the journey and the Guardian of our families in our absence. O Allah, accompany us on our journey and look after our families in our absence.”

If created beings, whether considered generally or specifically, were neither distinct from Him nor with Him—if, rather, nothing else were with Him—then it would be impossible for Him to be with His own self and essence. For being “with” entails two entities: one being with the other. Thus, when Allah states that He is with these people, the falsehood of their claim becomes clear: “He is now as He was before”—nothing is with Him; rather, He is identical with created beings.

Furthermore, being “with” necessarily applies to both sides, since it means association and companionship. If one thing is with another, it is impossible for the other not to be with it. It is therefore impossible for Allah to be with His creation while they have no existence with Him and no reality at all, but are instead identical with Him.

---

## Page 659

(The second)

Allah says in His Book:

“Do not set up another god alongside Allah, lest you be cast into Hell, blamed and rejected.”

He, the Most High, also says:

“So do not invoke another god alongside Allah, or you will be among those who are punished.”

And He says:

“Do not invoke another god alongside Allah. There is no god but He. Everything will perish except His Face.”

Thus, He forbade setting up or invoking another god alongside Him. He did not forbid affirming that a created being exists with Him, or saying that with Him there is a servant whom He owns, a needy being subject to His lordship, or something existent that He created. Likewise, He said:

“There is no god but He.”

He did not say, “Nothing exists but He,” “There is no ‘He’ but He,” or “Nothing is with Him except Himself,” in the sense that He is the very identity and essence of existing things.

Similarly, He said:

“Your God is one God.”

He thereby affirmed His oneness in divinity; He did not say that all existing things are one. The affirmation of oneness taught in Allah’s Book is therefore the affirmation of His exclusive divinity: that you neither set up nor invoke any other god alongside Him. How can this be equated with making existence itself identical with Him?

Furthermore, His prohibition against setting up or invoking another god alongside Him proves that this is possible, as was done by the polytheists who invoked other gods alongside Allah. If those gods were identical with Him—and nothing whatsoever were with Him—it would be impossible to invoke other gods alongside Him.

These texts therefore show that there are things with Him that are not gods, and that they must neither be made into gods nor claimed to be gods. Moreover, according to the heretics, anything may be worshipped and anything may be invoked, since worshipping anything other than Him is inconceivable: He is those very things.

---

## Page 660

A person may therefore invoke any of the gods worshipped besides Allah, yet, according to the heretics, he has not invoked another god alongside Him. He thus turns the very thing that Allah has forbidden and declared to be polytheism into the affirmation of His oneness; polytheism, in his view, is utterly inconceivable.

(The third argument)

When Allah existed and nothing was with Him, there was with Him neither heaven nor earth, neither sun nor moon, neither jinn nor human beings, neither animals nor trees, neither Paradise nor Hell, neither mountains nor seas. If He is now as He was then, it must follow that none of these entities exists with Him. This is a wilful denial of what is plainly visible and a rejection of the Quran and faith.

(The fourth argument)

Allah existed and nothing was with Him; then He wrote everything in the Record, as related in the authentic hadith. If nothing was with Him thereafter, what difference is there between the time of writing and the time before it, when, according to the Pharaoh-like heretics, He is identical with the writing and the Tablet?

---

## Page 661

Section

A group of these monists—who have deviated concerning Allah’s names and signs—claimed that Pharaoh was a believer and would not enter the Fire. They claimed that nothing in the Quran indicates that he will be punished; rather, it contains passages denying this, such as His words:

“Admit Pharaoh’s people into the severest punishment.”

They said: “Only his people were admitted, not Pharaoh himself.” And His words:

“He will lead his people on the Day of Resurrection and bring them into the Fire.”

They said: “He merely brought them into it; he did not enter it himself.” They also said: “Moreover, he believed that there is no god except the One in whom the Children of Israel believed, and Gabriel’s putting mud into his mouth does not negate the faith in his heart.”

This assertion constitutes disbelief, and its falsity is self-evident in the religion of Islam. To my knowledge, no one among those who pray toward the qiblah had advanced it before Ibn ‘Arabī—not even any Jew or Christian. Rather, the adherents of all religions unanimously affirm Pharaoh’s disbelief. This is so clear to scholars and ordinary people alike that it needs no proof, for no one has rivalled Pharaoh in disbelief in Allah and in claiming lordship and divinity for himself. For this reason, Allah has repeated his story in several places in the Quran, for these narratives are but parables

---

## Page 662

set forth to point to faith. No disbeliever’s disbelief is greater than his, and the Quran establishes his disbelief and his punishment in the Hereafter in several passages:

(First: His words, Most High, in Sūrat al-Qasas:

“These are two proofs from your Lord to Pharaoh and his chiefs. Indeed, they are a defiantly disobedient people.”

The passage continues as far as His words:

“We caused a curse to follow them in this world, and on the Day of Resurrection they will be among the rejected.”

He, glorified be He, states that He sent Moses to Pharaoh and his people, and that they were a defiantly disobedient people. He states that they said:

“This is nothing but fabricated magic.”

He states that Pharaoh said:

“I know of no god for you other than myself.”

He also states that Pharaoh ordered a lofty tower to be built so that he might look upon the God of Moses, and that he thought Moses was a liar. He states that Pharaoh and his soldiers were arrogant and thought that they would not return to Allah; that He seized Pharaoh and his soldiers and cast them into the sea—so consider the fate of the wrongdoers; that He made them leaders who are called to the Fire and who will not be helped on the Day of Resurrection; and that He caused a curse to follow them in this world, while on the Day of Resurrection they will be among the rejected.

This explicitly establishes that Pharaoh was among those who were defiantly disobedient, who rejected Moses, who committed wrongdoing, who called others to the Fire, who were cursed in this world after they drowned, and who will be rejected in the abode of the Hereafter.

It explicitly establishes that Pharaoh is cursed after his drowning and will be rejected in the Hereafter, with no one to help him. This describes the utmost punishment and accords with the second passage, in Sūrat al-Mu’min, namely His words:

“And a terrible punishment engulfed Pharaoh’s people.”

---

## Page 663

“the Fire, to which they are exposed morning and evening. And on the Day the Hour arrives: ‘Admit Pharaoh’s people to the severest punishment.’”

This states that Pharaoh and his people were engulfed by a terrible punishment in the intermediate state, and that at the Resurrection they will enter the severest punishment. This verse is among those the scholars have cited as evidence for punishment in the intermediate state.

These ignorant people fell into confusion only because, when they heard “Pharaoh’s people,” they supposed that Pharaoh himself was excluded. This is a distortion of words from their proper meanings. Rather, Pharaoh is included among Pharaoh’s people, without any disagreement among those knowledgeable about the Quran and the Arabic language. This is evident in the following ways:

(The first of the two)

In the Quran and Sunnah, the expression “the family or people of so-and-so” includes that person himself. For example, Allah relates the words of the angels who were Abraham’s guests:

“We have been sent to a criminal people, except the family of Lot; we will surely save them all, except his wife.”

Then He says:

“When the messengers came to the family of Lot, he said”—meaning Lot—“You are people unknown to me.”

Likewise, He says:

“We sent against them a storm of stones, except the family of Lot; We saved them before dawn.”

Then He says further on:

“And warnings certainly came to Pharaoh’s people. They denied all Our signs, so We seized them with the seizure of One Almighty and All-Powerful.”

It is well known that Lot is included among the family of Lot in these passages. Likewise, Pharaoh is included among Pharaoh’s people, who denied the signs and were seized. Another example is the statement of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Say: ‘O Allah, send blessings upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, as You sent blessings upon the family of Abraham.’”

---

## Page 664

and likewise his words:

“as You blessed the family of Abraham.”

Abraham himself is included in this. Likewise, he said to al-Hasan:

“Charity is not lawful for the family of Muhammad.”

The authentic hadith collection relates that ‘Abdullāh ibn Abī Awfā said:

“When people brought charity to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he would invoke blessings upon them. My father brought charity, and he said: ‘O Allah, send blessings upon the family of Abū Awfā.’”

Abū Awfā himself was the one who brought the charity.

A similar expression is “the household,” for a man is included among the members of his own household. An example is the angels’ statement:

“May Allah’s mercy and blessings be upon you, members of the household.”

Another is the statement of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Salmān is one of us, the members of the household.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Allah only intends to remove impurity from you, members of the household.”

This is because a man’s family comprises those who belong to him, and he himself is among those who belong to him. The members of his household are those who belong with him, and he in turn belongs with the members of his household.

It has thus become clear that the verse they supposed to be evidence in their favour is actually evidence against them: Pharaoh is punished along with the rest of Pharaoh’s people, both in the intermediate state and on the Day of Resurrection. This is further demonstrated by the fact that the entire narrative concerns Pharaoh and his people. Allah Most High says:

“We certainly sent Moses with Our signs and clear authority to Pharaoh, Hāmān and Qārūn, but they said: ‘A lying sorcerer!’”

The passage continues to His words:

“Pharaoh said: ‘I show you only what I see, and I guide you only to the path of right guidance.’”

And further to His words:

“Pharaoh said: ‘O Hāmān, build me a lofty tower so that I may reach the ways—the ways of the heavens—and look upon the God of Moses.’”

And further to His words:

“And Pharaoh’s people were engulfed by a terrible punishment.”

“The Fire

---

## Page 665

—they are exposed to it morning and evening.” The passage continues to His words:

“Those who were arrogant will say: ‘We are all in it. Allah has already judged between His servants.’”

Immediately after His words:

“Admit Pharaoh’s people to the severest punishment,”

He describes their disputing in the Fire, what the weak say to those who were arrogant, and the arrogant ones’ reply to the weak:

“We are all in it.”

It is well known that Pharaoh is the foremost of the arrogant. He was the one who treated his people as fools, and they obeyed him. No one displayed arrogance like Pharaoh’s, so this description and judgment apply to him more fully than to anyone else among his people.

(The second passage)—which is evidence against them, not in their favour—is the statement of the Most High, exalted is He:

“They followed Pharaoh’s command, but Pharaoh’s command was not rightly guided.”

“He will precede his people on the Day of Resurrection and lead them into the Fire. Wretched is the watering place to which they are led!”

The passage continues to His words:

“Wretched is the gift bestowed!”

He states that Pharaoh precedes his people; He does not say that he drives them from behind. He also states that Pharaoh leads them into the Fire. Clearly, when someone leading the way brings those following him into the Fire, he is the first to enter it. Otherwise, he would not be leading them from the front, but driving them from behind. This is further clarified by His words:

“They were pursued by a curse in this world, and on the Day of Resurrection.”

Thus, it is clear that both he and they enter the Fire, and that they are all cursed in this world and the Hereafter. How fitting it would be for someone who argues in Pharaoh’s defence to share this fate! For a person will be with those whom he loves:

“Those who disbelieve are allies of one another.”

Furthermore, Allah Most High says:

“Why was there no town that believed and benefited from its faith, except the people of Jonah when they believed?”

That is: Why did no people believe and benefit from their faith, except the people of Jonah?

---

## Page 666

Allah Most High also says:

“Have they not travelled through the land and seen how those before them met their end? They were more numerous than them, mightier in strength, and left greater traces upon the land.”

The passage continues to His words:

“Such is Allah’s established way with His servants. And there the disbelievers lost.”

He thus tells us that the nations who rejected the messengers believed when they saw the punishment, but that their faith did not benefit them at that point. This is Allah’s established way with His servants.

This agrees with what Allah says to Pharaoh:

“Now, when you had disobeyed before and were one of the corrupters?”

This address is a rhetorical question expressing rejection: “Do you believe now, when you had disobeyed before?” He thereby denies that this faith could be beneficial or accepted. Whoever says that it was beneficial and accepted has contradicted the explicit text of the Quran and Allah’s established way with His servants.

This is further clarified by the fact that, had his faith been accepted at that point, it would have spared him the punishment, just as it spared the people of Jonah. When their faith was accepted, they were allowed to enjoy life for a time. His drowning was a punishment for his disbelief; if he were not a disbeliever, he would not deserve punishment.

His words after this:

“Today We will save your body so that you may be a sign for those who come after you,”

call upon those who come after him to take heed. Had he died a believer, the destruction and drowning of a believer would not have served as such a warning.

Furthermore, when Ibn Masʿūd told the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that Abū Jahl had been killed, he said:

“This is the Pharaoh of this nation.”

Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, likened the leader of the disbelievers who rejected him to the leader of the disbelievers who rejected Moses.

---

## Page 667

This shows that he represents the utmost extreme of disbelief. How, then, could he have died a believer?

It is well known that someone who dies a believer may not be branded or described as a disbeliever, for Islam wipes away what came before it. In the Musnad collections of Ahmad and Ishāq and the Sahīh of Abū Hātim, it is reported from ‘Awf ibn Mālik, from ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said concerning one who abandons prayer:

“He will come with Korah, Pharaoh, Haman, and Ubayy ibn Khalaf.”

---

## Page 668

The shaykh, the devout imam, Shaykh al-Islām, the ocean of knowledge, the imam of imams, the defender of the Sunnah, the preeminent scholar of mankind, the heir of the prophets, Abū al-‘Abbās Ahmad ibn ‘Abd al-Halīm ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked about statements found in the handwriting of a trustworthy person and reported on his authority by a number of people, including some who professed adherence to religion.

Among these were the following:

One of the predecessors said: “Allah made His essence subtle and called it the Real; He unveiled it and called it creation.”

Shaykh Najm al-Dīn ibn Isrā’īl said: “Allah manifested Himself in things in reality and concealed Himself through them metaphorically. Those who belong to the people of reality and unity behold them as sites of manifestation and disclosure; those who belong to the people of metaphor and differentiation behold them as screens and veils.”

He related: “He also said in a poem of his:

Indeed, it is right for me to renounce existence and its inhabitants …
For both my hands together have clung to my Maker.”

---

## Page 669

Then, after some time, he changed the verse to:

“Indeed, it is right for me to love existence and its inhabitants.”

I asked him about this, and he replied:

“The initial station is to see created things as veils and thus renounce them. Then one sees them as sites of manifestation and disclosure, and so it becomes right to love them, as one poet said:

‘I kiss the ground where her beauty passed …
How much more the house where her beauty moved!’”

He related: Ibn ‘Arabī said, after reciting these two verses by Abū Nuwās:

“The glass grew delicate, the wine grew clear …
Their likeness made the matter indistinct.
As though there were wine but no cup …
As though there were a cup but no wine.”

“He clothed Himself in the form of the world; its outward aspect is His creation, and its inward aspect is His reality.”

One of the predecessors said:

“The very thing you see is an essence you do not see, and the essence you do not see is the very thing you see. Allah alone; multiplicity is an illusion.”

Shaykh Qutb al-Dīn ibn Sab‘īn said:

“A sovereign Lord and a perishing servant—and you are that. Allah alone; multiplicity is an illusion.”

Shaykh Muhyī al-Dīn ibn ‘Arabī said:

“O human form, whose secret is My meaning …
You would not, you see, have been created for the command, were it not for Me.
We willed you and brought you into being as a human creature …
That you might behold Us in the most perfect of things.”

---

## Page 670

It also contains the following:

One of the shaykhs’ sons asked his father to let him perform Hajj. The shaykh said to him: “My son, circumambulate a house that Allah has never left, even for the blink of an eye.”

He related:

It was reported of Rābi‘a al-‘Adawiyya that she performed Hajj and said: “This is the idol worshipped on earth. By Allah, Allah has neither entered it nor has it ever been empty of Him.”

It also contains these verses by al-Hallāj:

“Glory to Him whose human nature was revealed …
By the mystery of His piercing divine radiance.
Then He appeared, concealed yet manifest …
In the form of one who eats and drinks.”

He related these further verses by him:

“People have formed beliefs concerning God …
And I have embraced all that they believed.”

And these also:

“Between me and You, my selfhood crowds me …
By Your right, remove my selfhood from between us.”

He related:

Shaykh Shihāb al-Dīn al-Suhrawardī of Aleppo, who was put to death, said: “It was through this selfhood, whose removal al-Hallāj requested, that others gained power to shed his blood. Hence the predecessors said: ‘Al-Hallāj is half a man.’ His selfhood was not removed in substance, so it was removed in form.”

It also contains these verses by Muhyī al-Dīn ibn ‘Arabī:

“By Allah, it is nothing but bewilderment made manifest …
It swore by me, yet the one who swears is Allah.”

He also said therein:

It is reported that Jesus, peace be upon him, said: “Indeed, Allah—blessed

---

## Page 671

and exalted—longed to see His holy Essence, so He created Adam, peace be upon him, from His light and made him like a mirror in which He beholds His holy Essence. I am that light, and Adam is the mirror.”

Ibn al-Fārid said in his poem al-Sulūk (The Spiritual Journey):

“When you behold yourself, observe whom you see …
Beyond dispute, in the polished mirror.
Is it another who appears there, or are you looking …
At yourself within it as the rays reflect?”

He related:

Ibn Isrā’īl said: “Commands are of two kinds: a command conveyed through an intermediary and a command without an intermediary. The command conveyed through intermediaries is rejected by whomever Allah wills and accepted by whomever Allah wills. The command without an intermediary cannot be rejected. This is His statement, exalted is He: ‘Our word to a thing, when We intend it, is only that We say to it, “Be,” and it is.’”

A mendicant said to him: “Allah told Adam, without an intermediary, ‘Do not approach the tree’—yet he approached it and ate.”

He replied: “You are right. That is because Adam is a perfect man. Hence our shaykh, ‘Alī al-Harīrī, said: ‘Adam, Allah’s chosen one, affirmed divine oneness both outwardly and inwardly. His words to Adam, “Do not approach the tree,” were outward, while His command, “Eat,” was inward; so he ate. Thus it was with His words, exalted is He. Iblīs, however, affirmed divine oneness outwardly. He was commanded to prostrate to Adam, but regarded him as other and did not prostrate. So Allah changed his condition and said: “Get out of it.”’”

Someone said to my master: “My master Hasan, if Allah says to His Prophet, ‘You have no say in the matter,’ what, then, are we?”

My master replied: “It is not as you say or suppose. His words to him, ‘You have no say in the matter,’ are themselves an affirmation concerning the Prophet—may Allah bless

---

## Page 672

him and grant him peace—as in His words, exalted is He:

‘It was not you who threw when you threw, but Allah who threw.’

‘Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are pledging allegiance to Allah. Allah’s hand is above their hands.’”

It also contains these verses by Awhad al-Dīn al-Kirmānī:

“You have never been absent from my heart or my eyes;
There is no separation between you and us.”

Another poet said:

“Do not suppose that through prayer and fasting you attain
Closeness and nearness to Beauty and Majesty.
Leave behind your nature’s wrongdoing and become united
With Allah; otherwise, all your claims are impossible.”

And these verses are by al-Hallāj:

“When the ardent lover reaches perfection in love
And loses awareness of the One remembered under remembrance’s overpowering force,
He truly beholds, when love grants him vision,
That the prayer of those who know Allah is unbelief.”

Shaykh Najm al-Dīn ibn Isrā’īl wrote:

“The universe calls to you: ‘Do you not hear me?
Who gathered my scattered parts, and who dispersed me?
Look, and see in me a sight worthy of reflection:
Within me is nothing but the existence of Him who brought me into being.’”

He also wrote:

“The particles of the universe’s existence bear witness to the Real:
Nothing that exists has existence apart from the Real.
Though the universe’s constituents are many,
From Him it emerges, and to His sublimity it returns.”

---

## Page 673

He also wrote:

“Before You I renounce my words and deeds,
And my very self, as one seeking release.
I am nothing in the fabric of the universe,
For I am like a shifting shadow.”

Al-‘Afīf al-Tilimsānī wrote:

“I yearn for Him, yet He is my heart;
Has anyone seen another ardent lover yearning for his own heart?
My sight is veiled from Him, for He is my very eye;
He is distant only because He is exceedingly near.”

One of the predecessors said:

“The affirmation of divine oneness has no tongue, yet all tongues are its tongue.”

Another such statement is:

“Only the One knows the affirmation of divine oneness, and the One cannot properly be expressed in words, for He can only be expressed through something other than Himself. Whoever affirms an other has not affirmed oneness.”

He said:

“I heard Shaykh Muhammad ibn Bishr al-Nawāwī say: ‘Our master, Shaykh ‘Alī al-Harīrī, came to the congregational mosque of Nawā.’ Shaykh Muhammad said: ‘I went to him, kissed the ground before him, and sat down. He said: “My son, I devoted myself to love for a time, but found that it was not the intended goal, because love can only be from one being for another, and there is no other there. Then I devoted myself to affirming divine oneness for a time and found it likewise, because affirming divine oneness can only be from a servant to a Lord. If people were fair, they would see neither a servant nor one worshipped.”’”

It also contains:

“I heard Shaykh Najm al-Dīn ibn Isrā’īl confide to me that he had heard from

---

## Page 674

our shaykh, Shaykh ‘Alī al-Harīrī, in the year he died. He said: ‘O Najm, I saw the uvula at the roof of my mouth above the heavens and my jaw beneath the earths. My tongue uttered a word which, had it been heard from me, would have meant that not a drop of my blood reached the ground.’”

Some time later, someone said in the presence of my master, Shaykh Hasan ibn ‘Alī al-Harīrī:

“My master Hasan, Allah has created no one more foolish than those who claimed to be gods, such as Pharaoh, Nimrod, and their like.”

He replied: “Only the most ignorant of Allah’s creation or the most knowledgeable among them would make such a claim.”

I said to him: “You have spoken the truth, for I heard your grandfather say: ‘I saw such and such.’” He then recounted what Shaykh Najm al-Dīn had related from the shaykh.

It also contains a saying of one of the predecessors:

“Whoever is himself the very veil over himself—there is neither a veil nor anyone veiled.”

The respected scholars are therefore requested:

- To explain these statements: Are they true or false? By what can their meaning be understood? What establishes their truth or falsehood? Is it obligatory to reject them, affirm them, or defer to those who uttered them? Do they admit of an acceptable interpretation? What is the ruling concerning someone who believes in their meaning, whether knowing their true import or merely accepting, in general terms, the authority of whoever uttered them?

---

## Page 675

And did those who uttered these statements intend a sound meaning consistent with reason and transmitted religious texts? Can their problematic aspects be interpreted in a way that accords with that meaning? Is it obligatory to explain their meaning and uncover their underlying purport when there are people who believe in them without understanding their true import? Or should one remain silent on the matter and leave people to revere them and believe in them without knowing what they mean? Please explain this, and may you be rewarded.

---

## Page 676

He replied, may Allah be pleased with him:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds. The statements mentioned contain two false principles that contradict the religion of Muslims, Jews, and Christians, as well as transmitted revelation and reason.

(The first)

Divine indwelling and union, and related doctrines such as the unity of existence. Those who hold this view say that existence is one: the necessary existence of the Creator is the contingent existence of the created being. This is maintained by proponents of the unity of existence, such as Ibn ‘Arabī and his companion al-Qūnawī; Ibn Sab‘īn; Ibn al-Fārid, author of the poem rhyming in t—The Spiritual Path in Verse; and ‘Āmir al-Basrī al-Sīwāsī, who composed a poem comparable to that of Ibn al-Fārid. They also include al-Tilimsānī, who wrote a commentary on The Stations of al-Nifrī and a commentary on the Most Beautiful Names following the approach of these men; Sa‘īd al-Farghānī, who wrote a commentary on Ibn al-Fārid’s poem; al-Shushtarī, author of zajal poems (vernacular verse) and a disciple of Ibn Sab‘īn; ‘Abd Allah al-Balyānī; Ibn Abī al-Mansūr, the Egyptian Sufi, author of Unfastening the Buttons from the Necks of Mysteries; and others like them.

Some of these men distinguish between existence and subsistence—as Ibn ‘Arabī does—and claim

---

## Page 677

that entities subsist in nonexistence, independent of Allah in themselves; that the existence of the Real is their existence; and that the Creator depends upon these entities for His existence to become manifest through them, while they depend upon Him to acquire their existence, which is His very existence.

His position combines the assertion that what is nonexistent is nevertheless a thing with the assertion that the Creator’s existence is the existence of the created being. He further says that the existence that is created is the existence that creates, and the existence that creates is the existence that is created, as explained at length elsewhere.

Others distinguish between absoluteness and particularization, as al-Qūnawī and others like him do. They say that the Necessary Being is absolute existence without stipulating absoluteness. Yet existence in this absolute form is found only in minds, not in concrete reality: what is universal in the mind can exist in concrete reality only as something particular. If it is said that the absolute is part of the particular, it follows that the Creator’s existence is part of the created being’s existence. But a part cannot originate and create the whole; thus, the Creator would not exist.

Others say that the Maker is absolute existence subject to the condition of absoluteness, as Ibn Sīnā and his followers maintain. This position is even more unsound, for what is absolute subject to the condition of absoluteness exists only in minds, not in concrete reality. Thus, the position of these men, insofar as it agrees with those among them whose position entails denying the Creator’s existence, is worse than that of those who resemble the proponents of divine indwelling and union.

Still others regard necessary existence and contingent existence) as analogous to matter

---

## Page 678

and form as conceived by the philosophical thinkers, or something close to this, as maintained by Ibn Sabʿīn and others like him. Their views contain contradictions and errors, and amount to the unity of existence and divine indwelling or union. They affirm unrestricted divine indwelling, unrestricted unity, and unrestricted union, unlike those who affirm these concerning particular individuals, such as the Christians and the extremists (among the Shīʿa who claim divinity for ʿAlī, al-Hākim, al-Hallāj, Yūnus al-Qunīnī, or others for whom divinity has been claimed.

The latter may affirm a restricted, particular divine indwelling, whereas the former affirm it without restriction and universally. Hence they say that the Christians’ error lay only in confining it to a particular individual. Likewise, they say that the error of the polytheists who worship idols lay only in limiting themselves to some manifestations to the exclusion of others. They permit polytheism and idol worship without restriction, on an unrestricted and universal basis.

There is no doubt that the unbelief and misguidance contained in these people’s views exceed the unbelief of the Jews and Christians. This doctrine is widespread among many later thinkers, and groups among the Jahmiyya also professed it. The writings of Ibn ʿArabī in Fusūs al-Hikam (The Bezels of Wisdom) and elsewhere, and the writings of Ibn Sabʿīn

---

## Page 679

and his companion al-Shushtarī, Ibn al-Fārid’s poem (Nuzum al-Sulūk—Verses on the Spiritual Path), the poem of ‘Āmir al-Basrī, the writings of al-‘Afīf al-Tilimsānī, ‘Abd Allāh al-Balyānī and al-Sadr al-Qūnawī, much of Ibn Isrā’īl’s poetry, and what he transmits on this subject from his shaykh al-Harīrī—as well as similar statements found in the writings of many others—are based on this doctrine: divine indwelling, union and the unity of existence.

Many who follow the spiritual path without believing in this doctrine hear the poetry of Ibn al-Fārid and others without realizing that this doctrine is what he intends. Indeed, this subject has become so clouded by confusion and misguidance that it has bewildered many people.

The root of these people’s misguidance is that they did not recognize that Allah is separate from His creatures and above them. They knew that He exists, but assumed that His existence did not lie outside theirs. They are like someone who sees the rays of the sun and imagines them to be the sun itself.

When the Jahmiyya appeared, denying that Allah is separate from His creation and above it, people divided into four positions on this subject.

The early generations and the leading scholars maintain that Allah is above His heavens, established over His Throne and separate from His creation. This is indicated by the Quran, the Sunnah and the consensus of the early generations of this nation. His being separate from His creation and above it is also known through sound reason, which agrees with authentic transmitted revelation. Allah has likewise endowed His creatures with an innate disposition to acknowledge Him and turn toward Him—glorified and exalted is He.

---

## Page 680

(The second position)

This is the position of those among the Jahmiyya who deny and negate the divine attributes. They say that He is neither inside the world nor outside it, neither separate from it nor occupying the same place as it. Thus, they deny both opposing descriptions, one of which must apply to anything that exists. This is the view of most of the Muʿtazila and others who agree with them.

(The third position)

This is the position of those Jahmiyya who affirm divine indwelling, saying that He is present in His very Essence everywhere. This is the view of the Najjāriyya—the followers of Husayn al-Najjār—and other Jahmiyya. Those who advocate divine indwelling and union belong to this category. Belief in divine indwelling predominates among the Jahmiyya’s devotees, Sufis and ordinary followers, while negation and denial of the divine attributes predominate among their speculative thinkers and theologians. As has been said: “The theologians of the Jahmiyya worship nothing, while their Sufis worship everything.”

This is because worship involves seeking, directing oneself toward something, willing and loving; these cannot be directed toward something nonexistent. The heart seeks something that exists, so if it does not seek what is above the world, it seeks what is within it.

Theological discourse, knowledge and speculative inquiry, however, can concern both what exists and what does not. Thus, when theologians and speculative thinkers describe the Lord through negative attributes and denials—which describe only something nonexistent—mere knowledge and theological discourse do not rule out the nonexistence of the object of worship being described. Seeking, willing and worship, by contrast, are incompatible with the nonexistence of the object of worship.

This is why you find an individual among them inclined toward negation when engaged in speculation and investigation, but toward divine indwelling when engaged in worship and Sufi practice. When told, “This contradicts that,” he replies: “This is dictated by

---

## Page 681

my reason and speculative inquiry, whereas that is dictated by my spiritual experience and knowledge.” Yet it is clear that if spiritual experience and inward feeling do not accord with reason and speculative inquiry, then either both are unsound or one of them is.

(The fourth position)

This is the position of those who say that Allah, in His very Essence, is above the world and, in His very Essence, is everywhere. This is the position of certain groups among speculative theologians and Sufis, such as Abū Mu‘ādh and others like him. Al-Ash‘arī attributed this position to various groups in al-Maqālāt (The Doctrinal Positions). The writings of the Sālimiyya, such as Abū Tālib al-Makkī and his followers—including Abū al-Hakam ibn Barrajān and others like him—contain statements suggesting something along these lines, though their writings also contain statements contradicting it.

In general, many later Sufis have fallen into belief in divine indwelling or something akin to it. For this reason, the leading figures among the Sufis warned against it. Thus, when al-Junayd was asked about monotheism, he said:

“Monotheism is to distinguish what originates in time from what is eternal.”

He thus made clear that monotheism means distinguishing the eternal from that which is brought into existence. Ibn ‘Arabī, the author of al-Fusūs (The Bezels), objected to this statement, claiming that al-Junayd and others like him died without understanding monotheism because they affirmed a distinction between the Lord and the servant. This was based on his claim that monotheism admits no distinction between the Lord and the servant. He further alleged that only someone who is neither eternal nor brought into existence can distinguish between the eternal and that which is brought into existence.

This is ignorance. Knowing that one thing is not another, and distinguishing between the two, does not require the person who knows and distinguishes them to be neither of them. Rather, a person knows that he is not that other person, even though he is one of the two. How, then, could he fail to know that he is distinct from his Lord, even though he is one of the two?

---

## Page 682

(The second principle)

Invoking divine decree to justify sins, neglecting what is commanded, and doing what is forbidden

Belief in divine decree is obligatory, but it must not be invoked to justify contravening Allah’s commands and prohibitions or disregarding His promises and threats. Those who have gone astray concerning divine decree fall into three groups:

One group believes in the commands and prohibitions, the promises and threats, but denies divine decree, claiming that some events are not created by Allah. Such are the Mu‘tazila and others like them.

Another group believes in divine ordainment and decree, agreeing with Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah that whatever Allah wills happens, and whatever He does not will does not happen, and that He is the Creator, Lord, and Sovereign of everything. Yet they set this in opposition to the commands and prohibitions, calling it “reality” and treating it as contrary to the Sharia.

Some among them say that witnessing divine decree rules out blame and punishment, and that one who possesses spiritual knowledge regards both alike.

In this they contradict themselves and go against revealed law, reason, spiritual experience, and inward feeling. They do not treat those who do them good as equal to those who wrong them, nor do they equate the knowledgeable with the ignorant, the capable with the incapable, the good with the wicked, or the just with the unjust. Rather, they distinguish between them. Moreover, they make these distinctions according to their own desires and aims, not according to the commands and prohibitions. They adhere neither to divine decree nor to the divine command; rather, as

---

## Page 683

some scholars have said:

“When it comes to obedience, you are a Qadarī; when it comes to disobedience, you are a Jabrī. Whichever doctrine agrees with your desires, you adopt.”

Everyone who invokes divine decree to justify neglecting an obligation or committing what is forbidden contradicts himself: he does not accept it as an excuse when his own desires are opposed. Rather, he is hostile towards anyone who hurts him, even if that person is in the right, and loves anyone who supports his aims, even if that person is an enemy of Allah. His love and hatred, allegiance and enmity thus follow his desires, aims, spiritual experience, and inward feelings—not Allah’s commands and prohibitions, His love and hatred, or His friendship and enmity. For he cannot accept divine decree as an excuse for everyone. That would necessarily entail corruption incompatible with any good order, and evil containing no good. If everyone were permitted to invoke divine decree as an excuse, no aggressor would be punished, no retribution would be exacted from an oppressive transgressor, and no wronged person’s rights would be recovered from the one who wronged him. Everyone would do whatever he desired, with no one opposing him. The corruption this would entail is known only to the Lord of all His servants.

It is self-evident that actions fall into those that benefit people and those that harm them. Allah has sent His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to enjoin what is right upon the believers and forbid them what is wrong, to make wholesome things lawful for them and foul things unlawful. Whoever does not follow Allah’s law and religion follows their opposite: desires and religious innovations. His appeal to divine decree is then an instance of arguing with falsehood to refute the truth, not of relying upon divine decree. He would also have to regard everyone subject to divine decrees as excused.

---

## Page 684

And if he says:

“I excuse on the grounds of divine decree someone who witnesses it and knows that Allah creates his actions and moves him to act—not someone who does not witness this or who denies it.”

He is told:

“Then we ask you: are not this person’s witnessing and that person’s denial themselves part of divine decree? Does divine decree not encompass both? If this warrants distinguishing between them, even though divine decree encompasses them both, then you have deemed some people praiseworthy and others blameworthy despite divine decree encompassing both. This is a return to making distinctions and holding fast to commands and prohibitions. You have thus overturned your own principle and contradicted yourself. The same consequence follows for everyone who joins you in adopting it.”

Moreover, quite apart from this principle’s unsoundness and inconsistency, it is a false doctrine and a misleading religious innovation. Who has made belief in divine decree and witnessing it an excuse for neglecting obligations and committing forbidden acts?

Rather, belief in divine decree is one good deed among others, and this good deed cannot cancel out all sins. If someone associated partners with Allah and denied His Messenger, considering that this had been decreed for him, that would neither secure forgiveness for his denial nor prevent his punishment. For Allah does not forgive the association of partners with Him, whether the person doing so affirms divine decree and considers it, denies it, or is heedless of it. Iblīs said:

“Because You have put me in error, I will surely make [disobedience] attractive to them on earth, and I will mislead them all.”

He persisted and invoked divine decree as an excuse, thereby increasing his disbelief and bringing greater punishment upon himself.

Adam, peace be upon him, however, said:

“Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves, and if You do not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we will surely be among the losers.”

Allah Most High said:

“Then Adam received words from his Lord, and He accepted his repentance. Indeed, He

---

## Page 685

is the Accepting of Repentance, the Merciful.”

Whoever seeks forgiveness and repents follows Adam’s example and is blessed; whoever persists and invokes divine decree as an excuse follows Iblīs’s example and is wretched. Allah Most High said to Iblīs:

“I will surely fill Hell with you and those of them who follow you, all together.”

Many who delve into realities have gone astray on this point. They pursue various kinds of realities that they experience and taste, and invoke divine decree as an excuse for violating Allah’s commands. They thus resemble the polytheists who used to invent a religion that Allah had not prescribed and invoke divine decree to justify disobeying Him.

(The third group) of those who have gone astray concerning divine decree consists of those who dispute with the Lord over His combining divine ordainment and decree with commands and prohibitions—as in the accounts they give in Iblīs’s voice. These are Allah’s adversaries and enemies.

As for the people of faith, they believe in divine ordainment and decree, and in commands and prohibitions. They do what is commanded, refrain from what is forbidden, and patiently bear what is decreed. As Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, whoever fears Allah and is patient—Allah does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.”

Fearing Allah encompasses doing what is commanded and refraining from what is forbidden, while patience includes patiently bearing what is decreed. When a calamity strikes them on earth or in their own persons, they know that it is recorded in a Book, that whatever has befallen them could never have missed them, and that whatever has missed them could never have befallen them. They therefore submit the matter to Allah and patiently bear the trials He puts them through. But when Allah’s command comes, they hasten to good deeds and race toward

---

## Page 686

acts of obedience, and call upon their Lord with hope and fear. They avoid what He has forbidden, observe His limits, and seek Allah’s forgiveness and repent to Him for falling short in fulfilling His commands and transgressing His limits. They do so knowing that repentance is always obligatory upon His servants, and following the example of their Prophet, who says in an authentic hadith:

“O people, repent to your Lord! By Him in whose hand is my soul, I seek Allah’s forgiveness and repent to Him a hundred times a day.”

Another narration states:

“More than seventy times.”

The last surah revealed to him was:

“When Allah’s help comes and the conquest, and you see people entering Allah’s religion in multitudes, then glorify your Lord with praise and seek His forgiveness. Indeed, He is ever Accepting of Repentance.”

Once these two principles are understood, they provide the foundation for answering the statements contained in this question and identifying the errors that have entered into these matters.

The statement, “Allah made His essence subtle and called it ‘the Real,’ and unveiled it and called it ‘creation,’” is among the claims of those who advocate the oneness of existence, divine indwelling, and union, and it is false. If the subtle is identical to the dense, then the Real is creation, and there is neither a making subtle nor a making dense. If, however, the subtle is distinct from the dense, then the distinction between the Real and creation is established—and this is the truth. In that case, the Real cannot be creation. It is therefore inconceivable that the essence of the Real should be creation in any respect, just as the essence of a created being cannot be the essence of the Creator in any respect.

The same applies to the other person’s statement:

“He manifested Himself in them in reality, and was concealed from them metaphorically.”

If the One who manifests is distinct from the loci of His manifestation, then the distinction between the Lord and the servant is established. If neither is distinct from the other, then neither manifestation nor concealment is conceivable.

---

## Page 687

Then his statement:

“Whoever is among the people of the Real perceives them as loci of manifestation and disclosure, while whoever upholds distinction perceives them as screens and veils,”

is self-contradictory. For if existence were one, neither observer would be distinct from the other, nor would the observer be distinct from what he observes.

Thus, one of their shaykhs said:

“Whoever says that anything other than Allah exists in the universe has lied.”

Another asked him, “Then who is it that has lied?” This left him speechless.

For if nothing existed except the Being whose existence is necessary in Himself, it would be He who lies, commits injustice, eats, and drinks. Their leading figures state this explicitly. The author of al-Fusūs and others say that He is described by every blameworthy attribute, and that it is He who falls ill, strikes, suffers afflictions, and is described as having faults and deficiencies, just as it is He who is described by qualities of praise and blame.

He said:

“The One who is exalted in Himself is He who possesses all affirmative and negative attributes, whether considered praiseworthy by reason, religious law, and custom, or blameworthy by reason, religious law, and custom. This belongs exclusively to the One named Allah.”

He also said:

“Do you not see the Real manifesting with the attributes of originated things—as He has stated concerning Himself—and with the attributes of the self and blameworthy attributes? Do you not see the created being manifesting with the attributes of the Creator, all of which rightfully belong to it, just as the attributes of the created being rightfully belong to the Creator?”

The poet’s statement:

“Indeed, I am entitled to passionately love existence and all who inhabit it,”

implies that he passionately loves Iblīs, Pharaoh, Hāmān, and every unbeliever, and that he passionately loves dogs

---

## Page 688

and pigs, urine, excrement, and every foul thing. Besides being false according to both reason and religious law, this claim is a lie and contradicts his own conduct. For if someone harmed him and caused him severe pain, he would hate that person and become his enemy; indeed, he would go to excess in harming him. A man’s passionate love for everything that exists is therefore rationally impossible and religiously forbidden.

The statement he attributes to one of them:

“The very thing you see is an essence you do not see, and an essence you do not see is the very thing you see,”

comes from Ibn Sab‘īn, one of the foremost proponents of polytheism, heresy, sorcery, and union with God. He was among their most accomplished and intelligent figures, and among those most knowledgeable about philosophy and the Sufism of those who profess philosophy.

Ibn ‘Arabī’s statement:

“His outward aspect is His creation, and His inward aspect is the Real,”

expresses the doctrine of divine indwelling. Yet he contradicts himself here, since he advocates oneness of existence. There can therefore be no two beings, one inward and the other outward. The distinction between existence and the entity itself has no basis in reality; rather, it is one of the claims of those given to lies and falsehood.

Ibn Sab‘īn’s statement:

“A sovereign Lord and a perishing servant—and you are that; Allah alone, and multiplicity is an illusion,”

accords with his corrupt premise that the existence of the created being is the existence of the Creator. This is why he said, “And you are that.” He declared the servant to be perishing—that is, to have no existence—so that only the Lord’s existence remained. Hence his statement, “And you are that.” Likewise, he said, “Allah alone, and multiplicity is an illusion,” since, according to his doctrine, nothing exists except Allah.

This is why he and his followers used to say in their remembrance:

“There is nothing but Allah,”

instead of the Muslims’ declaration:

“There is no god but Allah.”

---

## Page 689
