And Shaykh Qutb al-Dīn ibn al-Qastallānī used to call them “al-Laysiyya,” saying, “Beware of these al-Laysiyya.” Hence Ibn Sab‘īn’s statement, “Multiplicity is an illusion.” This is contradictory, for his word “illusion” presupposes something imagined. If what is imagined is the illusion itself, then Allah would be the illusion. If what is imagined is other than the illusion, then existence has become multiple. Likewise, if what is imagined is Allah, then Allah has been described as a false illusion. Besides being disbelief, this contradicts his statement, “Existence is one.” If what is imagined is other than Him, then he has affirmed something other than Allah, contradicting his own premise. Once he affirms something other, multiplicity necessarily follows; multiplicity would therefore be a reality, not an illusion.

The two verses quoted from Ibn ‘Arabī, despite their contradictions, rest upon this premise. His words:

“O form of intimacy, whose secret is My inner reality,”

are spoken in the voice of the Real, addressing the human form: “O form of intimacy, whose secret is My inner reality.” That is, it is the form, and I am its inner reality. This implies that the inner reality is other than the form, entailing multiplicity and a distinction between the inner reality and the form. If the existence of the inner reality is the very existence of the form—as he explicitly states—then there is no multiplicity. But if the existence of the one differs from that of the other, then his words are contradictory.

And his statement:

“You would not have been created to obey the command, you see, were it not for Me”

---

## Page 690

is an ambiguous statement that could bear a sound meaning: without the Creator, those subject to religious obligations would not exist, nor would anyone have been created to obey Allah’s command. Yet it is known that he does not hold this view, and that what he means is oneness of existence, indwelling, and union. Hence his words:

“We willed you, then brought you forth as a human being,
That you might witness Us in the most perfect of things.”

He thus explains that the servants witness Him in the most perfect of things, namely the human form. This points to indwelling—the indwelling of the Real in creation—but his words are contradictory. He does not accept indwelling or affirm two existent beings, one dwelling within the other. Rather, according to him, the existence of the indwelling being is identical to the existence of its receptacle. Yet he affirms indwelling between fixed reality and existence: the existence of the Real dwells within the fixed reality of contingent things, and their fixed reality dwells within His existence. These words have no basis in reality, for there is no difference between the two. Nevertheless, this is his inherently contradictory doctrine.

As for the man who asked his father for permission to perform Hajj, whereupon his father instructed him to circumambulate the father himself, saying:

“Circumambulate a house that Allah has never left, even for the blink of an eye.”

This is disbelief by the consensus of Muslims. Circumambulating the Ancient House is something Allah and His Messenger have commanded, whereas circumambulating prophets and righteous people is forbidden by the consensus of Muslims. Whoever believes this to be part of religion is a disbeliever, whether he circumambulates the person’s body or his grave.

As for his words, “Allah has never left it, even for the blink of an eye”: if he means unrestricted, universal indwelling, then, besides being false, this is contradictory. There would then be no difference between the one circumambulating and the one being circumambulated, so circumambulation would not be

---

## Page 691

more appropriate for this one to circumambulate that one than the reverse. Indeed, this would entail circumambulating dogs, pigs, disbelievers, impurities, filth, every vile thing, and every accursed thing, since universal indwelling and union encompass all of these.

Their shaykh al-Shīrāzī once said to his own shaykh, al-Tilimsānī, on passing a dead, mangy dog:
“Is this, too, of Allah’s essence?”
He replied:
“Is there anything outside Him?”

Al-Tilimsānī was once passing a dog with another person, who kicked it. He said:
“Do not kick it, for it is of Him.”

Besides being among the gravest forms of disbelief and falsehood, invalid both rationally and religiously, this is contradictory: the one kicking and the one being kicked are one and the same, as are the one issuing the prohibition and the one addressed by it. Thus, none of these is more entitled to command and prohibit than any other. Multiplicity is inconceivable alongside oneness. If they speak of “loci of manifestation and self-disclosure,” the reply is: if these have an existence distinct from that of the being who manifests and discloses himself, then multiplicity is established and oneness is disproved. But if the existence of the one is the very existence of the other, no distinction remains between what manifests, what makes it manifest, and that in which it discloses itself.

If, by saying, “Allah has never left it, even for the blink of an eye,” he means a particular indwelling—as Christians maintain concerning the Messiah—then this indwelling must have been present in him from the moment he was created, as Christians maintain concerning the Messiah. It would therefore not have come about through his knowledge, worship, realization, or spiritual insight. In that case, there would be no difference between him and other human beings. Why, then, should indwelling be established in him rather than in others?

This is worse than the Christians’ claim, for Christians claimed this concerning the Messiah because he was created without a father. These shaykhs, however, were not distinguished in the manner of their creation; rather, they were distinguished only through worship, knowledge, realization, and affirmation of Allah’s oneness.

---

## Page 692

And this is something they acquired after previously lacking it. If this is the cause of indwelling, then indwelling in them must have begun later, rather than accompanied their creation. Their claim that the Lord has never departed from their bodies or hearts, even for the blink of an eye, is therefore false however it is understood.

As for the statement attributed to Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya concerning the House:

“It is the idol worshipped on earth,”

this is falsely attributed to Rābiʿa. Whoever said such a thing would be a disbeliever who must be called upon to repent; if he repents, so be it, otherwise he is to be put to death. The statement itself is false, for Muslims do not worship the House. Rather, they worship the Lord of the House by circumambulating it and praying towards it.

Likewise, the statement attributed to her:

“By Allah, Allah has never entered it, nor has it ever been devoid of Him,”

is falsely attributed to her. According to the doctrine of those who believe in indwelling, there is no difference in this respect between that House and any other. What distinction, then, warrants circumambulating it, praying towards it, and making pilgrimage to it rather than to other houses?

The statement, “Allah has not entered it,” is correct. As for the statement, “It has never been devoid of Him,” if he means that Allah’s essence indwells in it, or anything similar, this is false and contradicts his statement that Allah has not entered it. But if he means that union is inseparable from it, that no new entry into it has occurred, and that Allah has never indwelt it, then, besides being disbelief and falsehood, this entails that the House has no distinction over other houses, since, according to them, all existing things are likewise.

---

## Page 693

As for the two verses attributed to al-Hallāj:

“Glory to Him whose human nature was revealed
By the mystery of His divinity’s piercing radiance,
Until He appeared openly among His creation,
In the form of one who eats and drinks.”

In these verses he clearly expresses particular indwelling, as Christians teach concerning Christ. Abū Abd Allāh ibn Khafīf al-Shīrāzī used to defend al-Hallāj before discovering the truth about him. When he recited these two verses, he said:

“May Allah curse whoever said this.”

As for the statement that he also composed the following verse:

“People have formed beliefs concerning God;
I have embraced all that they believed.”

This verse is known as a verse by Ibn Arabī. If al-Hallāj had composed it before him and Ibn Arabī had quoted it, then attributing it to al-Hallāj is correct. It is, however, a contradictory and false statement. To combine contradictory beliefs is utterly unsound. Two propositions that contradict one another through negation and affirmation, such that the truth of one necessarily entails the falsity of the other, cannot both be true.

These people claim that spiritual unveiling establishes for them things that contradict plain reason. They maintain that both contradictories and contraries can hold together, and that whoever follows their path affirms what conflicts with reason and transmitted texts. There is no doubt that this is among the most unsound positions adopted by the sophists.

---

## Page 694

It is well known that the prophets, peace be upon them, are greater than Allah’s allies. The prophets brought matters that reason cannot discover, not matters that reason knows to be false. They therefore convey things that bewilder reason, not things that reason deems impossible. These heretics, however, claim that rational impossibilities are true, that two contradictory propositions can both be true, and that whatever conflicts with plain reason and authentic transmitted texts is true.

There is no doubt that they are given to fantasies and delusions. They imagine and conceive things within themselves, then suppose that these exist in external reality, although they are nothing but their own imaginings. False imagination can conceive things that have no reality.

For this reason, they say:

“The realm of reality is the realm of imagination,”

as Ibn Arabī and others say. They therefore relate a story mentioned by Sa‘īd al-Farghānī, the commentator on Ibn al-Fārid’s ode, who was one of their shaykhs.

As for his words:

“Between me and You, my selfhood presses upon me;
By Your right, remove my selfhood from between us.”

These words admit three interpretations: they may be uttered by a heretic, a zindīq, or a person of consummate truthfulness.

In the first interpretation, he means to ask that the actual existence of his selfhood be removed, so that it may be said that his existence is the existence of the True One, and his selfhood is the selfhood of the True One. Thus, he would not be described as distinct from Allah or as anything other than Him.

---

## Page 695

For this reason, the predecessors of these heretics said:

“Al-Hallāj is half a man.”

This was because his selfhood had not been effaced in reality, so it was effaced in outward form. They say that, since his selfhood had not ceased to exist in his actual spiritual perception, it was effaced in outward form, and he was killed.

Besides the unbelief and heresy it contains, this statement is self-contradictory: one part invalidates another. His words,

“Between me and You, my selfhood presses upon me,”

address someone other than himself and affirm a selfhood between him and his Lord. This affirms three things. Thus, when he says,

“By Your right, remove my selfhood from between us,”

he asks someone other than himself to remove his selfhood. This likewise affirms three things.

This false meaning is the corrupt form of fanā’ (annihilation): the effacement of the existence of all other than Allah. These words contain a request to remove selfhood—which is a request for annihilation.

Fanā’ takes three forms: the effacement of the existence of all other than Allah, the effacement of awareness of all other than Allah, and the abandonment of worship of all other than Allah.

The first is the annihilation advocated by the heretical adherents of the unity of existence, as in their interpretation of al-Hallāj’s words. It consists in treating all existence as one existence.

The second—the effacement of awareness of all other than Allah—is something that befalls many spiritual wayfarers, as is related of Abū Yazīd and others like him. This is the station of overwhelming absorption: in his absorption in the One whose existence he perceives, he loses awareness of his own existence; in the One he worships, of his own worship; in the One he witnesses, of his own witnessing; and in the One he remembers, of his own remembrance. Thus, the one who once did not exist passes away, while the One who has always existed remains. This is like the story related that

---

## Page 696

a man loved another. The beloved threw himself into the water, and the lover threw himself in after him. The beloved said:

“I fell in, but why did you fall in?”

He replied:

“I became so absorbed in you that I lost awareness of myself and thought that you were me.”

This is the state of someone who cannot perceive any created thing when his heart perceives the existence of the Creator. It is something that befalls a group of spiritual wayfarers. Some regard it as part of the spiritual path, while others regard it as its ultimate goal. They thus make annihilation in the recognition of Allah’s sole lordship the ultimate goal, no longer distinguishing between what is commanded and what is forbidden, or between what is loved and what is disliked.

This is a grave error. Their awareness of divine decree and the workings of divine lordship has diverted them from awareness of the revealed Law, its commands and prohibitions, worshipping Allah alone, and obeying His Messenger. Whoever seeks the removal of his selfhood in this sense is not to be praised for it, though he may be excused.

As for the third form—the abandonment of worship of all other than Allah—this is the state of the prophets and their followers. It means abandoning the worship of all others by worshipping Allah, love for all others by loving Him, fear of all others by fearing Him, obedience to all others by obeying Him, and reliance upon all others by relying upon Him. This is the realization of worshipping Allah alone, without any partner. It is pure monotheism, the religion of Abraham.

This includes abandoning the pursuit of one’s own desires by obeying Allah, so that one loves only for Allah’s sake, hates only for Allah’s sake, gives only for Allah’s sake, and withholds only for Allah’s sake. This is the religiously prescribed form of fanā’ (annihilation) with which Allah sent His messengers and revealed His scriptures.

---

## Page 697

If someone says:

“By Your right, remove my selfhood from between us,”

meaning that he should set his own self aside, neither following his desires nor relying upon himself or his own might and strength, but acting for Allah rather than for his desires, and acting through Allah’s help and strength rather than his own might and strength—as Allah Most High says:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help”—

then this meaning is true and praiseworthy.

This is like the account related of Abū Yazīd, who said: “I saw the Lord of Glory in a dream and said, ‘My God, what is the way to You?’ He replied, ‘Leave yourself behind and come.’” That is, abandon following your desires and relying upon yourself, so that your deeds are for Allah and you seek help from Allah, as Allah Most High says:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

The saying attributed to Ibn ‘Arabī:

“I swore by myself, and the one swearing was Allah,”

is likewise part of their heresy and falsehood. He made himself the one swearing by himself, and made Allah the one swearing; thus he is both the one who swears and the one sworn by. This resembles their saying: “He sent from himself to himself a messenger who was himself.” Thus he is the sender, the recipient, and the messenger.

Likewise, Ibn al-Fārid said in his poem Nazm al-Sulūk (The Ordering of the Spiritual Path):

“For her I establish my prayers at the station, … and witness in them that she has prayed to me.
We both are one worshipper, prostrating … to his own reality, in union, at every prostration.

---

## Page 698

And none but myself prayed through me, … nor was my prayer for another in any unit of prayer.”

He goes on to say:

“I have always been her, and she has always been me; … there is no distinction: my essence has loved itself.
I have removed the letter tā, marking ‘you’, from between us; … removing it raises me above the divisions of the sects.
If I am called, I am the one who answers; … if I am the caller, she answers whoever calls me and responds.
To myself I was sent, a messenger from myself; … through my signs, my essence found proof of me.”

As for what is attributed to Jesus son of Mary—may Allah’s blessings be upon him—it is falsely attributed to him. These are the words of a lying heretic who fabricated them and attributed them to the Messiah. No Muslim or Christian has transmitted this from him, for it does not accord with what Christians say. The statement reads:

“Allah longed to see His holy essence, so He created Adam from His light and made him like a mirror in which He could behold His holy essence. I am that light, and Adam is the mirror.”

Besides containing disbelief and heresy, this statement is self-contradictory. Allah, glorified be He, sees Himself just as He hears His own speech. Indeed, the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who is a servant created by Allah, said to his Companions:

“I see you behind my back just as I see you in front of me.”

If a created being can see what is behind him—which is more extraordinary than seeing himself—how could the Creator, Most High, not see Himself?

Moreover, the claim that He longed to see Himself and therefore created Adam implies that, throughout eternity past, He did not see Himself until He created Adam.

---

## Page 699

Then, if that longing was eternal, He should have acted upon it in eternity past. If it arose anew, there must have been a cause that brought it about. Moreover, it may be said that longing is itself an attribute of deficiency; hence it has not been established as an attribute of Allah, Most High. It has been reported:

“The righteous have long yearned to meet Me, and I yearn even more to meet them.”

But this is a weak hadith.

The statement, “He therefore created Adam from His light and made him like a mirror; I am that light, and Adam is the mirror,” implies that Adam was created from the Messiah. This is contrary to what actually happened: Adam was created before the Messiah, the Messiah was created from Mary, and Mary was one of Adam’s descendants. How, then, could Adam have been created from his own descendants?

If it is said, “The Messiah is Allah’s light,” then this statement, although akin to what Christians say, is worse than their claim. Christians say that the Messiah comprises humanity and divinity, and that the divinity—the Word—is the substance of the Son. They say that the union of divinity and humanity came into being when the Messiah’s body was created. They do not say that Adam was created from the Messiah, since, for them, “the Messiah” denotes divinity and humanity together, and Adam could not have been created from these. Nor do they say that Adam was created from the Messiah’s divinity.

Furthermore, regarding the claim that Adam was created from Allah’s light, which is the Messiah: if the speaker means His light that is an attribute of Allah, then that is not the Messiah, who is a self-subsisting being. A self-subsisting being cannot be an attribute of another. If, however, by His light the speaker means a light separate from Him, it is well known that the Messiah did not exist as a separate being before Adam was created. Thus, on either interpretation, Adam could not have been created from Allah’s light understood as the Messiah.

---

## Page 700

Furthermore, if Adam is like a mirror in which He beholds His holy essence, it follows that what appears in Adam is an image of His essence—not that Adam is His essence, an image of His essence, or like His essence.

If this means that Adam knows Allah, Most High, so that He sees an image of His essence in Adam’s knowledge, then the Lord, Most High, knows Himself. If an image contained in knowledge could be seen, it would be more fitting for Him to behold the knowledge that truly corresponds to Him and subsists in His own essence than the knowledge that subsists in Adam. If, however, what is meant is that Adam himself is an image of Allah, then Adam would not be the mirror; rather, he would be like the image in the mirror.

Furthermore, singling out the Messiah as that light is the position of the Christians, who single him out as Allah or the son of Allah. These proponents of union have combined the Christians’ position with their own doctrine of universal union, attributing to others besides the Messiah something of the same kind as what Christians attribute to the Messiah.

As for Ibn al-Fārid’s words:

“When you view yourself clearly, behold whom you see,
Beyond dispute, in the polished mirror.
Is it someone other than you who appears within it,
Or are you looking at yourself through it as the rays reflect?”

This is a flawed analogy. Someone looking into a mirror sees an image of himself, and thus sees himself through the mirror, not without an intermediary. Their doctrine of the unity of existence is therefore false; and even if its truth were assumed, this analogy would not correspond to it.

---

## Page 701

Furthermore, these people maintain that oneness, union, and indwelling extend to all things. Their subsequent singling out of Adam or someone such as the Messiah therefore contradicts their claim of universality. Only those who affirm union restricted to particular individuals single out the Messiah and others like him, as do the Christians, the extremists among the Shia, ignorant ascetics, and others of their kind.

Furthermore, even supposing that a person sees himself in a mirror, the mirror is external to him. He thus sees himself, or an image of himself, in something other than himself. Yet, according to them, there is nothing other or distinct in existence. There is therefore no manifestation distinct from what manifests, nor any mirror distinct from the beholder.

They say:

“The cosmos consists of manifestations of the Real.”

If they say that the manifestations are distinct from what manifests, plurality necessarily follows, and oneness is invalidated. If they say that the manifestations are themselves what manifests, then nothing has appeared in anything, nothing has disclosed itself in anything, nothing has appeared to anything, and nothing has disclosed itself to anything. His words would then be self-contradictory:

“When you view yourself clearly, behold whom you see.”

For here there is a speaker, someone addressed, and a mirror in which the self is viewed: these are three distinct entities. If existence is a single entity, these words are invalid. Every word they utter undermines their fundamental premise.

---

## Page 702

Section

As for his citation of Ibn Isrā’īl’s statement:

“Commands are of two kinds: a command conveyed through an intermediary and a command conveyed without an intermediary,”

and so forth, its substance is that the command conveyed through an intermediary is the religious, prescriptive command, while the command conveyed without an intermediary is the command pertaining to divine decree and creation. His claim that one kind involves an intermediary while the other does not is false. Religious commands may be conveyed either through an intermediary or without one. Allah spoke to Moses and commanded him without an intermediary. Likewise, He spoke to Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and commanded him on the night of the Ascension. He also spoke to Adam and commanded him without an intermediary. These were all religious, prescriptive commands.

As for the command pertaining to creation, the claim that it involves no intermediary is mistaken. Rather, Allah Most High created things by means of other things. The command that brings something into being is not an address heard by the created thing being brought into existence, for that is impossible.

Hence it has been said:

“If this is an address to it after it exists, then it was not brought into being by ‘Be’; rather, it had already been brought into being before the address. If, however, it is an address to it before it exists, then addressing what does not exist is impossible.”

The following answer has been given to this:

“It is an address to something known, since it is present in knowledge, even though it does not exist in concrete reality.”

As for what the faqīr has mentioned, it is undoubtedly a valid objection.

---

## Page 703

As for what he relates from his teacher—that Adam affirmed divine oneness both outwardly and inwardly, so that His words, “Do not approach,” were outward, while His command, “Eat,” was inward—the reply is as follows:

If saying that He inwardly commanded him to “Eat” means that He gave him an inward religious, prescriptive command to do so, then this is a lie and disbelief. If, however, he means that He created, decreed, and brought it into being, then this applies equally to Adam and all other created beings. For His command, when He wills a thing, is only to say to it, “Be,” and it is.

If it is said that Adam witnessed the command pertaining to divine decree and creation, and was obedient to Allah by complying with it—as these people say that the gnostic who witnesses divine decree is no longer subject to blame—then, besides its falsity being necessarily known in the religion of Islam, this is disbelief by the consensus of the Muslims.

The reply is that the command pertaining to creation exists before the thing brought into being exists. The servant does not hear it, nor is complying with it within his power. Rather, it is the Lord who, by His will and power, creates what He brings into being. Allah Most High has no partner in creating and bringing things into being.

Although the servant acts by his own will and power, Allah is the Creator of all of this. Thus, Allah’s bringing the servant into being is not a command addressed to a servant already existing in concrete reality who can comply with it. Likewise, whatever He has created of the servant’s states and deeds, He has created by His will and power:

“His command, when He wills a thing, is only to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

Everything brought into being therefore falls within this command.

---

## Page 704

And Adam’s eating from the tree, and all other events, fall within this command just as Adam himself does. It is Adam’s very act of eating that falls within this command, just as Adam himself does.

Thus, saying that He inwardly said to Adam, “Eat,” is like saying that He said to the disbeliever, “Disbelieve,” and to the transgressor, “Transgress.” Yet Allah does not command indecency, does not love corruption, and does not approve of disbelief for His servants. Neither inwardly nor outwardly does He address disbelievers, transgressors, and sinners with a command to commit disbelief, transgression, or disobedience, even though these occur through His will, power, creation, and command pertaining to creation. For the command pertaining to creation is not a command instructing the servant to perform that act. Rather, it is a command bringing that act into being within the servant, or bringing the servant into that state.

It is He, glorified be He, who created man prone to anxiety:

“When evil touches him, impatient,”

“And when good touches him, withholding.”

And it is He who made Muslims submit to Him, as Abraham, His intimate friend, said:

“Our Lord, make us both submissive to You, and from our descendants a nation submissive to You.”

Thus He, glorified be He, placed His servants in the states in which He created them. His command to them to that effect is a command pertaining to creation, meaning that He says to them, “Be thus,” and they are thus, just as He says to an inanimate thing, “Be,” and it is. The command pertaining to creation makes no distinction between inanimate things and living beings. It does not depend on the knowledge, will, or power of the one commanded.

The servant may, however, know what divine decree has brought about in his own circumstances, just as he knows what divine decree has brought about in the circumstances of others. This does not mean that he knows Allah has inwardly commanded him to do the opposite of what He has outwardly commanded him. Rather, He has commanded him to obey Him inwardly

---

## Page 705

and outwardly, and has forbidden him to disobey Him inwardly and outwardly. He has decreed whatever obedience and disobedience occur in him, inwardly and outwardly, and has created the servant and all his deeds, inwardly and outwardly. All this comes into being through His saying, “Be,” inwardly and outwardly.

Divine decree provides the son of Adam with neither a justification nor an excuse. Rather, divine decree is to be believed in, not invoked as a justification. Whoever invokes it as a justification is unsound in reason and religion and contradicts himself. For if divine decree were a justification and an excuse, it would follow that no one could be blamed, punished, or subjected to retribution. Thus, when someone who invokes divine decree is wronged in his person, property, honor, or inviolability, he would have to refrain from seeking redress against the wrongdoer, becoming angry with him, or condemning him. This is contrary to human nature: no one can do it. It is therefore impossible by nature and forbidden by religious law.

If divine decree were a justification and an excuse, neither Iblīs nor Pharaoh, the people of Noah, ʿĀd, Thamūd, or any other disbelievers would deserve blame or punishment. Nor would fighting the disbelievers or enforcing the prescribed punishments be permissible: neither amputating a thief’s hand, nor flogging or stoning someone who commits fornication or adultery, nor putting a killer to death, nor punishing an aggressor in any way whatsoever.

Since invoking divine decree as a justification is invalid according to people’s innate dispositions and reason, no nation has adopted it, nor is it the position of any rational person who consistently follows through the implications of his view. No one’s welfare, either in this world or in the Hereafter, can be maintained on this basis. Indeed, two people cannot live together for even a single hour unless one observes some form of law in his dealings with the other. Religious law is Allah’s light on His earth and His justice among His servants.

---

## Page 706

But religious laws vary. Sometimes they are revealed by Allah, as conveyed by the messengers, and sometimes they are not. Revealed laws may subsequently be altered and changed—as the People of the Book altered their religious laws—or may remain unaltered and unchanged. Sometimes parts of them are abrogated, and sometimes they are not.

As for divine decree, no one invokes it as a justification except when following his own desires. When someone commits a forbidden act merely on the basis of his desire, personal taste, and inner feelings, without knowing that the act is good or beneficial, he appeals to divine decree, as the polytheists said:

“If Allah had willed, neither we nor our forefathers would have associated partners with Him, nor would we have forbidden anything.”

Allah Most High said:

“Likewise, those before them denied the truth until they tasted Our punishment. Say, ‘Do you have any knowledge that you can produce for us? You follow nothing but conjecture, and you do nothing but guess.’”

“Say, ‘To Allah belongs the conclusive argument. Had He willed, He would have guided you all.’”

He thus made clear that they had no knowledge supporting the religion they followed; they were merely following conjecture. These people did not regard it as permissible for everyone to invoke divine decree as a justification. Had someone destroyed the Kaaba, insulted Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, or disparaged their religion, they would have opposed and harmed him. Indeed, they opposed the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, because of the religion he brought, even though his actions were also divinely decreed.

If invoking divine decree were a valid justification, it would have been one for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions. If everything that occurs in existence is decreed, then those in the right and those in the wrong could equally invoke divine decree—if invoking it were valid. But they deliberately took their stand on

---

## Page 707

what they believed to be part of their religion, though in this they were following conjecture, having no knowledge to support it; they were merely guessing.

“When Moses said to Adam, ‘Why did you cause us and yourself to be expelled from Paradise?’ Adam, peace be upon him, said among his replies to Moses, ‘Why do you blame me for something Allah decreed for me forty years before I was created?’ Thus Adam prevailed in argument over Moses.”

Adam, peace be upon him, was not invoking divine decree to justify doing what he had been forbidden to do. Nor was Moses someone who would accept such a justification. Indeed, even ordinary believers would not do such a thing—how, then, could Adam and Moses? Adam had repented of what he had done, and his Lord had chosen him and guided him. Moses knew Allah too well to blame even someone below the rank of a prophet for an act of which he had repented—let alone a prophet!

Adam knew that, had divine decree been a valid justification, he would not have needed to repent, nor would his expulsion from Paradise and the other consequences have occurred. Had divine decree been a valid justification, it would have served as one for Iblīs and others as well. Likewise, Moses knew that, had divine decree been a valid justification, Pharaoh would not have been punished by drowning, nor the Children of Israel by the thunderbolt and other punishments. How could it be otherwise, when Moses himself said:

“My Lord, I have wronged myself, so forgive me.”

And he said:

“You are our Protector, so forgive us and have mercy on us. You are the best of those who forgive.”

This is a vast subject. Moses blamed Adam only because of the calamity that had befallen them through Adam’s eating from the tree. This is why he said:

“Why did you cause us and yourself to be expelled from Paradise?”

Blaming someone for a calamity that has befallen a person is one thing; blaming someone for a sin, where Allah’s right is concerned, is another.

---

## Page 708

For if a father were to do something that impoverished him, causing his children to suffer, and they began blaming him for the poverty that had befallen them, this would not be the same as blaming him for having sinned. The servant is commanded to bear patiently what has been decreed, to obey what has been commanded, and, whenever he sins, to seek forgiveness. As Allah Most High says:

“So be patient. Indeed, the promise of Allah is true. And ask forgiveness for your sin.”

And Allah Most High says:

“No disaster strikes except by permission of Allah. And whoever believes in Allah—He will guide his heart.”

A number of the early generations said: “This refers to a man whom a calamity befalls: he knows that it is from Allah, so he accepts it and submits.”

Whoever invokes divine decree to justify neglecting what he has been commanded to do, yet reacts impatiently when something he dislikes befalls him by divine decree, has turned faith and religion upside down and joined the party of heretical hypocrites. Such is the condition of those who invoke divine decree as a justification. When calamity strikes one of them, his distress is great and his patience scant; he neither considers divine decree nor submits to it. Yet when he commits a sin, he begins invoking divine decree to justify it. He neither does what is commanded, nor refrains from what is forbidden, nor patiently bears what is decreed. Despite this, he claims to be among Allah’s greatest, most God-fearing allies and among the leading authorities of those who truly affirm His oneness. In reality, he is one of Allah’s heretical enemies and belongs to the party of the accursed Satan.

Only those furthest removed from goodness, religion, and faith follow this path. You will find one of them the most domineering of people when he has power, and the greatest in injustice and aggression; yet when subdued, he is the most abject of people, and the greatest in distress and weakness. People have witnessed this among factions far removed from faith in the Book and among combatants of various kinds.

---

## Page 709

The believer, by contrast, acts justly and does good when he has power; when subdued and defeated, he is patient and seeks Allah’s reward. As Ka'b ibn Zuhayr said in describing the believers in the poem he recited to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—which begins, “Su'ād has departed…”:

“They do not exult when their spears strike home one day,
Nor are they overcome with anguish when struck in turn.”

One of the Arabs was asked about some aspect of the Prophet’s conduct, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and replied: “I saw him prevail without arrogance, and suffer defeat without impatience.”

Allah Most High says:

“They said, ‘Are you indeed Joseph?’ He said, ‘I am Joseph, and this is my brother. Allah has certainly been gracious to us. Indeed, whoever fears Allah and is patient—Allah does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.’”

And Allah Most High says:

“And if you are patient and fear Allah, their plotting will not harm you at all.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Yes, if you are patient and fear Allah, and they come upon you at once, your Lord will reinforce you with five thousand angels bearing marks.”

And Allah Most High says:

“And if you are patient and fear Allah, that is indeed a matter requiring firm resolve.”

He mentions patience and fear of Allah in these four passages. Patience includes patiently bearing what has been decreed, while fear of Allah includes doing what is commanded and refraining from what is forbidden.

Whoever is granted both has been granted goodness in its entirety. The opposite is true of someone who reverses this: he does not fear Allah, but abandons obedience to Him, follows his own desires, and invokes divine decree as a justification. Yet when tested, he is not patient and gives no thought to divine decree. Such is the condition of the wretched, as one of the scholars said:

“When obeying, you are a Qadarī (affirming independent human agency); when sinning, a Jabrī (affirming compulsion). You adopt whichever doctrine suits your desires.”

---

## Page 710

He means:

“When you obey, you regard yourself as the creator of your obedience, forgetting Allah’s favor upon you in making you obedient to Him. When you disobey, however, you do not acknowledge that you committed the sin. Instead, you regard yourself as someone compelled to do it against his will, or as something set in motion that has neither will, ability, nor knowledge. Both are mistaken.”

Abū Tālib al-Makkī related that Sahl ibn ‘Abd Allāh al-Tustarī said:

“When a servant performs a good deed and says, ‘My Lord, I performed this good deed,’ his Lord says to him, ‘I enabled you to do it and helped you with it.’ If he says, ‘My Lord, You helped me with it and enabled me to do it,’ his Lord says to him, ‘You performed it, and its reward is yours.’

“When he commits an evil deed and says, ‘My Lord, You decreed this evil deed for me,’ his Lord says to him, ‘You committed it, and you bear its burden.’ If he says, ‘My Lord, I committed this sin, and I repent of it,’ his Lord says to him, ‘I decreed it for you, and I forgive you for it.’”

This subject has been discussed at length elsewhere. Among many of those associated with spiritual leadership and Sufism, it has become common to see only divine decree at work, without regard for divine commands and prohibitions, and to invoke it as a justification for neglecting what is commanded and doing what is forbidden. This is the gravest misguidance.

Whoever consistently follows this position and accepts its necessary implications would be more unbelieving than the Jews, Christians, and polytheists. Most of those who fall into this, however, contradict themselves and do not follow their position consistently.

The statement of the person in question belongs to this category. For his statement:

“Adam was inwardly commanded to eat, so he ate. Iblīs’s affirmation of Allah’s oneness was outward, so he was commanded to prostrate to Adam, but regarded him as other than Allah and did not prostrate

---

## Page 711

so Allah changed his condition and said, ‘Get out of it’—and the rest of the verse.”

This statement, besides the heresy it contains, falsely represents both Adam and Iblīs. Adam acknowledged that he himself had committed the sin and wronged himself, and he repented of it. He did not say, “Allah wronged me,” or, “Allah inwardly commanded me to eat.” Allah Most High says:

“Then Adam received words from his Lord, and He accepted his repentance. Indeed, He is the Accepting of repentance, the Merciful.”

And Allah Most High says:

“They said, ‘Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves, and if You do not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we will surely be among the losers.’”

Iblīs, however, persisted and invoked divine decree as a justification, saying:

“My Lord, because You have led me astray, I will surely make disobedience attractive to them on earth, and I will mislead them all.”

As for his statement, “He regarded him as other than Allah and did not prostrate,” this is worse than invoking divine decree as a justification. It is the position of the heretical advocates of the unity of existence, and it falsely represents Iblīs. Iblīs did not refuse to prostrate because Adam was other than Allah. Rather, he said:

“I am better than him. You created me from fire and created him from clay.”

Nor were the angels commanded to prostrate because Adam was not other than Allah. Rather, the distinction between the angels and Adam is established and well known. Allah Most High says:

“And He taught Adam the names—all of them. Then He presented them to the angels and said, ‘Inform Me of the names of these, if you are truthful.’”

“They said, ‘Exalted are You; we have no knowledge except what You have taught us. Indeed, You are the Knowing, the Wise.’”

Both the angels and Adam acknowledged that Allah is separate from them and that they are other than Him. Thus, they called upon Him as a servant calls upon his Lord. Adam says:

“Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves.”

The angels say:

“We have no knowledge except what You have taught us.”

And they say:

“Our Lord, You encompass all things in mercy and knowledge, so forgive those who have repented and followed Your way, and protect them from the punishment of Hellfire”—and the rest of the verse.

Allah Most High has said:

“Say, ‘Is it other than Allah that you order me to worship, O ignorant ones?’”

And Allah Most High says:

“Shall I take as a protector anyone other than Allah

---

## Page 712

, the Originator of the heavens and the earth, who feeds and is not fed?” And He says:

“Then is it other than Allah I should seek as judge, while it is He who has revealed to you the Book explained in detail?”

If there were nothing other than Him, the polytheists would not have told him to worship anyone other than Allah, or to take anyone other than Allah as a protector or judge. They would therefore not have deserved censure. Since He censured them for this, it establishes that there exists something other than Him that can be worshipped and taken as a protector or judge, and that whoever does so associates partners with Allah. As He, Most High, says:

“So do not invoke another god alongside Allah, lest you be among those who are punished.”

And He says:

“Do not set up another god alongside Allah, lest you sit condemned and forsaken.”

There are other similar verses.

As for the claim that His words,

“You have no part in the decision,”

are themselves an affirmation of authority for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, like His words,

“And you did not throw when you threw, but it was Allah who threw,”

and,

“Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are actually pledging allegiance to Allah. The hand of Allah is over their hands,”

this is based on the doctrine of the advocates of unity and union. His words, “You have no part in the decision,” are thus interpreted to mean that your action is Allah’s action, because there is no distinction between the two. This is grave misguidance for several reasons.

The first of the two is that His words, “You have no part in the decision,” were revealed in the context of His words:

“That He might cut off a portion of those who disbelieve or subdue them so that they turn back disappointed.”

“You have no part in the decision—whether He turns to them in mercy or punishes them, for indeed, they are wrongdoers.”

---

## Page 713

It is established in the authentic hadith collection:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to supplicate against some disbelievers or curse them during the supplication in prayer. When Allah revealed this verse, he ceased doing so.”

Thus, the verse means that the decision belongs exclusively to the Lord, Most High, and that no one else has any share in it. Rather, if Allah, Most High, wills, He cuts off a portion of the disbelievers; if He wills, He subdues them so that they turn back having suffered loss; if He wills, He turns to them in mercy; and if He wills, He punishes them.

This is like His statement in another verse:

“Say, ‘I have no power to benefit or harm myself except as Allah wills. Had I known the unseen, I would have acquired much good, and no harm would have touched me.’”

Similar to this are His words, Most High:

“They say, ‘If we had any part in the decision, we would not have been killed here.’”

“Say, ‘Indeed, the decision belongs entirely to Allah.’”

The second reason is that His words,

“And you did not throw when you threw, but it was Allah who threw,”

do not mean that the servant’s action is Allah’s action—as some who are mistaken suppose. If that were true, the same would have to be said to everyone. One would even say to someone walking, “You did not walk when you walked, but it was Allah who walked”; to someone riding, “You did not ride when you rode, but it was Allah who rode”; and to someone speaking, “You did not speak when you spoke, but it was Allah who spoke.” The same would be said to someone eating, drinking, fasting, praying, and so forth.

Taking this to its logical conclusion would require saying to a disbeliever, “You did not disbelieve when you disbelieved, but it was Allah who disbelieved”; and to a liar, “You did not lie when you lied, but it was Allah who lied.” Anyone who says such things is a disbeliever and a heretic, outside the bounds of reason and religion.

---

## Page 714

Rather, the verse means that “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, threw at them on the day of Badr, but it was not within his power to make what he threw reach them all; for when he threw dust at them and said, ‘May their faces be disfigured!’” he could not make it reach them all. Allah, Most High, made what he threw reach them all by His power.

The meaning is: “You did not make it reach them when you cast it, but Allah made it reach them.” Thus, the throwing affirmed of him is not the throwing denied of him, for that would entail combining two contradictions. Rather, making it reach them was denied of him, while casting and throwing it were affirmed of him. Likewise, if he shot an arrow and Allah made it reach the enemy in a manner beyond the ordinary course of events, it was Allah who made it reach its target by His power.

(The third reason)

Even if we suppose that this verse means that Allah is the Creator of His servants’ actions, this meaning is true. Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, said:

“Our Lord, make us both submissive to You.”

It is Allah who makes a Muslim a Muslim. He, Most High, also says:

“Indeed, man was created anxious:
when evil touches him, impatient,
and when good touches him, withholding.”

It is Allah who created him anxious. Yet none of this means that Allah is the servant, that the Creator’s existence is the existence of the created being, or that Allah dwells within the servant. The assertion that Allah is the Creator of His servants’ actions is true, while the assertion that creation inheres in the created being, or that its existence is the existence of the created being, is false. These people move from affirming Allah’s oneness in His lordship to asserting indwelling and union. This is the very essence of misguidance and heresy.

---

## Page 715

(The fourth reason)

In His saying, Most High:

“Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are actually pledging allegiance to Allah,”

He did not mean, “You are Allah.” Rather, He meant, “You are Allah’s Messenger, conveying His commands and prohibitions; whoever pledges allegiance to you has pledged allegiance to Allah, just as whoever obeys you has obeyed Allah.” He did not mean that the Messenger is Allah. Rather, the Messenger commands what Allah has commanded, so whoever obeys him has obeyed Allah. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever obeys me has obeyed Allah, and whoever obeys the commander I appoint has obeyed me. Whoever disobeys me has disobeyed Allah, and whoever disobeys the commander I appoint has disobeyed me.”

It is well known that the commander he appoints is not the Prophet himself.

Whoever supposes that His saying:

“Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are actually pledging allegiance to Allah,”

means that your action is Allah’s action, or that Allah dwells within you, or anything similar, has—in addition to his ignorance and misguidance, indeed, his disbelief and heresy—stripped the Messenger of his distinctive status and made him like everyone else. For if the intended meaning were that Allah performs your action, this would be something the Messenger shares with all other created beings. Whoever pledged allegiance to Abū Jahl would then have pledged allegiance to Allah; whoever pledged allegiance to Musaylima the Liar would have pledged allegiance to Allah; and whoever pledged allegiance to the leaders of the Confederates would have pledged allegiance to Allah. On this assumption, the one pledging allegiance would also be Allah, so Allah would have pledged allegiance to Allah, since Allah is the Creator of both parties. Likewise, according to the doctrine of those who advocate indwelling, the oneness of existence, and union, it applies universally to both parties, so Allah would have pledged allegiance to Allah.

Many of the shaykhs who advocate indwelling and union say this, to the point that, when one of them is ordered to fight the enemy, he says: “Am I to fight Allah? I cannot fight Allah,” and other such

---

## Page 716

statements that we have heard from their shaykhs, and whose falsity and the misguidance they contain we have explained to them more than once.

As for indwelling in particular individuals, this is not their doctrine. Rather, it is the doctrine of the Christians and those extremists who agree with them. This too is false, for Allah, glorified be He, said to him:

“Not for you is the decision.”

And He said:

“And when the servant of Allah stood up supplicating Him.”

And He said:

“Glorified is He who took His servant by night.”

And He said:

“And if you are in doubt about what We have sent down upon Our servant.”

And He said:

“Certainly Allah was pleased with the believers when they pledged allegiance to you under the tree. He knew what was in their hearts, so He sent down tranquillity upon them and rewarded them with a victory near at hand,”

“and abundant spoils which they would take. Allah is Almighty, All-Wise.”

His saying, “Certainly Allah was pleased with the believers when they pledged allegiance to you under the tree,” explains His saying, “Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are actually pledging allegiance to Allah.” Hence He said: “The hand of Allah is above their hands.”

It is well known that the hand of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was with their hands: they would shake his hand and strike their palms against his hand when pledging allegiance. Thus it is clear that the hand of Allah, which is above their hands, is not the hand of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, the Messenger is Allah’s servant and Messenger. He accepted their allegiance on Allah’s behalf and entered into covenants and agreements with them on Allah’s behalf. Those who pledged allegiance to him therefore pledged allegiance to Allah, who sent him and commanded him to accept their allegiance.

Do you not see that, when someone appoints an agent and a contract is concluded with that agent, this is a contract with the principal? And if someone appoints a representative to make a covenant with a people, and the representative makes that covenant on behalf of the one who appointed him, have they not entered into a covenant with the one who appointed him? And if someone appoints a man to give someone in marriage or contract a marriage, is the appointed agent the husband for whom the contract was concluded?

Allah Most High has said:

“Indeed, Allah has purchased from the believers their lives and their wealth in return for

---

## Page 717

Paradise being theirs,” to the end of the verse. For this reason, He said at the end of the verse:

“And whoever fulfils what he has pledged to Allah, He will grant him a great reward.”

Thus it becomes clear that what that faqīr said is correct, and that if Allah has said to His Prophet:

“Not for you is the decision,”

then what are we?

It is authentically reported from him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the Sahīh that he said:

“Do not exaggerate in praising me as the Christians exaggerated in praising the Messiah, son of Mary. I am only a servant, so say: ‘Allah’s servant and Messenger.’”

As for the words:

“You have never been absent from my heart or my sight …
Between you and us there is no separation,”

this statement rests on the doctrine of these people and is false and self-contradictory, for it assumes that the speaker sees Allah with his own eyes. Yet it is authentically reported in the Sahīh from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he said:

“Know that none of you will see his Lord until he dies.”

The leading scholars of the Muslims have agreed that no believer sees Allah with his own eyes in this world. Their disagreement concerned only the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, although the overwhelming majority of these scholars held that he did not see Him with his own eyes in this world. This is what the authentic, established reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the Companions, and the leading scholars of the Muslims indicate.

It has not been authentically established that Ibn ʿAbbās, Imam Ahmad, or others like them said that Muhammad saw his Lord with his own eyes. Rather, what is established from them either mentions seeing Him without qualification or specifies that it was with the heart.

---

## Page 718

and none of the authenticated hadiths concerning the Ascension states that he saw Him with his own eyes. As for his saying:

“My Lord came to me last night in the most beautiful form,”

the hadith related by al-Tirmidhī and others describes a dream in Medina, as the report explicitly explains. Likewise, the accounts of his seeing his Lord in the hadiths of Umm al-Tufayl, Ibn Abbās, and others concern events that took place in Medina, as explicitly explained in those hadiths. The Ascension, however, took place in Mecca, as Allah Most High said:

“Glory be to Him who took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque.”

This matter has been discussed at length elsewhere.

The Quran explicitly establishes that Moses was told:

“You will not see Me,”

and that seeing Allah is a greater matter than having a book sent down from heaven, as Allah Most High said:

“The People of the Book ask you to bring down for them a book from heaven. They asked Moses for something even greater than that, saying, ‘Show us Allah openly.’”

Thus, whoever claims that any person sees Him is claiming that this person is greater than Moses son of Imrān. His claim is greater than that of someone who claims that Allah has sent down a book to him from heaven.

People hold three positions regarding seeing Allah:

- The Companions, the Successors, and the leading scholars of the Muslims hold that Allah will be seen directly with the eyes in the Hereafter, and that no one sees Him with his own eyes in this world. He may, however, be seen in dreams, and hearts experience disclosures and visions appropriate to their condition. Some people’s vision within the heart becomes so powerful that they think they have seen it with their own eyes;

---

## Page 719

but they are mistaken. Visions of the heart occur in a representational form, according to the servant’s faith and knowledge, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

(The second position)

The position of the negationists among the Jahmiyya is that He is seen neither in this world nor in the Hereafter.

(The third)

The position of those who claim that He is seen both in this world and in the Hereafter.

The proponents of divine indwelling among the Jahmiyya combine negation and affirmation, saying that He is seen neither in this world nor in the Hereafter, and that He is seen both in this world and in the Hereafter. This is the position of Ibn Arabī—the author of al-Fusūs—and others like him, because the absolute existence pervading all beings cannot be seen, and this, according to them, is the existence of the Real.

Those who affirm the essence say: “He is seen manifesting Himself in it.” Those who distinguish between the absolute and the particular say: “He is seen only as restricted to a form.”

Their position revolves around two things: denying that Allah is seen and affirming that created beings are seen. They identify the created being with the Creator, or hold that the Creator indwells the created being. Otherwise, their distinction between entities established in external reality and their existence amounts to the position of those who say that the nonexistent is a thing in external reality. This is a false position, to which they have added the claim that the very existence of the created being is the existence of the Creator.

As for distinguishing between the absolute and the particular—although the absolute does not itself exist in external reality as something absolute—this entails that the Lord is nonexistent. This is denial and negation of the Lord.

---

## Page 720

And if they posit it as existing in external reality, they make it a part of existing beings. The Creator would then be part of the created being, or an accident inhering in it. All of this is self-evidently false, and it has been discussed at length elsewhere.

As for his contradiction, his words:

“You have never been absent from my heart or my eye;
Between you and us there is no separation”

imply a distinction: the one addressed is other than the speaker, and the speaker has an eye and a heart from which the one addressed is never absent. Rather, the heart and eye witness him, and the witness is other than what is witnessed.

His words:

“Between you and us there is no separation”

affirm both a first-person pronoun and a second-person pronoun, thereby affirming two distinct beings. If they say, “These are loci of manifestation and self-disclosure,” the reply is: if the loci of manifestation and self-disclosure are other than the one who manifests and discloses himself, then duality is established and unity disproved. If he is identical with them, then multiplicity is disproved. Combining the two is a contradiction.

As for the words:

“Abandon the wrongdoing of your natural disposition and be united with Allah …
Otherwise, all your claims are untenable”—

if the speaker means unrestricted union, then the one who parts is identical with what he parts from: he is the natural disposition and its wrongdoing. He is also the one addressed by the words, “and be united with Allah,” and by the words, “all your claims are untenable.” He is, moreover, the one uttering these words. The contradiction in this is plain.

---

## Page 721

But if he means restricted union, this is impossible. For if the Creator and the created being unite, yet remain two after their union—as they were before it—then this is multiplicity, not union. If, however, they are transformed into a third thing—as water unites with milk, or fire with iron, and in similar forms of union affirmed by Christians in their doctrine of union—this would entail that the Creator has undergone transformation and that His reality has changed, like anything else that unites with another, since it must undergo transformation. This is impossible for Allah Most High, who is exalted above it.

For transformation entails the cessation of something that previously existed. The Lord Most High necessarily exists in His essence and His inseparable attributes; none of these can cease to exist. Moreover, the Lord’s inseparable attributes are attributes of perfection, so the absence of any one of them would constitute a deficiency, above which Allah is exalted.

Furthermore, the union of the created being with the Creator would entail that the servant possesses the beginningless attributes inseparable from the Lord’s essence. This is impossible for the servant, who was brought into existence and created. For having a beginning, dependence, and lowliness are inseparable from the servant, whereas beginninglessness, self-sufficiency, and might are inseparable from the Lord Most High. He—glory be to Him—is beginningless, self-sufficient, and mighty in Himself; the opposite of any of these is impossible for Him.

Thus, the union of either with the other would entail that the Lord possesses the opposites of His attributes—having a beginning, need, and lowliness—and that the servant possesses the opposites of his attributes—beginninglessness, intrinsic self-sufficiency, and intrinsic might. All of this is impossible, and a detailed discussion would be lengthy.

---

## Page 722

For this reason, when al-Junayd was asked about tawhīd (affirming Allah’s oneness), he replied: “Tawhīd means distinguishing what has a beginning from what is beginningless.” He thus explained that what has been brought into existence must be distinguished from the Beginningless.

For this reason, the leading scholars of the Muslims agreed that the Creator is distinct from His creatures: nothing of His essence is within His creatures, nor is anything of His creatures within His essence. Rather, the Lord is the Lord, and the servant is the servant:

“There is no one in the heavens and earth but that he comes to the Most Merciful as a servant.”

“He has certainly counted them and numbered them precisely.”

“And all of them are coming to Him on the Day of Resurrection alone.”

If, however, the speaker of this verse meant union in attributes)—that is, that the servant loves what Allah loves, hates what Allah hates, is pleased with what pleases Allah, is angered by what angers Allah, commands what Allah commands, forbids what Allah forbids, takes as allies those whom Allah takes as allies, takes as enemies those whom Allah takes as enemies, loves for Allah’s sake, hates for Allah’s sake, gives for Allah’s sake, and withholds for Allah’s sake, so that he is in accord with his Lord Most High—then this meaning is true. It is the very essence and perfection of faith, as in the hadith narrated by al-Bukhārī from Abū Hurayrah, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Allah Most High says: ‘Whoever shows enmity toward a friend of Mine has challenged Me to war. Nothing by which My servant draws near to Me compares with fulfilling what I have made obligatory upon him. My servant continues to draw near to Me through voluntary acts of worship until I love him. When I love him, I am his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees, his hand with which he strikes, and his foot with which he walks. Through Me he hears, through Me he sees, through Me he strikes, and through Me he walks. If he asks Me, I will surely give to him; and if he seeks refuge with Me, I will surely grant him refuge. I have never hesitated over

---

## Page 723

anything I am to do as I hesitate over taking the soul of My believing servant: he dislikes death, and I dislike causing him distress, yet he must undergo it.’”

The proponents of the unity of existence invoke this hadith as evidence, yet it is evidence against them in many respects. Among them is that He said:

“Whoever shows enmity toward a friend of Mine has challenged Me to war.”

He thus affirmed Himself, His friend, and His friend’s enemy: these are three. Then He said:

“Nothing by which My servant draws near to Me compares with fulfilling what I have made obligatory upon him. My servant continues to draw near to Me through voluntary acts of worship until I love him.”

He thus affirmed a servant who draws near to Him through obligatory acts, then through voluntary acts, and who continues drawing near through voluntary acts until He loves him. When He loves him, the servant hears through Him, sees through Him, strikes through Him, and walks through Him.

According to these people, however, both before the servant draws near through voluntary acts and afterward, He is the very servant himself and every other created being. Thus, He is the servant’s abdomen and thigh; they do not restrict this to the four bodily members mentioned in the hadith. The hadith concerns a particular, qualified state, whereas they make an unrestricted, universal claim. How far apart the two are!

Likewise, they may invoke the following passage from the authentic hadith:

“Allah manifests Himself to them on the Day of Resurrection, then comes to them in a form other than the form in which they saw Him the first time. He says, ‘I am your Lord.’ They say, ‘We seek refuge in Allah from you. We shall remain here until our Lord comes to us. When our Lord comes, we shall recognize Him.’ Then He comes to them in the form in which they saw Him the first time and says, ‘I am your Lord.’ They say, ‘You are our Lord.’”

They make this evidence for their assertion that He is seen in this world in every form—indeed, that He is every form.

---

## Page 724

This hadith is evidence against them in this respect as well, for they draw no distinction between this world and the Hereafter. In their view, even in the Hereafter, He is the very people who refused to recognize Him and said, “We seek refuge in Allah from you. We shall remain here until our Lord comes to us.” These heretics say that one who knows Him recognizes Him in every form, and that those who failed to recognize Him in certain forms on the Day of Resurrection did so because their knowledge was deficient.

This reveals their ignorance. Those who refused to recognize Him on the Day of Resurrection, then recognized Him when He manifested Himself to them in the form in which they had seen Him the first time, are the prophets and the believers. Their refusal was something for which He—glorified and exalted be He—praised them. He tested them in this way so that they would not follow anyone other than the Lord whom they had worshipped. Hence the hadith states:

“He questions them and makes them steadfast, while the caller has proclaimed, ‘Let every people follow what they used to worship.’”

These heretics may then be told: If, according to you, He is the one manifest in every form, then He is the one rejected, and He is the one rejected. Thus, one of them said to another, “Whoever tells you that anything other than Allah exists in the universe has lied.” The other replied, “Then who is it that lied?”

Ibn ‘Arabī mentioned that he went in to see a disciple of his who was in seclusion and needed to defecate. The disciple said, “I see nothing other than Him to urinate on.” His shaykh replied, “Then where does what comes out of your belly come from?” He answered, “You have relieved my distress.”

Two of their shaykhs—this al-Tilimsānī and al-Shīrāzī—passed a dead, mangy dog. Al-Shīrāzī asked al-Tilimsānī, “Is this also part of His essence?” Al-Tilimsānī replied, “Is there anything outside it?”

---

## Page 725

Al-Tilimsānī had also led astray an ascetic, devout shaykh in Jerusalem named Abū Ya‘qūb al-Maghribī al-Mubtalā (the Afflicted), to the point that he would say:

“Existence is one, and it is Allah; yet I do not see the One, nor do I see Allah.”

He would also say:

“The Quran and the Sunnah speak of a duality of existence, yet existence is one, with no duality in it.”

He made these words his formula of glorification, reciting them as one recites words glorifying Allah.

As for the poet’s words:

“When the yearning lover reaches perfection in love,
And loses awareness of the One remembered in remembrance’s overwhelming force,
It is truly witnessed, when love beholds him,
That the prayers of those who know Allah are unbelief.”

These words, besides being unbelief, are the words of an ignorant person who does not understand what he is saying. For annihilation and loss of awareness consist in becoming so absorbed in the One remembered that one loses awareness of remembrance, so absorbed in the One known that one loses awareness of knowing, and so absorbed in the One worshipped that one loses awareness of worship—until that which once did not exist vanishes from awareness, and He who has always existed remains. This is the station of annihilation that befalls many who tread the spiritual path because they cannot attain complete spiritual perception corresponding to reality.

By contrast, annihilation sanctioned by the revealed law consists in being so devoted to worshipping Him that one abandons worship of anything besides Him; so devoted to loving Him that one abandons love of anything besides Him; so devoted to fearing Him that one abandons fear of anything besides Him; and so devoted to obeying Him that one abandons obedience to anything besides Him. This is the realization of monotheism and faith.

As for the third kind of annihilation—losing awareness of the existence of anything other than Allah, to the point of regarding the Creator’s existence as the very existence of creation—this is the doctrine of these heretics who profess the unity of existence.

---

## Page 726

The point here is that his words, “He loses awareness of the One remembered,” are the words of an ignorant person. This is not praiseworthy at all. Rather, what is praiseworthy is to become so absorbed in the One remembered that one loses awareness of remembrance—not to lose awareness of the One remembered amid remembrance’s overwhelming force. Unless, of course, he means that he lost awareness of the One remembered, beheld the created being and perceived it as the Creator, and perceived existence as one, or something similar among these corrupt perceptions. This is the spiritual perception of the heretics, not that of those who affirm Allah’s oneness. By my life, anyone who experiences this heretical perception will indeed regard the prayers of those who know Allah as unbelief.

The cosmos calls to you: “Do you not hear me?
Who joined my scattered parts, and who dispersed me?
Look upon me and take heed:
Within me is only the existence of Him who brought me into being.”

These are among the sayings of these heretics. Their sayings are contradictory unbelief, invalid both in reason and in religion. For if the cosmos contains only the existence of Him who brought it into being, then that existence would itself be the cosmos that calls, the addressee being called, the scattered parts that are joined together and dispersed, and the addressee who is told, “Look.”

It would then follow that this necessary, eternal, beginningless existence had brought itself into being, dispersed itself, and joined itself together. This combines two contradictories: that which exists necessarily in itself cannot be produced or made. One and the same thing cannot be both creator and created, beginningless and brought into being, necessary in itself and necessary through another. This combines two contradictories.

---

## Page 727

For a necessary being is one whose essence does not admit nonexistence, whereas a contingent being is one whose essence admits nonexistence. It is therefore impossible for one and the same thing both to admit and not to admit nonexistence. A beginningless being is one whose existence has no beginning, whereas a being brought into existence has a beginning. It is therefore impossible for one and the same thing to be both beginningless and brought into existence.

Were their intent in these words not already known, they could conceivably mean: “Within me is only the existence created by Him who brought me into being.” Attributing existence to Allah would then denote His ownership of it. Yet it is known that the speaker did not intend this. Moreover, this expression is not used in that sense: “Allah’s existence” means the existence of His own essence, not the existence of His creatures.

The same applies to the verse:

“The very existence of the cosmos is a witness to creation
That no being other than the Real has existence.”

The speaker means that the existence of the cosmos is the very existence of the Real. This is the doctrine of those who advocate the oneness of existence. If, however, he meant that the existence of every created being comes from the Real, Most High—that nothing has existence of itself, but only from its Lord; that things, considered in themselves, have no claim to anything but nonexistence, and acquire existence only from their Creator and Maker; and that they are perpetually dependent upon Him, never independent of Him for even a moment, whether in this world or the Hereafter—then he would have intended a sound meaning. This is the position held by people of reason and religion, both earlier and later generations.

But the statements of these advocates of the oneness of existence are contradictory. Hence they say:

---

## Page 728

a thing and its opposite. Yet his words:

“From Him, and to His exalted height, it comes forth and returns,”

contradict the oneness of existence. For what is it that comes forth from Him and returns to Him, if there is only one being?

His words:

“I am nothing in the fabric of the cosmos,
For I am like a shifting shadow,”

also contradict the oneness of existence, because a shadow is distinct from that which casts it. Thus, likening the created being to a shadow necessarily entails affirming two distinct beings. The same applies to likening it to a ray of light: the sun’s rays are not the sun’s disk itself. Likewise, this applies to likening it to the light of a lamp or anything similar. Christians use these comparisons to illustrate divine indwelling and union.

(I said to one of them who was present with me and spoke along these lines: “If you liken the created being to the rays of the sun and fire, and the Creator to fire and the sun, then this comparison makes no distinction between Christ and anyone else. On this view, everything other than Allah is comparable to rays and light. What, then, distinguishes Christ from Abraham and Moses? Indeed, on this view, what distinguishes him from any other created being?”

I kept repeating this argument to him, with a group of people present, until he understood it thoroughly. It became clear to him and those present that their claim was false and had no basis in reality, and that what they affirmed concerning Christ was either impossible for anyone or shared by Christ and others. In either case, singling Christ out in this way was invalid.

(I also explained to him) that there was no miraculous sign brought by Christ for which Moses had not brought a greater one.

---

## Page 729

for although Christ, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought the dead back to life, those whom Allah brought back to life through Moses were more numerous, including those who said:

“‘We will never believe you until we see Allah openly’—then the thunderbolt seized you.”

Allah then raised them after their death, as He said:

“Then We raised you after your death.”

There was also the man who was struck with part of the cow, and other instances. Several other prophets brought the dead back to life, and Christians acknowledge this.

As for turning the staff into a snake, this is greater than bringing a dead person back to life. The dead person had previously been alive, so life was restored to a place where it had already existed. But turning a piece of dry wood into a living creature that swallows staffs and ropes is a greater demonstration of power and a rarer occurrence: Allah brings the dead back to life, but He does not turn wood into snakes.

As for sending down the table from heaven, the manna and quails that descended daily upon the people of Moses, together with the water that gushed from the rock for them, were greater than that. A constant supply of sweet food or meat is finer in kind and greater in quantity than the olives, fish, and other things that were on the table.

I mentioned similar points to him, showing that singling Christ out for union with God and claiming divinity for him) has no basis. Everything else mentioned concerning him is either shared by him and other created beings, or shared by him and other prophets and messengers. Moreover, some messengers, such as Abraham and Moses, may have been more perfect in these respects than he was. As for

---

## Page 730

his creation from a woman without a man, the creation of Eve from a man without a woman is more wondrous still. He was created in a woman’s womb, which is familiar, unlike creation from a man’s rib, which is not. Nothing is mentioned concerning Christ, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, without some other descendant of Adam having shared it, or something greater. It is therefore known with certainty that singling Christ out is groundless: if what they claim for him is possible, it is not exclusive to him; if it is impossible, it exists neither in him nor in anyone else.

For this reason, these advocates of union say: “The Christians fell into unbelief only by making it exclusive to Christ.” This, too, is false, for union has both general and particular forms.

The point here is that these advocates of union contradict their doctrine of oneness when they liken one of themselves to a changing shadow). The same applies to another’s words:

“I yearn for Him, though He is my heart;
Is any man of ardent longing seen, other than me, yearning for his own heart?
He veils my gaze from Him, for He is my very eye;
His distance is only due to His exceeding nearness.”
