Quite apart from the unbelief and union he intends by these words, they are self-contradictory, for a thing’s yearning for itself is contradictory. Hence his words:

“Is any man of ardent longing seen, other than me, yearning for his own heart?”

His statement, “His distance is only due to His exceeding nearness,” is also contradictory, for there is neither nearness nor distance according to

---

## Page 731

the advocates of oneness, for nearness and distance require two entities, one of which draws near to the other. A single entity neither draws near to itself nor moves away from itself.

As for the statement:

“Monotheism has no tongue, yet all tongues are its tongue,”

this, too, is a saying of the advocates of oneness. Besides being unbelief, it is self-contradictory. For it is necessarily known in Islam that the tongue of polytheism cannot be the tongue of monotheism. Nor are the words of the polytheists who said:

“Never abandon your gods, and never abandon Wadd, Suwāʿ, Yaghūth, Yaʿūq, or Nasr,”

or those who said:

“We worship them only so that they may bring us nearer to Allah,”

or those who said:

“We will not abandon our gods on your word, nor will we believe in you.”

“We say only that some of our gods have afflicted you with evil,”

or those who said:

“Burn him and support your gods,”

or the words of others like them, the tongue of monotheism.

As for this statement’s contradiction of their own fundamental premise: if existence is one, affirming multiplicity is a contradiction. Thus, if someone says, “Existence is one,” and another says, “It is not one; rather, it is multiple,” these two statements contradict each other, so neither can be identical to the other.

When someone says, “All tongues are its tongue,” he explicitly affirms multiplicity by saying “all tongues.” This entails that one tongue is not identical to another. Multiplicity is thus established, and oneness disproved.

---

## Page 732

And everything these people or anyone else says contradicts their fundamental premise, for they are compelled to affirm multiplicity.

If they say, “Existence is one, in the sense that existing things share the designation ‘existence,’” this is correct. However, when existing things share a single designation, the existence of one is not identical to the existence of another. Rather, they share a general, universal name, just as they share the names that grammarians call generic nouns and that logicians classify as genus, species, differentia, property, and common accident.

Sharing these names necessarily entails that the individual entities are distinct and that one of the things sharing a name is not the other. This is among the considerations by which we know that the existence of the True One is distinct from the existence of created things. Indeed, this distinction is greater than the distinction between one existing thing and another. If the existence of the celestial sphere is distinct from and unlike the existence of a tiny ant or a mosquito, then the existence of the True One, Most High, is far more distinct from the existence of every created thing than the existence of that created thing is from the existence of another.

This and other considerations demonstrate the falsity of that shaykh’s statement:

“Only the One knows monotheism. Monotheism cannot be properly expressed, for it can be expressed only through something other; and whoever affirms something other has no monotheism.”

Besides being unbelief, this statement is self-contradictory. His saying, “Only one knows monotheism,” entails that there is one who knows it and that someone other than him does not know it. This distinguishes between one who knows it and one who does not, and affirms two: one who knows it and the other who does not

---

## Page 733

and affirms a distinction between the one who knows it and the one who does not. His subsequent statement, “Whoever affirms something other has no monotheism,” contradicts this.

His statement, “Monotheism cannot be properly expressed,” is unbelief by the consensus of Muslims. Allah has expressed His oneness, and His Messenger has expressed it. The Quran is filled with references to monotheism; indeed, it was to proclaim monotheism that Allah sent the messengers and revealed the scriptures. The Most High says:

“And ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: Have We appointed gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

The Most High also says:

“And We sent no messenger before you without revealing to him: There is no deity except Me, so worship Me.”

If monotheism could not be properly expressed, no one would ever have spoken of it. Yet the finest words anyone has ever uttered are the affirmation of monotheism, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best remembrance is ‘There is no deity except Allah,’ and the best supplication is ‘Praise be to Allah.’”

He also said:

“Whoever’s last words are ‘There is no deity except Allah’ will enter Paradise.”

However, the monotheism to which these heretics refer—namely, the unity of existence—is inherently impossible: its realization in external reality is inconceivable. Concrete, individual oneness cannot apply to two distinct things. Existence is one only in kind, meaning that the designation ‘existent’ is a general designation encompassing every existent, just as the designations ‘body,’ ‘human being,’ and the like encompass every body and every human being. This body is not that body, and this human being is not that human being. Likewise, this existence is not that existence.

---

## Page 734

As for his statement, “It can only be expressed through something other,” the reply is:

(First, monotheism is expressed through speech, and Allah expresses His oneness through His speech. According to the early generations and the imams, Allah’s speech, knowledge, power, and other attributes are not to be described without qualification as either “Allah” or “other than Allah.”

The word “other” may mean something that exists separately from another, and Allah’s attributes do not exist separately from Him. It may also mean something that is not identical to Him, and an attribute of Allah is not identical to Him. Thus, according to one usage, it is said to be other than Him, while according to the other, it is not. Neither expression should therefore be used without explaining what is intended.

Otherwise, an innovator might argue, “If Allah’s attribute is other than Him, then everything other than Allah is created,” and use this to claim that Allah’s knowledge, power, and speech are not attributes subsisting in Him, but are instead created in something other than Him. Such denial of the Creator’s attributes and repudiation of His perfection constitute one of the gravest forms of heresy. This is the doctrine of the Jahmiyyah, whom the early generations and the imams declared unbelievers in general terms, although a particular individual is not judged an unbeliever until the proof whose rejection constitutes unbelief has been established against him.

Furthermore, these heretics may be told: If nothing other than Him exists in any respect, it necessarily follows that created beings’ speech, eating, drinking, sexual intercourse, fornication, unbelief, polytheism, and all the abominable acts they commit are the very existence of Allah.

It is well known that anyone who makes these things attributes of Allah is among the worst of people in unbelief and misguidance. Whoever says that they are identical with Allah’s existence is even more unbelieving and misguided, for attributes and accidents are not identical with the existent entity that subsists in itself. Leaders of these heretics, such as Ibn ‘Arabī, say:

---

## Page 735

“All speech in existence is His speech;
Its prose and verse are alike to us.”

Thus they make the speech of created beings—including unbelief, lies, and other such things—the speech of Allah. This heretic, however, went further than they did, making the speech and worship of created beings identical with His existence. He did not regard these as His speech; rather, he denied that they were His speech, lest he affirm anything other than Him.

The Quran, the Sunnah, consensus, and self-evident rational knowledge establish that things other than Allah Most High exist, and that all created things besides Him are other than Allah Most High: they are neither Allah nor any of His attributes. This is why Allah condemned those who worshipped others besides Him—if nothing other than Him existed, such condemnation would not be valid. Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘Is it other than Allah that you order me to worship, O ignorant ones?’”

And He Most High says:

“Say, ‘Should I take as a protector anyone other than Allah, the Creator of the heavens and the earth?’”

And He Most High says:

“Is there any creator other than Allah who provides for you from the heaven and earth?”

And He Most High says:

“Then should I seek a judge other than Allah, when it is He who has sent down to you the Book explained in detail?”

Likewise, the statement:

“I found that love is not the intended goal, because it can only be from one to another, and no other exists. And I found that monotheism is not the intended goal, because monotheism can only be a servant’s affirmation of a Lord’s oneness. If people were fair, they would see neither a servant nor an object of worship.”

This is speech whose unbelief, heresy, and contradiction are plain.

---

## Page 736

For the Book, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the Muslims affirm Allah’s love for His believing servants and their love for Him, as in His saying, Most High:

“But those who believe are stronger in love for Allah.”

And His saying:

“He loves them and they love Him.”

And His saying:

“More beloved to you than Allah and His Messenger.”

And His sayings:

“Indeed, Allah loves the righteous.”

“He loves those who do good.”

“He loves those who constantly repent and loves those who purify themselves.”

“He loves those who act justly.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“Whoever possesses three qualities will thereby taste the sweetness of faith: that Allah and His Messenger are dearer to him than all else; that he loves another person solely for Allah’s sake; and that he hates returning to unbelief, after Allah has saved him from it, as much as he hates being thrown into the Fire.”

The early generations of the Muslim community and its leading scholars unanimously affirmed Allah Most High’s love for His believing servants and their love for Him. This is the foundation of the religion of Allah’s intimate friend, the leader of those devoted to pure monotheism, peace be upon him.

The first to proclaim this in Islam was al-Ja'd ibn Dirham. Khālid ibn 'Abd Allah al-Qasrī sacrificed him on the Day of Sacrifice in Wāsit, saying:

“O people, offer your sacrifices; may Allah accept them. I am sacrificing al-Ja'd ibn Dirham, for he claimed that Allah did not take Abraham as an intimate friend and did not speak to Moses with actual speech. Exalted is Allah far above what al-Ja'd says!”

Then he came down and slaughtered him.

His statement, “Love can only be from one to another, and no other exists,” is false in every respect.

His claim that love “can only be from one to another” is incorrect, for a person loves himself and is not other than himself, and Allah loves Himself. His statement, “No other exists,” is false, for the created being

---

## Page 737

is distinct from the Creator, and the believers are distinct from Allah, yet they love Him. The claim is therefore false. Each of the argument’s two premises is false: his statement, “Love can only be from one to another,” and his statement, “No other exists.” For another does exist, and one who loves may love himself. Thus, many of the monists contradict him on this point and speak as Ibn al-Fārid did.

Likewise, he says:

“The affirmation of divine oneness can only be a servant’s affirmation of his Lord’s oneness. If people were fair-minded, they would see neither a worshipper nor an object of worship.”

Both premises are false. Allah Himself affirms His own oneness, as He, Most High, says:

“Allah bears witness that there is no deity except Him.”

The Quran is full of Allah’s affirmation of His own oneness. He Himself affirms it in such statements as:

“And your God is one God.”

And:

“Allah has said, ‘Do not take two gods. He is but one God.’”

And:

“So know that there is no deity except Allah.”

And other such passages.

As for the second premise, his statement that, if people were fair-minded, they would see neither a worshipper nor an object of worship—besides being the utmost unbelief and heresy—is self-contradictory. If there is neither a worshipper nor an object of worship, but everything is one, then who are those who are not fair-minded? If they are Allah, then Allah would be the one who is not fair-minded. If they are other than Allah, then the existence of another has been established.

Then, if they interpret this in accordance with their unbelief and say that Allah is the one who is not fair-minded, and that He is the one who eats, drinks, and disbelieves—as many of them do, as when one of them said to his shaykh, “When the Sufi’s state is sound, he eats through Allah,” and the other replied, “When the Sufi’s state is sound, it is Allah who eats”—and indeed Ibn ‘Arabī and other shaykhs of theirs have explicitly stated that it is He who grows hungry and thirsty

---

## Page 738

and falls ill, urinates, and engages in sexual intercourse as both the active and the passive partner, and that He is characterized by every deficiency and fault, because that, in their view, is perfection. As he said in al-Fusūs (The Bezels):

“The One who is exalted in Himself is He whose perfection encompasses all matters of existence and all relations of nonexistence, whether praiseworthy according to custom, reason, and religious law, or blameworthy according to custom, reason, and religious law. This belongs exclusively to the one designated by the name Allah.”

He also said:

“Do you not see that the Real manifests Himself with the attributes of originated beings—and has said this about Himself—and with attributes of deficiency and blame? Do you not see that the creature manifests the attributes of the Creator? All of them, from first to last, are attributes of the servant, just as all the servant’s attributes, from first to last, are attributes of Allah, Most High.”

One who speaks in such terms contradicts himself, for he may be told:

“You, who are perfect in yourself and see neither a worshipper nor an object of worship—we shall treat you as your doctrine requires: you will be beaten, hurt, humiliated, and slapped.”

If he protests against whoever does this to him, complains, cries out, and weeps, he is told:

“There is no other, no worshipper, and no object of worship. No one other than yourself has done this to you. Rather, the one who strikes is the one who is struck; the one who insults is the one who is insulted; and the worshipper is the object of worship.”

If he says, “He protested against himself and complained about himself,” he is also told, “Then say: ‘He worshipped himself.’” If he affirms a wrongdoer and one who is wronged, while holding that they are one, he is told, “Then affirm a worshipper and an object of worship, while holding that they are one.”

Then he is asked:

“Is the one who laughs and strikes the very same as the one who weeps and cries out? Is the one whose hunger and thirst have been satisfied the very same as the one who is hungry and thirsty?”

If he acknowledges that he is

---

## Page 739

distinct from the other, he has affirmed a distinction. If he affirms a distinction between these two, then distinguishing between the worshipper and the object of worship is all the more warranted.

If he says, “Rather, they are one and the same,” he is treated as the sophists are treated, for this statement is among the most abhorrent forms of sophistry. He is told:

“If they are one and the same, then we shall strike you and kill you, and the same thing will have killed and destroyed itself.”

A person may wrong himself through sins and say:

“Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves.”

This is because his own soul urged him to evil, and the soul continually urges evil. Yet its commanding and its acting are distinct aspects: there must be some multiplicity, either in the essence or in the attributes. Everyone knows through sensory perception and immediate certainty that the man who wronged another is not that other man, nor is his case like that of a man who wronged himself.

If this is true of created beings, then the Creator is far more distinct from created beings than one of them is from another. Glory be to Him! Exalted is He far above what the wrongdoers say.

Were it not that the adherents of this doctrine have grown numerous, gained prominence, and spread widely—and that many people regard them as the foremost of mankind, the spiritual masters of Islam, masters of monotheism and realization, and the finest followers of the spiritual path, even preferring them to the prophets, the messengers, and the greatest religious masters—we would have had no need to expose the falsity of these statements and make this misguidance clear.

But it should be understood that misguidance has no limit, and that when minds become corrupt, their misguidance no longer has any conceivable limit. Glory be to Him who has made such distinctions within humankind, making some the finest of all beings and others worse than devils. But likening these people to the prophets

---

## Page 740

, Allah’s allies—like likening Musaylima the Liar to the foremost of those endowed with understanding—is precisely what makes it obligatory to wage jihad against these heretics who corrupt both worldly life and religion.

The purpose here is to refute these statements and distinguish guidance from misguidance.

As for whether those who uttered them repented and died as Muslims, that rests with the All-Knowing King. Allah accepts His servants’ repentance and pardons their misdeeds. It is possible that He has accepted the repentance of those who held these views. Allah Most High is the Forgiver of sin, the Accepter of repentance, severe in punishment. However great the sin, and however grave and monstrous the disbelief, repentance erases it all. No sin is too great for Allah, glorified be He, to forgive when its perpetrator repents. Indeed, He forgives those who repent for associating partners with Him and for other sins, as He Most High says:

“Say, ‘O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of Allah’s mercy. Indeed, Allah forgives all sins. He is indeed the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.’”

This verse is general and unrestricted in scope, because it concerns those who repent. As for His statement:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whom He wills.”

Its scope is restricted and specific, because it concerns those who do not repent. He does not forgive them for associating partners with Him, while forgiveness for lesser sins depends upon the will of Allah Most High.

The aforementioned account of the man who said that he had swallowed the entire world and wished to say, “I am the Truth,” and its counterpart, in which it was said, “Only the most ignorant of Allah’s creatures or the most knowledgeable of them would claim divinity,” belong to this same category.

---

## Page 741

And the faqīr who said, “Allah has created no one less rational than someone who claims to be a god—such as Pharaoh, Nimrod, and others like them,” was the one who was right: he spoke correctly and expressed himself aptly. Yet these heretics exalt Pharaoh and others like him, claiming that they are better than Moses and others like him.

Indeed, Bahā’ al-Dīn ‘Abd al-Sayyid, who had been a judge of the Jews and embraced Islam, becoming a good Muslim—may Allah have mercy on him—told me about this. He had met al-Shīrāzī, one of their shaykhs, who invited him to adopt this doctrine and made it seem appealing to him. When he told me about it, I explained their misguidance and disbelief to him, and that their doctrine was of the same kind as Pharaoh’s.

He then told me that when Hasan al-Shīrāzī invited him to adopt this doctrine, he said to him, “This doctrine of yours resembles Pharaoh’s doctrine.”

He replied, “Yes, and we hold Pharaoh’s doctrine.”

‘Abd al-Sayyid had not yet embraced Islam at that time. He said, “I will not abandon Moses and turn to Pharaoh.”

“Why not?” he asked.

“Because Moses drowned Pharaoh,” he replied.

This left al-Shīrāzī unable to answer. Thus, ‘Abd al-Sayyid argued against him on the basis of the victory Allah had granted Moses by His decree, rather than on the basis that Moses was a truthful messenger.

I said to ‘Abd al-Sayyid, “And he admitted to you that he held Pharaoh’s doctrine?”

“Yes,” he replied.

I said, “When an opponent admits the point, no evidence is needed. I had intended to explain to you that their doctrine is Pharaoh’s doctrine. If he has already admitted this, then the purpose has been achieved.”

These statements and others like them are among the gravest falsehoods. We have pointed out some of what enables their meaning to be understood and their falsehood recognized. It is obligatory to condemn them, for condemning this evil, which has spread among many Muslims, takes precedence over condemning the religion of the Jews and Christians, which does not lead Muslims astray—especially since these people’s statements are worse than those of the Jews, Christians, and Pharaoh. And whoever has understood

---

## Page 742

their meaning and embraced them is among the hypocrites against whom Allah commanded us to strive, saying, exalted be He:

“Strive against the disbelievers and the hypocrites, and be harsh with them.”

When hypocrisy becomes severe, the person marked by it is worse than the disbelievers among the People of the Book and is in the lowest depth of the Fire. These statements admit no defensible interpretation. Even supposing that some of them could linguistically bear a sound meaning, they may be understood in that sense only when their speaker’s intent is unknown. Yet these people’s intent is known, just as the religion of the Jews, Christians, and Rāfidah (Rejectors) is known. They have written books and composed poems on the subject, and their statements explain one another. Their intent is unmistakably known; none disputes it except an ignorant person whose objections deserve no attention.

It is obligatory to explain these statements’ meaning and expose their underlying intent to anyone who thinks well of them, and to anyone whom it is feared may think well of them or go astray. Their harm to Muslims is greater than the harm of poisons they consume without knowing they are poisons, and greater than the harm of thieves and traitors whom they do not recognize as such.

The utmost harm these thieves and traitors can cause is a person’s death or the loss of his wealth. This is a calamity in his worldly life that may become a cause of mercy for him in the Hereafter. These people, however, give others the drink of disbelief and heresy in the vessels of Allah’s prophets and allies. They wear the garb of those who strive in Allah’s cause, while inwardly they are among those who wage war against Allah and His Messenger. They present the words of disbelievers and hypocrites in the verbal forms used by Allah’s true allies. A man joins them intending to become a believer and an ally of Allah, only to become a hypocrite and an enemy of Allah.

---

## Page 743

I once likened them to people who undertook to lead a group of pilgrims on Hajj, but instead took them to Cyprus to convert them to Christianity. One of their followers who had come to recognize their misguidance said to me, “Had they taken us to Cyprus, they would have made us Christians, whereas these people were making us worse than Christians.” It is just as he said.

I have seen and heard of countless good, religious people who thought these people were among Allah’s allies and that their words were those of people with authentic spiritual understanding. Some embraced their heresy, understood it, and became one of them. Others believed what they did not know, revered what they did not understand, and accepted things unknown to them as true. These are the most upright of the misguided groups. They are like someone who reveres the enemies of Allah and His Messenger without knowing that they are their enemies, and who allies himself with polytheists and the People of the Book, thinking them to be people of faith and understanding. Through these ignorant people who revere them, harm has befallen the Muslims that none but the Lord of all worlds can measure.

This response has allowed for no more than these remarks. Allah knows best what is correct.

---

## Page 744

He was also asked:

What do the esteemed scholars, the leaders of the religion and guides of the Muslims—may Allah be pleased with them all—say about the statements contained in “The Bezels of Wisdom” and similar statements which plainly indicate their speaker’s belief that the Lord and the servant are one and the same, with no distinction between them, and that nothing else exists? Examples include the poetic line:

“He and I are one; there is nothing else with us.”

And likewise:

“I am the one I love, and the one I love is me.”

And:

“If I am Laylā and Laylā is me.”

Another example is the saying: “If people knew the Truth, they would see neither a worshipper nor an object of worship.”

The substance of these statements is found neither in the Book of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, nor in the Sunnah, nor in the words of the rightly guided caliphs and the righteous predecessors.

Those who make such statements claim to love Allah, glorified and exalted. Yet Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you.’”

And Allah, glorified and exalted, has described the best of

---

## Page 745

His creation as servants in several passages. Thus, the Most High says concerning the last of His messengers, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Then He revealed to His servant what He revealed.”

Likewise, He says concerning Jesus, peace be upon him:

“He was only a servant upon whom We bestowed Our favour.”

And the Most High says:

“The Messiah would never disdain to be a servant of Allah, nor would the angels who are near to Him”—and the rest of the verse.

Christians are disbelievers for making such a claim about Jesus alone. What, then, of someone who holds this belief, sometimes concerning himself and sometimes concerning beautiful forms—women and beardless youths? They say that this belief contains a hidden secret and a true inner meaning, and that it belongs to those truths known only to the very elite of creation.

Do these statements contain a hidden secret such that anyone who believes in Allah, the Last Day, His Books and His messengers must strive to hold fast to them and attain their underlying truths, as these people claim? Or is their inner meaning just like their outward meaning? Is this belief the very essence of faith in Allah, His Messenger and what he brought, or is it unbelief itself?

Must a Muslim follow, in this matter, the words of the Muslim scholars, the heirs of the prophets and messengers, or adhere to the words of these people who are astray and lead others astray? If he abandons what the leading scholars of the Muslims have unanimously agreed upon and agrees with these people, what will Allah’s judgment concerning him be on the Day of Recompense?

Give us a ruling—may you be rewarded, and may Allah, the Most Generous, reward you.

---

## Page 746

Shaykh al-Islām Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-‘Abbās Ahmad ibn ‘Abd al-Halīm ibn ‘Abd al-Salām Ibn Taymiyya—may Allah have mercy on him—replied:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds. What is contained in the book “Fusūs al-Hikam” (The Bezels of Wisdom), and in similar writings, is unbelief in both its inward and outward meanings; its inward meaning is more abhorrent than its outward meaning. This is known as the doctrine of the proponents of the oneness of existence, divine indwelling, and union. They call themselves “those who have attained true realization.”

These people are of two types:

One type professes this without qualification. Such is the doctrine of Ibn ‘Arabī, the author of the Fusūs, and others like him, including Ibn Sab‘īn, Ibn al-Fārid, al-Qūnawī, al-Shushtarī, al-Tilimsānī, and their like, who say that existence is one. They say that the existence of the creation is the existence of the Creator. They do not acknowledge two beings, one of which created the other. Rather, they say: “The Creator is the creation, and the creation is the Creator.”

---

## Page 747

And they say: “The existence of idols is the existence of Allah, and those who worship idols worship nothing but Allah.”

They also say: “The Real is described by every deficient and blameworthy attribute ascribed to created beings.”

They say that those who worshipped the calf worshipped none but Allah, and that Moses rebuked Aaron because Aaron had condemned their worship of the calf. They claim that Moses was among those possessing spiritual knowledge who see the Real in everything—indeed, who see Him as everything itself—and that Pharaoh spoke truthfully when he said:

“I am your lord, the Most High.”

Indeed, they say that he is the Real Himself, along with similar statements made by the author of the Fusūs.

The foremost of their masters of true realization says: “The entire Quran is polytheism, because it distinguishes between the Lord and the servant. Monotheism is found only in our words.”

He was asked: “If existence is one, why is a wife lawful and a mother forbidden?”

He replied: “To us, all are one. But these veiled people said, ‘Forbidden,’ so we said, ‘Forbidden to you.’”

The same is found in Ibn al-Fārid’s poetry, in the ode he entitled Nazm al-Sulūk (The Poem of the Spiritual Path), such as his words:

To her I offer my prayers at the station … and witness in them that she has prayed to me.
We both are one who prays, prostrating … to his own reality in unity, in every prostration.

---

## Page 748

And none but myself ever prayed to me, … nor, in any prostration, was my prayer offered to another.

And his words:

I have always been her, and she has always been me … there is no difference; rather, my own self loved itself.

And his words:

I was a messenger sent from myself to myself … and my own self took my signs as proof of me.

The underlying meaning of these people’s statements, and others like them, contains greater unbelief and heresy than their outward wording. Their outward wording may be thought to resemble the words of spiritually knowledgeable shaykhs devoted to true realization and monotheism. Their underlying meaning, however, contains greater unbelief, falsehood, and ignorance than the words of Jews, Christians, and idol-worshippers.

Thus, the more any of them understood the inner meaning and true nature of this doctrine, the greater his unbelief and wickedness became. Such was al-Tilimsānī, who was among those most knowledgeable about this doctrine and most familiar with its true nature. This led him to put it into practice: he revered Jews, Christians, and polytheists, deemed forbidden things lawful, and wrote books for the Nusayrīs according to their doctrine, affirming their polytheistic creed in them. Likewise, Ibn Sab‘īn was one of their leading figures, and he had his share of unbelief and sorcery

---

## Page 749

called sīmiyā, as well as agreement with Christians, the Qarmatians, and the Rāfidah, all in keeping with his principles. Thus, the more someone knew the inner meaning of this doctrine and agreed with its adherents, the more openly he displayed unbelief and heresy.

As for the ignorant who think well of these people’s statements without understanding them, believing them to resemble the words of spiritually knowledgeable shaykhs whose sound statements many people do not understand, you will find in them Islam, faith, and adherence to the Quran and Sunnah in proportion to their faith based on imitation. You will also find in them approval of these people, a favorable opinion of them, and deference to them in proportion to their ignorance and misguidance. It is inconceivable that anyone would praise these people except a heretical unbeliever or an ignorant, misguided person.

These people belong to the same category as the Jahmiyyah, who say:

“Allah, in His very essence, dwells in every place.”

However, the advocates of the oneness of existence have developed this doctrine more fully than the other Jahmiyyah.

As for the second type, it is the view of those who affirm divine indwelling and union in a particular individual, such as the Christians who affirmed this concerning Jesus the Messiah; the extremists who affirm it concerning ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib and a group from his household; the Hākimiyyah, who affirm it concerning al-Hākim; the Hallājiyyah, who affirm it concerning al-Hallāj; and the Yūnusiyyah, who affirm

---

## Page 750

this concerning Yūnus, and others like them who affirm the divinity of certain human beings and divine indwelling and union in them, without applying this universally to everything. Some of these people affirm this concerning certain women, beardless youths, kings, or others.

Their unbelief is worse than that of the Christians who said:

“Allah is the Messiah, the son of Mary.”

As for the first group, they affirm this without restriction, saying:

“The Christians disbelieved only because they restricted it to a particular individual.”

These people’s statements are worse than those of the Christians and contain contradictions of the same kind as those found in Christian statements. Thus, they sometimes speak of divine indwelling, sometimes of union, and sometimes of oneness, for their doctrine is inherently contradictory. This is why they mislead those who do not understand it.

All of this is unbelief, inwardly and outwardly, by the unanimous agreement of all Muslims. Whoever doubts these people’s unbelief after learning what they say and understanding the religion of Islam is himself an unbeliever, just like someone who doubts the unbelief of the Jews, Christians, and polytheists.

Yet these people confuse this with something else: what may happen to some who possess spiritual knowledge in the states of self-effacement, gathered awareness, overwhelming ecstasy, and spiritual intoxication. Because of the powerful hold that ecstasy and remembrance have over a person, he may experience a state in which he loses awareness of himself and others. Absorbed in the One he worships, he loses awareness of his worship; in the One he knows, of his knowledge; in the One he remembers, of his remembrance; and in the One whose presence he experiences, of his own existence.

---

## Page 751

Something similar may happen to those who love another created being. They relate, for example, that a man loved another man. The beloved threw himself into the sea, and the lover threw himself in after him. The beloved asked him:

“I fell in; what made you fall in?”

He replied:

“Absorbed in you, I lost awareness of myself and thought that you were me.”

They also recite:

- The glass is thin, the wine is clear;
So alike are they that the distinction is blurred.
It is as though there were wine but no cup,
And as though there were a cup but no wine.

This state occurs in many who follow the spiritual path, but it is neither inevitable for everyone on that path nor a praiseworthy ultimate goal. Rather, retaining reason, understanding, and knowledge, together with inward and outward affirmation of Allah’s oneness—as exemplified by our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions—is more perfect and complete.

What they call “self-effacement” takes three forms: ceasing to worship anything other than Allah; losing awareness of anything other than Allah; and the effacement of the existence of anything other than Allah.

The first is to become so absorbed in worshipping Allah that one ceases to worship anything else; through fearing Him, to cease fearing anything else; through hoping in Him, to cease hoping in anything else; through relying on Him, to cease relying on anything else; and through loving Him, to cease loving anything else. This is the essence of affirming Allah’s oneness and of sincere devotion to Him, with which Allah sent His messengers and revealed His Books. It is the realization of:

“There is no deity worthy of worship except Allah.”

For all devotion to anything other than Allah vanishes from his heart, and no devotion to anything other than Allah remains there. The more perfectly a person realizes this affirmation of Allah’s oneness, the greater his merit before Allah.

---

## Page 752

The second is to lose awareness of everything other than Allah. This is what many Sufis call the state of overwhelming absorption, self-effacement, unified awareness, and the like.

This has merit insofar as the heart turns towards Allah, but it is deficient insofar as the person does not perceive things as they truly are. For if he perceives that Allah is the Lord, Sovereign, and Creator of everything; that He alone is worthy of worship, with no deity besides Him; and that He sent the messengers, revealed the Books, commanded obedience to Himself and His messengers, and forbade disobedience to Himself and His messengers—thus perceiving the realities of His names, attributes, and decrees concerning creation and command—then his knowledge, perception, faith, and realization are more complete than if his perception of one meaning absorbs him so entirely that he loses awareness of another. And perceiving distinctions within unified awareness, and multiplicity within unity, is the sound perception that accords with reality. Yet if a person is overcome by a state that leaves him unable to perceive both, he is excused for his incapacity, not praised for his deficiency and ignorance.

The third is the effacement of the existence of anything other than Allah. This is the doctrine of the heretical proponents of the unity of existence, such as the author of al-Fusūs and his followers, who say:

“The existence of the Creator is the very existence of the created, and in reality there is nothing else whatsoever.”

Their doctrine constitutes greater disbelief than the doctrines of the Jews, Christians, and idol-worshippers.

Furthermore, friendship with Allah consists in being in accord with Him: loving what He loves, hating what He hates, being pleased with what pleases Him, being displeased with what displeases Him, commanding what He commands, forbidding what He forbids, befriending His friends, and opposing His enemies, as related in Sahīh al-Bukhārī

---

## Page 753

from Abū Hurayra, who reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Allah Most High says: ‘Whoever shows enmity towards a friend of Mine has challenged Me to war. Nothing by which My servant draws near to Me compares to fulfilling what I have made obligatory upon him. My servant continues to draw near to Me through voluntary acts of worship until I love him. When I love him, I become his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees, his hand with which he takes hold, and his foot with which he walks. Through Me he hears, through Me he sees, through Me he takes hold, and through Me he moves. If he asks Me, I will surely give to him; and if he seeks My protection, I will surely protect him. I have never hesitated over anything I am going to do as I hesitate over taking the soul of My believing servant: he dislikes death, and I dislike causing him distress, yet he cannot escape it.’”

This is the most authentic hadith narrated concerning the friends of Allah.

The heretics and proponents of union cite it in support of their doctrine, because He says:

“I become his hearing, his sight, his hand, and his foot.”

Yet the hadith is evidence against them in many respects. Among these is His statement:

“Whoever shows enmity towards a friend of Mine has challenged Me to war.”

He thus affirms an enemy who wages war and a friend distinct from that enemy, and He—glory be to Him—affirms both in relation to Himself.

Another is His statement:

“Nothing by which My servant draws near to Me compares to fulfilling what I have made obligatory upon him.”

He thus affirms a servant who draws near to his Lord, and a Lord who has imposed obligations upon him.

Another is His statement:

“My servant continues to draw near to Me through voluntary acts of worship until I love him.”

He thus affirms one who draws near and One to whom he draws near, and One who loves and a beloved distinct from Him.

All of this refutes their assertion that existence is one.

Another is His statement:

“When I love him, I become his hearing with which he hears, and his sight with which he sees

---

## Page 754

,” and so on.

For He grants His servant these things after loving him, whereas, according to them, He is one and the same before and after that love. According to them, He is also these bodily parts: the servant’s belly, private parts, hair, and everything else. They recognize neither plurality nor multiplicity in existence, yet they affirm ranks, forms of disclosure, and manifestations. If they regard these as existent, they contradict their own doctrine. If they regard them as subsisting in nonexistence—as Ibn ʿArabī says—or identify them with particular entities while holding that the Absolute is the Real, then they have based this on the view that the nonexistent is a thing, and on the view that universals subsist in external reality in addition to particular entities.

The first is the view of a group of the Muʿtazila, and it is Ibn ʿArabī’s view. The second is the view of a group of philosophers, and it is the view of al-Qūnawī, Ibn ʿArabī’s companion. Both views are false according to people of sound reason. Hence al-Tilimsānī was more astute than either of them: he affirmed nothing beyond existence itself. As it has been said:

The sea is nothing but the waves—nothing else,
Though the multitude of waves divides it.

Yet these misguided philosophers and Muʿtazila did not say that the existence of the creation is the existence of the Creator. These heretics, however, said that the two are identical. Thus, in one respect, they came to advocate indwelling, because existence is within every entity—or the reverse—and, in another respect, union, because the two are united. The essence of their doctrine is the oneness of existence.

---

## Page 755

The hadith also contains other indications that demonstrate the falsity of their doctrine.

The hadith is true, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stated. For Allah’s friend, through his perfect love for Allah and obedience to Him, comes to perceive and act for Allah and through Allah. Whatever he hears that the Real loves, he loves; whatever he hears that the Real hates, he hates. Whatever he sees that the Real loves, he loves; whatever he sees that the Real hates, he hates. His hearing and sight come to possess a light by which he distinguishes truth from falsehood, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the hadith whose authenticity is agreed upon:

“O Allah, place light in my heart, light in my sight, light in my hearing, light to my right, light to my left, light above me, light beneath me, light before me, and light behind me; and grant me light.”

Allah’s friend is in such accord with Allah that what is loved, hated, commanded, forbidden, and the like becomes the same for both. Thus, what the Real loves becomes what he loves; what the Real hates becomes what he hates; what the Real commands becomes what he commands; the Real’s friend becomes his friend, and the Real’s enemy becomes his enemy. Indeed, when one created being loves another with complete love, something of this kind arises between them, to the point that one may feel pain at the other’s pain and pleasure at the other’s pleasure. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The believers, in their mutual love, mercy, and compassion, are like a single body: when one limb suffers, the rest of the body responds with fever and sleeplessness.”

Thus, a believer is pleased by what pleases the believers and distressed by what distresses them. Whoever is not like this is not one of them.

---

## Page 756

Thus, this unity among believers does not mean that the essence of one is identical to that of the other, or dwells within it. Rather, it means their accord and unity in faith in Allah and His Messenger and in the branches of that faith, such as loving Allah and His Messenger and loving what Allah and His Messenger love.

If this is intelligible among believers, then when a servant is in accord with his Lord Most High concerning what He loves and hates, commands and forbids, and other such things that the Lord loves from His servant, how could the essence of one be the essence of the other, or dwell within it?

Once you understand these principles concerning the false doctrines of indwelling and union—whether universal or confined to particular individuals—as well as the states of people of faith, friendship with Allah Most High, accord with Him in what He loves and is pleased with, and the consequences of all this, the answers to the questioner’s questions will become clear to you. These people may find ambiguous, imprecise statements in the words of certain shaykhs and interpret them according to corrupt meanings, just as the Christians did with what was transmitted to them from the prophets. They abandon what is clear and follow what is ambiguous.

The statement, “The Lord and the servant are one and the same, with no distinction between them,” is outright disbelief, especially if it includes every created servant. If, however, what is meant is Allah’s believing servants and His God-fearing friends, then these are people whom He loves and who love Him, and who are in accord with Him concerning what He loves, is pleased with, and commands. Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him.

Since they are pleased with what He is pleased with and are displeased with what displeases Him, the Real is pleased when they are pleased and becomes angry when they are angry, for these necessarily go together on both sides.

---

## Page 757

Nor can it be said of even the best of these people: “The Lord and the servant are one and the same, with no distinction between them.” Rather, concerning the best of creation, one should say what Allah Most High has said:

“Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are only pledging allegiance to Allah. The hand of Allah is above their hands.”

And He said:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed Allah.”

And He said:

“Allah and His Messenger have a greater right that they should please Him.”

And He said:

“Indeed, those who offend Allah and His Messenger—Allah has cursed them in this world and the Hereafter.”

There are other statements of this kind.

As for all other servants, Allah is their Creator, Owner and Lord, and the Creator of their abilities and acts. When their acts accord with what He loves and is pleased with, He loves and honours those who perform them. When their acts provoke His displeasure and are hateful to Him, He hates and humiliates those who perform them. The servants’ acts are brought about and created by Allah; they are neither an attribute of His nor an act subsisting in His essence.

As for His words, Most High:

“It was not you who threw when you threw, but Allah who threw,”

they mean: it was not you who made what was thrown reach its target when you cast it; rather, Allah made it reach its target. For “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had thrown a handful of dust at the polytheists and said, ‘May the faces be disfigured!’” Allah then made it reach the polytheists’ faces and eyes, whereas the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, lacked the power to make it reach them.

Throwing has an initial stage, which is casting, and a final stage, which is reaching the target. Allah affirmed the initial stage for His Prophet in His words:

“When you threw,”

but denied that the Prophet brought about the final stage and attributed it to Himself in His words:

“But Allah who threw.”

Otherwise, what is affirmed cannot be identical to what is denied, for that would be a contradiction.

---

## Page 758

And although Allah Most High is the Creator of His servants’ acts, He does not describe Himself as those who perform these acts are described. He does not call Himself one who prays, fasts, eats or drinks. Glory be to Him! Exalted is He far above what the wrongdoers say.

As for the statement:

“There is no other,”

if the speaker means what the adherents of unity mean—namely, that nothing exists other than Allah—then this is outright unbelief.

If there were no other, He would not have said:

“Is it other than Allah I should take as a protector?”

Nor would He have said:

“Is it other than Allah that you order me to worship, O ignorant ones?”

For they were ordering him to worship idols. If there were nothing other than Allah, His words would not have been valid:

“Is it other than Allah that you order me to worship, O ignorant ones?”

Nor would He have said:

“Then is it other than Allah I should seek as judge, when it is He who has sent down to you the Book, explained in detail?”

Nor would Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, have said:

“Have you considered what you have been worshipping—you and your forefathers? Indeed, they are enemies to me, except the Lord of all worlds.”

Nor would he have said:

“Indeed, I am free of what you worship, except the One who created me; He will surely guide me.”

Abraham did not take his Lord as an enemy, nor did he disavow his Lord. If those gods whom they and their forefathers worshipped were not other than Allah, then Abraham would have disavowed Allah and taken Allah as an enemy. Far be it from Abraham to do such a thing!

These heretics initially deny the divine attributes and say:

“The Quran is either Allah or other than Allah.”

When they are told:

“Other than Allah,”

they say:

“Whatever is other than Allah is created.”

Yet ultimately they say:

“Nothing exists other than Allah.”

Or they say:

“The world is neither Allah nor other than Him.”

And they say:

---

## Page 759

“And every utterance in existence is His speech;
its prose and verse are all the same to us.”

They therefore object to the people of the Sunnah when they affirm the divine attributes without calling them “other than Allah,” yet they themselves do not call created things “other than Allah.” I have heard this contradiction from their own shaykhs. They are indeed in manifest error.

As for the poet’s words:

“I am the one I love, and the one I love is I?”

And his words:

“If I am Laylā and Laylā is I.”

The poet intended only union in the conventional sense, like the union between two lovers: each loves what the other loves, hates what the other hates, says what the other says, and does what the other does. This is similarity and likeness, not the union of one individual being with another. He had become so absorbed in his beloved that he lost awareness of himself, as another poet said:

“Absorbed in you, I lost sight of myself,
and thought that you were I.”

Either he is mistaken, overwhelmed by the loss of self-awareness, or he means similarity, likeness, and unity in what is sought and feared—not union of essences. If, however, he intends union of essences while understanding what he is saying, then he is a liar and fabricator who deserves the punishment of fabricators.

As for the statement:

“If people saw the Real, they would see neither a worshipper nor one worshipped,”

this is of the same kind as the statements of the heretical adherents of unity, who make no distinction between the Lord and the servant;

---

## Page 760

and their views have already been explained. These people combine error with depravity: the depraved appetites of their bellies and sexual organs, together with trials that lead astray.

A hadith reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“What I fear most for you is the depraved appetites of your bellies and sexual organs.”

This can go so far that one of them becomes infatuated with beardless youths and claims that the Lord Most High has manifested Himself in one of them. They say, “He is the monk in the hermitage,” and, “These are manifestations of beauty.” One of them kisses a beardless youth and says, “You are Allah.”

It is reported that one of them used to have intercourse with his son and claim that he was Allah, Lord of all worlds, or that he had created the heavens and the earth. One of them says to his companion, “You created this, and you are He,” and other such things. May Allah disgrace a group whose god, whom they worship, is the very one with whom they have intercourse and whom they take to bed! Upon them is the curse of Allah, the angels, and all mankind. Allah will accept neither sarf nor ʿadl from them.

Anyone who says, “Their words contain a hidden secret and an inward truth, and this belongs to realities accessible only to the most select of the select among creation,” is one of two kinds of men: either one of the foremost heretics devoted to unbelief and al-mahāll, or one of the foremost people of ignorance and error. The heretic must be put to death. The ignorant person is to be informed of the true state of affairs; if he persists in this false belief after the proof has been established against him, he must be put to death.

---

## Page 761

But their words do have a hidden secret and an inward reality known only to the select among creation. This secret is even more steeped in unbelief and heresy than their outward meaning. Their doctrine contains subtleties, obscurities, and hidden meanings that many people may not understand. Thus, you find many ordinary people of religious devotion, goodness, and worship reciting Ibn al-Fārid’s poem, responding to it with displays of ecstasy, and venerating it, supposing that it expresses the words of people of monotheism and spiritual knowledge. Yet they understand neither the poem nor its author’s intent.

Likewise, groups of people renowned for learning and religious devotion hear these people’s words without understanding their true meaning. They may withhold judgment or describe their doctrine in terms that reflect a failure to understand its true nature. Alternatively, they may reject it in general terms without knowing what it really means, and so forth. This is how most people stand in relation to them.

When their leaders see someone who has not understood the true meaning of their doctrine, they see an opportunity in him and say:

“He is one of the scholars of outward forms, one of those concerned with outward appearances and mere husks.”

They say, “This knowledge of ours can be attained only through unveiling and direct witnessing, and this requires certain conditions.” They also say, “This is not your nest; move along,” and similar things that exalt this knowledge, arouse a longing for it, and portray anyone who has not attained it as ignorant.

If, however, they see that he understands their doctrine, they claim him as one of their own and say:

“He is one of the greatest masters of spiritual knowledge.”

---

## Page 762

And if he openly denounces them and declares them unbelievers, they say:

“He has assumed the role of one who objects, to complete the ranks and manifestations.”

They say the same about the prophets and their prohibition of idol worship. All this, and similar things, I have seen and heard from them. Their misguidance is immense, their lies grievous, and their deception profound. Allah Most High makes manifest the guidance and the religion of truth with which He sent His Messenger, to make it prevail over all religion. And sufficient is Allah as Witness. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 763

Section

On what the people of knowledge and faith, both earlier and later, uphold, which resembles the false doctrines of union and indwelling but is true—even if it is called indwelling or union. This is what Muslims, the people of the Sunnah and the community, and those possessing knowledge and certainty among all groups uphold, as evidenced by the Quran and Sunnah.

As for indwelling:

There is no doubt that when someone knows something, an impression and a quality arising from it must remain in his heart. His state after knowing it is not the same as his state before knowing it, as though knowledge were merely a relation, like being above or below. Someone who is above ceases to be above when he descends, and someone who is below ceases to be below when he ascends. Knowledge, however, does not disappear; rather, its effect remains in every case.

If, in addition to knowing it, he loves it, looks to it with hope, or fears it, these states produce a further impression and quality beyond knowledge and awareness, although the two may go hand in hand.

If he mentions it with his tongue, these effects become greater.

If he submits to it with the rest of his bodily faculties, this becomes greater still. In principle, these meanings are common to every perceiver and object of perception, everyone who loves and the object of that love, and everyone who mentions something and what is mentioned—whether this takes the form of worship, such as worshipping Allah

---

## Page 764

alone, with no partner, or worshipping rivals, as do those who take rivals besides Allah and love them as they love Allah; or takes a form other than worship, such as love for brothers, children, women, homelands, and other created things.

The faith of the believer who believes in Allah with his heart and bodily faculties encompasses both his heart’s knowledge and its state: the heart’s affirmation and submission. It also encompasses the words of his tongue and the actions of his bodily faculties. Although the foundation of faith is what is in the heart, or what is in the heart and on the tongue, his heart must affirm Allah and submit to Him. The former is the heart’s speech, and the latter is the heart’s action; this constitutes acknowledgment of Allah.

Knowledge precedes action, perception precedes movement, affirmation precedes submission, and knowledge precedes love, even though the two go hand in hand. Yet the heart’s knowledge necessarily gives rise to its action unless an opposing factor outweighs it, and its action entails its affirmation: neither voluntary movement nor love can arise without awareness. Movement and love, however, may be corrupt if awareness and perception are unsound.

Umar ibn Abd al-Azīz said:

“Whoever worships Allah without knowledge does more harm than good.”

As for righteous inward and outward action, it can arise only from knowledge. This is why Allah and His Messenger commanded worshipping Allah, turning to Him in repentance, devoting religion sincerely to Him, and similar things. These terms encompass both knowledge and action: the heart’s knowledge and its state, though the words of the tongue and the actions of the bodily faculties are also included. The presence of sound branches necessarily entails the presence of their roots. This is clear, and the purpose here is not to elaborate upon it; rather, the purpose is (1).

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 765

Section:

that the believer must have in his heart such knowledge of Allah and love for Him that the One he knows and loves leaves effects in his heart resembling indwelling in certain respects. This is not the indwelling of the very essence of the One known and loved; rather, it is faith in Him and knowledge of His names and attributes. Allah Most High says:

“The Light of the heavens and the earth. The likeness of His light is as a niche…”

—and so the verse continues. Ubayy ibn Kaʿb said:

“The likeness of His light in the believer’s heart.”

These, then, are the lights that arise in believers’ hearts. Concerning His words, Most High:

“Whoever rejects faith, his deeds have become worthless,”

it has been said that this refers to rejecting that faith. Whoever rejects acknowledgment—which consists of affirming Allah, His angels, His Books and His messengers, and submitting to Him, encompassing both belief and submission to the prescription of obligations, the prohibition of forbidden things and the permission of permissible things—is a disbeliever. For the intended benefit to us of the revelation of the Books and the sending of the messengers is that we attain faith. Whoever rejects this therefore rejects those.

This may be called a likeness and a representation, for it may be said that knowledge is a representation of what is known within the knower. Likewise, love involves a representation of the beloved within the lover. Some people claim that every instance of knowledge and love involves this representation, as some philosophers maintain. Others deny that any such representation arises in any instance of knowledge or love.

The precise conclusion is that mental representations and imagined images may arise in some who know and love, to the point that

---

## Page 766

they imagine the form of the beloved, though no sensory image may arise. This representation is not of the same kind as the reality itself at all. Rather, since knowledge corresponds to what is known and accords with it rather than contradicting it, there is a certain correspondence and resemblance between the two, and thus a kind of representation. An analogy is drawn between things because they share certain features; here, too, there is certainly some shared feature and some resemblance.

It has been said that this is what is meant by His words, Most High:

“There is nothing like Him,”

and His words:

“To Him belongs the highest description in the heavens and the earth.”

In a transmitted hadith:

“Neither My earth nor My heaven encompasses Me, but the heart of My believing servant—pure, pious, tranquil and tender—encompasses Me.”

It is also said: “The heart is the house of the Lord.” This is the servants’ share from their Lord and their portion of faith in Him. As one of the early Muslims is reported to have said:

“If any of you wishes to know his standing with Allah, let him consider Allah’s standing in his heart. For Allah accords the servant a standing with Him corresponding to the standing the servant accords Him in his heart.”

This has also been reported as traced back to the Prophet, through Ayyūb ibn Abd Allāh ibn Khālid ibn Safwān, from Jābir ibn Abd Allāh. It was transmitted by Abū Ya‘lā al-Mawsilī and by Ibn Abī al-Dunyā in The Book of Remembrance. For this reason, Jacob’s sons said:

“We worship your God and the God of your fathers, Abraham and Isaac.”

Their hearts’ devotion to Allah as God varies enormously in degree, increasing and decreasing. Their differences in this respect are so great that neither extreme can be precisely delimited. Indeed, it is established in an authentic report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said concerning two men:

“This man is better than an earth full of men like that one.”

Thus, a single individual became

---

## Page 767

among humankind better than an earth full of his own kind; this is an immense disparity, the like of which does not occur among other animals.

This is the meaning indicated by the saying:

“Abū Bakr did not surpass you through greater prayer or fasting, but through something firmly settled in his heart.”

That is certainty and faith. Along the same lines are the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I was weighed against the community and outweighed it; then Abū Bakr was weighed against the community and outweighed it; then Umar was weighed against the community and outweighed it; then the scales were raised.”

He also said, as reported from him by al-Siddīq:

“O people, ask Allah for certainty and well-being, for after certainty no one has been given anything better than well-being.”

This was transmitted by al-Tirmidhī, al-Nasā’ī in The Day and Night, and Ibn Mājah. Raqabah ibn Misqalah said to al-Sha‘bī:

“May Allah grant you the certainty in which alone souls find rest, and upon which alone one can rely in matters of religion.”

In Imam Ahmad’s Book of Asceticism, it is reported from [Sayyār, and Ja‘far narrated to us, from ‘Imrān al-Qasīr] (1), who said:

“Moses said, ‘My Lord, where may I find You?’

“He said, ‘O Moses, with those whose hearts are broken for My sake. I draw nearer to those hearts by a handspan each day; were it not for that, their hearts would burn up.’”

This meaning may be expressed more expansively, to the point that one says, “There is nothing in my heart but Allah; I have nothing but Allah.” Likewise, in the authentic hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, reported that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, said:

“Did you not know that My servant so-and-so fell ill? Had you visited him, you would have found Me with him.”

And it is said:

“He dwells within the heart and fills it;
I do not forget Him and then recall Him.”

And it is said:

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Omitted from the printed edition; restored from Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal’s Kitāb al-Zuhd (Book of Asceticism) (389).

---

## Page 768

“Your image is before my eyes, your remembrance on my lips;
Your dwelling is within my heart—where, then, could you be absent?”

This state can grow immensely powerful, to the point that it is described as manifestation, unveiling, and the like, as all rational people agree. Along with it comes nearness to Him, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The servant is nearest to his Lord when he is prostrating.”

And Allah Most High said in the divine hadith:

“Whoever draws near to Me by a handspan, I draw near to him by a cubit.”

But does the servant’s drawing near to Allah involve movement toward Allah, or toward certain places?

They agree that the servant’s body may move toward certain exalted places where faith in Allah is manifested through knowing Him, remembering Him, and worshipping Him—for example, by making pilgrimage to His House or going to His mosques. This includes Abraham’s words:

“Indeed, I am going to my Lord; He will guide me.”

As for the movement of his soul toward places such as the heavens and elsewhere, the majority of Muslims affirm it, while the Sabian Peripatetic philosophers and those who agree with them deny it. The movement of his soul or body toward Allah is affirmed by those of sound innate disposition and the adherents of the Sunnah and the community, but denied by many practitioners of speculative theology.

As for Allah’s nearness to His servant: does it follow the servant’s drawing near and being brought near—which consists in his knowledge or action—or is there another nearness on the Lord’s part? This is a matter of discussion, but this is not the place for it.

---

## Page 769

Those who affirm only the former hold two views concerning the Lord’s nearness. The first is that it consists in His manifesting Himself and becoming apparent to the servant. The second is that, in addition to this, it includes the servant’s approaching Him and drawing near through his deeds and movement. Nearness also has another meaning: similarity or correspondence, as when one says, “This closely resembles that.” This, however, is not the place to discuss it.

Section

As for what resembles union, two distinct entities cannot become one and the same entity, nor can their respective attributes become one and the same attribute, unless, after uniting, they are transformed into a third entity. This is like the union of water and milk: after uniting, they constitute a third thing, neither pure water nor pure milk.

For them to unite while each remains as it was before is impossible. From this, it is clear that Allah cannot unite with His creation, since His undergoing transformation is impossible. It is only causes and effects that are united in their particular object, while names and attributes are united in kind—as with two people at war with one another who are also intimate friends, one of whom comes to love exactly what the other loves, hate what he hates, take pleasure in what gives him pleasure, and suffer from what causes him suffering. This has innumerable levels and degrees; thus, their names and attributes have come to be of the same kind.

---

## Page 770

and the particular effects and causes associated with them—for example, the object of love or hatred—are one and the same, as with the Messenger whom all believers love. They are united in loving him in the sense that the one they love is the same, and one person’s love is of the same kind as another’s—not that they are the very same love. This concerns people’s union with one another: the brotherhood and intimate friendship of faith concerning which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The believers, in their mutual love, mercy, and compassion, are like a single body: when one limb suffers, the rest of the body responds with fever and sleeplessness.”

This is recorded in both Sahīh collections. Thus, one believer’s relationship to another is likened to that of one limb to another, both joined by a single soul.

For this reason, Allah refers to a fellow believer as one’s own self in several places in the Book and the Sunnah. He, Most High, said:

“So do not claim purity for yourselves.”

And He said:

“There has certainly come to you a Messenger from among yourselves.”

And He said:

“Allah has certainly bestowed His favour upon the believers by sending among them a Messenger from among themselves.”

And He said:

“So greet yourselves.”

And He said:

“So kill yourselves.”

When a believing servant turns to his Lord, worships Him, and acts in accord with Him, until he loves what his Lord loves, hates what his Lord hates, commands what his Lord commands, forbids what his Lord forbids, is pleased with what pleases his Lord, is angered by what angers his Lord, gives to those to whom his Lord gives, and withholds from those from whom his Lord withholds, he is the servant concerning whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, as reported by Abū Dāwūd through al-Qāsim from Abū Umāmah:

“Whoever loves for Allah’s sake and hates

---

## Page 771

for Allah’s sake, gives for Allah’s sake, and withholds for Allah’s sake has perfected his faith.” This servant’s religion has become wholly devoted to Allah, and he has fulfilled the worship for which he was created. Thus, the effects and causes of these attributes of his are united with the effects and causes of the Lord’s attributes. Servants attain this to varying degrees: if one is a prophet, he has a degree of accord with Allah that others do not; the messengers rank higher still, and the messengers of firm resolve higher yet. Our Prophet Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has the highest rank of nearness to Allah in every station. This accord is the permissible form of union, whether obligatory or recommended. It is concerning such things that the texts of the Book and the Sunnah speak.

Allah, Most High, said:

“Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are actually pledging allegiance to Allah. The hand of Allah is over their hands.”

And He said:

“Allah and His Messenger are more worthy that they should please Him.”

And He, Most High, said:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.”

And He, Most High, said:

“Indeed, those who offend Allah and His Messenger.”

And He, Most High, said:

“Dearer to you than Allah and His Messenger.”

And He, Most High, said:

“Say, ‘The spoils belong to Allah and the Messenger.’”

The Messiah’s saying—if this wording is authentically reported from him—also falls into this category:

“I and my Father are one. Whoever has seen me has seen my Father.”

The same applies to similar sayings, for this resembles His saying, Most High:

“Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are actually pledging allegiance to Allah.”

And His saying:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.”

And other such expressions whose wording admits more than one interpretation.

---

## Page 772

Section

Something of this kind is also reported concerning the “friends of Allah,” who are the God-fearing. Al-Bukhārī narrated in his Sahīh from Abū Hurayra that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah Most High says: ‘Whoever shows hostility to a friend of Mine has challenged Me to war. Nothing by which My servant draws near to Me equals his fulfillment of what I have made obligatory upon him. My servant continues drawing near to Me through voluntary acts of worship until I love him. When I love him, I become his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees, his hand with which he strikes, and his foot with which he walks. If he asks Me, I will surely give him; if he seeks My protection, I will surely protect him. I do not hesitate over anything I am to do as I hesitate over taking the soul of My believing servant: he dislikes death, and I dislike causing him distress, yet he must undergo it.’”

The first statement in the hadith is His saying:

“Whoever shows hostility to a friend of Mine has challenged Me to war.”

He thus makes hostility toward His servant who is His friend hostility toward Himself. His enemy and His servant’s enemy are one and the same, and hostility toward His friend is the very same hostility toward Him: these are not two distinct things. Yet Allah is not His servant, nor is the aspect of hostility toward His servant identical to the aspect of hostility toward Himself. Rather, the two coincide only in kind.

He then said:

“When I love him, I become his hearing, his sight, his hand, and his foot.”

In a narration outside the Sahīh, it reads:

“Through Me he hears, through Me he sees, through Me he strikes, and through Me he walks.”

Thus, His words:

---

## Page 773

“Through Me he hears, through Me he sees, through Me he strikes, and through Me he walks”

explain the meaning of His words:

“I become his hearing, his sight, his hand, and his foot.”

This does not mean that He becomes the very eyeball, fatty tissue, nerve, or foot. Rather, He remains the One sought through these organs and faculties, and in this respect He occupies their place. For the servant’s perception and movement depend upon his organs and faculties. When his perception and movement are through the True One, this does not refer to the creation of perception and movement, since that is common to those whom He loves and those whom He does not love. Rather, the beloved servant has his rightful share of this assistance from the True One, in proportion to his share in Allah’s being with His servants, His lordship, and His divinity; for each of these has both a general and a particular form.

In his Sahīh, Muslim narrates from Abū Hurayra that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah Most High says: ‘My servant, I was ill, but you did not visit Me.’ He replies: ‘My Lord, how could I visit You when You are the Lord of all worlds?’ He says: ‘Did you not know that My servant so-and-so was ill? Had you visited him, you would have found Me with him. My servant, I was hungry, but you did not feed Me.’ He replies: ‘My Lord, how could I feed You when You are the Lord of all worlds?’ He says: ‘Did you not know that My servant so-and-so was hungry? Had you fed him, you would have found that with Me.’”

In this hadith, He mentions the two true meanings, excludes the two false meanings, and explains them. His words:

“I was hungry and ill”

are language of union that affirms the true meaning. His words:

“You would have found Me with him, and you would have found that with Me”

deny identity of essence by excluding the false meaning, and affirm the distinction between the Lord and the servant.

---

## Page 774

And His words:

“You would have found Me with him”

are an expression denoting location. Each expression affirms the true meaning of true indwelling—through faith, not in essence—and explains His words:

“I was ill, but you did not visit Me.”

For if the Lord were identical with the sick person and the hungry person, then, upon visiting and feeding that person, one would have found Him to be that very person, and found that He had eaten the food.

There is a fine distinction between His words concerning the sick person:

“You would have found Me with him,”

and His words concerning the hungry person:

“You would have found that with Me.”

The sick person whom it is recommended to visit, and with whom one finds Allah, is the believer in his Lord who is in accord with his God and bound to Him in devoted friendship. As for the person receiving food, the wording may encompass every hungry person whom it is recommended to feed, for Allah says:

“Who is it that will lend Allah a good loan, so that He may multiply it for him many times over?”

Thus, anyone who gives obligatory or recommended charity has lent to Allah, glorified is He, through what he has given to His servant.

It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever gives in charity the equivalent of a date from wholesome earnings—and Allah accepts only what is wholesome—Allah takes it in His right hand and nurtures it as one of you nurtures his foal or weaned camel calf, until it becomes like a mighty mountain.”

He also said:

“Charity indeed reaches the hand of the True One before it reaches the hand of the beggar.”

The more likely interpretation, however, is that the servant mentioned in connection with hunger is the same as the one mentioned in connection with illness: the servant who is Allah’s devoted ally, in whom there is a kind of union, even though Allah also rewards feeding an openly sinful person or a non-Muslim under Muslim protection.

A parallel to lending is giving support, as in the words of the Most High:

“And so that Allah may know who supports Him

---

## Page 775

and His messengers, though He is unseen,” and His words:

“If you support Allah, He will support you,”

and similar passages. Support, however, involves a certain meaning; yet in such a case one does not say, “I was hungry.” Allah has mentioned lending and support in the Quran and attributed both to Himself: the former concerns provision, and the latter support. Visiting the sick is mentioned in the hadith. These three matters are mentioned in His words, Most High:

“and those who remain patient in poverty, hardship, and battle,”

and His words:

“They were afflicted by poverty and hardship and were shaken.”

The hadith concerns only poverty and hardship, because these are matters that the individual addressed in His words can attend to alone:

“My servant, I was ill and hungry.”

For this reason, He reproached him. Support, however, ordinarily requires a number of people, so generally no particular individual is reproached over it. Alternatively, the purpose of the hadith is to draw attention to these matters.
