The Quran mentions support and provision, but not visiting the sick, because support and lending have a general scope and are not restricted to one person rather than another. Visiting the sick, however, is only for those with whom one finds the True One.

---

## Page 776

Section

These two meanings are sound and firmly established; indeed, they constitute the very essence of religion, certainty, and faith. The first—Allah being in a person’s heart through knowledge and love of Him—is obligatory upon everyone and indispensable to every believer. If he fulfills his obligation in this regard, he follows the middle course; if he neglects part of it, he wrongs himself; and if he abandons it entirely, he disbelieves in his Lord.

The second—being in accord with his Lord concerning what He loves and dislikes, what pleases Him and what angers Him—in its fullness belongs only to the foremost, those brought near to Allah. They draw near to Allah through voluntary acts of worship, which He loves but has not made obligatory, after fulfilling the obligations that He loves, requires, and punishes those who abandon.

Thus, when these people performed the inward and outward words and deeds beloved to the True One, encompassing knowledge, spiritual states, and burdens, Allah Most High loved them. He said:

“My servant continues to draw near to Me through voluntary acts of worship until I love him.”

They did what He loved, so He loved them. For recompense corresponds to the deed, bearing the same relationship to it as an effect bears to its cause.

It should not be imagined that this means the servant must perform every single action that Allah loves, for that is impossible. Rather, what is meant is that he should perform whatever inward and outward deeds he is capable of;

---

## Page 777

and he can perform more inward deeds than outward deeds, as one of the early generations said:

“The believer’s strength lies in his heart, and his weakness in his body; the hypocrite’s strength lies in his body, and his weakness in his heart.”

For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A person will be with those he loves.”

He also said:

“There are men in Medina who have been with you on every journey you have made and in every valley you have crossed; a valid excuse kept them behind.”

He also said, “The two are equal in reward,” in the hadith concerning the person able to spend and the one unable to do so, who said, “If I had wealth like so-and-so’s, I would use it as he does.” Since their deeds of the heart were equal, and one of them had a valid excuse for being unable to act physically, their recompense was equal. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a servant falls ill or travels, he is credited with deeds like those he used to perform when healthy and not travelling.”

---

## Page 778

Section:

Some people who are overwhelmed by a spiritual state may fall into a form of divine indwelling or union. Union has both true and false senses. Yet when something comes upon a person that deprives him of reason or makes him oblivious to everything except his beloved, and this is not due to any sin on his part, he is excused and is not punished for it as long as he lacks reason. For an insane person is exempt from accountability until he regains his senses. Even if he is mistaken in this, he falls within His words:

“Our Lord, do not impose blame upon us if we have forgotten or erred.”

And He said:

“There is no blame on you for what you do by mistake.”

This resembles the story told of two men, one of whom loved the other. The beloved fell into the sea, and the other threw himself in after him. The first said, “I fell in, but what made you fall in?” He replied, “I became so absorbed in you that I lost awareness of myself, and thought that you were me.”

This state affects many of those devoted to love and spiritual aspiration, whether directed toward Allah or toward something else, although it involves deficiency and error. A person becomes so absorbed in his beloved that he loses awareness of his love and of himself; in the one he remembers, that he loses awareness of his remembrance; in the one he knows, that he loses awareness of his knowing; in the one he witnesses, that he loses awareness of his witnessing; and in the one whose presence he experiences, that he loses awareness of his own existence. At that point, he no longer perceives distinctions or his own existence.

In this state he may say, “I am the Truth,” “Glory be to me,” “There is nothing in this robe but Allah,” or similar things, while intoxicated by the ecstasy of love—a pleasure and joy unaccompanied by discernment.

---

## Page 779

As for someone in this state of intoxication, his utterances should be passed over, not repeated, provided that his intoxication was not caused by something prohibited. If its cause was prohibited, however, he is not excused.

Among those who hold to divine indwelling are some who become so overwhelmed by the heart’s witnessing and the manifestation it experiences that they imagine they have seen Allah with their physical eyes. This is why some otherwise sound worshippers have mistakenly reported such an experience. It is authentically reported in Sahīh Muslim from al-Nawwās ibn Sam‘ān:

“When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, spoke of the Dajjāl and his claim to lordship, he said: ‘Know that none of you will see his Lord until he dies.’”

The same meaning has also been reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through several other sound chains of transmission in accounts concerning the Dajjāl.

When the Dajjāl claimed lordship, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, mentioned two distinguishing signs evident to everyone. The first is that the Dajjāl is blind in one eye, whereas Allah is not blind in one eye. The second is that none of us will see his Lord until he dies. He mentioned this specifically concerning the Dajjāl, despite his being a disbeliever, because extraordinary feats occur at his hands that strengthen the misleading impression in the hearts of ordinary people.

---

## Page 780

Section

Once particular union has been understood, including what resembles indwelling or a union containing an element of truth, the corresponding issues concerning absolute union also become clear.

We therefore say:

There is no doubt that Allah is the Lord of all worlds, the Lord of the heavens and the earths and all that lies between them, and the Lord of the Mighty Throne; the Lord of the East and the West—there is no deity but Him, so take Him as your Trustee; your Lord and the Lord of your forefathers; the Lord of mankind, the King of mankind, the God of mankind.

He is the Creator of all things, and He is the Trustee over all things. He created the two sexes, male and female, from a sperm-drop when emitted. He is the Lord and Sovereign of everything, and the Owner of all dominion. He grants dominion to whom He wills and takes dominion away from whom He wills; He honours whom He wills and abases whom He wills. All good is in His hand, and He has power over all things.

To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens, whatever is on earth, whatever lies between them, and whatever is beneath the soil. The Most Merciful rose over the Throne. To Him belongs all dominion, and to Him belongs all praise, and He has power over all things.

“There is no creature but that He holds it by its forelock. Indeed, my Lord is on a straight path.”

The hearts and forelocks of His servants are in His hand. There is no heart except that it is between two fingers of the Most Merciful. If He wills to set it aright, He sets it aright; and if He wills to cause it to deviate, He causes it to deviate. And He is the One who

---

## Page 781

causes laughter and weeping and grants wealth and possessions. He is the One who sends the winds as heralds of His mercy and sends down water from the sky, reviving the earth after its death and spreading throughout it creatures of every kind. He is

“the One who created the heavens and the earth and made darkness and light; yet those who disbelieve equate others with their Lord.”

“Whoever Allah wills to guide, He opens his heart to Islam; and whoever He wills to lead astray, He makes his heart tight and constricted, as though he were climbing into the sky. Thus Allah places defilement upon those who do not believe.”

“He is Allah; there is no deity except Him. To Him belongs all praise in this life and the Hereafter. His is the judgement, and to Him you will be returned.”

He is the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all, whom neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes. He upholds justice and watches over every soul and what it earns. He is the Creator, the Originator, the Fashioner.

“There is no creature on earth whose provision does not rest with Allah.”

What Allah has willed! There is no power except through Allah. Whatever Allah wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not come to pass. There is neither might nor power except through Allah, and there is no refuge from Him except with Him.

These meanings, and others like them, concern His Lordship, sovereignty, creation, provision, guidance, aid, beneficence, kindness, governance and workmanship. Connected with these is the fact that He knows all things and has power over all things, and that He is All-Hearing, All-Seeing. Hearing one thing does not distract Him from hearing another; requests do not confound Him, nor does He grow weary of those who persist in their pleas. He sees the black ant crawling across a solid rock in the darkness of night.

All of this is true, and it is the pure affirmation of His oneness in His Lordship. Along with this, He has given each thing its form and then guided it; He has made everything He created excellent and began the creation of man from clay.

---

## Page 782

and this is the workmanship of Allah, who has perfected everything. All good is in His two hands. He is the Most Merciful of those who show mercy, and He is more merciful to His servants than a mother is to her child, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, affirmed with an oath:

“By Allah, Allah is more merciful to His servants than this mother is to her child.”

These and similar meanings affirm His all-encompassing wisdom, the perfection and excellence with which He created everything, and the vastness and greatness of His mercy, which precedes His wrath. All of this is true.

These two principles—the all-encompassing nature of His creation and Lordship, and the all-encompassing nature of His beneficence and wisdom—are two great principles. Yet some people disbelieve in part of the first, such as the Qadariyyah, who exclude human actions from His creation and attribute them solely to the action of an agent endowed with choice; or those who appeal to nature, who sever the attribution of action to Allah, glorified is He, and attribute it instead to an inherent nature, a body endowed with such a nature, a celestial sphere, a soul, or some other created thing. These creatures cannot sustain themselves and are even less capable of sustaining anything else.

Others deny part of the second principle or turn away from it, imagining that some of His creatures lack the excellence and perfection of His workmanship and His wisdom, and supposing His mercy to be limited and powerless. These include the Iblīs-like or Magian Qadariyyah and others.

Since this is so, all existing things are signs of Him: they bear witness to, indicate and reveal the most beautiful names and exalted attributes that rightfully belong to Him. He created all existing things in accordance with what His names and attributes entail. Thus, the tie of kinship (rahim) is a bond stemming from the All-Merciful (al-Rahmān): He created the tie of kinship and derived its name from His own; and He is the Provider

---

## Page 783

, the Possessor of Power, the Ever-Firm, who provides for whomever He wills without measure. He is the Guide and Helper: He guides whomever He wills to a straight path and helps His messengers and those who believe, both in this worldly life and on the Day when the witnesses rise. He is the All-Wise, the All-Knowing, the Merciful, who has made manifest effects of His knowledge, wisdom, and mercy that none but He can enumerate.

He is the Lord of all worlds, and the worlds are filled with the effects of His names and attributes. Everything glorifies Him with praise, but you do not understand their glorification. Some people, through knowledge and understanding, perceive the indications and testimony these things contain. Those whose hearing Allah opens hear the mountains and birds repeating His praises and understand the speech of birds.

If His manifestation and self-disclosure are interpreted in this sense, this is correct. However, the terms “manifestation” and “self-disclosure” are ambiguous, as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills.

If someone says, “I have never seen anything without seeing Allah before it,” because He is its Lord, and the Lord precedes the servant; or, “I saw Allah after it,” because it is His sign, evidence pointing to Him, and a witness to Him, and knowledge of what is indicated follows knowledge of the evidence; or, “I saw Allah in it,” meaning that the effects of the Maker are manifest in His handiwork—then this is correct.

Indeed, the entire Quran explains this and points to it. This is the religion of the messengers and the path of those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous. It is the belief of the Muslims who belong to Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah, and of those included among them: people of knowledge and faith, endowed with understanding and certainty—the pious allies of Allah.

---

## Page 784

Section:
Errors in This Matter

Many of those who turn toward Allah, when they devote themselves to remembering Him, worshipping Him, and returning to Him in repentance, perceive in their hearts this all-embracing lordship and this encompassing of all things. For He encompasses everything, and He—glorified be He—is the Truth who created the heavens and the earth. Among His signs is that the heaven and the earth stand by His command. The sun, the moon, and the stars are subjected to His command. Surely, creation and command belong to Him. He created the heavens and the earth and everything between them only for a true purpose. He—glorified be He—is the Light of the heavens and the earth:

“Allah is the Light of the heavens and the earth. The parable of His light is that of a niche containing a lamp”—to the end of the verse.

With Him—glorified be He—there is neither night nor day. The light of the heavens comes from the light of His Face. This is what ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd said.

“He does not sleep, nor is it fitting for Him to sleep. He lowers and raises the balance. The deeds of the night are raised to Him before the deeds of the day, and the deeds of the day before the deeds of the night. His veil is light—or fire. Were He to remove it, the radiance of His Face would burn everything in His creation that His sight reaches.”

This is what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the hadith reported by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim, narrated by Abū Mūsā.

---

## Page 785

Thus, a servant may perceive the element common to created things—the reality present in them and encompassing them—and suppose that it is the Creator, because it resembles Him in being universal in a certain respect. Yet it is only His handiwork and creation. He may then ascend to one of His veils of light or fire and suppose that it is He, then ascend to His light and the manifest effects of His attributes. Some of these people may thus fall into something akin to the doctrine of absolute, universal union of Creator and creation.

But if Allah comes to their aid in His mercy, and they hold fast to the rope of Allah and follow His guidance, they realize that all this is created by Allah, that the Creator is not the creation, and that they are all servants of Allah.

Sometimes this may happen to someone in a state of loss of self-awareness or spiritual intoxication. He is then mistaken and in error, although this is forgiven him if it arises from a cause that is not prohibited, as we have mentioned in the analogous case of union with a particular individual.

---

## Page 786

Section

Just as he witnesses His lordship, His all-encompassing governance of the world, His wisdom and His mercy, so too does he witness His universal divinity. For He is the One who is God in heaven and God on earth—God in heaven and God on earth.

“All who are in the heavens and the earth ask of Him; every day He is engaged in a matter.”

Likewise, His words:

“And He is Allah in the heavens and on the earth.”

This verse is understood according to one of the two interpretations, following those who pause at His words, “and on the earth.” The meaning is that He is Allah in the heavens and Allah on earth; there is none in either who is Allah other than Him. Although this resembles His words:

“And He is the One who is God in heaven and God on earth,”

it is more emphatic. A comparable statement is His saying:

“Had there been gods in them other than Allah, both would have fallen into ruin.”

He has also said:

“To Him belongs the highest attribute in the heavens and the earth, and He is the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

And He, exalted is He, has said:

“The seven heavens, the earth, and all who are in them glorify Him. There is not a thing that does not glorify Him with praise, but you do not understand their glorification.”

And He has said:

“Do they seek a religion other than Allah’s, when all who are in the heavens and the earth have submitted to Him, willingly or unwillingly, and to Him they will be returned?”

And His words, exalted is He:

“To Allah prostrates whoever is in the heavens and the earth, willingly or unwillingly, as do their shadows in the mornings

---

## Page 787

and the evenings.” And His words:

“Have you not seen that to Allah prostrates whoever is in the heavens and whoever is on earth, as do the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountains, the trees, the animals, and many people?”

And His words, exalted is He:

“To Him belongs whoever is in the heavens and the earth; all are obedient to Him.”

“And He is the One who originates creation, then repeats it, and that is easier for Him. To Him belongs the highest attribute in the heavens and the earth.”

And His words:

“Whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth glorifies Allah, and He is the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

“Whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth glorifies Allah, the Sovereign, the Most Holy, the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

These and similar passages express His divinity, the humility and submission of created beings before Him, their need for Him, their requests of Him, and creation’s invocation of Him—through worship, through petition, or through both together.

As for those who turn away from Him when free to choose:

“When harm touches you at sea, all those you invoke vanish except Him.”

“Who answers the distressed when he calls upon Him?”

We witness that every object of worship other than Him, from His Throne to the depths of His earth, is false, except His noble Face. Just as we witness that all created things depend on Him for their beginning, we witness that they depend on Him for their end; otherwise, they would be without reality. These are the meanings that express created beings’ worship of Him and their attachment to Him.

These meanings, together with the earlier meanings expressing His lordship over them and His creation of them, require us to recognize that He is the Lord of mankind, the Sovereign of mankind, the God of mankind, and the Lord of all worlds. There is no god but Him. Created beings possess nothing of themselves; rather, they are pure nonexistence and utter negation. Whatever existence they possess is from Him and through Him.

---

## Page 788

Moreover, their final destination and return are to Him. He is their object of worship and their God. None but Him is worthy of worship, just as none but Him created them, because of the attributes of divinity that are His by right and His alone. He has no partner in these attributes, no peer, and nothing is like Him.

He is the First, before whom there is nothing; the Last, after whom there is nothing; the Outward, above whom there is nothing; and the Inward, nearer than whom there is nothing. He is with us wherever we are. We know that His being with His servants takes various forms, and that they occupy different degrees in this regard.

The same applies to His lordship over them and their servitude, through which they are made subject to Him; to their taking Him as their God, His being their God, and their worship, through which they are worshippers; and likewise to His nearness to them and their nearness to Him.

---

## Page 789

Section

This, then, concerns what resembles union or indwelling in a particular individual, such as a prophet, a righteous man, or someone similar. We have explained what it contains of pure truth and of truth mingled with falsehood. Allah willing, we shall explain what it contains of pure falsehood.

This category occurs only in someone who worships Allah, glorified is He, and is devoted to Him, or who is thought to do so. Through this, Allah’s divinity becomes manifest in His servant, as do the servant’s turning to his Lord and his concordance with Him in what He loves and approves, commands and forbids.

Another category may be confused with this: the effects of His lordship that the Lord manifests in some of His servants, even though this is neither something He has commanded nor an act of worship of Him. Examples include the share of His dominion and authority that He grants to certain rulers invested with power, who may or may not be Muslims, such as Pharaoh, Genghis Khan, and others like them; the provision and wealth He bestows upon some of His servants; and the beauty He apportions to some of His servants, both men and women. Likewise, there is the knowledge and understanding He bestows, the states He grants, or what He gives him

---

## Page 790

of extraordinary phenomena, including various forms of unveiling and influence, whether these people are believers or unbelievers, such as the one-eyed Dajjāl and others like him. In this category, the effects of lordship and the workings of divine power are more manifest in a particular servant than in others, just as the effects of divinity and the prescriptions of religious law are more manifest in those belonging to the first category than in others.

Both categories may come together in a servant, as they do in the angels, prophets, and allies of Allah, such as our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Messiah, son of Mary, and others. This category alone suffices with respect to the effects of the creative words, just as the first category does with respect to the effects of the religious words. For events occur only through Allah’s will and power.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would seek and take refuge in Allah’s perfect words, beyond which neither the righteous nor the wicked can pass, and would instruct others to seek refuge in them. Neither the righteous nor the wicked can escape the words by which Allah brought all things into being. Thus, no dominion, authority, wealth, beauty, knowledge, state, unveiling, or exercise of influence exists except through His will, His power, and His perfect words. Yet some of this is beloved to Allah and commanded by Him, while some is hated by Allah and forbidden—rather, permissible or pardoned.

Since it occurs through Allah’s will, power, and word, and none but He has the power to bring it about, it is attributed to Allah with respect to His lordship and dominion. It therefore shares enough common ground and resemblance with the first category that some people have erred concerning Allah’s command, treating it as one and the same in both categories.

---

## Page 791

Worse still, they also erred concerning the Lord Himself, equating some of the servants held in subjection in the second category with some of the worshipping servants in the first. Through this confusion, they embraced the doctrines of union and indwelling, to the point that some worshipped Pharaoh and the Dajjāl, while others worshipped beautiful forms and the like, claiming that these were manifestations of beauty. These people rejected worship and faith at times, and the One worshipped at others.

Since our purpose here is to explain the truth of these matters, or whatever truth they contain, we have mentioned this.

As for the first: Allah, glorified be He, has distinguished, through the Quran and faith, between His religious command and His creation of all that exists. Allah, glorified be He, is the Creator, Lord, and Sovereign of everything, including all beings, their attributes, and their actions. Whatever Allah wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not come to pass. Nothing falls outside His will, and nothing occurs except by His will.

Some of this has been denied by the Magian Qadariyya, both within this community and outside it. They claim that Allah did not create the actions of His servants—angels, jinn, human beings, and animals—and that He cannot bestow any more good upon His servants than He has already bestowed upon them. Indeed, they claim that He has no power over their actions, and thus is not powerful over all things; or that evil deeds occur contrary to His will and intent. They are misguided innovators who oppose the Quran, the Sunnah, the consensus of the early generations of this community, and what is known through reason and experience.

Then another group, worse than they, opposed them: the polytheistic Qadariyya, who regarded actions

---

## Page 792

as occurring by His will and power. They therefore said:

“Had Allah willed, neither we nor our fathers would have associated partners with Him, nor would we have forbidden anything.”

“If Allah disliked anything, He would remove it. Nothing exists in the world except what Allah loves and approves of. There is no one who disobeys, and I reject a Lord who can be disobeyed. Even if this person has disobeyed the command, he has obeyed the will.” Sometimes they invoke compulsion, claiming that the servant is compelled, that someone compelled is excused, and that the act is Allah’s, performed within the servant, rather than the servant’s own; thus, he incurs no blame.

These people undoubtedly disbelieve in Allah’s Books and Messengers, His commands and prohibitions, His reward and punishment, His promise and threat, and His religion and revealed law. Their disbelief is greater than that of the Jews and Christians—indeed, greater than that of the Sabians and Brahmins who uphold rational systems of governance. For these people reject divinely revealed religions and laws, as well as signs and rational systems of governance.

As for the former group, declaring them unbelievers requires distinctions that are not to be discussed here. These people are enemies of Allah and of all His Messengers—indeed, enemies of all rational human beings, and even enemies of themselves.

No one can consistently apply this position or act upon it for even an hour, since it entails neither resisting an oppressor’s injustice nor punishing an aggressor or a wrongdoer, whether with a punishment equal to his wrongdoing or greater than it. Most of these people invoke this only when it serves their own desires, to deflect blame from themselves. Otherwise, when they have such a grievance against someone, they confront him, fight him, and even transgress against him, and they do not stop

---

## Page 793

at any limit, nor do they honor any bond of kinship or covenant with a believer. Rather, they are as Allah says:

“But man undertook it; indeed, he was unjust and ignorant.”

They are unjust and ignorant, like a predatory beast, acting purely according to their desires. They deflect blame and censure, or evade their obligation to enjoin good and forbid evil, by invoking a false notion of compulsion and focusing on the divine decree that inevitably takes effect, while disregarding commands and prohibitions. Yet they do not apply the same reasoning to those who assault, wrong, or harm them—not even to those who neglect their rights, nor even to those who obey Allah by enjoining what Allah commands and forbidding what He prohibits.

I have discussed these Qadariyyah and the first group at length, and have addressed the Iblīs-like Qadariyyah elsewhere. The purpose here is merely to draw attention to the central tenets of these positions.

In His Book, Allah distinguishes between the two categories: those in whom His creative words are realized and those who follow His religious words. This distinction applies to His command, will, decree, judgment, permission, raising up, and sending forth.

Concerning His religious command prescribed in His revealed law, He says:

“Indeed, Allah commands justice, good conduct, and giving to relatives.”

“Indeed, Allah commands you to return trusts to their rightful owners.”

“Indeed, Allah commands you to slaughter a cow.”

Concerning His creative command pertaining to divine decree, He says:

“His command, when He wills something, is only to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

“The command of Allah has come, so do not seek to hasten it.”

The same applies, according to one interpretation, to His saying:

“And when We intend to destroy a town, We command its affluent inhabitants, and they transgress within it.”

Concerning His religious will expressed in His revealed law, He says:

“Allah intends ease for you and does not intend hardship for you.”

---

## Page 794

“Allah intends to make clear to you and guide you to the ways of those before you and accept your repentance.”

“Allah does not intend to place any hardship upon you.”

Concerning His creative will pertaining to divine decree, He says:

“Whoever Allah wills to guide, He opens his heart to Islam; and whoever He wills to lead astray, He makes his heart tight and constricted.”

“My advice will not benefit you, even if I wish to advise you, if Allah intends to lead you astray.”

“Those are the ones whose hearts Allah did not intend to purify.”

By considering these usages together and distinguishing between them, the confusion concerning the command prescribed in the revealed law is dispelled: does it necessarily entail the creative will or not? The correct conclusion is that it does not necessarily entail the creative will pertaining to divine decree, although it does necessarily entail the religious will expressed in the revealed law.

Concerning His religious permission, He says:

“Whatever date palms you cut down or left standing on their roots, it was by Allah’s permission.”

Concerning His creative permission, He says:

“They cannot harm anyone through it except by Allah’s permission.”

Concerning His religious decree, He says:

“Your Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him.”

That is, your Lord has commanded this.

Concerning His creative decree, He says:

“And He completed them as seven heavens in two days.”

Concerning His religious judgment, He says:

“O you who have believed, fulfill your contracts. Made lawful

---

## Page 795

to you are livestock, except for what is recited to you; hunting is not permitted while you are in a state of ihrām (ritual consecration). Indeed, Allah ordains what He wills.”

He also says:

“That is Allah’s judgment; He judges between you.”

And He says:

“Is it the judgment of ignorance they seek? And who is better than Allah in judgment for a people who are certain?”

Concerning His creative judgment, He says:

“I will never leave this land until my father permits me or Allah judges for me, and He is the best of judges.”

Both kinds of judgment may be encompassed together, as in His saying:

“Judgment belongs to Allah alone.”

The same applies to His judging:

“And Allah judges in truth.”

Concerning the two kinds of sending forth and of sending, He says:

“It is He who sent forth among the unlettered a Messenger from among themselves.”

“We sent forth against you servants of Ours possessing great military might.”

And His sayings:

“Indeed, We have sent you as a witness, a bearer of good tidings, and a warner.”

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs.”

He has also said:

“Do you not see that We have sent the devils against the disbelievers, inciting them relentlessly?”

And He says:

“And We sent the winds as fertilizing agents.”

---

## Page 796

Section

As for their disbelief concerning the One worshipped:

If they are drawn by desire to certain created beings, they may worship them on the mistaken pretext of false indwelling or union. Such is the case of someone who worships beautiful forms and says, “This is a manifestation of Beauty,” or worships a tyrannical king whom people obey and says, “He is a manifestation of Majesty,” or “a divine manifestation,” and similar things.

These created beings involve no union or indwelling in the true sense. Yet, in one respect, they resemble cases that do contain truth: both exist through Allah, come from Allah, and belong to Allah. For this reason, proponents of unrestricted indwelling and union equate the two, as we shall explain, Allah willing.

These proponents of union and indwelling—who restrict it to certain created beings in whom there is neither worship nor reward for it—are an offshoot of those others. They possess no truth, nor even a semblance of truth such as those others have in ambiguous expressions transmitted from some prophets and righteous people. Rather, what these people have is Pharaoh’s saying:

“I am your most exalted lord,”

and:

“I know of no god for you other than myself,”

and the Dajjāl’s saying:

“I am your lord,”

and similar statements.

Thus, the expressions they rely upon are those of disbelievers and hypocrites. They also liken matters pertaining to Allah’s creative decree to matters pertaining to His religion. Matters pertaining to His creative decree are universal, with no particular distinction among beings. Hence, in belief and speech, these people are more thoroughly committed to unrestricted union and indwelling than to union and indwelling restricted to particular beings, although, from the standpoint of

---

## Page 797

their states and desires, they single out certain individuals—as indeed happens—because of the mistaken impression that such an individual enjoys a distinctive status under certain aspects of the divine creative decree. We shall discuss them, Allah willing, when addressing false indwelling. I have mentioned them here only because I wished to discuss everything containing an element of union or indwelling with some basis in truth. I therefore drew attention to this so that the point at which they go astray might be recognized.

Once the true nature of these matters is understood, the true meaning of the Prophet’s saying, peace and blessings be upon him, becomes clear:

“The truest words ever spoken by a poet are the words of Labīd: ‘Indeed, everything apart from Allah is vain.’”

For bātil (the false or vain) is the opposite of haqq (the true or right), and Allah is the Manifest Truth. Haqq has two meanings: first, that which exists and is established; second, that which is intended and beneficial, as in the Prophet’s saying:

“The Witr prayer is right and proper.”

Bātil likewise has two meanings:

First, that which does not exist. If something does not exist, believing that it exists and reporting its existence are false. Belief and a report depend upon the thing believed in and reported: they are true if it is true, and false if it is false. Thus, if the thing believed in and reported is false, the belief and the report are likewise false; this is falsehood.

Second, that which is neither beneficial nor useful, as in the Most High’s saying:

“We did not create the heaven and the earth and everything between them in vain.”

And the Prophet’s saying:

“Every amusement indulged in

---

## Page 798

by a man is vain except his shooting with his bow, training his horse, and playing with his wife, for these are right and proper.”

And his saying about Umar:

“This is a man who does not like what is vain.”

As for that which offers no benefit, commanding it is vain, as are intending it and doing it; for acting upon it, directing one’s intention toward it, and commanding it are vain.

This is also the sense intended when scholars say that acts of worship and contracts are either valid or invalid. A valid act or contract is one that produces its intended effect and achieves its purpose. An invalid one does not produce its intended effect or achieve its purpose.

This is why the deeds of the disbelievers are vain. A disbeliever, insofar as he is a disbeliever, believes in something that does not exist and makes assertions about it, which is false. He worships that whose worship brings him no benefit, acts for its sake, and enjoins this upon others, which is likewise vain. Yet because they have deeds and statements, they come to resemble the people of truth. Thus the Most High says:

“But those who disbelieve—their deeds are like a mirage on a level plain, which a thirsty man thinks is water, until, when he comes to it, he finds it to be nothing, but finds Allah there, who pays him his account in full. And Allah is swift in reckoning.”

And the Most High says:

“Those who disbelieve and turn others away from the path of Allah—He has rendered their deeds fruitless.

“But those who believe, do righteous deeds, and believe in what has been sent down to Muhammad—and it is the truth from their Lord—He has removed their misdeeds from them and set their affairs right.

“That is because those who disbelieve follow falsehood, while those who

---

## Page 799

believe follow the truth from their Lord. Thus Allah sets forth for people their examples.” The passage continues to His saying:

“Do not render your deeds vain.”

He also says:

“And We shall turn to whatever deeds they have done and make them scattered dust.”

And the Most High says:

“Do not invalidate your charities with reminders of your generosity and hurt, like one who spends his wealth to be seen by people and does not believe in Allah and the Last Day. His likeness is that of a smooth rock covered with dust: a downpour strikes it and leaves it bare. They can gain nothing from what they have earned.”

He thus makes clear that reminders of one’s generosity and causing hurt invalidate charity, rendering it invalid rather than valid, just as showing off and lack of faith also invalidate spending.

He makes this general in His saying:

“Do not render your deeds vain.”

That is, do not make them worthless, yielding no benefit, reward, or gain. A group of people among the Ittihādiyya and others, such as Ibn ʿArabī, have erred by regarding truth as that which exists, so that everything that exists is true.

They said, “There is nothing false in the world, since there is no nonexistence in the world.” They also said, “Disbelief is merely the nonexistence of a partner, for example.” Their error arose from an ambiguous expression.

For a thing has two levels of consideration. The first concerns the thing itself: either it exists and is therefore real, or it does not exist and is therefore unreal. The second concerns its existence in minds, on tongues, and through the fingers—that is, knowledge and speech

---

## Page 800

and writing. Beliefs, statements, and written accounts depend on the thing they concern: if they correspond to it and agree with it, they are true; otherwise, they are false. Thus, if we describe what is real and existent as real and existent, and what is unreal and nonexistent as unreal and nonexistent, the statement and the belief are true. If we describe them the other way round, they are false—even though the statement and the belief themselves exist. Their truth or falsehood is determined by the reality they describe, not by their own existence. It is not permissible to call something true without qualification merely because it exists, unless a contextual indication clarifies what is meant.

Likewise, an action, an intention, or a command is valid only with respect to its intended outcome. If that outcome is achieved and is beneficial, it is valid; if the outcome is not achieved, or if what is achieved brings no benefit, it is futile. On these two grounds, things that are false or futile do exist, as the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus demonstrate, together with the corroborating evidence of reason, spiritual experience, and unveiling—contrary to the claim of this misguided group that leads others astray.

Allah Most High says:

“He sends down water from the sky, and valleys flow according to their capacity. The torrent carries swelling foam, and similar foam arises from what they heat in the fire to make ornaments or utensils. Thus Allah illustrates truth and falsehood. As for the foam, it is swept away; but what benefits people remains in the earth. Thus Allah sets forth parables.”

---

## Page 801

He likens the faith and Quran that descend from heaven upon hearts and mingle with doubts and misleading desires to rain whose torrent carries foam, and to gold, silver, iron, and similar metals that carry dross when melted in fire. This foam and dross are then cast far from the heart. He makes the foam an image of falsehood, which brings no benefit. By contrast, the water and metals that benefit people represent beneficial truth, which settles and remains in the heart.

The words of Allah Most High have already been cited:

“Those who disbelieve and turn others away from the path of Allah—He will render their deeds fruitless,”

through to His words:

“That is because those who disbelieve follow falsehood, while those who believe follow the truth from their Lord. Thus Allah sets forth their examples for people.”

He, glorified be He, explains why He rendered the deeds of those who disbelieved fruitless, so that they brought them no benefit, whereas the deeds of those who believed benefited them, expiating their sins, and Allah set their affairs right: the former followed falsehood in speech and action, in belief and in the course they pursued, in statements and commands. The latter followed the truth from their Lord; they did not follow what came from other than their Lord, even if it was true in some respect. This confirms what we have said: a statement depends on what it describes, and an action depends on its intended outcome. If that is false and has no reality, what depends upon it is likewise false or futile, even though it exists.

The same applies to His previously cited words:

“Do not nullify your charitable deeds,”

and His words:

“Do not nullify your deeds,”

and similar passages concerning the nullification of deeds that have already occurred and existed. Their nullity consists in the absence of benefit, not in the nonexistence of the deeds themselves: the deeds have come to an end, just as deeds that have not been nullified have come to an end. How, then,

---

## Page 802

can it be said that there is no falsehood in existence? Then he uses this as a pretext for claiming that the existent reality containing both truth and falsehood is Allah Himself, because He is the Real, without distinguishing between the Real who is the Creator and what is real but created? Reflect, then, on how such words encompass these two false premises, and how they have led weak minds astray through this specious argument.

They argued: The saying,

“Indeed, everything except Allah is vain,”

means that what is vain is nonexistent. Thus, everything other than Allah is nonexistent, whereas what exists is not nonexistent. Hence, nothing other than Him exists; what is other than Him is merely nonexistence.

This rests upon the two false premises.

The first is their claim that what is false is nonexistent. This is not so. Rather, what is nonexistent is false, but not everything that exists is false. Among existing things, some are true and some are false, as explained above: deeds that bring no benefit, statements that are untrue, and the intentions and beliefs that fall within these two categories.

The second: if nothing were false except what is nonexistent, then what exists would be real. Anything that exists may indeed be called real, with a contextual qualification explaining the intended sense, insofar as it exists—even though it is false because it lacks the reality that would justify calling it real. Yet there are two kinds of reality: the Real who is the Creator, and what is real but created.

---

## Page 803

And the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would say when he rose at night, as reported in the hadith narrated by Ibn ‘Abbās and recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

“O Allah, to You belongs all praise. You are the Lord of the heavens and the earth and all who are in them. To You belongs all praise. You are the Light of the heavens and the earth and all who are in them. To You belongs all praise. You are the Sustainer of the heavens and the earth and all who are in them. You are the Real; Your word is true; Your promise is true; Paradise is real; Hell is real; the prophets are true; and Muhammad is true. O Allah, to You I submit, in You I believe, upon You I rely, and to You I turn. With Your aid I contend, and to You I submit for judgment.”

Once it becomes clear that some existing things are false in reality, while others are real creations of Allah but are not Allah Himself, the deception in their claim becomes apparent: “What is false is what is other than Him, and that is nonexistence; as for what exists, it is He Himself.”

Moreover, the hadith itself is evidence against them. The saying,

“Indeed, everything except Allah is vain,”

is a general statement encompassing everything that exists other than Allah. For the word “thing” encompasses everything that exists, by common agreement. It also includes nonexistent and impossible things that have an existence in the mind, in speech, or in written form, even though they have no actual existence. This explicitly establishes that many existing things are vain. It cannot mean, “Everything nonexistent, except Allah, is vain,” for five reasons:

First: Allah Most High, who is the manifest Real, has been excepted from an affirmative statement. An exception of this kind indicates that the one excepted falls within the scope of the general expression, unlike an exception made from a non-affirmative statement

---

## Page 804

as in His saying:

“They have no knowledge of it except the following of conjecture.”

Such an exception does not indicate inclusion. If the intended meaning were, “Everything nonexistent, except Allah, is vain,” it would follow that the Real, Most High, is nonexistent. This is the most utterly false of claims.

Second: “Everything” explicitly includes existing things; by common agreement, it cannot be restricted to nonexistent things.

Third: According to the people of the Sunnah and rational people generally, what is nonexistent does not fall within the expression “everything,” let alone constitute its exclusive meaning.

Fourth: If the meaning were, “Everything nonexistent is vain,” this would be a mere tautology. Indeed, the word “nonexistence” indicates negation more clearly than the word “vain.” How, then, could what is clear be explained by what is obscure?

Fifth: Had he intended this, he would have said, “Everything other than Allah is vain.” This wording would more readily admit the meaning intended by these heretics than the actual wording does, although even that expression does not indicate their intended meaning.

Since the hadith does not mean what they claim, it is now clear that everything other than Allah is vain in the two senses of vanity explained earlier.

The first concerns a beneficial object of pursuit. What is vain is that which brings no benefit when pursued. Anything other than Allah—if intentions and deeds are directed towards it—is vain, and the command to do so

---

## Page 805

is vain. This resembles the condition of the polytheists who worshipped something other than Allah, or worshipped Allah without His command or His prescribed law.

If it is said, “What is vain is the intention and action themselves, not the object intended,” I reply: Rather, the object itself is vain in respect of the purpose for which it is intended, as stated in the hadith:

“I bear witness that every object of worship, from Your Throne to the depths of Your earth, is vain except Your noble Face.”

The explanation is this: in its original sense, what is vain is what is nonexistent, and what is nonexistent is what is negated. A thing may thus be negated because it does not exist at all, as in His saying, Most High:

“He neither begets nor is born.”

“And there is none comparable to Him.”

And:

“There is nothing like Him.”

And His saying:

“Allah has not taken any son, nor has there ever been any god alongside Him.”

And His saying:

“There is no god but Allah.”

And the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“There is no prophet after me.”

A thing may also be negated because it lacks its benefit, its intended purpose, and the distinctive characteristic that makes it what it is, as we have explained. For whatever has no benefit is vain, and what is vain is nonexistent. An example is the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when he was asked about soothsayers:

“They are of no account.”

Another example is His saying—exalted is He, exalted is He:

“O People of the Scripture, you have no ground to stand upon until you uphold the Torah, the Gospel, and what has been revealed to you from your Lord.”

A thing may also be negated because it lacks perfection and completeness, either absolutely or in comparison with something else, as in the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The needy person is not the one who goes around begging and is turned away with a morsel or two, or a date or two. Rather, the needy person is the one who does not have sufficient means to sustain himself

---

## Page 806

nor is his need noticed so that charity is given to him, and he does not beg people persistently.” Similar examples include his sayings concerning the bankrupt person and the person bereft of children. There are many examples of each of these three categories.

Something sought for a particular purpose is vain and counts as nonexistent when its benefit and intended purpose are absent. Nothing other than Allah may rightly be worshipped or sought for help. Thus, this intended purpose is absent from everything other than Allah, and it is therefore vain. Nothing other than Allah may rightly be a master to whom one turns, or an object of worship. There is no benefit in seeking it, nor any advantage in worshipping it or seeking its help: it is therefore vain. This is clear, and it is a universal statement admitting no exceptions.

The explanation is that everything other than Allah is sought either for its own sake or for the sake of something else.

Things sought for the sake of something else include bread sought for eating, clothing for wearing, weapons for defence, and the like—the material things Allah has created for the benefit of humankind. These are sought only for the sake of something else, not for their own sake. Likewise, wealth used to obtain a benefit or avert harm is sought for the sake of something else, not for its own sake. Whenever something is sought for the sake of something else, that other thing is what is truly sought.

The means is desired for the sake of that end. Unless that end, sought for its own sake, is attained, the means has no benefit or use. It therefore falls within the category of vain things whose significance is denied and of which it is said: “It is of no account.” It is vain and is treated as nonexistent.

---

## Page 807

It follows, then, that every pursuit is vain unless it attains an end sought for its own sake. If that end is not Allah, it too is vain, for what is sought for its own sake is what is worshipped. When someone worships anything other than Allah, it is vain, and his worship is vain, because there is no benefit in it or in worshipping it; rather, that is pure harm.

Allah Most High says:

“He invokes one whose harm is closer than his benefit.”

This applies to every object of worship other than Allah. This is the very essence of religion. Allah created His creatures only to worship Him alone, without any partner, and subjected to them whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth so that they might use it to help them worship Him. Whoever does not use these things to help him worship Allah—all his work and his purpose are vain and yield no benefit; rather, they cause harm. Thus, every pursuit and every object sought other than Allah is vain, whether it is sought for its own sake or for the sake of something other than Allah. What is true is to seek Allah, or to seek what helps one seek Allah.

This establishes the meaning of the saying:

“Indeed, everything apart from Allah is vain,”

in one of the two senses of what is true and what is vain: namely, with regard to what is intended and sought. This is the more apparent of the two senses.

The second:

Everything apart from Allah is nonexistent in itself. Of itself, it has no existence, movement, action, or benefit to offer another, for all of that is Allah’s creation, origination, bringing into being, and fashioning. Thus, if Allah leaves things to themselves, they are vain. Nothing more is needed for their nonexistence and nullity than His leaving them to themselves and not sustaining them through His creation and provision. Since they are vain in themselves—while what is real belongs to Allah alone, is through Allah, and comes from Allah—the saying:

“Indeed, everything apart from Allah is vain,”

is true in two respects:

---

## Page 808

first, that His handiwork, on this understanding, is neither independent of Him, nor sustained by anything other than Him, nor outside Him. It is therefore included under His name as a dependent adjunct, not because it forms part of what the name denotes. Inclusive designations and general names often encompass things as adjuncts, rather than as parts of what they denote. For example, if someone says, “I have sold you this horse,” its horseshoes are included.

Similarly, if someone says, “Zayd entered my house,” his clothes fall within the scope of his name. The same applies to “I carried Zayd” and “Zayd rode the animal.” When “the descendants of Hāshim” are mentioned, their clients are included, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A people’s client is one of them.”

An ally or a sister’s son may also be included among them. This is well known in Arabic usage and among historians of the Prophet’s campaigns.

The second respect is that, when someone says, “The people came, except Zayd,” khalā here is a defective verb belonging to the same grammatical class as kāna (to be), and Zaydan is put into the accusative by it. The verb contains a nominative pronoun referring back to mā, which is a relative pronoun like al-ladhī (who or which). This clause is the relative clause attached to mā. The underlying wording is: “The people who are without Zayd stood up.” However, mā can refer to one, two, or a whole group, and the pronoun referring back to it more often follows its grammatical form than its meaning.

Thus, ra’aytu mā ra’aytuhu min al-rijāl (“I saw those whom I saw among the men”) is better than saying mā ra’aytuhum min al-rijāl (“those whom I saw among the men”). Likewise, the construction wa-minhum man yastami‘u ilayka—“And among them are those who listen to you”—is more common and more eloquent than man yastami‘ūna (“those who listen”).

For this reason, the construction became firmly established, so that mā khalā Zaydan (“except Zayd”) stands in place of al-ladhī khalā (“the one who is without”), al-ladhīna khalaw and al-lātī khalawna—the corresponding masculine and feminine plural forms—and similar expressions. Thus, you say: “The women stood up, except Hind.” The word mā either has a syntactic position, namely that of an adjectival modifier

---

## Page 809

of what precedes it, or that of an accusative circumstantial qualifier—or it has no syntactic position.

If the underlying wording is: “Everything, when devoid of Allah, is vain”; or “Everything apart from Allah is vain”; or “All things, while apart from Allah, are vain”; or “The things that are apart from Allah are vain”—then their being apart from Allah may carry the sense of their being devoid of Him.

It is known that whenever they are apart from Him—that is, devoid of Him—they are vain. They subsist only by not being devoid of Him; rather, their subsistence is through Him. And this … (1) in its original usage, unlike the other particles of exception.

The basic sense of this meaning is intended by this … (2) in the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

This affirmation of Allah’s oneness, and its explanation in his words,

“Indeed, everything apart from Allah is vain,”

is akin to what is mentioned in the saying of Allah Most High:

“Everything will perish except His Face,”

after His words:

“So do not be a supporter of the disbelievers.”

“And do not let them turn you away from Allah’s revelations after they have been sent down to you. Call people to your Lord, and do not be among those who associate partners with Him.”

“And do not invoke another god alongside Allah. There is no god but Him. Everything will perish except His Face. His is the judgment, and to Him you will be returned.”

His mentioning this after forbidding the association of partners with Him and the invocation of another god alongside Him, and after saying, “There is no god but Him,” entails the more evident of the two interpretations: everything will perish except whatever is for the sake of His Face, whether entities, deeds, or other things.

It is reported that Abū al-‘Āliyah said, “Except that by which His Face is sought,” and that Ja‘far al-Sādiq said, “Except His religion.” The two mean the same.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2) Blank spaces in the original.

---

## Page 810

It is also reported that ‘Ubādah ibn al-Sāmit said:

“This world will be brought forth on the Day of Resurrection, and it will be said, ‘Separate out whatever in it was for Allah.’ Then,” he said, “whatever in it was for Allah will be separated out, and the remainder will be ordered to be cast into the Fire.”

A statement of broader scope is also reported from ‘Alī. In al-Tha‘labī’s Quran commentary, Sālih ibn Muhammad narrates from Sulaymān ibn ‘Amr, from Sālim al-Aftas, from al-Hasan and Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib:

“A man asked him for something, but he gave him nothing. The man then said, ‘I ask you by the Face of Allah.’ ‘Alī replied, ‘You are lying. You have not asked me by the Face of Allah. The Face of Allah is none other than the truth. Do you not see His saying, “Everything will perish except His Face”—meaning the truth? Rather, you have asked me by your own worn-out face.’”

Mujāhid is reported to have said, “Except Him”; al-Dahhāk, “Everything will perish except Allah, Paradise, the Fire, and the Throne”; and Ibn Kaysān, “Except His dominion.”

The word wajh (face) appears originally to have been related to jihah (direction), like wa‘d and ‘idah (promise), wazn (weighing) and zinah (weight), wasl (joining) and silah (connection), and wasm (marking) and simah (mark). In the latter form, however, the initial root consonant is dropped, and its meaning is more specific than the general action, as with akl (eating) and iklah (manner of eating).

Thus, it would be a verbal noun meaning turning towards something or intending it, as the poet said:

I seek Allah’s forgiveness for sins I cannot count—
The Lord of His servants; to Him are directed purpose and deeds.

The word is then used to name the object: that which is intended and towards which one turns. This resembles the use of khalq (creation) for what is created, or the expression “a dirham of the ruler’s minting,” and similar usages. It is also used to name the subject that turns towards something, as with an animal’s face. One says, “I intended this wajh,” meaning this direction or side.

An example is His saying:

“To Allah belongs the east

---

## Page 811

and the west. So wherever you turn, there is the Face of Allah.” That is, the direction of prayer appointed by Allah and the direction towards which He directs people. This is how the majority of the early generations explained it, although some counted it among the verses concerning the divine attributes. It may also indicate the attribute through a line of reasoning that is open to question. His words:

“Wherever you turn”

mean tatawallaw (“you turn towards”), that is, you direct yourselves towards something and face it. The verb takes a single direct object, in the sense of yatawallāhā (“he turns towards it”). Comparable to the pair waliya and tawallā are qadima and taqaddama, and bayyana and tabayyana, as He says:

“Do not put yourselves before Allah and His Messenger.”

And He says:

“With a manifest indecency.”

This is the direction that belongs to Allah and that Allah has commanded us to face. His words:

“To Allah belongs the east and the west”

indicate that the Face of Allah mentioned here is among the directions of east and west that belong to Allah, as in the verse concerning the direction of prayer:

“The foolish among the people will say, ‘What has turned them away from the direction of prayer they used to face?’ Say, ‘To Allah belongs the east and the west. He guides whom He wills to a straight path.’”

Thus, when they asked why people had turned away from the direction of prayer, He declared that the east and the west belong to Him.

As for the word wijhah (“direction”), as in His saying:

“For everyone there is a direction towards which he turns,”

one might likewise suppose that it is a verbal noun, like wajh, just as wa‘dah relates to wa‘d (“promise”), and that it has been left intact without dropping its first root consonant. But this is not so. Had it been a verbal noun, its wāw would have been dropped, yielding jihah. The wording would then have been, “For everyone there is a jihah,” or “a wajh.” Rather, the fi‘lah pattern here denotes the object of the action, as in qiblah, bid‘ah, dhibhah, and similar words.

Thus, qiblah is what is faced; wijhah is what one turns towards; bid‘ah is what is newly introduced; and dhibhah is what is slaughtered. This is why the form remains intact and its first root consonant is not dropped: such elision occurs only in verbal nouns, not in

---

## Page 812

the other classes of nouns, such as adjectives and similar forms, including nouns of place, time and instrument, passive participles, and so forth.

As for the claim made by some jurists that wajh (“face”) is derived from muwājahah (“facing one another”), there is no evidence for it. Others have opposed this claim, saying that it is derived from wajāhah (“eminence”); both views are weak. Rather, muwājahah is derived from wajh, just as mushāfahah (“speaking face to face”) is derived from shafah (“lip”), munāzarah—in the sense of facing one another—from nazar (“looking”), and mu‘āyanah (“direct observation”) from ‘ayn (“eye”).

As for deriving wajh, meaning that which turns towards something, from wajh, meaning the act of turning towards something, this is more plausible. A person’s turning is an action peculiar to him that does not require another party, whereas facing one another requires two parties. A human being acts and forms intentions, and his intention is his turning towards something. It is with this bodily member that he turns towards whatever he desires and directs himself towards.

Among the expressions of this kind are the words of Allah Most High:

“Yes indeed, whoever submits his face to Allah while doing good will have his reward with his Lord.”

And His words:

“Who is better in religion than one who submits his face to Allah while doing good and follows the way of Abraham, inclining to truth?”

And the words spoken by Allah’s intimate friend, by our Prophet, and by the believers in prayer:

“I have turned my face towards Him who created the heavens and the earth, inclining to truth, and I am not one of those who associate partners with Allah.”

And the words of Allah Most High in the verse:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Set your faces aright at every place of worship, and call upon Him, devoting your worship sincerely to Him. Just as He brought you into being, so you will return.’”

And His words:

“So set your face towards the religion, inclining to truth—the natural disposition in which Allah created mankind.”

And His words:

“So set your face towards the upright religion.”

And His words:

“And set your face towards the religion, inclining to truth, and never be one of those who associate partners with Allah.”

And the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace

---

## Page 813

to the man whom he taught the supplication to recite before sleep:

“O Allah, I have submitted myself to You and turned my face towards You.”

Zayd ibn ‘Amr ibn Nufayl said:

“I have submitted my face to Him
To whom the clouds submit, bearing water sweet and pure.”

These, then, are three expressions: “he submitted his face,” “he turned his face,” and “he set his face aright.”

The early Quran commentators explained the words of Allah Most High, “submits his face,” as meaning that he devotes his religion and deeds sincerely to Allah. Some said that he entrusts his affairs to Allah; it has also been explained as submitting and humbling himself before Allah. This third interpretation is appropriate to the intransitive use of “submit.” For his “face” is his intention and orientation, which form the foundation of his deeds. This is the action of his heart, which has command over his body. When his heart turns towards something, his face follows, turning towards it as well. Thus the intention, which is primary and proceeds from the heart—the foundation of action—brings with it the dependent action of the face and the rest of the body, which likewise follow. He has therefore submitted his inward and outward deeds, and his inward and outward organs, to Allah: that is, he has surrendered them to Him and devoted them sincerely to Him. The same meaning is conveyed by the intransitive use of “submit,” as in His words:

“I have submitted to the Lord of the worlds.”

And His words concerning Bilqīs:

“Indeed, I have wronged myself, and I submit with Solomon to Allah, the Lord of the worlds.”

And His words concerning Abraham and Ishmael:

“Our Lord, make us both submissive to You, and from our descendants a community submissive to You.”

That is, obedient and sincere in their devotion.

Likewise, turning one’s face towards Him who created the heavens and the earth means directing one’s intention, will, and worship towards Him. This brings the face and the other bodily members with it. Otherwise, merely turning the bodily organ, without any action of the heart, achieves nothing.

---

## Page 814

Al-Zajjāj said concerning His words, “I have turned my face”: that is, I have directed my intention, through my worship and affirmation of His oneness, to Allah, the Lord of the worlds.

The same applies to His words, “And set your faces aright.” Here, “faces” refers to aims and intentions—the actions of the heart and the foundation of religion—which may be set aright or made to deviate. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There is no heart among the hearts of the servants but that it is between two fingers of the Most Merciful. If He wills to set it aright, He does so; and if He wills to cause it to deviate, He does so.”

Setting one’s face aright is thus the opposite of causing it to deviate or turn aside; this is the straight path. When a person sets his intention aright and directs it correctly, without veering to the right or left, his intention is directed towards Allah, the Lord of the worlds. As He said:

“Neither eastern nor western.”

Likewise, al-Rabī‘ ibn Anas said: “Make your prostration sincerely for Allah”—so prostrate to none but Allah. It is reported from al-Dahhāk and Ibn Qutaybah:

“When the time for prayer comes and you are near a mosque, pray in it. Let none of you say, ‘I will pray in my own mosque.’”

It is as though he meant: Pray to Allah at every mosque; do not single out one mosque over another. On either of these two interpretations, the explanation we have given applies.

It is reported from Mujāhid, al-Suddī, and Ibn Zayd:

“Wherever you are, face the Kaaba when praying.”

According to this interpretation, setting one’s face aright means facing the Kaaba. This, however, is open to question, for this verse was revealed in Mecca, whereas facing the Kaaba was made obligatory only in Medina—unless setting one’s face aright is understood to mean facing the direction one was commanded to face.

---

## Page 815

The disagreement here arose only because of His words, “at every mosque,” unlike His words, “So set your face towards the religion, inclining to the truth.”

Thus, His words, “Everything will perish except His Face,” mean His religion, seeking Him, and worshipping Him. A verbal noun is sometimes attributed to the agent and sometimes to the object of the action. This is expressed in their saying, “Whatever is intended to seek His Face.” It is analogous to His words:

“Had there been gods in the two of them besides Allah, both would have fallen into ruin.”

Every object of worship besides Allah is false, and everything that is not for His sake is perishing, corrupt, and false. The context of the verse indicates this, and it also encompasses the other meaning, for divinity necessarily entails lordship. Hence His words:

“Judgment belongs to Him, and to Him you will be returned.”

There is another view on this, held by many scholars: that “face” in such expressions as “submits his face,” “set your face aright,” and “I have turned my face” means the outward, physical face, just as it does, by common agreement, in His words:

“We have indeed seen you turning your face towards heaven”; “So turn your faces towards it”; and “Wash your faces.”

The Face is also mentioned among Allah’s attributes in various passages of the Quran and Sunnah, but this is not the place to discuss them.

They explain, however, that when the face is directed towards something, the rest of the person follows; when it is submitted, the rest of the person has been submitted; and when it is set aright, the rest of the person has been set aright. This is because, of the outward bodily members, it is the face that turns first towards what a person intends and seeks. For this reason, the face is often mentioned with the rest of the person necessarily implied,

---

## Page 816

and is used to refer to the person as a whole. But is this a literal meaning established by customary usage, which broadens the term from a specific to a general sense? Or does its original linguistic meaning remain, with the whole being conveyed by necessary implication? There are two views on this.

The same applies to the other bodily members. For example, suppose a man says to his slave, “Your hand or your foot is free,” or to his wife, “Your hand or your foot is divorced,” on condition that he is given a thousand, and then cuts off the limb before payment. Those who hold that the expression refers to the whole person consider the divorce and emancipation effective. Those who hold that the term refers only to the limb maintain that emancipation does not extend to the rest of the person, since no partial emancipation has occurred. They therefore hold that neither takes effect in this case.

This same principle underlies the interpretation given by those who explain “Everything will perish except His Face” in this way, as has also been said concerning His words:

“Everyone upon it will perish.”

“And the Face of your Lord, full of majesty and honor, will remain.”

For the abiding of His Face, described as possessing majesty and honor, is the abiding of His very Being.

---

## Page 817

Section

As for the union of the servant’s being with the Lord’s being—or, indeed, the union of one servant’s being with another’s—or one reality dwelling within another, as water dwells within a vessel: this is unquestionably false. Indeed, it is false even of one servant in relation to another: their beings do not unite, nor does the being of either dwell within the being of the other. This is the error into which the proponents of union and indwelling have fallen, among Christians and others, including extremists within this religious community and other communities. It involves a newly arising union between two beings that were distinct and then became united, or the indwelling of one within the other. This is plainly false.

More false still is the assertion of someone who says:

“It has always been one, and there is no multiplicity whatsoever. Multiplicity exists only in the veil. When the matter was unveiled, I saw that I myself and everything are Allah.”

This applies whether he maintains unity without qualification, the unity of absolute existence but not particular existence, or the unity of existence but not of the fixed entities in the state of nonexistence. These doctrines, and those preceding them, are the doctrines of the people of disbelief and misguidance, just as the first view is that of the people of faith, knowledge, and guidance. And as for those who reject what is true in these matters or believe what is false,

---

## Page 818

they stand at opposite extremes, like the Jews and Christians.

As for the believers, they believe what is true in these matters, not what is false. Allah’s Book and His Messenger’s Sunnah contain guidance and light, and explain the straight path: the path of those whom Allah has blessed—the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous.

As for affirming what is true in these matters, this includes what Allah’s prophets and His allies experience—the God-fearing, both those who are foremost and those who follow a middle course—and what any believer may experience, such as their love for Allah Most High and His love for them, their being pleased with Him and His being pleased with them. Allah Most High has said:

“Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him, humble toward the believers and firm toward the disbelievers, striving in Allah’s cause and fearing no blame from anyone.”

And He, Most High, has said:

“Among people are those who take others besides Allah as equals, loving them as they love Allah. But those who believe are stronger in their love for Allah.”

And He, Most High, has said:

“Spend in Allah’s cause, and do not throw yourselves into destruction with your own hands. And do good; indeed, Allah loves those who do good.”

And He, Most High, has said:

“Yes, whoever fulfils his pledge and fears Allah—indeed, Allah loves the God-fearing.”

And He, Most High, has said:

“So long as they remain true to you, remain true to them. Indeed, Allah loves the God-fearing.”

And He has said:

“Fulfil your treaty with them until its term has ended. Indeed, Allah loves the God-fearing.”

And He has said:

“Then approach them from where Allah has commanded you. Indeed, Allah loves those who repeatedly repent and loves those who purify themselves.”

And He has said:

“In it are men who love to purify themselves, and Allah loves those who purify themselves.”

And He has said:

“Then reconcile the two with justice, and act equitably. Indeed, Allah loves those who act equitably.”

And He has said:

“Indeed, Allah loves those who fight in His cause in ranks, as though they were a solidly joined structure.”

And He has said:

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you.’”

And He has said:

“Say, ‘If your fathers and your sons’”

---

## Page 819

up to His words:

“are dearer to you than Allah and His Messenger and striving in His cause.”

And He has said:

“Allah took Abraham as an intimate friend.”

And He has said:

“The first forerunners among the Emigrants and the Helpers, and those who followed them in goodness—Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him.”

And He has said:

“He has inscribed faith in their hearts and strengthened them with a spirit from Him. He will admit them into gardens beneath which rivers flow, to abide therein. Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him.”

And He has said:

“Those are the best of creation.”

“Their reward with their Lord is gardens of everlasting residence beneath which rivers flow, where they will abide forever. Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him.”

And the Prophet said:

“Indeed, Allah loves the servant who is God-fearing, free of want, and unobtrusive.”

“Indeed, Allah is beautiful and loves beauty.”

“Indeed, Allah is clean and loves cleanliness.”

“Indeed, Allah is One and loves what is odd-numbered.”

“Indeed, Allah loves noble character and dislikes base character.”

And he said:

“Indeed, Allah is pleased with three things for you: that you worship Him without associating anything with Him; that you all hold firmly to the rope of Allah and do not become divided; and that you sincerely counsel those whom Allah has placed in charge of your affairs.”

The Quran contains many references to choosing, selecting, bringing near, speaking intimately, calling, intimate friendship, and similar matters. The same is true of the Sunnah. This is among the matters upon which the early generations of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah (the people of the Sunnah and the community), and people of understanding, worship, learning, and faith, agreed.

A group of heretical hypocrites disputed the reality of these matters: those who resemble the Sabians and those who agree with them, and those who resemble the Jews and Christians among the Jahmiyyah, or those influenced by Jahmī doctrines, even if adherence to the Sunnah predominates in them.

---

## Page 820

Thus, at times they deny that Allah takes anyone as an intimate friend, loves anyone, has affection for anyone, speaks to anyone, or speaks at all. They distort words from their proper meanings, interpreting these matters sometimes as His beneficence toward His servants, and sometimes as His will to bestow beneficence upon them. At other times, they deny that Allah loves or takes anyone as an intimate friend. They also distort words from their proper meanings concerning the servant’s love for Him, interpreting it as the desire to obey Him, or as love for Him on account of His beneficence.

As for rejecting falsehood:

Allah has declared Himself free of any parent or child, and has declared those who ascribe to Him a child, a parent, or a partner to be disbelievers. He, Most High, says in the surah that is equivalent to one-third of the Quran and describes the Most Merciful—no other surah of the Quran has as much authentically reported from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, concerning its merit.
