Some people may reach a state in which absorption in the One they worship makes them lose awareness of their worship; absorption in the One they remember makes them lose awareness of their remembrance; and absorption in the One they know makes them lose awareness of their knowledge. If such a person loses his discernment and his reason—to the point that he is exempt from accountability—he is not punished for what he says in this state, even though it is known to be error and misguidance, and a deficient state that does not occur among the friends of Allah.

---

## Page 865

As for the reports that miracles appeared at al-Hallāj’s execution—such as his blood writing “Allah, Allah” on the ground, his showing joy at being executed, or similar things—they are all false. Muslims have compiled accounts of al-Hallāj in many works: Thābit ibn Sinān recorded them in Akhbār al-Khulafā’ (Accounts of the Caliphs), having witnessed his execution; Ismā‘īl ibn ‘Alī al-Hatfī recorded them in Tārīkh Baghdād (History of Baghdad), likewise having witnessed his execution; and the hadith scholar Abū Bakr al-Khatīb recorded them in his history. They were also recorded by Judge Abū Ya‘lā in al-Mu‘tamad, by Judge Abū Bakr ibn al-Tayyib, Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm, and others, and by Abū Yūsuf al-Qazwīnī and Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī in their compilations of accounts concerning him.

Shaykh Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī stated in Tabaqāt al-Sūfiyya (Biographies of the Sufis) that most of the shaykhs excluded him from the spiritual path. Nor did Abū al-Qāsim al-Qushayrī include him in his epistle among the shaykhs whom he counted as masters of that path. We know of no leading Muslim authority, whether among the scholars or the spiritual shaykhs, who spoke well of al-Hallāj. Some people, however, withhold judgment about him because they do not know his circumstances. Even those most favorably disposed toward him say:

“On the basis of his outward conduct, his execution was obligatory; thus, his executioner was striving in Allah’s cause, while the man executed was a martyr.”

This, too, is mistaken.

The claim that he was executed unjustly is false. Muslims agree that his execution was obligatory on account of the heresy he openly manifested. However, because he outwardly professed Islam while secretly revealing his heresy to his followers, he became a covert unbeliever. When he was arrested and imprisoned, he professed repentance. The jurists disagree over whether the repentance of a covert unbeliever is to be accepted. Most do not accept it; this is the position of Mālik and the people of

---

## Page 866

Medina, and the position of Ahmad according to the better-known of the two reports transmitted from him. It is also one of the two opinions in the school of Abū Hanīfa and an opinion within the school of al-Shāfi‘ī. The other opinion is that his repentance is accepted. They agree, however, that when someone of this kind is executed, it cannot be said that he was executed unjustly.

As for the claim:

“Al-Hallāj is one of Allah’s friends,”

anyone who says this is certainly ignorant and speaks without knowledge—even if al-Hallāj had not expressed the views of the heretics. For a friend of Allah is someone who dies in a state of friendship with Allah, whom Allah loves and with whom He is pleased. According to many scholars, or most of them, such testimony may not be given concerning anyone unless the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, testified that he would enter Paradise.

A group of the early generations, including Ibn al-Hanafiyya and ‘Alī ibn al-Madīnī, held that such testimony may not be given concerning anyone other than the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Others said that such testimony may indeed be given concerning someone widely praised among Muslims, because:

“A funeral procession passed by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the people spoke well of the deceased. He said, ‘It is assured, it is assured.’ Another funeral procession passed by him, and they spoke ill of the deceased. He said, ‘It is assured, it is assured.’ He explained: ‘You spoke well of the deceased in this funeral procession, so I said that Paradise was assured for that person. You spoke ill of the deceased in the other funeral procession, so I said that the Fire was assured for that person. You are Allah’s witnesses on earth.’”

Thus, if it is permissible to testify that certain people are inwardly friends of Allah—whether on the basis of an explicit text or the testimony of the Muslim community—al-Hallāj is not among them. The great majority of the Muslim community condemn him and regard him as one of

---

## Page 867

heretics—even if he is able to discern that certain people are friends of Allah, or other such matters accessible only to some righteous people.

Thus, the person who praised al-Hallāj and shared his beliefs is misguided in several respects:

First, no one executed under Islamic law for heresy is known to have been killed unjustly while being a friend of Allah. Al-Jahm ibn Safwān, al-Ja‘d ibn Dirham, Ghaylān al-Qadarī, Muhammad ibn Sa‘īd, known as “the Crucified,” Bashshār ibn Burd the blind, and al-Suhrawardī were all executed, and there are many others like them. Yet scholars and people of religious devotion did not say that these men were killed unjustly or that they were friends of Allah. Why, then, should al-Hallāj alone be treated differently?

As for the prophets, they were killed by the unbelievers, as were the Companions who died as martyrs. ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, al-Husayn, and others like them were killed by Khārijite rebels; they were not executed under Islamic law according to the teachings of the leading jurists, such as Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Abū Hanīfa, Ahmad, and others. These imams unanimously agree that killing those men was forbidden, whereas they unanimously agree that executing al-Hallāj and others like him was lawful.

Second:

Only someone who knows the path to friendship with Allah—faith and piety—can recognize Allah’s friends. Among the greatest requirements of faith and piety is to avoid the doctrines of the heretics, such as those who advocate divine indwelling and union. Whoever agrees with al-Hallāj on such a doctrine has no understanding of faith

---

## Page 868

and piety, and therefore does not understand the path of Allah’s friends; he thus has no right to distinguish between Allah’s friends and others.

Third:

This speaker has stated that he agrees with al-Hallāj’s doctrine, so he belongs to the same category. His testimony that al-Hallāj is a friend of Allah is therefore testimony in his own favor, like the testimony of the Jews, Christians, and Rāfida that they themselves follow the truth. A person’s testimony in his own favor is rejected even when it is not known whether he is lying or telling the truth. How much less can such testimony be accepted in favor of himself and his group, when the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus establish that they are misguided?

Fourth:

It should be said: Whether al-Hallāj repented privately before Allah at the time of his death or did not repent is an unseen matter known to Allah. As for the claim that he uttered these things only while overwhelmed by spiritual ecstasy, that is not the case. Rather, he wrote books and made these statements while fully conscious and alert. It has already been explained that loss of reason is an excuse that suspends accountability. Likewise, a doubt that prevents the proof from being established may constitute an excuse in outward judgment.

Even if this were assumed to be the case, it would not be permissible to say that he was executed unjustly, nor should anyone profess agreement with his beliefs or testify to what they do not know. How much more so when the facts are otherwise! The most a believing Muslim can do, if he seeks to excuse al-Hallāj, is to claim that he was overwhelmed by spiritual ecstasy and affected by doubt. As for agreeing with him on the beliefs for which he was executed, this is the condition of those guilty of covert unbelief and heresy. Likewise, whoever does not regard the execution of someone like him as lawful has departed from the religion of Islam.

---

## Page 869

Our duty is only to understand the monotheism we have been commanded to uphold and the path of Allah we have been commanded to follow. From both, we know that what al-Hallāj said was false and that someone like him must be executed.

As for the particular individual himself, did he inwardly have something—repentance or otherwise—on account of which Allah would forgive him? That is a matter for Allah, and no one needs to know the truth of it. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 870

Shaykh al-Islām and an authority for mankind, Abū al-ʿAbbās Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked about someone who says, “There is nothing but Allah,” to which another person replied, “Anyone who says this has disbelieved.”

He answered—may Allah be pleased with him:

Praise be to Allah.

The statement “There is nothing but Allah” is ambiguous: it may convey either a sound meaning or a false one. If the speaker means that there is no Creator but Allah, no Lord but Allah, and none but Allah answers those in distress and provides for His servants, then He is the One who gives and withholds, lowers and raises, honours and abases. He alone deserves to be sought for help, relied upon, and sought for protection, and it is to Him that His servants turn for refuge. For none can withhold what He gives, none can give what He withholds, and a wealthy person’s wealth is of no avail before Him. As the Most High says in the opening surah of the Quran:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

The Most High also says:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

And He says:

“Say, ‘He is my Lord; there is no deity but Him. In Him I put my trust, and to Him is my return.’”

All these meanings are sound. They express monotheism explicitly, and the Quran teaches them.

---

## Page 871

Thus, His servants should fear none but Allah, as the Most High says:

“So do not fear people, but fear Me.”

The Most High also says:

“Those to whom people said, ‘The people have gathered against you, so fear them,’ but this only increased their faith, and they said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.’”

“So they returned with blessings and bounty from Allah, untouched by any harm.”

Then, further on, He says:

“It is only Satan who frightens you with his allies. So do not fear them, but fear Me.”

Likewise, they should place their hope in none but Allah. Allah the Most High says:

“Whatever mercy Allah opens up for people, none can withhold; and whatever He withholds, none can release after Him. And He is the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

The Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Have you considered those you invoke besides Allah? If Allah intends harm for me, can they remove His harm? Or if He intends mercy for me, can they withhold His mercy?’ Say, ‘Allah is sufficient for me. In Him alone those who trust must put their trust.’”

They should put their trust in none but Allah, as the Most High says:

“And in Allah let those who trust put their trust.”

They should worship none but Allah, as the Most High says:

“They were commanded only to worship Allah, sincerely devoting their religion to Him, inclining to truth, to establish prayer and to give the prescribed charity. That is the upright religion.”

They should invoke none but Allah, as the Most High says:

“The mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone alongside Allah.”

The Most High also says:

“So do not invoke another deity alongside Allah, lest you be among those punished.”

This applies whether their invocation takes the form of worship or of asking.

---

## Page 872

But if, by saying “There is nothing but Allah,” the speaker intends what is meant by those who hold that the Creator and creation are one—namely, that nothing exists except Allah—then they say, “There is only Allah,” meaning that nothing exists except Allah. They say that the existence of created things is the existence of the Creator, that the Creator is the creation and the creation is the Creator, and that the servant is the Lord and the Lord is the servant. These and similar notions belong to those who identify the Creator with creation: they do not distinguish between the Creator and creation, nor affirm that the Lord is separate from the servant. Such notions are found in the writings of Ibn ʿArabī al-Tā’ī, Ibn Sabʿīn, Ibn al-Fārid, al-Tilimsānī, and others who hold this doctrine.

The same applies to those who affirm divine indwelling, as do the Jahmiyyah, who say that Allah Himself is in every place and regard Him as intermingled with created things. They even place Him within dogs, pigs, and impure substances, or identify the existence of these things with His existence. Whoever intends these meanings is a misguided heretic who must be called upon to repent. If he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is to be put to death. And Allah, glorified and exalted, knows best.

---

## Page 873

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Do not revile time, for Allah is time.”

“Does this agree with what is said by those who identify the Creator with creation? Explain this to us.”

He answered:

All praise belongs to Allah. His saying, “Do not revile time, for Allah is time,” is also narrated in other wordings, such as:

“Allah says: ‘The son of Adam offends Me: he reviles time, and I am time. In My hand is all command; I alternate the night and the day.’”

Another wording reads:

“Do not revile time, for Allah is time; He alternates the night and the day.”

And another reads:

“The son of Adam says, ‘Woe to time!’ Yet I am time.”

The statement in the hadith, “In My hand is all command; I alternate the night and the day,” makes clear that it does not mean that He is time itself. He has stated that He alternates the night and the day, while time consists of night and day. Thus, the hadith itself indicates that He is the One who alternates and governs time, as is also indicated by His words, Most High:

“Do you not see that Allah drives clouds, then joins them together, then piles them into a mass, so that you see rain emerge from within them? And He sends down hail from mountains in the sky, striking with it whom He wills and averting it from whom He wills. The flash of its lightning almost takes away the sight.”

“Allah alternates the night and the day. Indeed, in that is a lesson for those who have insight.”

Izjā’ al-sahāb means driving the clouds, and wadq means rain.

---

## Page 874

Thus, He, glorified be He, has explained how He creates rain and sends it down upon the earth, for it is a cause of life on earth. He, glorified be He, made every living thing from water. Then He said:

“Allah alternates the night and the day.”

His alternating the night and the day changes the conditions of the world through the sending down of rain, which is a cause of the creation of plants, animals, and minerals. This, in turn, changes people’s circumstances, raising some and lowering others.

He, glorified be He, has stated in several places that He created time, as in His words:

“And made the darkness and the light.”

And His words:

“And He is the One who created the night and the day, and the sun and the moon, each floating in an orbit.”

And His words:

“And He is the One who made the night and the day succeed one another, for whoever wishes to take heed or wishes to give thanks.”

And His words:

“Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the alternation of the night and the day are signs for people of understanding.”

There are other scriptural texts, too, which make clear that He is the Creator of time. No rational person would imagine that Allah is time itself, for time is the measure of motion. As for motion, its measure belongs to the category of accidents and attributes that subsist in something else, such as motion and rest, blackness and whiteness.

No rational person would say that the Creator of the world belongs to the category of accidents and attributes that depend upon substances and concrete entities. Accidents do not subsist by themselves; rather, they require a subject in which to subsist. Whatever depends upon something other than itself does not exist independently, but through that other thing. It therefore depends upon something other than itself for its very existence. How, then, could it be the Creator?

Furthermore, being self-sufficient, with everything else dependent upon Him, is the attribute of the Creator, glorified be He. How, then, can He be imagined to belong to the former category?

---

## Page 875

The heretics—those who profess oneness, indwelling, or union—do not say that He is time itself, or that He belongs to the category of accidents and attributes. Rather, they say that He is the totality of the world, or that He indwells the totality of the world. Even if the hadith had not explained that He, glorified be He, alternates the night and the day, it would offer them no grounds for their argument. How, then, could it do so when the hadith itself states that all affairs are in His hand and that He alternates the night and the day?

Once this is clear, there are two well-known interpretations of the hadith among the scholars of Ahmad’s school and others.

The first, which is the view of Abū ʿUbayd and most scholars, is that the wording of this hadith was intended to refute what the people of the pre-Islamic age of ignorance and those who resemble them would say. Whenever a calamity befell them or their aims were thwarted, they would begin reviling time. One of them would say, “May Allah disgrace the time that scattered us, and may Allah curse the time in which such-and-such occurred.”

Expressions of this kind frequently appear in the words of poets and others, such as:

“O Time, you have done such-and-such!”

They intend to revile whoever brought about those events, but attribute them to time. Their abuse thus falls upon Allah Most High, for He is the One who brought about those events and caused them to occur. Time is His creation; He is the One who makes it alternate and directs it.

The implied meaning is: “The son of Adam reviles the one who brought about these events, and I brought them about.” Thus, when he reviles time, his intention is to revile the doer, even though he attributes the deed to time. Yet time does nothing; Allah alone is the One who acts.

---

## Page 876

This is like a man against whom a judge rules rightly, or to whom a mufti gives a correct legal opinion, who then begins saying, “May Allah curse whoever issued this ruling or gave this legal opinion!” Yet the ruling or legal opinion may be that of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, so the abuse falls upon him, even though the person uttering it has, in his ignorance, actually attributed the matter to the one conveying it. The one conveying it does perform an act of transmission, unlike time, which Allah makes alternate and directs.

The second interpretation is that of Nuʿaym ibn Hammād and a group of hadith scholars and Sufis who share his view: Dahr is one of the names of Allah Most High, meaning the Eternal, without beginning. They have also reported the wording of certain supplications: “O Dahr, O Dayhūr, O Dayhār!”

This meaning is correct, for Allah, glorified be He, is the First, before whom there is nothing, and the Last, after whom there is nothing. Thus, this meaning is correct; the dispute concerns only whether He may be called Dahr at all. In any case, Muslims unanimously agree—and this is also known through sound reason—that Allah, glorified and exalted be He, is not dahr in the sense of time or anything equivalent to time. For people agree that time consists of night and day. There is likewise an equivalent in Paradise, as the Most High says:

“There they will have their provision, morning and evening.”

They explained this as intervals corresponding to morning and evening in this world. In the Hereafter, Friday is the Day of Increase. Paradise has neither sun nor moon; rather, times are known by other lights, which have been reported to appear from beneath the Throne. Time there is therefore the measure of the movement through which those lights appear.

---

## Page 877

But beyond this, is there a self-subsisting, continuously flowing substance that is dahr (time)? This is a matter over which people have disagreed. A group of philosophizers among Plato’s followers affirmed its existence, just as they affirmed the external existence of abstract universals, known as Platonic Forms and absolute Forms. They also affirmed prime matter, which is matter abstracted from forms, and the void as a self-subsisting substance.

The great majority of rational thinkers, philosophers and others, however, know that none of these has any reality outside the mind. These are merely things that the mind conceives and postulates. Those who fall into error suppose that what exists in their minds exists in precisely the same way outside their minds. They have made the same mistake concerning absolute existence, despite knowing that the absolute, subject to the condition of absoluteness, exists only in the mind. Outside the mind, there are only particular things: individual entities and the attributes that inhere in them.

Thus, there is no place except a body or something inhering in it, and no time except the measure of motion. There is no matter abstracted from forms; indeed, even matter conjoined with forms is nothing other than the body in which accidents inhere. Nor is there any form except an accident inhering in a body, or a body in which an accident inheres.

These matters and others like them are discussed at length elsewhere. The purpose here is merely to draw brief attention to what bears on this subject. And Allah knows best.
